### CHAPTER 3: IMMUNE CELL POPULATIONS IN SEPSIS—MAPPING DYSFUNCTION TO CLINICAL PHENOTYPES

### 3.1 T-Cell Exhaustion and Adoptive Immunotherapy Prospects

### The Central Role of T Cells in Sepsis Immunosuppression

While the early phase of sepsis is dominated by innate immunity (macrophage activation, neutrophil recruitment, complement cascade), the transition to immunosuppression is fundamentally a disorder of adaptive immunity—specifically, a catastrophic loss of T-cell function.

This is not merely theoretical. Autopsy studies of septic patients reveal profound lymphoid tissue atrophy: thymic involution, marked reduction in splenic white pulp, and depletion of lymph node cellularity. Flow cytometric analysis of blood in septic patients shows dramatic lymphopenia (total lymphocyte count often <1,000/μL, compared to normal 1,500–4,500/μL), driven primarily by T-cell loss.

Yet this simple accounting—"fewer T cells"—obscures the more profound pathology: surviving T cells are dysfunctional.

### T-Cell Exhaustion: The Molecular Phenotype

### Definition and Mechanistic Basis

T-cell exhaustion is a state of T-cell dysfunction characterized by:

Reduced effector function: Inability to produce cytokines (IL-2, IFN-γ) upon antigenic stimulation

Reduced cytotoxic capacity: Minimal killing of infected or tumor cells

Reduced proliferative capacity: Limited ability to expand clonally in response to antigen

Altered metabolic state: Energy deficit and anabolic insufficiency

This state is not due to simple "tiredness" that resolves with rest. Rather, it reflects active, transcriptional repression of effector functions—a programmed state of immune suppression.

### The Checkpoint Inhibitor Axis: PD-1, TIM-3, and CTLA-4

Checkpoint inhibitors are molecules expressed on the surface of T cells that serve as "brakes" on T-cell activation. They are essential for preventing excessive immune responses that could damage healthy tissue (autoimmunity). However, in sepsis, their upregulation leads to pathological immunosuppression.

### Programmed Death-1 (PD-1)

Structure and expression:

PD-1 is a transmembrane receptor belonging to the CD28/CTLA-4 immunoglobulin superfamily

Expressed on T cells (both CD4⁺ and CD8⁺), B cells, and NK cells

Expression is upregulated upon T-cell activation, serving as a "brake" to prevent excessive response

Ligands: PD-1 binds two ligands:

PD-L1 (B7-H1): Expressed on APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages), endothelial cells, and cancer cells

PD-L2 (B7-DC): Expressed primarily on dendritic cells and macrophages

Mechanism of immunosuppression:

When PD-1 engages its ligands, it recruits phosphatases (SHP-2, SHP-1) to the T-cell receptor (TCR)

These phosphatases dephosphorylate critical signaling molecules downstream of the TCR (ZAP-70, CD3ζ)

Result: blockade of TCR signaling → reduced IL-2 production → reduced T-cell proliferation and effector function

In sepsis: PD-1 expression on circulating CD8⁺ T cells is markedly elevated compared to healthy controls. PD-1⁺ T cells are dysfunctional (produce minimal IFN-γ), and PD-1 expression correlates with mortality in septic patients.

### T-Cell Immunoglobulin and Mucin-Domain-3 (TIM-3)

Structure and expression:

TIM-3 is a transmembrane receptor expressed on T cells, NK cells, and macrophages

Expression is upregulated on exhausted T cells in chronic viral infections and cancer

Ligands:

Galectin-9: Highly expressed in sepsis; produced by macrophages and endothelial cells

CEACAM-1: Expressed on myeloid cells

Mechanism of immunosuppression:

TIM-3 ligation recruits protein tyrosine phosphatase SHP-2 to the cell membrane

SHP-2 suppresses TCR and IL-2 signaling

Result: reduced T-cell proliferation and cytokine production

In sepsis: TIM-3 expression on CD8⁺ T cells is elevated, particularly in severe sepsis and septic shock. Combined PD-1 and TIM-3 co-expression marks a highly dysfunctional T-cell population with minimal ability to respond to infection.

### Cytotoxic T-Lymphocyte-Associated Protein-4 (CTLA-4)

Structure and expression:

CTLA-4 is structurally similar to CD28 but conveys an inhibitory signal

Expressed primarily on regulatory T cells (Tregs), but also on activated conventional T cells (Tconv)

Ligands:

CD80 (B7-1) and CD86 (B7-2): Expressed on antigen-presenting cells

Mechanism of immunosuppression:

CTLA-4 has higher affinity for CD80/86 than CD28, effectively outcompeting CD28 for ligand binding

CTLA-4 ligation recruits phosphatases and suppresses TCR signaling

CTLA-4 also mediates trans-endocytosis of CD80/86 from APCs, reducing co-stimulation

In sepsis: CTLA-4 expression is increased on Tregs, contributing to their immunosuppressive function.

### The T-Cell Exhaustion Spectrum in Sepsis

Not all T cells in septic patients are equally exhausted. Rather, there exists a spectrum of exhaustion:

### Single Positive (PD-1⁺, TIM-3⁻)

Characteristics:

Early exhaustion marker

Reduced effector function compared to negative T cells, but not completely anergic

Potential for functional restoration with PD-1 blockade

### Double Positive (PD-1⁺TIM-3⁺)

Characteristics:

Advanced exhaustion

Severely reduced cytokine production and proliferative capacity

May require multi-checkpoint blockade (anti-PD-1 + anti-TIM-3) for functional restoration

### Triple Positive (PD-1⁺TIM-3⁺CTLA-4⁺)

Characteristics:

Terminal exhaustion

Minimal residual function

Uncertain whether functional restoration is possible even with checkpoint blockade

### Mechanisms of T-Cell Exhaustion in Sepsis

Multiple mechanisms contribute to the development and maintenance of T-cell exhaustion:

### 1. Chronic Antigen Stimulation

In persistent sepsis (inadequate source control), continuous bacterial antigen exposure drives chronic TCR signaling, which induces exhaustion marker expression. This is distinct from the transient T-cell activation seen in acute, self-limited infections.

### 2. IL-10 and TGF-β Production by Regulatory Cells

Regulatory T cells (Tregs) and myeloid-derived suppressor cells (MDSCs) produce immunosuppressive cytokines:

IL-10 (Interleukin-10):

Produced by Tregs, macrophages, and some T cells in sepsis

Directly suppresses pro-inflammatory cytokine production by T cells

Blocks IL-2 production, essential for T-cell proliferation

Inhibits MHC-II and co-stimulatory molecule expression on APCs

TGF-β (Transforming Growth Factor-Beta):

Produced by Tregs and fibroblasts

Suppresses T-cell proliferation

Promotes Foxp3 expression, favoring Treg differentiation

### 3. Metabolic Exhaustion

Septic T cells exhibit metabolic dysfunction:

Reduced glucose uptake: Despite high glucose availability, T cells show reduced glycolytic capacity

Impaired OXPHOS: Oxidative phosphorylation is reduced, limiting ATP production

Amino acid starvation: High consumption of amino acids (glutamine, arginine) by competing cells and tissues

Lipid accumulation: T cells accumulate lipids (particularly cholesterol esters), indicating dysfunction in lipid metabolism

Mechanistic explanation:

Activated T cells require high energy (ATP) production to support proliferation and effector functions

In sepsis, multiple factors compete for limited resources (glucose, amino acids, lipids)

MDSCs actively consume arginine and glutamine, depriving T cells of essential amino acids

Result: T cells cannot sustain energy production necessary for effector functions

### 4. Apoptosis and Lymphocyte Death

Beyond exhaustion, septic patients experience massive lymphocyte apoptosis, particularly of CD4⁺ T cells. Mechanisms include:

Fas-FasL interaction:

Fas (CD95) is upregulated on T cells during sepsis

FasL (CD95L) is expressed on activated immune cells and endothelial cells

Fas-FasL engagement triggers apoptotic signaling

Extrinsic apoptosis: Engagement of death receptors (TNF-R1, Fas) recruits FADD and caspase-8, directly activating effector caspases (caspase-3, -7)

Intrinsic apoptosis: ROS and mitochondrial dysfunction lead to cytochrome c release and caspase-9 activation

Clinical consequence: Absolute lymphocyte counts drop precipitously in severe sepsis. This lymphopenia is a marker of severity and predictor of poor outcome.

### Immune Checkpoint Inhibition in Sepsis: The Emerging Therapeutic Frontier

### Rationale for Anti-PD-1 Therapy

If T-cell exhaustion driven by checkpoint inhibitor upregulation is a central mechanism of sepsis-associated immunosuppression, then blocking these checkpoints should restore T-cell function and improve outcomes.

Proof of concept in animal models:

Murine models of polymicrobial sepsis (cecal ligation and puncture—CLP model) show that blockade of PD-1 or PD-L1 improves survival

Mechanistically, PD-1 blockade restores CD8⁺ T-cell production of IFN-γ and TNF-α, and enhances CD8⁺ T-cell proliferation

Notably, the benefit is greatest in the late phase of sepsis (days 5–7), when immunosuppression dominates, rather than in the early phase

Human clinical trials:

Phase 1b trial data (2024–2025) of anti-PD-1 monoclonal antibodies (e.g., nivolumab) in septic shock are beginning to emerge

Preliminary results suggest safety and signals of benefit in subgroups of immunosuppressed patients

Full Phase 2/3 trials are currently enrolling

### Challenges and Considerations

Timing of intervention: The critical question is when to administer checkpoint inhibitors. Too early (day 1–2 of sepsis) may paradoxically worsen outcomes by reducing the controlled inflammation necessary for pathogen containment. Too late (day 14+) may miss the window of maximal immunosuppression.

Current evidence suggests days 3–7 as the likely therapeutic window, when immunosuppression is emerging but not yet terminal.

Patient selection: Not all septic patients require immune checkpoint inhibition. Rather, immunophenotyping may identify patients at high risk of immunosuppression (elevated HLA-DR, high exhaustion marker expression, specific pathogen characteristics) who would benefit most.

Risk of autoimmunity: Checkpoint inhibitors, by releasing immunological "brakes," can paradoxically trigger autoimmune responses. In the context of sepsis, anti-PD-1 therapy carries a theoretical risk of precipitating autoimmune complications. However, preliminary data suggest this risk may be acceptable given the mortality of untreated sepsis.

Synergy with other interventions: Anti-PD-1 therapy may be most effective when combined with other immunostimulatory approaches (IL-7, GM-CSF, IFN-γ) or with source control that reduces ongoing antigen stimulation.

### 3.2 Regulatory T Cells and the Paradox of Immunosuppression

### Definition and Function of Regulatory T Cells

Regulatory T cells (Tregs) are a subset of CD4⁺ T cells characterized by:

CD25 expression: The IL-2 receptor α-chain, expressed constitutively on Tregs

Foxp3 expression: The transcription factor Foxp3 (forkhead box P3) drives Treg lineage commitment and maintenance

CTLA-4 and PD-1 expression: Markers of their suppressive function

Immunosuppressive cytokine production: IL-10, TGF-β, and IL-35

Normal function: Tregs comprise approximately 5–10% of circulating CD4⁺ T cells in healthy individuals. They play a critical role in:

Preventing autoimmunity

Maintaining immune tolerance

Controlling excessive inflammation

### Treg Expansion in Sepsis

In septic patients, Tregs undergo marked expansion—often increasing to 15–30% of CD4⁺ T cells by day 3–5 of sepsis. This expansion occurs preferentially in secondary lymphoid organs (spleen, lymph nodes) and can be detected in blood as an elevated percentage or absolute count.

### Mechanisms of Treg Expansion

IL-2 signaling:

Early sepsis produces high levels of IL-2 from activated conventional T cells

IL-2 signaling through CD25 (the high-affinity IL-2 receptor on Tregs) promotes Treg proliferation

This is one of the primary drivers of Treg expansion in the first week of sepsis

TGF-β and IL-10 signaling:

These anti-inflammatory cytokines, produced by macrophages, dendritic cells, and stromal cells, promote differentiation of naive CD4⁺ T cells into Foxp3⁺ Tregs (induced Tregs, iTregs)

The Foxp3 induction is mediated by TGF-β signaling through Smad2/3 and subsequent chromatin remodeling

Pathogen-specific factors:

Some pathogens (particularly gram-positive bacteria and fungi) elicit relatively strong Treg responses

This may reflect evolutionary adaptation of pathogens to evade immune responses through Treg induction

### The Treg Paradox: Protective versus Pathogenic Roles

Tregs present a fundamental immunological paradox in sepsis:

### Protective Role: Prevention of Excessive Inflammation

Tregs are essential for preventing:

Sepsis-induced acute lung injury: Excessive inflammation in the lung can cause diffuse alveolar damage and ARDS. Tregs dampen pulmonary inflammation.

Sepsis-associated vasculitis: Unchecked systemic inflammation leads to vasculitis and multi-organ ischemia. Tregs control this response.

Autoimmune complications: Sepsis can trigger autoimmune responses (e.g., anti-thyroid antibodies, anti-nuclear antibodies). Tregs prevent these complications.

Evidence: Experimental depletion of Tregs in mice with severe sepsis paradoxically worsens mortality, suggesting that Treg-mediated immunosuppression prevents lethal inflammation in the acute phase.

### Pathogenic Role: Suppression of Anti-Pathogen Immunity

Tregs simultaneously impair host defense against pathogens by:

Suppressing CD8⁺ T-cell responses: Tregs produce IL-10 and TGF-β, which directly inhibit CD8⁺ cytotoxic T-lymphocyte (CTL) differentiation and function

Suppressing CD4⁺ Th1 and Th17 responses: These are essential for intracellular pathogens (e.g., Mycobacterium tuberculosis) and extracellular pathogens (e.g., Candida). Treg suppression increases susceptibility.

Suppressing antibody responses: Tregs suppress B-cell activation and antibody production, reducing humoral immunity

Evidence: Experimental enhancement of Tregs in mice with sepsis paradoxically worsens mortality and increases susceptibility to secondary infections.

### Clinical Manifestations of Treg-Driven Immunosuppression

The consequence of excessive Treg expansion is:

Reduced ability to clear primary pathogen: Even with adequate source control, the microorganism persists longer in tissues due to inadequate immune clearance

Prolonged fever and systemic inflammation: The immune response is simultaneously suppressed (reduced bacterial clearance) and inflamed (persistent cytokine production)

Susceptibility to secondary infections: This is the defining feature of late-phase sepsis

Clinical pattern:

Week 1: Patient improves with source control and antibiotics, fever abates, organ function begins recovering

Week 2: Patient re-spikes fever; cultures grow a different organism (Candida, Aspergillus, Pseudomonas, Acinetobacter)

Outcome: If this secondary infection is not rapidly controlled, mortality increases sharply

### Therapeutic Approaches to Treg Dysfunction

### Approach 1: Selective Treg Depletion (Anti-CTLA-4)

Rationale: If excess Tregs drive immunosuppression, reducing their number should restore immunity.

Mechanism: CTLA-4 is expressed constitutively on Tregs (much more so than on conventional T cells). Anti-CTLA-4 monoclonal antibodies (e.g., ipilimumab) can bind CTLA-4 and, through Fc-receptor engagement, promote Treg depletion via antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC).

Challenges:

CTLA-4 is also expressed on activated conventional T cells, so anti-CTLA-4 therapy is not Treg-selective

Risk of unleashing pathological inflammation or autoimmunity

Most experience with anti-CTLA-4 is in cancer (where tumor destruction is desired); the safety profile in sepsis is uncertain

Current status: Experimental; no clinical trials yet in sepsis

### Approach 2: Preservation of Protective Treg Functions While Suppressing Pathogenic Functions

Rationale: Rather than depleting Tregs (which risks excessive inflammation), selectively enhance those Treg functions that suppress pathogenic inflammation while reducing those that suppress anti-pathogen immunity.

Mechanistic insight: Tregs produce both pro-survival signals (IL-10, TGF-β) and pro-death signals (CTLA-4 ligation, PD-1 ligation) on conventional T cells. By modulating specific pathways, it may be possible to retain inflammation suppression while restoring CD8⁺ T-cell function.

Experimental approaches:

JAK inhibitors: JAK signaling downstream of IL-10 and TGF-β receptors drives Treg-mediated immunosuppression. JAK inhibitors might selectively reduce this effect.

IL-2 modulation: High-dose IL-2 (IL-2 therapy) enhances Treg expansion but also enhances conventional T-cell activation. Low-dose IL-2 preferentially expands Tregs; intermediate doses may achieve a balance.

Current status: Preclinical; investigating in animal models

### Approach 3: Functional Restoration of Exhausted T Cells Overrides Treg Suppression

Rationale: If checkpoint inhibitors (anti-PD-1, anti-TIM-3) restore CD8⁺ T-cell function, even in the presence of Tregs, the anti-pathogen immune response may be restored.

Mechanistic insight: While Tregs suppress T-cell activation through IL-10/TGF-β signaling, checkpoint blockade may overcome this suppression by providing strong co-stimulation and TCR signaling.

Evidence: Combination of anti-PD-1 with anti-CTLA-4 in murine sepsis models shows synergistic restoration of T-cell function compared to monotherapy.

Current status: Experimental; early clinical trials combining checkpoint inhibitors underway

### 3.3 Myeloid-Derived Suppressor Cells: Active Suppressors of Antimicrobial Immunity

### Definition and Heterogeneity of MDSCs

Myeloid-Derived Suppressor Cells (MDSCs) are a heterogeneous population of immature myeloid cells that actively suppress immune responses. Unlike simple "immature" cells, MDSCs are characterized by:

Immature morphology: Resemble cells at intermediate stages of myeloid differentiation

Active immunosuppressive function: Produce immunosuppressive mediators and directly inhibit T-cell function

Phenotypic markers: CD33⁺CD11b⁺ in humans; CD33⁺Gr-1⁺ in mice

### MDSC Subtypes

MDSCs are divided into two major subtypes based on morphology and markers:

Granulocytic MDSCs (G-MDSCs):

Markers: CD33⁺CD15⁺ (humans) or CD33⁺Ly6G⁺ (mice)

Morphology: Nuclear segmentation similar to mature neutrophils

Frequency: Comprise 40–80% of total MDSCs

Immunosuppressive mechanisms: Arginase-1, ROS production

Monocytic MDSCs (M-MDSCs):

Markers: CD33⁺CD14⁺ (humans) or CD33⁺Ly6C⁺ (mice)

Morphology: Monocyte-like appearance

Frequency: Comprise 20–60% of total MDSCs

Immunosuppressive mechanisms: IL-10, TGF-β, iNOS, arginase-1

### MDSC Accumulation in Sepsis

In septic patients, particularly those with severe sepsis and septic shock, MDSCs accumulate dramatically in the bone marrow, spleen, and peripheral blood—often comprising 20–40% of peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs), compared to <1% in healthy individuals.

### Drivers of MDSC Expansion

Granulocyte-Macrophage Colony-Stimulating Factor (GM-CSF):

Produced by activated T cells, macrophages, and endothelial cells in sepsis

Promotes emergency myelopoiesis in the bone marrow, releasing immature myeloid cells

Promotes survival and functional activation of MDSCs

G-CSF (Granulocyte Colony-Stimulating Factor):

Produced by macrophages and fibroblasts

Drives neutrophil production in bone marrow

Contributes to emergency myelopoiesis producing immature cells

IL-6:

Elevated in sepsis; promotes MDSC expansion through STAT3 signaling

STAT3 is a key transcription factor maintaining MDSC immunosuppressive function

HMGB1 and DAMPs:

Released from damaged/dead cells

Promote MDSC accumulation and activation

### Mechanisms of MDSC-Mediated Immunosuppression

MDSCs suppress T-cell immunity through multiple overlapping mechanisms:

### 1. Arginine Depletion

Enzyme: Arginase-1, highly expressed by MDSCs

Mechanism:

Arginase-1 catalyzes hydrolysis of arginine to ornithine and urea

Depletes arginine from the microenvironment

T cells require arginine for both proliferation (protein synthesis) and survival (regulation of CD3ζ expression)

Consequence:

T cells in MDSC-rich microenvironments are starved of arginine

CD3ζ (a critical component of the T-cell receptor complex) is downregulated on the T-cell surface

T cells cannot effectively respond to TCR stimulation

### 2. Nitric Oxide (NO) Production

Enzyme: iNOS (inducible nitric oxide synthase), upregulated in MDSCs

Mechanism:

iNOS catalyzes synthesis of nitric oxide from arginine

NO diffuses into adjacent T cells

NO nitrosylates critical proteins in T-cell signaling (e.g., Lck, ZAP-70), disrupting their function

Consequence:

T-cell receptor signaling is impaired

Reduced IL-2 production and proliferation

### 3. Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS) Production

Mechanism:

MDSCs generate ROS through NADPH oxidase (NOX2) complex

ROS directly injure T cells and suppress T-cell function

Specific effects:

ROS damage mitochondrial function in T cells → reduced ATP production → reduced proliferation

ROS trigger apoptosis in T cells

### 4. TGF-β Production

Mechanism:

M-MDSCs produce high levels of TGF-β

TGF-β suppresses T-cell proliferation and promotes Treg differentiation

Consequence:

Further amplification of immunosuppression

### 5. IL-10 Production

Mechanism:

M-MDSCs produce IL-10

IL-10 suppresses pro-inflammatory cytokine production and T-cell function

### 6. Direct Contact-Dependent Suppression

Beyond soluble mediators, MDSCs can suppress T cells through direct contact mechanisms:

Programmed cell death ligand-1 (PD-L1): Some MDSCs express PD-L1, engaging PD-1 on T cells

Lymphocyte activation gene-3 (LAG-3): Some MDSCs express LAG-3 ligand, engaging LAG-3 on T cells

### MDSC Accumulation and Secondary Infections

The dramatic accumulation of MDSCs in sepsis directly correlates with the clinical syndrome of late-phase immunosuppression and secondary infections.

Clinical pattern:

By day 5–7 of sepsis, MDSC frequency in peripheral blood is often 30–40% of PBMCs (compared to <1% in healthy controls)

Simultaneously, absolute CD8⁺ T-cell count is low, and remaining CD8⁺ T cells are dysfunctional (elevated PD-1, low IFN-γ production)

Patients develop secondary infections (Candida, Aspergillus, Pseudomonas) that overwhelm the impaired immune response

### Targeting MDSCs: Therapeutic Approaches

### Approach 1: MDSC Expansion Inhibition

Rationale: If we can prevent MDSC expansion, we maintain T-cell function and prevent immunosuppression.

Mechanism: Block the signals driving MDSC expansion and survival

Specific strategies:

Anti-GM-CSF: GM-CSF is a primary driver of MDSC accumulation. Anti-GM-CSF monoclonal antibodies (e.g., lenzilumab) block this signal.

G-CSF antagonism: Trials blocking G-CSF signaling in sepsis; preliminary data show potential benefit in specific subgroups

JAK inhibitors: STAT3 signaling (activated by IL-6, G-CSF) maintains MDSC immunosuppressive function. JAK inhibitors block STAT3 signaling.

Challenges:

GM-CSF and G-CSF are also essential for normal neutrophil production during infection

Blocking these signals may impair innate immunity and bacterial clearance

Risk of increased bacterial burden if GM-CSF/G-CSF are blocked without concurrent source control

Current status: Several clinical trials underway (2024–2025)

### Approach 2: Restoration of T-Cell Function Despite MDSC Presence

Rationale: If checkpoint inhibitors restore T-cell function, even high MDSC burdens may not prevent anti-pathogen immunity.

Evidence: Combination of anti-PD-1 + IL-7 (which promotes T-cell proliferation) in murine sepsis shows synergistic restoration of T-cell immunity despite high MDSC levels.

Current status: Preclinical and early clinical trials

### Approach 3: Functional Reprogramming of MDSCs

Rationale: Rather than depleting MDSCs (which may impair some beneficial myeloid functions), reprogram them toward pro-inflammatory, pro-immunity functions.

Mechanisms under investigation:

Histone deacetylase (HDAC) inhibitors: Alter MDSC epigenetic state and reduce immunosuppressive function

STAT3 inhibitors: Disrupt STAT3-dependent immunosuppressive gene programs while preserving some myeloid functions

All-trans retinoic acid (ATRA): Differentiation agent that can drive MDSC maturation and loss of immunosuppressive function

Current status: Preclinical

### 3.4 B-Cell Dysfunction and Humoral Immunity Failure

### B-Cell Depletion and Apoptosis in Sepsis

In septic patients, B cells undergo marked depletion through increased apoptosis. Mechanisms include:

Fas-FasL interaction:

Activated B cells upregulate Fas (CD95)

FasL is expressed on activated T cells and neutrophils

Fas-FasL engagement triggers apoptotic signaling in B cells

Reduced survival signals:

IL-4 and IL-10, which promote B-cell survival, are reduced

IL-2, which promotes B-cell proliferation, is reduced

Direct pathogen effects:

Some pathogens produce toxins or superantigens that trigger B-cell apoptosis

Endotoxin (LPS) can directly trigger B-cell death through TLR-4

### Impaired Antibody Responses in Sepsis

Beyond B-cell depletion, surviving B cells show impaired function:

### Reduced Antigen-Specific IgG Production

Normal response to infection: Upon encountering a new pathogen, B cells undergo:

Clonal selection (B cells with high-affinity receptors for the antigen are preferentially activated)

Germinal center formation (in secondary lymphoid organs)

Somatic hypermutation (to increase antibody affinity)

Class switching from IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE

Differentiation into long-lived plasma cells (in bone marrow) and memory B cells (in spleen and lymph nodes)

Sepsis response: These processes are severely impaired:

Germinal center formation is reduced

Somatic hypermutation is ineffective

Class switching is incomplete

Plasma cell differentiation is insufficient

Result: Reduced production of high-affinity, opsonizing IgG antibodies

### Impaired Opsonophagocytosis

Antibodies promote killing of pathogens through opsonization—coating the pathogen surface with antibody, which facilitates recognition by phagocytes (macrophages, neutrophils) through Fc receptors.

In sepsis:

Reduced antibody quantity (fewer molecules available to coat pathogen surface)

Reduced antibody quality (lower affinity due to impaired somatic hypermutation)

Fc receptor dysfunction on phagocytes (see below)

Result: Impaired clearance of pathogens despite presence of antibodies

### Defective Humoral Memory in Sepsis Survivors

Survivors of sepsis frequently have defective immune memory to the pathogen that caused sepsis. This manifests as:

Increased susceptibility to re-infection: Higher rates of recurrent infection with the same pathogen compared to non-septic individuals

Reduced antibody titers to vaccine antigens: Sepsis survivors show reduced response to vaccination (particularly relevant for influenza and pneumococcal vaccines)

Impaired B-cell memory formation: Fewer long-lived plasma cells and memory B cells persist after sepsis

Mechanistic explanation: The profound perturbation of the immune system during sepsis, including B-cell apoptosis and impaired germinal center formation, leaves survivors with a "weak" immune memory to the primary pathogen and reduced capacity to respond to new antigens.

### Clinical Consequences: Recurrent and Secondary Infections

The combination of:

B-cell depletion and apoptosis

Impaired antibody production

Defective humoral memory

...results in reduced antibody-mediated immunity. This contributes to:

Failure to clear primary pathogen if it persists despite source control (e.g., low-abundance biofilm bacteria)

Susceptibility to secondary infections due to reduced opsonization and complement-mediated killing

Prolonged infection due to inadequate immune memory

### 3.5 Neutrophil Dysregulation and NETosis

### Neutrophil Recruitment and Activation in Sepsis

Neutrophils are the most abundant innate immune cells and play a critical role in early sepsis. However

Continue

30 дек. 2025 г.

, their dysregulation contributes significantly to sepsis pathophysiology.

### Early Phase: Neutrophil Recruitment and Activation

In the first hours of sepsis:

Chemotaxis: C5a, CXCL8, and other chemokines recruit neutrophils from bone marrow to infection site

Priming: Bacterial PAMPs and inflammatory mediators prime neutrophils for maximal responses

Activation: Neutrophils produce ROS, proteases, and inflammatory mediators

Outcome: Pathogen killing and localization of infection

### Late Phase: Neutrophil Dysfunction and Death

As sepsis progresses into the immunosuppressive phase:

Reduced antimicrobial function: Neutrophils show reduced phagocytic capacity and ROS production

Apoptosis: Many neutrophils undergo apoptosis (both spontaneous and accelerated by FasL)

Impaired chemotaxis: Neutrophils lose responsiveness to chemotactic signals

Outcome: Loss of antimicrobial effector function despite high neutrophil numbers

### Neutrophil Extracellular Traps (NETs) and Immunopathology

Neutrophils possess a specialized form of cell death called NETosis (or NET-associated cell death) that is distinct from apoptosis or necrosis.

### NETosis Mechanism

When neutrophils encounter pathogens or inflammatory mediators (TNF-α, LPS, complement components), they undergo NETosis:

Membrane permeabilization: The nuclear and granular membranes break down

DNA extrusion: Nuclear and mitochondrial DNA are released

NET formation: The DNA forms a web-like structure containing:

Histones (H1, H2A, H2B, H3, H4)

Granular proteins (elastase, cathepsin G, myeloperoxidase)

Other antimicrobial proteins

Pathogen entrapment: The NET physically traps bacteria, fungi, and parasites

Pathogen killing: Antimicrobial proteins on the NET surface kill trapped organisms

### NET-Mediated Tissue Damage

While NETs effectively trap and kill pathogens, excessive NETosis causes collateral tissue damage:

Histones as danger signals:

Free histones are highly toxic

Extracellular histones directly injure endothelial cells, causing apoptosis and increased permeability

Histones activate TLR-2 and TLR-4 on immune cells, amplifying inflammation

Histone-induced endothelial injury contributes to ARDS and multi-organ failure

Protease-mediated damage:

Elastase degrades tissue elastin and other extracellular matrix proteins

Cathepsin G damages endothelium and tissue structures

Clinical manifestation: Excessive NETosis in early sepsis contributes to endothelial damage, increased vascular permeability, ARDS, and multi-organ dysfunction.

### Regulation of NETosis and Therapeutic Approaches

Inhibitors of NETosis:

DNase: Exogenous DNase can degrade NETs, reducing histone-mediated damage

PAD4 inhibitors: Peptidylarginine deiminase-4 (PAD4) catalyzes histone citrullination, essential for NET formation. PAD4 inhibitors reduce NETosis.

Complement inhibitors: C5a, a potent NETosis trigger, can be blocked by C5a antagonists

Approach: Selective NETosis inhibition in early sepsis to reduce histamine-mediated endothelial damage, while preserving neutrophil antimicrobial function.

Current status: Preclinical studies; potential clinical trials in development

### Summary: The Immune Cell Orchestra in Sepsis

Sepsis represents a profound dysregulation of multiple immune cell populations:

T cells: Undergo exhaustion (PD-1⁺TIM-3⁺CTLA-4⁺) in response to chronic antigen stimulation and immunosuppressive signals from Tregs and MDSCs. Restoration of T-cell function through checkpoint inhibition is emerging as a therapeutic strategy.

Regulatory T cells: Expand to control excessive inflammation, but in doing so suppress anti-pathogen immunity and contribute to secondary infections. The challenge is harnessing their inflammation-suppressive functions while restoring anti-pathogen immunity.

Myeloid-derived suppressor cells: Accumulate dramatically and actively suppress T-cell function through multiple mechanisms (arginine depletion, ROS, NO, TGF-β). Targeting MDSC expansion or function may restore T-cell immunity.

B cells: Undergo apoptosis and produce inadequate antibody responses, reducing humoral immunity and opsonophagocytosis. Impaired B-cell memory contributes to recurrent infections.

Neutrophils: While initially antimicrobially active, undergo dysfunction in late sepsis. Excessive NETosis in early sepsis contributes to endothelial damage.

For the surgeon, understanding these immune cell dysfunctions informs:

Phase-specific immunotherapy: Different immune cell populations dominate different phases; therapy should be matched to phase

Patient phenotyping: Measuring immune markers (HLA-DR, PD-1, exhaustion scores) may identify patients likely to benefit from specific immunotherapies

Combination approaches: Multi-faceted immune dysfunction may require combinatorial interventions (anti-PD-1 + IL-7, anti-CTLA-4 + anti-PD-1)

The next chapter addresses programmed cell death—the molecular mechanisms by which cells die during sepsis and how these mechanisms might be targeted therapeutically.

### CHAPTER 4: PROGRAMMED CELL DEATH HIERARCHIES IN SEPSIS

### 4.1 Apoptosis: Lymphocyte Attrition and Therapeutic Targeting

### The Apoptotic Pathway: Extrinsic and Intrinsic Routes

Apoptosis is a controlled form of cell death characterized by:

Cell shrinkage and blebbing: The cytoplasm condenses; the plasma membrane protrudes into blebs

Nuclear condensation (pyknosis): Chromatin becomes hyperdense and fragments

DNA fragmentation: Internucleosomal cleavage produces characteristic "DNA ladder" on gel electrophoresis

Apoptotic body formation: The cell fragments into membrane-bound vesicles

Phagocytosis without inflammation: Apoptotic bodies are rapidly engulfed by neighboring cells or professional phagocytes, preventing inflammatory release of intracellular contents

This controlled death is in stark contrast to necrosis or unregulated cell death, which triggers inflammatory responses.

### The Extrinsic Apoptotic Pathway: Death Receptors

Death receptors are transmembrane proteins that, when engaged by extracellular ligands, directly trigger apoptosis. The primary death receptors in sepsis are:

Fas (CD95):

Ligand: FasL (CD95L), expressed on activated T cells, NK cells, and some endothelial cells

Mechanism:

FasL binding to Fas induces trimerization of Fas

Trimerized Fas recruits the adaptor protein FADD (Fas-associated protein with death domain)

FADD recruits pro-caspase-8 (an inactive form of caspase-8)

Proximity of pro-caspase-8 molecules facilitates autocleavage, generating active caspase-8

Active caspase-8 directly cleaves effector caspases (caspase-3 and caspase-7)

Caspase-3/7 cleave hundreds of intracellular substrates, executing apoptosis

TNF Receptor-1 (TNFR1):

Ligand: TNF-α, produced by macrophages and other cells in sepsis

Mechanism: Similar to Fas, though with more complex signaling (TNF-R1 can signal both pro-apoptotic and pro-survival pathways, depending on context)

Trail Death Receptors (DR4, DR5):

Ligand: TRAIL (TNF-related apoptosis-inducing ligand)

Mechanism: Similar to Fas and TNF-R1

### The Intrinsic Apoptotic Pathway: Mitochondrial Dysfunction

The intrinsic pathway is triggered by intracellular stress signals:

Mitochondrial outer membrane permeabilization (MOMP):

Stress signal: Oxidative stress (ROS), DNA damage, metabolic stress, or growth factor withdrawal

Bcl-2 protein regulation: Pro-apoptotic Bcl-2 proteins (Bax, Bak) are activated; anti-apoptotic proteins (Bcl-2, Bcl-xL) are downregulated

Bax/Bak oligomerization: Activated Bax and Bak oligomerize to form pores in the mitochondrial outer membrane

Cytochrome c release: These pores permit release of cytochrome c from the mitochondrial intermembrane space into the cytosol

Apoptosome formation: Cytochrome c binds Apaf-1 (apoptotic protease-activating factor-1) and pro-caspase-9

Caspase-9 activation: The apoptosome structure brings pro-caspase-9 molecules into proximity, facilitating autocleavage and activation

Caspase cascade: Active caspase-9 cleaves effector caspases (caspase-3/7), executing apoptosis

Additional mitochondrial effects:

Cytochrome c depletion: Loss of cytochrome c impairs electron transport chain function, reducing ATP production

AIF release: Apoptosis-inducing factor (AIF) is released; AIF translocates to the nucleus and, independent of caspases, triggers DNA fragmentation

Metabolic crisis: Reduced ATP production prevents execution of caspase-dependent apoptosis, leading to necrosis instead

### Apoptosis in Sepsis: Cell-Type Specific Patterns

Sepsis triggers apoptosis across multiple cell types, with distinct temporal patterns and consequences:

### Lymphocyte Apoptosis (T Cells and B Cells)

Timing: Maximal apoptosis occurs during days 3–7 of sepsis, paralleling the transition to immunosuppression

Magnitude: Dramatic—in severely septic patients, absolute lymphocyte counts may drop from 2,000/μL (normal) to <500/μL

Mechanisms:

Fas-FasL interaction:

Activated T cells upregulate Fas

FasL is expressed on activated T cells (providing trans-interaction) and on endothelial cells (providing extrinsic signals)

Fas-FasL engagement triggers extrinsic apoptosis

Endotoxin (LPS) direct effects:

LPS triggers TLR-4 signaling on B cells and T cells

TLR-4 can activate pro-apoptotic pathways through MyD88 and TRIF adaptor proteins

Corticosteroid effects (see below):

Sepsis often leads to altered corticosteroid signaling

Some studies suggest relative corticosteroid resistance; others suggest glucocorticoid-induced apoptosis

Consequences:

Lymphopenia (reduced absolute lymphocyte count)

Loss of adaptive immunity

Increased susceptibility to secondary infections

Impaired immune memory formation

### Endothelial Cell Apoptosis

Timing: Begins acutely (within hours) and continues through the first week

Magnitude: Widespread—autopsy studies show endothelial apoptosis in tissues from all organ systems

Mechanisms:

TNF-α-induced apoptosis:

TNF-α binds TNFR1 on endothelial cells

TNFR1 recruits FADD and pro-caspase-8

Activation of caspase-8 and effector caspases triggers apoptosis

ROS-induced apoptosis:

Neutrophils and macrophages produce ROS

ROS damage mitochondrial membranes → release of cytochrome c → intrinsic apoptosis pathway

Histones from NETs:

Extracellular histones are directly cytotoxic to endothelial cells

Histones activate TLR-2/4 and TLR-4, triggering pro-apoptotic signaling

Complement components:

C5a and MAC (membrane attack complex) injure endothelium

Can trigger both apoptotic and necrotic pathways

Consequences:

Loss of barrier function (vascular permeability)

Tissue edema and multi-organ dysfunction

Impaired vasodilation (less NO production)

Microvascular thrombosis (tissue factor expression)

### Hepatocyte Apoptosis

Timing: Acute phase (hours to days)

Mechanisms:

TNF-α-induced apoptosis

Mitochondrial dysfunction from ROS and metabolic failure

Direct bacterial toxin effects

Consequences:

Hepatocellular injury (elevated transaminases)

Loss of synthetic function (impaired coagulation factor production, albumin synthesis)

Loss of detoxification capacity

### Renal Tubular Cell Apoptosis

Timing: Hours to days, correlating with organ dysfunction

Mechanisms:

Renal hypoperfusion (septic shock) → acute tubular necrosis

Direct tubular epithelial cell apoptosis from TNF-α and ROS

Mitochondrial dysfunction

Consequences:

Acute kidney injury

Fluid and electrolyte abnormalities

Anemia (EPO production loss)

### The "Apoptosis Paradox" in Sepsis

Sepsis presents a fundamental paradox regarding apoptosis:

Early phase (days 0–3):

Limited apoptosis

Excessive inflammation that would benefit from more apoptosis (to remove activated inflammatory cells)

Yet blocking apoptosis experimentally (e.g., with caspase inhibitors) paradoxically worsens mortality, suggesting some apoptosis is protective

Late phase (days 3–7+):

Excessive apoptosis of lymphocytes and other cells

Loss of adaptive immunity that worsens outcomes

Yet blocking apoptosis experimentally (e.g., with anti-Fas or anti-FasL antibodies) shows benefit in late sepsis, reducing mortality

Explanation: This paradox reflects the biphasic nature of apoptosis in sepsis:

Early apoptosis of some inflammatory cells is beneficial (prevents excessive inflammation)

Late apoptosis of lymphocytes and other essential cells is harmful (causes immunosuppression)

### Therapeutic Targeting of Apoptosis

### Approach 1: Anti-Apoptotic Strategies in Late Sepsis

Rationale: If excessive lymphocyte apoptosis drives immunosuppression, blocking apoptosis should preserve immune function.

Mechanisms:

Anti-FasL antibodies:

FasL-engaging antibodies prevent FasL-Fas interaction

Experimental anti-FasL (e.g., Jo2 antibody in mice) or anti-Fas antibodies reduce lymphocyte apoptosis

Improves survival in late sepsis models

Caspase inhibitors:

Pan-caspase inhibitors (e.g., zVAD-fmk) block all caspase activities

Reduce apoptosis across cell types

Mixed results in clinical trials; some benefit in sepsis subgroups

Bcl-2 enhancers:

Promote anti-apoptotic Bcl-2 protein function

Prevent MOMP and cytochrome c release

Currently in preclinical development

Challenges:

Blocking apoptosis universally risks allowing excessive inflammation or permitting survival of damaged cells with malignant potential

Timing is critical; anti-apoptotic strategies must be applied during the late immunosuppressive phase, not early

Current status: Limited clinical translation; anti-FasL and anti-Fas antibodies abandoned due to toxicity in early trials

### Approach 2: Selective Apoptosis Targeting

Rather than globally blocking apoptosis, selective targeting of apoptosis in specific cell types:

Selective preservation of lymphocyte survival:

Growth factors (IL-7, IL-15) that promote lymphocyte survival

Interferon therapy (IFN-γ) that activates lymphocytes and promotes survival

Antioxidants that reduce ROS-induced apoptosis

Current status: IL-7 therapy showing promise in clinical trials; IFN-γ therapy in early studies

### 4.2 Pyroptosis and NLRP3 Inflammasome Activation

### Definition of Pyroptosis

Pyroptosis (from Greek "pyro" = fire, "ptosis" = falling) is a form of programmed cell death distinct from apoptosis, characterized by:

Rapid cell lysis: The cell swells and ruptures, releasing intracellular contents

Inflammatory mediator release: Caspase-1-cleaved cytokines (IL-1β, IL-18) are released

Gasdermin-mediated pore formation: Caspase-1 cleaves Gasdermin-D (GSDMD), generating an N-terminal fragment that forms pores in the plasma membrane

Inflammatory phenotype: Pyroptosis is pro-inflammatory, contrasting with apoptosis (anti-inflammatory)

### The NLRP3 Inflammasome: Activation and Function

### Assembly and Activation

The NLRP3 inflammasome is a multi-protein complex that serves as the primary sensor of PAMPs and DAMPs in the cytosol:

Components:

NLRP3 protein: A NOD-like receptor containing NACHT domain and leucine-rich repeats

ASC protein: Adaptor protein with a PYRIN domain

Pro-caspase-1: The inactive precursor of caspase-1

Two-signal model of activation:

Signal 1 (Priming):

TLR-4 engagement by LPS activates NF-κB

NF-κB transcriptionally upregulates NLRP3, pro-caspase-1, pro-IL-1β, and pro-IL-18

Result: Increased abundance of inflammasome components and substrates

Signal 2 (Activation):

Multiple triggers activate NLRP3:

K⁺ efflux: Cell membrane depolarization leads to loss of intracellular potassium

Ca²⁺ influx: Increased intracellular calcium

ROS generation: Mitochondrial ROS

Phagolysosomal rupture: Damage to lysosomes after phagocytosis of particulate matter

Metabolite accumulation: Adenosine and succinate

Assembly mechanism:

NLRP3 undergoes conformational change in response to Signal 2

NLRP3 recruits ASC through PYRIN-PYRIN domain interactions

ASC oligomerizes, forming a scaffold

Pro-caspase-1 is recruited to the ASC scaffold

Proximity of pro-caspase-1 molecules facilitates autocleavage and activation

### Substrate Cleavage by Caspase-1

Once activated, caspase-1 cleaves multiple substrates:

Pro-IL-1β → IL-1β:

IL-1β is released in active form (its precursor, pro-IL-1β, is inactive)

IL-1β is one of the most important pro-inflammatory cytokines in sepsis

Acts on endothelial cells, T cells, and neurons to amplify inflammation

Pro-IL-18 → IL-18:

IL-18 promotes IFN-γ production by T cells and NK cells

Can be pro- or anti-inflammatory depending on context (with IL-12, promotes Th1; alone, anti-inflammatory)

Gasdermin-D (GSDMD):

Caspase-1 cleaves full-length GSDMD to generate N-terminal fragment (GSDMD-N)

GSDMD-N is the executioner of pyroptosis

Forms oligomers that create pores (8 nm diameter) in plasma membrane and organellar membranes

These pores permit osmotic imbalance and rapid cell lysis

### Pyroptosis in Sepsis: Excessive Inflammasome Activation

### NLRP3 Activation in Sepsis

NLRP3 inflammasome is hyperactivated in sepsis:

Evidence:

Circulating levels of caspase-1 and cleaved IL-1β are elevated 10–100-fold above normal

Tissue biopsies show NLRP3 inflammasome components in septic tissues

Genetic deletion of NLRP3 in mice improves survival in sepsis models

Mechanisms of hyperactivation:

Continuous TLR signaling: LPS from persistent gram-negative bacteremia continuously primes inflammasomes

ROS from mitochondrial dysfunction: Septic conditions impair mitochondrial function, generating ROS that directly activate NLRP3

K⁺ efflux: Septic shock and catecholamine administration cause cellular stress and K⁺ loss

Phagolysosomal rupture: Phagocytosis of bacteria in macrophages can lead to lysosomal rupture and NLRP3 activation

DAMPs: Circulating HMGB1, cell-free DNA, and uric acid activate NLRP3

### Consequences of Excessive Pyroptosis

IL-1β-mediated inflammation:

IL-1β activates endothelial cells, promoting vasodilation, increased permeability, and tissue edema

IL-1β promotes coagulation (tissue factor expression)

IL-1β acts on the hypothalamus to induce fever

IL-1β can amplify Treg responses through IL-2 induction

Cell lysis and tissue damage:

Pyroptotic cell lysis releases intracellular contents (DAMPs, proteases)

DAMPs activate additional inflammasomes and TLRs, perpetuating inflammation

Tissue damage impairs organ function

Transition to immunosuppression:

Paradoxically, excessive IL-1β can also promote immunosuppression

IL-1β upregulates PD-L1 on immune cells, contributing to T-cell exhaustion

IL-1β promotes Treg differentiation

### Therapeutic Targeting of NLRP3 and Pyroptosis

### Approach 1: NLRP3 Inflammasome Inhibition

Rationale: Blocking NLRP3 assembly or caspase-1 activation should reduce pathological pyroptosis and IL-1β production.

Specific strategies:

Direct NLRP3 inhibitors:

MCC950 (CP-456773): Directly binds NLRP3 protein, preventing inflammasome assembly

Oridonin: Natural product that inhibits NLRP3 activation

Intermediate metabolites: Compounds blocking specific triggers (K⁺ efflux modulators, Ca²⁺ channel blockers)

Caspase-1 inhibitors:

VX-765 (Belnacasan): Selective caspase-1 inhibitor

Pan-caspase inhibitors: Block all caspase activities

IL-1β neutralization:

Anti-IL-1β monoclonal antibodies (e.g., canakinumab): Bind IL-1β, preventing receptor engagement

IL-1 receptor antagonist (IL-1Ra): Blocks IL-1 receptor on target cells

Anakinra: Recombinant IL-1Ra approved for use in some inflammatory conditions

Clinical evidence:

Animal studies (murine sepsis, polymicrobial peritonitis) show NLRP3 inhibitors improve survival when given early

Human trials of anakinra in sepsis (ARDS-associated sepsis) have shown mixed results; some subgroups show benefit

Ongoing Phase 2/3 trials of MCC950 and other NLRP3 inhibitors in sepsis

Challenges:

Timing is critical; NLRP3 inhibition is most effective early (day 0–2), when excessive inflammation dominates

Late NLRP3 inhibition (day 5+) may impair remaining innate immunity

IL-1β has both pro- and anti-inflammatory functions; blocking it universally may have unintended consequences

Current status: Several clinical trials active; preliminary safety data encouraging

### Approach 2: ROS Reduction to Prevent NLRP3 Activation

Rationale: ROS is a major trigger of NLRP3 activation. Reducing ROS should suppress inflammasome activation.

Strategies:

Antioxidants: N-acetylcysteine (NAC), vitamin E, vitamin C

Mitochondrial protectants: Compounds that stabilize mitochondrial function and reduce ROS production (e.g., MitoQ, Mito-CP)

Catalase and superoxide dismutase (SOD) mimetics: Enzymes or drug-like compounds that degrade ROS

Challenges:

ROS also serve important antimicrobial functions (neutrophil killing of bacteria)

Excessive antioxidant administration may impair pathogen clearance

Clinical trials of antioxidants in sepsis have been largely negative

Current status: Preclinical focus; clinical translation limited

### 4.3 Necroptosis: Endothelial Collapse and Inflammation Amplification

### Definition and Mechanistic Basis of Necroptosis

Necroptosis is a form of programmed cell death that morphologically resembles necrosis (cell swelling, membrane rupture) but is molecularly programmed (like apoptosis). It is characterized by:

Ripoptosome formation: A multi-protein complex nucleated by RIPK1

MLKL activation: Mixed-lineage kinase domain-like (MLKL) protein is phosphorylated by RIPK3

Plasma membrane permeabilization: Phosphorylated MLKL oligomerizes and forms pores in the plasma membrane

Cell lysis and inflammatory mediator release: Similar to pyroptosis, necroptosis causes cell rupture and release of intracellular contents

### The RIPK1-RIPK3-MLKL Axis

### Triggering Necroptosis

Necroptosis is typically triggered when:

Death receptors are engaged (Fas, TNF-R1, TRAIL receptors)

AND apoptosis is blocked (through caspase-8 inhibition or loss)

Molecular sequence:

### Step 1: Death receptor engagement and RIPK1 recruitment

TNF-α binds TNFR1

TNFR1 recruits RIPK1 (serine/threonine kinase)

Normally, TNFR1 signaling activates caspase-8, which cleaves RIPK1 and prevents necroptosis

However, if caspase-8 is inhibited, RIPK1 remains intact

### Step 2: Ripoptosome assembly

Intact RIPK1 recruits RIPK3 and MLKL to form the "ripoptosome"

The exact stoichiometry and assembly mechanisms remain incompletely understood

Assembly requires RHIM (RIP homotypic interaction motif) domains on RIPK1 and RIPK3

### Step 3: RIPK3 autophosphorylation and MLKL activation

RIPK3 undergoes autophosphorylation at T180/S204

Phosphorylated RIPK3 phosphorylates MLKL at T357/S358 (human coordinates)

Phosphorylated MLKL undergoes oligomerization

### Step 4: MLKL-mediated membrane permeabilization

Oligomerized MLKL translocates to plasma membrane

Forms pores (approximately 3 nm diameter)

Osmotic imbalance leads to cell swelling and lysis

### Cell-Type Specificity of Necroptosis

Particularly vulnerable to necroptosis:

Endothelial cells: Constitutively express death receptors and low caspase-8 activity

Intestinal epithelial cells: Essential barrier cells that undergo necroptosis in inflammatory conditions

Immune cells: Macrophages and dendritic cells can undergo necroptosis when caspase-8 is inhibited

Less vulnerable:

Most other parenchymal cells: Higher caspase-8 activity prevents ripoptosome formation

### Necroptosis in Sepsis: Endothelial Collapse

### Endothelial Cell Necroptosis in Sepsis

Mechanisms:

TNF-α-induced necroptosis:

TNF-α binds TNFR1 on endothelial cells

Normally, TNFR1 activation triggers caspase-8-mediated apoptosis

However, in sepsis, caspase-8 activity may be impaired or insufficient

Result: RIPK1 and RIPK3 remain active, leading to ripoptosome formation and necroptosis

Z-VAD-mediated caspase inhibition:

While not directly occurring in vivo, septic conditions may lead to reduced caspase-8 activity

This permits necroptosis even in response to TNF-α

TLR-induced necroptosis:

TLR-3 and TLR-4 can activate RIPK3 directly through TRIF adaptor

Leads to necroptosis independent of death receptor signals

Consequence: Lytic cell death of endothelial cells with release of intracellular contents, amplifying inflammation

### Barrier Dysfunction from Endothelial Necroptosis

Unlike apoptosis (which maintains membrane integrity until late stages), necroptosis causes rapid lysis of endothelial cells:

Immediate loss of barrier function: Endothelial cells are rapidly destroyed

Inflammatory mediator release: Intracellular proteases, ROS, and DAMPs are released

Perpetuation of inflammation: Released DAMPs activate additional TLRs and inflammasomes

Clinical manifestation: Sepsis-induced acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS), multi-organ edema, and shock progression

### Therapeutic Targeting of Necroptosis

### Approach 1: RIPK1 and RIPK3 Inhibitors

Rationale: Blocking the kinases necessary for necroptosis execution should prevent endothelial collapse and reduce inflammation.

Specific agents:

RIPK1 inhibitors:

Necrostatin-1 (Nec-1): First-in-class RIPK1 inhibitor; prevents RIPK1 kinase activity

Compound A: Improved RIPK1 inhibitor with better pharmacokinetics

Newer agents: GSK3011 and others with enhanced specificity

RIPK3 inhibitors:

GSK'872: Selective RIPK3 inhibitor

Multiple preclinical agents in development

Mechanism: Block phosphorylation of MLKL, preventing its oligomerization and membrane pore formation

Preclinical evidence:

RIPK1 and RIPK3 inhibitors reduce endothelial necroptosis in sepsis models

Improve survival in murine sepsis (CLP, LPS-induced endotoxemia)

Reduce ARDS progression and multi-organ dysfunction

Clinical status: Early Phase 1 trials in sepsis; safety data emerging

### Approach 2: MLKL Inhibitors

Rationale: MLKL is the executioner of necroptosis. Blocking MLKL should prevent membrane permeabilization.

Specific agents:

Compounds targeting MLKL oligomerization: Prevent formation of membrane pores

MLKL-blocking antibodies: Experimental approach to prevent MLKL function

Challenges:

Fewer clinical-stage inhibitors compared to kinase inhibitors

Mechanism-of-action studies ongoing

### Approach 3: TNF Neutralization

Rationale: TNF-α is a major trigger of both apoptosis and necroptosis. Blocking TNF-α should reduce both pathways.

Agents:

Anti-TNF-α monoclonal antibodies (e.g., infliximab, TNF receptor antagonists)

TNF receptor antagonists: Prevent TNF-α engagement of receptors

Clinical history: Major anti-TNF-α trials in sepsis (1990s–2010s) were uniformly negative or harmful. Current understanding suggests TNF blockade may be deleterious in early sepsis (when TNF-driven killing of pathogens is beneficial) but potentially beneficial in late sepsis (when TNF-driven endothelial necroptosis causes harm).

Current status: Not recommended in current sepsis guidelines; selective re-evaluation in late-phase sepsis under investigation

### 4.4 Ferroptosis: Iron-Dependent Cell Death and Novel Interventions

### Definition of Ferroptosis

Ferroptosis is an iron-dependent form of programmed cell death characterized by:

Iron accumulation: Increased intracellular free iron (Fe²⁺)

Lipid peroxidation: Accumulation of lipid hydroperoxides (reactive lipid species)

Mitochondrial dysfunction: Distinctive mitochondrial shrinkage (smaller than in apoptosis)

Minimal inflammation: Unlike pyroptosis and necroptosis, ferroptosis is non-lytic and produces minimal inflammatory mediator release

Dependence on iron metabolism: The process is prevented by iron chelation or ferroptosis inhibitors

### Ferroptosis Mechanism: The Lipid Peroxidation Cascade

### System xc⁻: Cystine-Glutamate Exchanger

Function: Exchanges extracellular cystine (Cys-Cys oxidized form of cysteine) for intracellular glutamate

Importance:

Cystine is imported intracellularly via system xc⁻

Intracellular cystine is reduced to cysteine

Cysteine is incorporated into glutathione (GSH) via glutathione synthetase

Glutathione is the primary intracellular antioxidant

Ferroptosis trigger: System xc⁻ inhibition → reduced cystine import → reduced glutathione synthesis → loss of antioxidant capacity

### Glutathione Peroxidase-4 (GPX4): The Critical Antioxidant

Function: GPX4 is a selenium-containing enzyme that metabolizes lipid peroxides to lipid alcohols

Normal role:

Lipids in cell membranes and organelles spontaneously undergo peroxidation (oxidation) via free radicals

GPX4 rapidly reduces lipid hydroperoxides to non-toxic lipid alcohols

This prevents accumulation of toxic lipid peroxides

Ferroptosis mechanism:

When glutathione is depleted, GPX4 cannot be reduced (GPX4 requires glutathione as a cofactor)

Inactive GPX4 cannot metabolize lipid peroxides

Lipid peroxides accumulate to toxic levels

Oxidative stress leads to mitochondrial dysfunction and cell death

### Iron's Role in Ferroptosis

Fenton chemistry: Free iron (Fe²⁺) catalyzes generation of highly reactive hydroxyl radicals (OH⁻) from hydrogen peroxide:

Fe²⁺ + H₂O₂ → Fe³⁺ + OH⁻ + OH•

These hydroxyl radicals oxidize lipids, initiating peroxidation cascades.

In ferroptosis: Increased intracellular iron combined with lipid peroxides creates a toxic environment:

Free iron catalyzes generation of additional ROS

ROS oxidize additional lipids

Lipid peroxidation propagates, damaging mitochondrial membranes

### Ferroptosis in Sepsis: Preliminary Evidence

The role of ferroptosis in sepsis pathophysiology is poorly characterized—this is a frontier area of research. Preliminary observations:

### Potential Ferroptosis in Septic Tissue

Preliminary evidence:

Markers of ferroptosis (lipid peroxidation products, iron accumulation) are elevated in septic tissues (lung, liver, kidney) in animal models

Ferroptosis inhibitors show potential benefit in sepsis models when administered early

Potential mechanisms:

Septic shock and hypoperfusion impair iron homeostasis, leading to intracellular iron accumulation

ROS from mitochondrial dysfunction oxidizes lipids

Glutathione depletion (from ROS scavenging) permits GPX4 inactivation

Cell types potentially affected:

Endothelial cells

Macrophages and myeloid cells

Parenchymal cells (hepatocytes, renal tubular cells)

### Ferroptosis versus Other Forms of Death

Relative contribution:

In early sepsis, apoptosis and pyroptosis likely predominate

In late sepsis, with profound metabolic dysfunction, ferroptosis may contribute alongside apoptosis

Exact relative contribution unknown

Clinical significance: If ferroptosis contributes substantially to sepsis mortality, ferroptosis inhibitors might offer unique therapeutic benefits

### Ferroptosis Inhibitors: Therapeutic Approaches

### Approach 1: Iron Chelation

Rationale: Reducing intracellular free iron should prevent Fenton chemistry and ferroptosis.

Agents:

Deferoxamine: Traditional iron chelator; long clinical history in iron overload

Deferiprone: Newer iron chelator

Ferrostatin-1: Selective ferroptosis inhibitor (experimental)

Challenges:

Iron is essential for normal cellular function (hemoglobin, myoglobin, iron-

Continue

30 дек. 2025 г.

sulfur clusters)

Excessive iron chelation impairs normal iron metabolism

Risk of impairing macrophage-mediated pathogen killing (iron necessary for various antimicrobial mechanisms)

Current status: Ferrostatin-1 and related agents in preclinical development for sepsis

### Approach 2: Glutathione Replenishment

Rationale: Restoring intracellular glutathione should permit GPX4 function and prevent ferroptosis.

Agents:

N-Acetylcysteine (NAC): Precursor to glutathione synthesis

Direct glutathione administration: Limited intracellular penetration but some benefit in specific conditions

Liposomal or nano-formulated glutathione: Improved cellular delivery

Preclinical evidence:

NAC reduces ferroptosis markers in sepsis models

Improves some outcome measures in animal sepsis

Clinical history:

NAC has been studied extensively in sepsis for antioxidant effects

Results mixed; some benefit in specific subgroups

Current status: Re-evaluation of NAC in context of ferroptosis inhibition

### Approach 3: Lipid Peroxidation Inhibitors

Rationale: Directly blocking lipid peroxidation should prevent ferroptosis independent of iron or glutathione status.

Agents:

Ferrostatin-1: Potent lipid peroxidation inhibitor (experimental)

Liproxstatin-1: Second-generation ferroptosis inhibitor

Natural antioxidants: Vitamin E (α-tocopherol), polyphenols

Mechanism: These agents directly interact with lipids or lipid radicals to prevent peroxidation chain reactions

Preclinical status: Promising in ferroptosis models; sepsis applications under investigation

### 4.5 Multi-Modal Death Cascades: Therapeutic Implications and Combinatorial Approaches

### The Complexity of Cell Death in Sepsis

A critical insight emerging from this review is that sepsis involves multiple, overlapping forms of programmed cell death, not a single pathway.

### Temporal Hierarchy of Death in Sepsis

Early phase (hours to 1–2 days):

Pyroptosis dominates: NLRP3 inflammasome hyperactivation produces IL-1β-mediated inflammation and cell lysis

Apoptosis: Beginning of lymphocyte and endothelial apoptosis

Minimal ferroptosis: Metabolic dysfunction limited

Necroptosis: Not dominant (requires caspase-8 inhibition or TLR-3/4-TRIF signaling)

Middle phase (days 2–5):

Apoptosis accelerates: Lymphocyte and endothelial apoptosis reaches peak

Pyroptosis persists: Continued NLRP3 activation if source control inadequate

Necroptosis emerges: As caspase-8 activity declines relative to RIPK3, necroptosis becomes more prominent

Ferroptosis begins: Metabolic exhaustion and iron dyshomeostasis begin

Late phase (days 5+):

Apoptosis decreases: Most apoptosis-sensitive cells (some lymphocytes) have already died

Necroptosis prominent: Continued endothelial loss drives continued barrier dysfunction

Ferroptosis prominent: Metabolic exhaustion severe, lipid peroxidation extensive

Pyroptosis declines: As innate immunity becomes exhausted

### Crosstalk Between Pathways

These pathways are not independent. Rather, they exhibit crosstalk:

Caspase-8 activity regulates the apoptosis-necroptosis balance:

When caspase-8 is active: TNF-α signaling leads to apoptosis

When caspase-8 is inactive or inhibited: TNF-α signaling leads to necroptosis

Factors regulating caspase-8 expression (inflammatory cytokines, oxidative stress) determine which pathway dominates

Inflammasome-derived IL-1β can promote or inhibit other death pathways:

IL-1β promotes endothelial cell apoptosis (extrinsic pathway)

IL-1β upregulates PD-L1, promoting T-cell death through exhaustion

IL-1β can promote ferroptosis through iron dysregulation

ROS generation is common to multiple pathways:

ROS trigger intrinsic apoptosis (mitochondrial dysfunction)

ROS activate NLRP3 inflammasome → pyroptosis

ROS oxidize lipids → ferroptosis

### Rational Combinatorial Approaches

Given the complexity of cell death in sepsis, rational therapy likely requires phase-specific, multi-targeted approaches rather than single-agent therapies.

### Early Sepsis (Day 0–2): Anti-Inflammatory Death Inhibition

Rationale: In early sepsis, excessive pyroptosis and inflammasome activation dominate. Blocking these should reduce harmful inflammation.

Combination approach:

NLRP3 inflammasome inhibitor (MCC950 or similar)

Caspase-1 inhibitor or IL-1β antagonist (anakinra)

ROS scavenger (NAC, vitamin C)

TNF neutralizer (in very high TNF, though controversial)

Rationale: Multiple targets of inflammasome axis simultaneously reduce pyroptosis, IL-1β release, and downstream inflammation

Challenges:

Multiple agents increase drug interactions and adverse events

Optimal dosing and timing uncertain

Balance between reducing harmful inflammation and impairing beneficial immune responses

### Middle Sepsis (Days 2–5): Apoptosis Prevention and Immune Support

Rationale: Lymphocyte apoptosis becomes dominant; preservation of adaptive immunity is essential.

Combination approach:

Anti-apoptotic agent: IL-7 (promotes lymphocyte survival), anti-FasL (blocks FasL-Fas apoptosis), or Bcl-2 enhancers

Checkpoint inhibitor: Anti-PD-1 or anti-TIM-3 (restores T-cell function)

MDSC inhibitor: Anti-GM-CSF or JAK inhibitor (reduces MDSC immunosuppression)

ROS scavenger: Continued antioxidant support

Rationale: Simultaneously prevent lymphocyte death while restoring function of surviving cells

### Late Sepsis (Day 5+): Ferroptosis and Necroptosis Prevention

Rationale: In late sepsis, ferroptosis and necroptosis may dominate. Preservation of remaining parenchymal cells and barrier function is essential.

Combination approach:

RIPK inhibitor (RIPK1 or RIPK3 inhibitor): Blocks necroptosis

Ferroptosis inhibitor: Iron chelation or lipid peroxidation inhibitor

Glutathione replenisher: NAC or direct glutathione administration

Continued immune support: Anti-PD-1, IL-7, or other immunostimulatory agents if source not controlled

Rationale: Prevent endothelial and parenchymal cell death while supporting immune response to secondary pathogens

### Clinical Trial Landscape (2024–2025)

Multiple clinical trials are now investigating these death-targeting strategies:

NLRP3 inhibitors (MCC950): Phase 2b trials in septic ARDS

Anti-PD-1: Phase 1b/2a trials in septic shock

IL-7: Phase 2 trials in critical illness

RIPK inhibitors: Phase 1 trials initiating

Ferroptosis inhibitors: Preclinical; moving toward IND-enabling studies

### Summary: The Death Code in Sepsis

Sepsis is a disease of disordered cell death—not too little death, not too much death, but the wrong kinds of death at the wrong times.

Early pyroptosis of innate cells amplifies inflammation

Middle apoptosis of lymphocytes eliminates essential immunity

Late necroptosis and ferroptosis destroy remaining barrier and parenchymal function

For the surgeon, understanding these death pathways informs:

Phase-specific drug selection: Different death pathways dominate different phases; therapy should target phase-appropriate pathways

Combination rationale: Multi-targeted approaches are scientifically justified—addressing apoptosis alone misses pyroptosis and ferroptosis

Source control timing: Early source control prevents prolonged PAMP exposure that perpetuates inflammasome activation and pyroptosis

Long-term sequelae: Understanding death mechanisms informs post-ICU rehabilitation strategies

The next section addresses the practical integration of these immune insights into clinical decision-making through biomarker-driven personalization.

### [END OF CHAPTER 4]

### CROSS-CHAPTER SYNTHESIS: FROM MECHANISM TO CLINICAL DECISION-MAKING

The preceding four chapters have established the scientific foundation for contemporary sepsis understanding:

Chapter 1: Sepsis-3 definitions and diagnostic criteria

Chapter 2: Immune dysregulation and endothelial dysfunction

Chapter 3: Immune cell populations and dysfunction

Chapter 4: Programmed cell death hierarchies

These chapters have laid out the scientific rationale for phase-specific immunotherapy. The remaining chapters translate these mechanisms into clinical practice, beginning with biomarkers and diagnostics (Chapter 5–6), then proceeding to interventional management (Chapters 7–14), advanced immunotherapy (Chapter 15), and long-term outcomes (Chapters 23–24).

The shift from traditional "one-size-fits-all" sepsis management to personalized, immunophenotypic-guided therapy is the defining challenge of contemporary sepsis surgery.
