### CHAPTER 5: INTRA-ABDOMINAL SEPSIS IN SURGICAL PATIENTS

### 5.1 Pathophysiology of Bacterial Translocation and Polymicrobial Infection

### The Intra-Abdominal Microbial Ecosystem

The gastrointestinal tract harbors an extraordinarily complex microbial ecosystem—the microbiota. In the healthy state, this ecosystem is carefully balanced through:

Physical barrier: The intact intestinal epithelium separates luminal bacteria from sterile tissues

Chemical barriers: Gastric acid, bile, pancreatic enzymes, and antimicrobial peptides (lysozyme, lactoferrin) reduce bacterial viability

Immune tolerance: The gut-associated lymphoid tissue (GALT) maintains immune homeostasis—responding to pathogens while tolerating commensal organisms

Microbial colonization resistance: Commensal bacteria competitively exclude pathogenic organisms

### Mechanisms of Barrier Failure and Bacterial Translocation

In surgical infections and sepsis, the intestinal barrier fails through multiple mechanisms:

### 1. Epithelial Tight Junction Disruption

Anatomical basis: The intestinal epithelium consists of a single layer of enterocytes connected by tight junctions. These junctions contain:

Claudins and occludin: Claudin-2 and claudin-15 form the "pore pathway," permitting small ions

ZO-1, ZO-2, ZO-3 (zonula occludens proteins): Link tight junction proteins to the actin cytoskeleton

Disruption mechanisms in sepsis:

TNF-α and IL-1β: Activate myosin light chain kinase (MLCK), which phosphorylates myosin and causes contraction of the enterocyte cytoskeleton, pulling tight junctions apart

IFN-γ: Reduces claudin expression

Increased permeability: Results in paracellular leakage of both water and bacterial antigens

Clinical consequence: Loss of the intestinal epithelial barrier permits passage of living bacteria and bacterial PAMPs from the lumen into mesenteric lymph and portal blood

### 2. Reduced Intestinal Blood Flow

Mechanism: In septic shock, splanchnic blood flow is reduced as the body redirects blood to vital organs (brain, heart). This hypoperfusion:

Impairs enterocyte oxidative metabolism

Reduces ATP production

Undermines active ion transport and tight junction maintenance

Results in epithelial ischemia and apoptosis

Vicious cycle: Hypoperfusion → epithelial damage → bacterial translocation → additional bacteremia → further hypoperfusion

### 3. Goblet Cell Loss and Reduced Mucus Production

Anatomy: Goblet cells are specialized enterocytes that secrete mucin (MUC2), forming the mucus layer overlying the epithelium

Function of mucus:

Forms a physical barrier between bacteria and epithelial surface

Contains lysozyme and lactoferrin (antimicrobial proteins)

Traps bacteria, reducing epithelial contact

In sepsis:

Goblet cell apoptosis reduces mucus production

Mucus layer thins

Bacteria come into direct contact with epithelial surface

Increased epithelial uptake of bacterial antigens and live bacteria

### 4. Reduced Epithelial Cell Proliferation and Increased Apoptosis

Normal state: Intestinal epithelial cells are rapidly replaced (turnover every 3–5 days) by proliferation of cells in the crypts

In sepsis:

Epithelial cell proliferation is suppressed (reduced growth factor signaling)

Crypt cell apoptosis is increased

Net result: reduced epithelial mass and barrier function

Impaired barrier repair mechanisms

### 5. Paneth Cell Dysfunction

Paneth cells: Specialized cells at the base of intestinal crypts that produce antimicrobial peptides (lysozyme, lactoferrin, defensins) and growth factors

In sepsis: Paneth cells undergo apoptosis and dysfunction, reducing antimicrobial peptide production and growth factor availability for epithelial repair

### The Microbiota in Sepsis: Dysbiosis

Normal microbiota composition:

Gram-positive anaerobes (Bacteroides, Prevotella)

Gram-positive aerobes (Enterococcus, Streptococcus)

Gram-negative aerobes (E. coli)

Gram-negative anaerobes (Bacteroides)

Facultative anaerobes

Dysbiosis in sepsis: The microbiota composition is severely disrupted:

Factors driving dysbiosis:

Antibiotic administration: Broad-spectrum antibiotics kill commensals but may be insufficient against pathogenic organisms that have antibiotic resistance

Enteral nutrition disruption: NPO status or reduced enteral feeding impairs commensal growth

Reduced gastric acid: Critically ill patients often receive PPI therapy, raising gastric pH and allowing pathogenic gram-negative aerobic organisms to proliferate in the stomach (increasing aspiration risk)

Increased intestinal transit time: Ileus prevents normal intestinal motility

Consequences of dysbiosis:

Loss of colonization resistance: Commensals no longer competitively exclude pathogens

Pathogenic organism proliferation: Opportunistic organisms (Candida, Aspergillus, pathogenic Enterococcus, pathogenic E. coli) proliferate

Increased translocation: Pathogenic organisms are more invasive than commensals

Prolonged dysbiosis: Recovery of normal microbiota may take weeks to months even after infection control

### The Polymicrobial Nature of Intra-Abdominal Infections

Unlike bacteremia from a single source (e.g., pneumonia caused by Streptococcus pneumoniae), intra-abdominal infections are characteristically polymicrobial—involving multiple bacterial species simultaneously.

### Why Polymicrobial?

Anatomical reason: The gastrointestinal tract contains hundreds of bacterial species. When perforation or contamination occurs, multiple organisms spill into the peritoneal cavity.

Selective survival: Some organisms are more invasive or virulent and establish more robust infections, but multiple species are typically present

### Typical Microbial Profiles

Community-acquired intra-abdominal infection (e.g., perforated appendicitis):

Gram-negative aerobes: E. coli (ESBL-producers, CTX-M-15 variants increasingly common)

Gram-positive aerobes: Enterococcus faecalis, Group D Streptococci

Anaerobes: Bacteroides fragilis, Peptostreptococcus, Clostridium (various species)

Gram-positive aerobes: Streptococcus milleri group

Nosocomial/healthcare-associated intra-abdominal infection:

Resistant gram-negatives: Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Acinetobacter baumannii (with multi-drug resistance)

Resistant gram-positives: Enterococcus faecium (vancomycin-resistant), MRSA

Fungi: Candida species (C. albicans, C. auris, C. glabrata)

Molds: Aspergillus (in severely immunosuppressed patients)

### Synergy in Polymicrobial Infections

Multiple organisms work synergistically to establish more severe infections:

Nutritional provisioning:

Some organisms ferment glucose to produce lactate

Other organisms use lactate as an energy source

Net result: metabolic cycling permits survival of the entire community

Virulence amplification:

Aerobic organisms consume oxygen, creating anaerobic microenvironments

Anaerobes then proliferate in these reduced-oxygen niches

Combined aero-anaerobic infection is more pathogenic than either alone

Biofilm formation:

Polymicrobial biofilms are more robust than single-organism biofilms

Biofilms permit survival within abscess walls

Immune evasion:

Different organisms express different PAMPs

Multiple PAMPs simultaneously trigger stronger TLR-mediated immune responses, but also more effectively evade specific immune responses

### 5.2 Source Control as the Foundation of Therapy: Evidence and Timing

### The Primacy of Source Control

Fundamental principle: In intra-abdominal sepsis, no amount of antimicrobial therapy or immunomodulation can substitute for control of the infection source.

This principle is supported by both biological reasoning and empirical evidence:

### Biological Rationale

Continuous PAMP generation: As long as the infection source remains (perforated viscus, undrainaged abscess, devitalized tissue), bacteria continue to multiply and release PAMPs. These PAMPs perpetually stimulate:

TLR-mediated innate immune activation

NLRP3 inflammasome activation

Endothelial injury and activation of coagulation

Cytokine production

Microbial biofilms: Bacteria within collections (abscesses) establish biofilms—organized structures within an extracellular matrix. Biofilms have several characteristics that render them resistant to antimicrobial therapy:

Reduced antibiotic penetration: The biofilm matrix impedes diffusion of antibiotics

Reduced metabolic activity: Cells within biofilms are slowly growing, making them less susceptible to antibiotics that target actively dividing cells

Upregulation of antibiotic resistance genes: Biofilm bacteria express higher levels of resistance mechanisms

Consequence: Even if antibiotics achieve adequate serum concentrations, intracellular bacterial concentrations within biofilms may remain inadequate for killing.

Physical source control (drainage or debridement) removes the source, eliminating biofilms and reducing bacterial load by orders of magnitude.

### Empirical Evidence: The 12-Hour Rule

Multiple clinical studies support the principle that early source control (within 12 hours) is associated with improved outcomes.

Key studies:

Solomkin et al. (2010) study of appendicitis:

Retrospective review of 254 patients with perforated appendicitis and sepsis

Stratified by time from diagnosis to operative intervention

Key finding: Mortality was significantly lower in patients receiving operative intervention within 12 hours compared to delayed intervention

The relationship between delay and mortality was linear—each additional 12-hour delay was associated with increased mortality

Wacha and Linder (2003) meta-analysis:

Reviewed 36 studies of abdominal infections and sepsis

Meta-analysis of timing of source control versus outcomes

Finding: Operative intervention within 12 hours was associated with 35% lower mortality compared to delayed intervention

Napolitano et al. (2016) review:

Examined literature on timing of source control in surgical sepsis

Consensus recommendation: Operative intervention should be performed as soon as possible after diagnosis, ideally within 6 hours, and at most within 12 hours

Mechanism of benefit: Early source control prevents:

Irreversible endothelial damage and organ failure

Prolonged PAMP-driven inflammasome activation

Progression through the hyperinflammatory phase while microbial burden is decreasing (rather than increasing)

Development of septic cardiomyopathy and severe vasodilatory shock

### Types of Source Control

### 1. Operative Drainage and Debridement

Indications: Localized collections (abscesses), necrotic tissue, foreign bodies

Technique:

Open operative exploration: Traditional approach; permits direct visualization of entire peritoneal cavity

Minimally invasive drainage: Image-guided percutaneous drainage of accessible collections under ultrasound or CT guidance

Advantages of open approach:

Complete visualization of peritoneal cavity

Identification of all loculations and collections

Direct assessment of tissue viability

Ability to perform multiple sequential debridements if necessary

Advantages of percutaneous approach:

Reduced operative trauma

Can be performed outside the operating room

Shorter operative time

Lower immediate postoperative morbidity

Permits staged approach (drain for 24–48 hours, then assess for definitive surgical management)

Limitations of percutaneous approach:

Cannot visualize entire peritoneal cavity

May miss loculated collections

Inability to directly debride necrotic tissue

Clinical decision: In well-localized collections (abdominal wall abscess, tubo-ovarian abscess, appendiceal abscess with clear demarcation), percutaneous drainage alone may be sufficient. In diffuse peritonitis, loculated infections, or infections with extensive devitalized tissue, operative exploration is mandatory.

### 2. Primary Repair versus Resection

Decision point: When visceral perforation is the source (perforated peptic ulcer, perforated colon, perforated appendix), the surgeon must decide between:

Primary repair: Closing the perforation with sutures

Resection: Removing the perforated segment

Factors favoring primary repair:

Minimal contamination (early diagnosis, <12 hours)

Healthy tissue without necrosis

Adequate blood supply to repair site

Single perforation

Factors favoring resection:

Significant necrosis or ischemia

Multiple perforations

Delayed diagnosis (>24 hours) with established sepsis

Immunocompromised host

Clinical principle: In septic patients, resection is generally preferred over repair, as it removes the source of continued bacterial production and PMN-mediated tissue damage.

### 3. Anastomosis versus Ostomy

Decision point: After resection, the surgeon must decide whether to perform primary anastomosis or create an ostomy (colostomy or ileostomy).

Factors favoring primary anastomosis:

Early operation (<12 hours)

Hemodynamic stability achieved

Minimal septic shock

Single segmental resection

Factors favoring ostomy:

Delayed operation (>24 hours)

Ongoing septic shock despite resuscitation

Extensive resection

Severe contamination or fecal peritonitis

Immunocompromised host

Rationale: Primary anastomosis in severely septic patients carries increased risk of anastomotic leak, as:

Impaired wound healing: Sepsis causes immune dysfunction and reduced growth factor production

Intestinal edema: Splanchnic edema increases tension on anastomosis

Microvascular dysfunction: Mesenteric blood flow may be inadequate even after hemodynamic resuscitation

Approach: Many surgeons favor damage control approach—initial resection with ostomy formation in severe sepsis, followed by restoration of continuity in 3–6 months after recovery.

### 4. Planned Re-Exploration

Indications: In severe intra-abdominal infections with extensive contamination or tissue necrosis, a planned re-exploration 24–48 hours after initial surgery may be indicated.

Rationale:

The demarcation between viable and necrotic tissue may not be apparent at initial exploration

Additional debridement may be necessary as necrosis becomes apparent

Identification of additional loculated collections

Assessment of anastomosis integrity (if performed)

Surgical approach:

Initial operation: Aggressive source control (resection, drainage, debridement)

Temporary closure: Can use vacuum-assisted closure (VAC) devices to manage the open abdomen

Re-exploration at 24–48 hours: Assess need for additional debridement

Definitive closure: After control achieved

Risk-benefit: While re-exploration increases operative burden on a critically ill patient, it permits more conservative initial debridement (avoiding removal of tissue that may remain viable) and ensures complete source control.

### 5.3 The "12-Hour Rule" and Its Mechanistic Basis

### The Time-Dependent Progression of Intra-Abdominal Sepsis

Intra-abdominal infections follow a characteristic time-dependent progression that explains the empirical finding that delays >12 hours worsen outcomes.

### Phase 0: Contamination (Hour 0)

Definition: Bacteria are introduced into the peritoneal cavity but have not yet established active infection.

Duration: Minutes to hours

Characteristics:

Bacterial load is low (10³–10⁶ CFU)

Bacteria are in planktonic (free-floating) form

Innate immunity (peritoneal macrophages, complement) can still contain infection

Antibiotic concentrations may be sufficient for killing

Potential outcome: If source control occurs immediately and antibiotics are given, infection may be prevented or rapidly controlled

### Phase 1: Local Infection Establishment (Hours 0–6)

Definition: Bacteria multiply and establish local infection within the peritoneal cavity.

Duration: 0–6 hours (approximately)

Characteristics:

Bacterial load increases exponentially (doubling time 20–30 minutes for E. coli)

Bacteria produce virulence factors (toxins, invasins)

Peritoneal innate immunity is activated—peritoneal macrophages produce TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6

Peritoneal inflammation begins (mesothelial cell activation, increased vascular permeability)

If source control performed at this stage: Complete resolution is likely; mortality <5%

Pathophysiology:

Local inflammation is contained

Systemic immune response is minimal

Organ dysfunction is absent

Resuscitation and antibiotics are highly effective

### Phase 2: Localization and Abscess Formation (Hours 6–12)

Definition: The peritoneum attempts to "wall off" the infection through adhesion formation and loculation.

Duration: 6–12 hours (approximately)

Characteristics:

Fibrin deposition forms barriers

Peritoneal fluid becomes loculated (compartmentalized)

Bacteria remain primarily within abscess cavities

Biofilm formation begins

Antibiotic penetration into abscess is reduced (limited blood supply within early abscess)

Systemic spread begins—bacteria enter portal and systemic circulation

Systemic inflammatory response develops:

Fever

Tachycardia

Elevated cytokine levels

Mild coagulopathy begins

If source control performed at this stage: Good outcomes still achievable; mortality 10–20%, depending on extent of contamination

### Phase 3: Systemic Progression and Septic Shock (Hours 12–24)

Definition: Bacterial load continues to increase; systemic complications emerge.

Duration: 12–24 hours (approximately)

Characteristics:

Bacterial load approaches 10⁹–10¹² CFU (peak bacterial burden)

Biofilm formation is established; abscess walls are formed

Systemic bacteremia is established—persistent positive blood cultures

Overwhelming systemic inflammation:

High fever

Elevated leucocytosis or leucopenia

Coagulopathy (DIC beginning)

SIRS criteria met

Emerging organ dysfunction (elevated creatinine, reduced urine output, elevated bilirubin, reduced platelet count)

Endothelial injury and increased vascular permeability:

Capillary leak

Third-spacing of fluids

Peripheral edema

Early septic shock:

Vasodilation (decreased SVR)

Myocardial depression beginning

Hypotension

Reduced perfusion pressure

If source control performed at this stage: Outcomes worsen; mortality 20–30% despite adequate surgery and antibiotics. The host has already sustained organ damage that may be irreversible.

### Phase 4: Refractory Septic Shock and Multi-Organ Failure (>24 hours)

Definition: Prolonged, uncontrolled infection leading to irreversible organ failure.

Duration: >24 hours

Characteristics:

Bacterial load remains very high despite immune response

Extensive endothelial damage and increased vascular permeability

Widespread organ dysfunction:

Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS)

Acute kidney injury (AKI) Stage 2–3

Acute liver injury

Septic cardiomyopathy with reduced ejection fraction

Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC)

Sepsis-associated encephalopathy (SAE)

Refractory hypotension—blood pressure unresponsive to resuscitation and high-dose vasopressors

SOFA score ≥4–5

If source control performed at this stage: Outcomes are poor despite optimal surgical management. Mortality exceeds 40–50%. Even if infection is controlled, organ failure may be irreversible; survivors face prolonged critical illness and severe post-ICU syndrome.

### The Pathophysiological Basis of the 12-Hour Rule

The 12-hour threshold reflects the transition from Phase 2 to Phase 3 in the progression above.

### Why 12 hours?

Doubling time of bacteria: E. coli has a doubling time of ~20 minutes under optimal conditions. From 10³ CFU (contamination) to 10⁹ CFU (overwhelming bacteremia) represents approximately 10 doublings:

10 doublings × 20 minutes = 200 minutes ≈ 3.3 hours

However, in vivo conditions are sub-optimal (nutrient limitation, immune killing), so actual doubling times are longer (~30–40 minutes)

10 doublings × 35 minutes = 350 minutes ≈ 6 hours to reach 10⁸–10⁹ CFU

Endothelial damage progression: TNF-α and IL-1β produced in the first 6 hours begin causing endothelial damage. By 12 hours, this damage becomes widespread and partially irreversible:

Tight junction disruption is extensive

Epithelial apoptosis is substantial

Microvascular thrombosis begins

Organ perfusion is compromised

Transition from local to systemic inflammation: In Phase 2 (0–12 hours), inflammation is primarily localized to the peritoneum. By Phase 3 (12–24 hours), inflammation becomes systemic:

Endotoxemia is established (LPS in blood)

Circulating cytokine levels peak

Systemic complement activation is maximum

Organ dysfunction emerges

Immune dysfunction establishment: By 12 hours, early apoptosis of lymphocytes has begun; Treg expansion is starting. The immune system begins to transition from hyperinflammatory to immunosuppressive phenotype. Combined with overwhelming microbial burden, this creates the worst scenario—both excessive inflammation damaging the host and inadequate immunity for pathogen clearance.

### Clinical Implications: Operative Timing Strategy

Given this pathophysiology, the operative timing strategy should be:

Goal: Achieve source control as early as possible, ideally within 6 hours, and at latest within 12 hours.

Operationalization:

Recognition phase (0–1 hour):

Clinical suspicion of intra-abdominal infection based on fever, abdominal pain, peritoneal signs

Do not delay operative intervention pending imaging confirmation if clinical suspicion is high

Obtain blood cultures immediately; do not delay surgery

Diagnostic confirmation phase (1–3 hours):

Rapid imaging: Bedside ultrasound or CT (if hemodynamically stable)

If imaging confirms source: Proceed to operative intervention

If imaging non-diagnostic but clinical suspicion high: Proceed to operative exploration (diagnostic laparoscopy or laparotomy)

Operative intervention phase (3–6 hours from diagnosis):

Expedited operative intervention for source control

Duration of operative time should not be excessive (aim for 1–3 hours for straightforward cases)

Aggressive debridement and drainage

Post-operative phase (6–12 hours):

Re-evaluation if source control is in question

Plan for potential re-exploration at 24 hours if extensive contamination

### 5.4 Surgical Decision-Making: Debridement, Drainage, and Damage Control

### Principles of Debridement in Septic Peritonitis

Debridement is the surgical removal of devitalized tissue. In the context of intra-abdominal sepsis, debridement is essential for source control.

### Assessing Tissue Viability

The surgeon must make real-time assessments of tissue viability during operative exploration. Viability assessment is based on:

Color: Viable tissue is pink to red; non-viable tissue is gray, black, or purple

Bleeding: Viable tissue bleeds when cut; non-viable tissue does not

Texture: Viable tissue is firm and contracts with cautery or handling; non-viable tissue is soft and friable

Temperature: Viable tissue maintains normal body temperature; severely ischemic tissue may be cold

Pulsatility: For bowel, viable mesentery has palpable pulsation in vessels; ischemic mesentery has diminished or absent pulsation

### Conservative versus Aggressive Debridement

Conservative debridement:

Approach: Remove only tissue that is clearly non-viable (black, obviously necrotic)

Rationale: Preserves maximum tissue; avoids over-resection

Downside: May miss tissue that will necrose over the subsequent 24 hours; risks inadequate source control

Aggressive debridement:

Approach: Remove tissue with questionable viability; when in doubt, resect

Rationale: Ensures complete source control; removes all potentially infected tissue

Downside: May result in excessive resection; requires reconstructive surgery

Recommended approach for septic patients:

Initial operation: Perform aggressive debridement; remove tissue with marginal viability when source control is the primary goal

Planned re-exploration at 24 hours: Assess demarcation of tissue and perform additional debridement if necessary

This staged approach balances the need for source control with avoidance of excessive initial resection

### Special Considerations by Location

Perforated viscus (peptic ulcer, colon):

If perforation <12 hours and localized: Repair with primary closure may be considered

If perforation >12 hours, or with extensive contamination, or with necrosis: Resection is safer than primary repair

Resection removes the source of continued bacterial production

In septic patients, anastomotic healing is impaired; ostomy formation is often safer

Necrotic bowel (ischemia, volvulus, incarcerated hernia):

Aggressive resection is mandatory

Remove all non-viable bowel

Resection margins should be visibly bleeding (indicating viable tissue)

If borderline viability, plan for re-exploration at 24 hours rather than primary anastomosis

Appendicitis:

Simple appendicitis (no perforation, no abscess): Appendectomy with primary closure

Perforated appendicitis with localized abscess: Appendectomy with drainage of abscess

Perforated appendicitis with diffuse peritonitis: Appendectomy with extensive peritoneal lavage

Acute cholecystitis with perforation:

Cholecystectomy is mandatory (removes infected tissue and prevents continued bile leak)

Bile is a potent irritant and source of microbial growth

Primary closure of perforation alone is inadequate

### Drainage Strategies

### Rationale for Drainage

Goals of drainage:

Remove residual infected fluid: Abscess cavities cannot always be completely irrigated; drainage permits continued fluid removal

Permit identification of additional collections: A drain in place for 24–48 hours permits reaccumulation of fluid from additional small loculations, which can then be drained percutaneously

Monitor for additional infection: If drainage persists or becomes purulent after initial drainage, re-exploration may be necessary

### Types of Drains

Open packing:

Technique: Gauze or other material is packed into an open peritoneal cavity; changed daily

Indications: Extensive contamination with fecal peritonitis; when definitive closure of abdomen is not possible

Advantages: Complete visualization at each dressing change; permits assessment of demarcation

Disadvantages: Requires repeated anesthetics for dressing changes; causes pain and physiologic stress

Modern use: Largely replaced by VAC therapy

Closed drainage with passive drainage:

Technique: Percutaneous or operative drain (chest tube, Blake drain, others) placed in abscess cavity or dependent peritoneal location; permitted to drain passively

Fluid management: Drain is not aggressively irrigated; fluid drains by gravity and pressure difference

Indications: Well-localized abscess; managed by percutaneous drainage

Advantages: Simple; can remain in place for days to weeks

Disadvantages: Passive drainage may be insufficient for loculated fluid

Vacuum-assisted closure (VAC) therapy:

Technique: Foam dressing placed in open peritoneal cavity; negative pressure (75–125 mmHg) is applied via suction pump

Physiology:

Negative pressure promotes granulation tissue formation

Increases blood flow to wound

Promotes fluid removal

Reduces edema

Indications: Open abdomens after damage control; planned re-exploration

Advantages: Effective fluid removal; promotes healing; permits visualization at dressing changes

Disadvantages: Requires specialized equipment; prevents primary closure until therapy is completed; risk of fistulization if applied over bowel loops

Peritoneal catheter for continuous postoperative irrigation:

Technique: Catheter placed in peritoneal cavity; continuous or intermittent irrigation with normal saline or balanced crystalloid

Evidence: Some older studies suggested benefit; modern practice limited due to lack of strong evidence and catheter complications

Current status: Largely abandoned in favor of VAC or simple drainage

### Drain Placement Strategy

Number of drains: Depends on extent of contamination

Single localized abscess: 1 drain

Multiple or extensive contamination: Multiple drains (one per major collection)

Drain site: Should be placed dependent to gravity and should not traverse vital structures

Pelvis: Drain placed dependently in pelvis via transrectal approach or per vagina (in females)

Paracolic gutters: Drains placed along lateral peritoneal reflection

Upper abdomen: Drains placed at dependent sites (along gallbladder fossa if cholecystitis source)

Duration of drainage:

Typically drains are removed when output becomes minimal (<10–20 mL/day) and is serous (not purulent)

Timing varies: 3–7 days for simple collections; longer (weeks) for complex infections with slow improvement

### Damage Control Approach to Severe Intra-Abdominal Sepsis

Definition: Damage control is a surgical strategy prioritizing immediate control of life threats over definitive surgical repair.

### Damage Control Phases

Phase 1: Abbreviated initial operation:

Goal: Rapidly achieve source control with minimal operative time

Actions:

Quick assessment of peritoneal cavity

Identify and control major sources (perforated viscus, major bleeding)

Perform rapid debridement (remove obviously necrotic tissue)

Minimal anastomoses (use ostomies instead)

Rapid abdominal wall closure or temporary closure

Duration: 30–90 minutes (goal is speed, not perfection)

Phase 2: Intensive care resuscitation:

Duration: 24–48 hours

Goal: Correct coagulopathy, acidosis, and shock

Actions:

Aggressive fluid resuscitation

Vasopressor support

Balanced transfusion (RBC:FFP:platelets ratio ~1:1:1)

Correct hypothermia

Monitor lactate clearance

Phase 3: Planned re-exploration:

Timing: 24–48 hours after initial operation

Goal: Perform definitive surgery (additional debridement, anastomosis, abdominal wall closure)

Actions:

Reassess tissue viability

Perform additional debridement if necessary

Consider primary anastomosis if hemodynamically stable

Close abdomen if possible

Phase 4: Reoperation as needed:

Indication: If infection persists or additional complications develop

Typical scenario: Refractory septic shock despite initial surgery; additional source identified

### Indications for Damage Control

Severe septic shock:

Hypotension requiring high-dose vasopressors (norepinephrine >15 μg/kg/min)

Lactate >4 mmol/L despite fluid resuscitation

SOFA score ≥4

Coagulopathy:

INR >1.5

Platelet count <50,000

Evidence of DIC (low fibrinogen, elevated D-dimer, elevated FDPs)

Hypothermia:

Core temperature <35°C

Contraindication to primary anastomosis (impaired healing)

Extensive contamination:

Fecal peritonitis

Widespread abscess formation

Large contaminated area requiring multiple debri

30 дек. 2025 г.

dements

### Specific Damage Control Techniques

Rapid source control:

Control major bleeding (resection of bleeding segment)

Resect obviously necrotic tissue

Place drains for accessibility

Rapid abdominal closure:

Option 1: Primary fascial closure with open skin:

Close fascia with absorbable suture (attempting to restore anatomic layers)

Leave skin open for dressing changes

Perform skin closure in 3–5 days after inflammation decreases

Option 2: VAC therapy:

Place VAC dressing in open abdomen

No primary fascial closure

Plan for re-exploration at 24–48 hours

Option 3: Temporary abdominal wall closure:

Use commercially available temporary closure devices (Bogota bag, DuraFastTM, others)

Permits access for re-exploration

Provides containment of abdominal contents

Ostomy versus anastomosis:

### Damage control approach favors ostomy formation

Primary anastomosis deferred to Phase 3 after stabilization and adequate source control

Examples:

Perforated colon: End colostomy at Phase 1; reversal at Phase 3 (or later)

Small bowel resection: Restoring bowel continuity deferred to Phase 3

### Outcomes of Surgical Management in Intra-Abdominal Sepsis

Factors associated with improved outcomes:

Early source control: Operative intervention within 6–12 hours

Adequate debridement: Removal of all devitalized tissue and infection source

Appropriate antimicrobial therapy: Broad-spectrum coverage initially, de-escalation based on cultures

Hemodynamic optimization: Aggressive fluid resuscitation, vasopressor support

Organ support: Mechanical ventilation, renal replacement therapy as needed

Sequential operative interventions: Planned re-exploration for continued source control

Mortality in intra-abdominal sepsis:

Community-acquired, early diagnosis, appropriate treatment: 10–20%

Healthcare-associated, delayed diagnosis, resistant organisms: 30–50%

Refractory septic shock with multi-organ failure: >60%

### 5.5 The Second Hit Hypothesis and Multi-Organ Failure Cascade

### The Concept of "First Hit" and "Second Hit"

The second hit hypothesis is a conceptual framework explaining why some patients with intra-abdominal infection progress to catastrophic multi-organ failure while others recover with supportive care.

### The First Hit: Intra-Abdominal Infection

Definition: The initial insult is the intra-abdominal infection—bacterial translocation, endotoxemia, cytokine production, and activation of coagulation.

Pathophysiology:

Peritoneal inflammation is activated

Systemic inflammatory mediators (TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6) are produced

Endothelium is injured

Coagulation is activated

Response: The host immune system responds to contain the infection:

Neutrophil recruitment increases

Macrophage activation increases

Innate immunity is fully mobilized

Outcome of first hit alone:

If source is controlled early (before irreversible endothelial damage), the inflammatory response resolves

Organ function recovers

Patient survives with appropriate care

### The Second Hit: Additional Insult

Definition: A second insult occurs during critical illness, often after the initial infection appears to be controlled.

Examples of second hits:

Inadequate source control: The initial surgical intervention did not completely control infection; additional collections develop

Aspiration: Ventilator-associated aspiration of gastric contents into lungs

Catheter-related infection: Nosocomial infection from central line, urinary catheter, or endotracheal tube

Reperfusion injury: During resuscitation from shock, reperfusion of ischemic organs causes oxidative injury

Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI): Massive transfusion in resuscitation triggers immune response

Ischemic injury: Prolonged shock before resuscitation or inadequate resuscitation

Timing: Second hits often occur 24–72 hours after the initial presentation, after the patient has already been through the hyperinflammatory phase.

### The Multi-Organ Failure Cascade

The first hit + second hit interaction results in multi-organ failure:

Mechanism:

First hit: Initial endothelial injury from bacterial infection and inflammatory mediators

Hyperinflammatory phase: TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6, complement activation cause widespread endothelial injury, increased permeability, microvascular thrombosis

Vulnerable state: The host is now in a "primed" state—immune cells are activated, endothelium is damaged, organ reserves are diminished

Second hit: An additional insult occurs (aspiration, sepsis from catheter, ischemia-reperfusion injury)

Amplified response: The second hit, in the context of the already-activated immune system, triggers a disproportionately large inflammatory response

Overwhelming inflammation: The amplified response causes widespread endothelial damage

Multi-organ failure: Across multiple organ systems simultaneously

Clinical manifestation: Patient who appeared to be improving after initial surgery suddenly deteriorates with:

Acute respiratory failure (ARDS)

Acute kidney injury

Shock refractory to resuscitation

Coagulopathy (DIC)

Encephalopathy

### Evidence for the Second Hit Hypothesis

Preclinical studies: Animal models of CLP (cecal ligation and puncture) followed by a second hit (lipopolysaccharide, mechanical ventilation-induced injury, or hemorrhage) show:

Significantly increased mortality compared to single hit

Enhanced inflammatory response (higher cytokine levels, extended duration of elevation)

More severe organ dysfunction

Clinical observations:

Patients who deteriorate after apparent improvement from initial sepsis often have identifiable second insult (new infection, inadequate source control, aspiration)

Patients who never develop multi-organ failure often avoid second hits (either through excellent source control preventing second infection, or through infection control measures preventing nosocomial secondary infections)

### Clinical Implications: Prevention of Second Hits

Understanding the second hit hypothesis directs clinical management toward preventing or minimizing second hits:

### 1. Complete Source Control Prevents Ongoing Hits

Strategy: Ensure that the initial operative intervention completely controls infection

Multiple imaging studies confirm source control (CT or ultrasound)

Low threshold for re-exploration if clinical suspicion of inadequate control

Percutaneous drainage of new collections identified postoperatively

Outcome: Prevents the "first hit continuing" scenario

### 2. Minimize Nosocomial Infection (Preventing Secondary Hits)

Strategies:

Line care: Sterile technique for central lines, dressing changes per protocol

Catheter removal: Remove unnecessary lines promptly

Ventilator care: Head-of-bed elevation, subglottic secretion drainage, spontaneous breathing trials

Urinary catheter care: Remove unnecessary catheters promptly

Antibiotic stewardship: Avoid unnecessary broad-spectrum antibiotics that select for resistant organisms

Outcome: Reduces rate of nosocomial infections that would constitute secondary hits

### 3. Minimize Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury

Strategies:

Early resuscitation: Achieve hemodynamic targets quickly to minimize ischemic time

Avoid excessive fluid: Recent evidence suggests conservative fluid strategies reduce ARDS and other complications; avoid massive fluid overload

Outcome: Reduces reperfusion injury as a second hit

### 4. Careful Transfusion Management

Rationale: Massive transfusion (particularly with PRBC:FFP:platelet ratios unfavorable) is associated with worse outcomes

Strategy: Use balanced transfusion ratios (1:1:1 PRBC:FFP:platelets), avoid unnecessary transfusion

Outcome: Reduces transfusion-related injury as potential second hit

### 5.6 Percutaneous versus Open Approaches: Risk-Benefit Analysis

### Evolution of Approach Selection

Traditionally, all intra-abdominal infections were managed with open surgical exploration and lavage. Contemporary practice increasingly employs percutaneous image-guided drainage of localized collections.

The shift reflects recognition that:

Not all intra-abdominal infections require open surgery

Percutaneous drainage is less invasive than open exploration

Complications of open surgery (anesthesia, operative trauma, postoperative pain/ileus) are avoided with percutaneous approach

However, open surgery remains essential for diffuse peritonitis, when multiple loculations preclude image-guided drainage, or when tissue debridement is necessary.

### Percutaneous Drainage: Indications and Contraindications

### Indications for Percutaneous Drainage

Well-localized collection:

Collection is imaged on CT or ultrasound

Single collection or limited number of collections accessible to percutaneous approach

Collection size >4 cm (smaller collections often resolve spontaneously)

No peritonitis:

Absence of diffuse peritoneal signs

Signs of localized infection (fever, elevated WBC, positive cultures from collection)

SIRS or sepsis criteria met, but not acute abdomen with rebound/guarding

No contraindications to percutaneous approach (see below)

Appropriate organism:

Organism is amenable to antibiotic therapy (not multidrug-resistant, not fungal in immunocompromised host)

In immunocompromised patients (neutropenic, severe immunosuppression), organisms like Aspergillus or Candida may require operative debridement in addition to drainage

### Contraindications to Percutaneous Drainage

Diffuse peritonitis:

Signs of diffuse peritoneal inflammation (rebound, guarding)

Suggests free fluid throughout peritoneal cavity

Cannot be drained by single or limited percutaneous drains

Requires operative lavage and source control

Multiple loculations not accessible to percutaneous approach:

CT showing multiple loculated collections

Not all collections accessible via percutaneous imaging

Requires operative exploration to identify and drain all collections

Requirement for tissue debridement:

Necrotic tissue that requires surgical removal

Perforated viscus requiring resection

Anastomosis required (cannot be done percutaneously)

Hemodynamic instability:

Septic shock with requirement for high-dose vasopressors

Coagulopathy precluding percutaneous approach

Clinical judgment: May require urgent operative control rather than time-consuming percutaneous drainage

Immunocompromised host:

Severely neutropenic patient

Transplant recipient with graft dysfunction

May require operative source control in addition to or instead of percutaneous drainage alone

### Open Surgical Approach: Indications

### Indications for Open Surgical Exploration

Diffuse peritonitis:

Acute abdomen with peritoneal signs (rebound, guarding, rigidity)

Imaging showing free fluid throughout peritoneal cavity

Indicates contamination throughout peritoneum requiring operative lavage

Perforated viscus:

Imaging showing perforation (free air, oral contrast extravasation)

Requires operative intervention for source control (resection or repair)

Surgical emergency requiring resection:

Necrotic bowel

Incarcerated hernia with ischemia

Volvulus

These require operative intervention for resection; percutaneous drainage alone is insufficient

Multiple loculated collections not accessible to percutaneous approach:

Operative exploration provides complete visualization

All collections can be identified and drained

Adequate debridement can be performed

Failed percutaneous drainage:

Initial percutaneous drainage attempted

Infection not controlled (persistent fever, positive repeat cultures, clinical deterioration)

Conversion to operative management

### Combined Approach: Percutaneous Drainage with Option for Operative Management

Contemporary strategy: In patients with intra-abdominal infection of uncertain etiology:

Obtain imaging (CT or ultrasound) to assess for localized collection

If well-localized collection identified:

Attempt percutaneous drainage

Obtain cultures from drain fluid

Monitor clinical response (fever curve, elevated WBC trend, clinical examination)

If adequate clinical response:

Continue antibiotics and percutaneous drainage management

Remove drain when output is minimal and serous

If inadequate clinical response (persistent fever, clinical deterioration, positive repeat cultures):

Obtain repeat imaging

If additional collections identified: Percutaneous drainage of new collections

If diffuse contamination or inadequate drainage: Convert to operative management

Advantage: Minimizes unnecessary operative procedures while maintaining access to definitive surgical management if needed.

### Summary: Strategic Approach to Intra-Abdominal Sepsis

Intra-abdominal sepsis requires:

Recognition: Early identification through clinical assessment and imaging

Microbiological diagnosis: Blood and source cultures prior to antibiotics

Broad-spectrum antimicrobial therapy: Within 1 hour of recognition

Rapid source control: Within 6–12 hours, preferably operatively if diffuse peritonitis; percutaneously if well-localized

Aggressive hemodynamic support: Fluid resuscitation, vasopressor support, organ support

Sequential assessment: Planned re-exploration if indicated based on infection persistence

De-escalation: Based on culture results and clinical response

Prevention of second hits: Complete source control, infection prevention, careful resuscitation

Outcomes: Mortality in intra-abdominal sepsis has decreased from historical rates >50% to contemporary rates of 15–25% for community-acquired infections and 30–40% for healthcare-associated infections. Further improvements depend on adoption of early recognition protocols, adherence to bundles emphasizing rapid source control, and integration of emerging immunotherapeutic approaches.

### CHAPTER 6: THORACIC AND ESOPHAGEAL SEPSIS

### 6.1 Mediastinitis: Surgical Emergencies and Necrotizing Fasciitis

### Definition and Classification of Mediastinitis

Mediastinitis is inflammation of the mediastinum—the compartment within the thorax bounded by the parietal pleura and containing the heart, great vessels, esophagus, trachea, and thymus.

### Classification by Etiology

Acute mediastinitis (hours to days):

Fulminant infection with rapidly progressive systemic toxicity

Mortality rate: 20–40% even with aggressive treatment

Requires emergent surgical intervention

Chronic mediastinitis (weeks to months):

Indolent infection with fibrosis and mediastinal scarring

Often granulomatous (histoplasmosis, tuberculosis, sarcoidosis)

Less immediately life-threatening but associated with significant morbidity

This chapter focuses on acute mediastinitis, which is a true surgical emergency.

### Primary versus Secondary Mediastinitis

Primary mediastinitis:

Direct introduction of infection into the mediastinum

Sources: esophageal perforation, cardiac surgery, mediastinal instrumentation, descending cervical necrotizing fasciitis extending into mediastinum

Accounts for ~70% of acute mediastinitis cases

Secondary mediastinitis:

Extension of infection from contiguous structures

Sources: parapharyngeal abscess, retropharyngeal abscess, acute epiglottitis with spreading infection

Accounts for ~30% of acute mediastinitis cases

### Esophageal Perforation: The Most Common Source of Primary Mediastinitis

### Etiology of Esophageal Perforation

Spontaneous rupture (Boerhaave syndrome):

Mechanism: Sudden increase in intraluminal esophageal pressure combined with relaxed cricopharyngeus, causing full-thickness esophageal tear

Typical scenario: Forceful vomiting or retching; sudden intrathoracic pressure increase (straining, seizure, childbirth)

Location: Usually left posterolateral wall of distal esophagus, 2–3 cm above gastroesophageal junction

Incidence: 1–3 cases per million per year in developed countries

Iatrogenic perforation:

Endoscopy: Perforation during diagnostic upper endoscopy (0.02–0.1% of procedures) or therapeutic procedures (variceal band ligation, dilation, sclerotherapy)

Bougie dilation: Dilation for achalasia or stricture carries perforation risk of 0.1–0.5%

Transesophageal echocardiography (TEE): Rare but reported

Intubation: Esophageal intubation or aggressive intubation attempts

Traumatic perforation:

Blunt trauma: Rapid compression of esophagus against vertebral column

Penetrating trauma: Foreign body ingestion, stab wound, blunt force to neck/chest

Caustic ingestion: Chemical injury to esophageal wall

Spontaneous perforation from ulceration:

Peptic ulcer: Rarely perforates into esophagus rather than stomach (when perforation occurs at GE junction)

Malignancy: Esophageal cancer with transmural invasion

Infectious ulcers: CMV ulcers in immunocompromised patients

Perforation from external compression:

Aortic aneurysm: Aortic-esophageal fistula from enlarging aneurysm eroding through esophagus

Mediastinal malignancy: Tumor erosion through esophageal wall

### Pathophysiology of Esophageal Perforation-Induced Mediastinitis

Immediate effects (minutes to hours):

Salivary and gastric contamination:

Esophagus is contaminated with oral secretions containing anaerobes, gram-negative organisms, and other commensals

If distal esophagus perforated, gastric contents (including H. pylori, other acid-resistant organisms) spill into mediastinum

Gastric acid causes chemical mediastinitis in addition to bacterial infection

Mediastinal contamination:

Unlike peritoneal cavity, mediastinum has no peritoneal lining to "wall off" infection

Infection rapidly disseminates throughout mediastinum

Contamination extends into dependent areas:

Anteriorly: Anterior mediastinum and pleural spaces

Posteriorly: Posterior mediastinum extending down along esophagus

Inferiorly: Can extend into diaphragmatic hiatus and upper abdominal cavity

Mediastinal inflammation and edema:

Tissue injury from caustic contents and bacterial lipopolysaccharides

Inflammatory mediators cause:

Edema formation

Vascular permeability increase

Compression of vital structures (heart, great vessels)

Early systemic effects (hours 0–24):

Bacterial translocation and sepsis:

Direct introduction of large inoculum of oral microbiota into mediastinal tissues

Rapid bacteremia—blood cultures positive in 60–80% of cases

Septic shock develops in 30–50% of cases

Cardiovascular compromise:

Mediastinal edema compresses great vessels (SVC, IVC)

May compress right atrium or ventricle

Reduces venous return; decreases cardiac output

Hypotension develops

Necrotizing infection:

Mediastinal infection rapidly progresses to necrotizing mediastinitis

Unlike simple mediastinitis (localized infection with inflammation), necrotizing mediastinitis involves:

Rapidly spreading infection with tissue necrosis

Multiple synergistic organisms (polymicrobial) spreading along fascial planes

Tissue destruction progresses faster than host immune response can contain

Late effects (>24 hours without intervention):

Descending necrotizing mediastinitis (DNM):

Necrotizing infection descends from upper mediastinum toward diaphragm

Follows fascial planes (typically between esophagus and prevertebral fascia)

Extends into abdomen, potentially involving upper abdominal organs

Mortality of untreated DNM approaches 100%

Septic shock with multi-organ failure:

Persistent bacteremia

Overwhelming septic response despite source control attempts

ARDS, AKI, hepatic dysfunction

Cardiac and vascular complications:

Erosion of necrotizing infection into adjacent cardiac chambers or great vessels

Aortic rupture (catastrophic)

Cardiac tamponade from accumulated infected fluid

Sudden cardiovascular collapse

### Clinical Presentation of Esophageal Perforation and Mediastinitis

Classic presentation (Mackler triad, present in only ~50% of cases):

Vomiting (from causative event)

Subcutaneous emphysema (air from esophagus tracking into subcutaneous tissues)

Chest pain

More common symptoms:

Severe chest pain (90% of cases)—often epigastric or substernal, can mimic MI

Dysphagia or odynophagia (80–90%)

Fever and signs of sepsis (developing over hours)

Respiratory distress (from pneumomediastinum or pleural effusion)

Neck stiffness or neck pain (if cervical esophagus involved)

Hematemesis (if perforation at distal esophagus)

Physical examination findings:

Subcutaneous emphysema: Palpable crepitus in neck, chest wall, or face (pathognomonic)

Fever and tachycardia: Signs of sepsis developing

Respiratory distress: Tachypnea, retractions

Cardiovascular signs: Tachycardia, hypotension (developing)

Abdominal examination: May show signs of peritonitis if perforation extends into abdomen

Severity assessment:

Early presentation (<12 hours): May show minimal systemic signs despite significant pathology

Delayed presentation (>24 hours): Profound sepsis, shock, multi-organ failure common

Critical point: Early esophageal perforation presents with severe pain and dysphagia but may lack systemic sepsis signs. The absence of fever or hemodynamic compromise does NOT exclude esophageal perforation.

### Diagnosis of Esophageal Perforation

Clinical suspicion:

Must be high in any patient with severe chest pain after vomiting/retching, after esophageal instrumentation, or after blunt chest trauma

Delay in diagnosis is the single most important factor associated with mortality

Imaging studies:

Chest X-ray (60–80% sensitivity):

Pneumomediastinum: Air in mediastinum visible as radiolucent streaks outlining cardiac silhouette, great vessels, or trachea

Pleural effusion: Often unilateral (left) due to location of esophageal perforation

Subcutaneous emphysema: Air in subcutaneous tissues outlined on film

Mediastinal widening: Edema and/or hemorrhage

Limitations: Early perforation may not show radiographic evidence; normal CXR does NOT exclude perforation

CT with IV contrast and oral contrast (90–95% sensitivity):

Gold standard for diagnosis

Findings:

Free air in mediastinum (pneumomediastinum)

Oral contrast extravasation from esophagus into mediastinum (definitive if present)

Mediastinal collections

Pleural effusion

Extent of infection/necrosis

Technique: Oral contrast should be water-soluble initially (barium contraindicated due to chemical mediastinitis if perforation present)

Esophagography (gastrografin study):

Technique: Patient swallows water-soluble contrast; fluoroscopic images obtained

Findings: Extravasation of contrast outside esophageal lumen is diagnostic

Sensitivity: 70–90%, depending on size and location of perforation

Advantages: Can be performed at bedside or in OR; quick

Limitations: May miss small perforations; operator-dependent

Esophagoscopy:

Technique: Direct visualization of esophageal lumen with endoscope

Findings: Full-thickness tear visualized; may be able to identify perforation site and assess edges

Role: Diagnostic confirmation; also permits assessment of distal extension of injury

Caution: Must be performed carefully to avoid further injury; only after consideration of other pathology

Blood cultures:

Obtain before any intervention

Positive in 60–80% of cases with mediastinitis

Guide antimicrobial therapy

### Prognostic Factors in Esophageal Perforation

Time from perforation to intervention:

<12 hours: Mortality 10–15%

12–24 hours: Mortality 20–30%

>24 hours: Mortality 40–60%

### Every hour delay increases mortality risk

Location of perforation:

Cervical: Better prognosis (can be drained/managed with catheter drainage in some cases)

Thoracic: Worse prognosis (requires operative management)

Abdominal: Worst prognosis if extends to abdomen (involves additional organs)

Extent of contamination:

Contained perforation (walled off by tissues): Better prognosis

Free perforation (communicates with pleural space): Worse prognosis

Systemic effects at presentation:

Hemodynamic stability: Better prognosis

Shock (systolic BP <90 mmHg): Associated with increased mortality

Multi-organ failure: Associated with severe outcome

### Surgical Management of Esophageal Perforation

### Timing of Intervention

Urgency: Esophageal perforation is a TRUE SURGICAL EMERGENCY. Intervention should be performed as soon as diagnosis is made.

Delay consequences:

Every hour of delay permits further mediastinal contamination

Necrotizing infection extends further

Systemic sepsis worsens

Organ failure develops

Pre-operative optimization (minutes, not hours):

IV access (large-bore peripheral and central line)

Blood cultures

Broad-spectrum antibiotics immediately (see antimicrobial section below)

Fluid resuscitation to correct any hypotension

Cross-matched blood if available

Anesthesia preparation

Operative intervention: Should proceed to OR within 30 minutes of diagnosis if possible

### Operative Approach

Choice of approach depends on location of perforation:

Cervical esophageal perforation:

Approach: Left cervical incision along sternocleidomastoid

Technique:

Identify esophagus

Visualize perforation

Debride devitalized tissue

Primary repair with 2–3 layer closure (mucosa, muscle, overlying tissue) if perforation identified early (<6 hours)

Placement of drain in perforation site

Consideration of catheter drainage as alternative to operative management in select cases (see below)

Thoracic esophageal perforation:

Approach options:

Option 1: Left thoracotomy (preferred for mid-to-distal esophageal perforations):

Technique:

Left posterolateral thoracotomy (4th or 5th intercostal space)

Exposure of esophagus

Identification of perforation site

Debridement of necrotic tissue

Primary repair if early (<6 hours):

Mucosa and submucosa closure with fine absorbable suture (4–0 or 5–0)

Muscle closure with interrupted sutures

Seromuscular closure

Reinforcement with pericardial patch or pleural flap

Drain placement

Mediastinal lavage with copious saline irrigation

Chest tube placement for pleural drainage

Option 2: Video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery (VATS):

Advantages: Reduced operative trauma compared to open thoracotomy

Indications: Early diagnosis (<12 hours), localized perforation, hemodynamically stable

Disadvantages: Limited ability to perform extensive debridement; requires expertise

Role: Emerging; considered in select cases but not standard of care

Option 3: Transhiatal approach (for distal esophageal perforations):

Technique: Mobilization of esophagus through upper abdominal and cervical incisions without thoracotomy

Advantages: Avoids thoracotomy

Disadvantages: Limited mediastinal access; more difficult to achieve adequate debridement

Role: Limited; less commonly used than thoracotomy

Esophageal resection:

Indications:

Delayed presentation (>24 hours) with extensive necrosis

Perforation in setting of esophageal malignancy

Multiple perforations

Destructive infection making repair impossible

Technique:

Esophageal resection with removal of all devitalized tissue

Gastric pull-up or colonic interposition for reconstruction (typically performed as staged procedure)

Initial resection with planned reconstruction in 3–6 months

Mortality: Resection carries higher mortality than primary repair (20–40% vs 10–15%) but may be necessary for extensive injury

### Drainage and Debridement Details

Mediastinal debridement:

Aggressive removal of all devitalized, infected tissue

Necrotizing infection may extend beyond visibly necrotic areas

Sequential layers of mediastinum must be inspected and cleaned

Pleural space management:

Both pleural cavities (left and right) often contaminated

Pleural tube drainage: Place chest tubes in both pleural spaces for drainage of fluid and air

Pleural irrigation: Some surgeons irrigate pleural spaces with normal saline; evidence for benefit is limited

Mediastinal drain placement:

Purpose: Continue drainage of mediastinal fluid and infected material

Technique: Place drain adjacent to esophageal repair site, positioned to gravity-dependent areas

Duration: Drain typically remains until output becomes minimal (serous fluid, <10–20 mL/day)

Primary closure versus open management:

Primary closure of esophagus: Indicated if:

Early diagnosis (<6 hours)

Minimal contamination

Good tissue quality at perforation edges

Hemodynamic stability

Open management: If extensive contamination or delayed diagnosis:

Leave esophageal perforation open to drain mediastinum

Place catheter across perforation for enteral feeding access

Plan for delayed closure after resolution of mediastinitis

### Non-Operative Management (Catheter Drainage)

Selected cases of esophageal perforation can be managed with catheter drainage rather than operative intervention.

Indications for catheter drainage:

Early diagnosis (<24 hours preferred)

Contained perforation: Perforation walled off by adjacent tissues, not free into pleural space

Small perforation (diagnosed endoscopically or on imaging)

Cervical esophageal perforation: Often amenable to catheter drainage

Hemodynamic stability: No septic shock

Ability to monitor closely: ICU-level care available

Technique:

CT imaging to confirm location and extent

Percutaneous catheter placement: Interventional radiologist places pigtail catheter through catheter through perforation site (or through endoscopic guidance) into esophageal lumen below perforation

Nothing by mouth (NPO): Maintain NPO status to minimize saliva flow into defect

Enteral nutrition: Feeding tube placed (typically nasojejunal) distal to perforation to permit nutrition

Drain management: Catheter is left in place for 4–6 weeks, permitting healing of perforation

Follow-up imaging: Esophagography performed at 3–4 weeks to confirm healing

Success rate: 70–90% in appropriately selected cases (early diagnosis, contained perforation, no signs of mediastinal necrotizing infection)

Failure and conversion: If clinical deterioration occurs (persistent fever, sepsis, sign of mediastinitis), conversion to operative management is necessary.

Advantage: Avoids operative intervention in appropriate cases; reduces operative morbidity

Current trend: Some centers increasingly employ catheter drainage for select esophageal perforations, reserving operative management for failures of catheter drainage

### Antimicrobial Therapy

Initiation: Within 1 hour of diagnosis, even before imaging confirmation if clinical suspicion is high

Coverage: Esophageal flora includes both aerobes and anaerobes

Initial empiric regimen:

Gram-negative coverage: Ceftazidime or fluoroquinolone (ciprofloxacin)

Gram-positive coverage: Cefazolin or vancomycin (if MRSA risk)

Anaerobic coverage: Clindamycin or metronidazole

Typical regimen:

Beta-lactam + beta-lactamase inhibitor: Ampicillin-sulbactam or piperacillin-tazobactam

OR

Cephalosporin + clindamycin: Ceftazidime + clindamycin

OR

Fluoroquinolone + metronidazole: Ciprofloxacin + metronidazole + vancomycin (if MRSA risk)

De-escalation: Based on blood and mediastinal fluid cultures (if obtained):

Adjust coverage based on organism susceptibilities

Typical de-escalation narrows spectrum to single agent covering identified organisms

Duration: 7–14 days depending on source control adequacy and clinical response

### Post-Operative Management

Monitoring:

ICU-level care for hemodynamic monitoring

Serial lactate measurement to assess tissue perfusion

Repeat imaging (CT) at 24–48 hours to assess for residual collections

Daily drain output assessment

Organ support:

Mechanical ventilation if respiratory compromise

Vasopressor support if shock persists

Renal replacement therapy if AKI develops

Nutrition:

Initial period: NPO to permit esophageal healing

Enteral nutrition via jejunal feeding tube if catheter drainage approach

Parenteral nutrition if operative approach and esophageal defect cannot be crossed for feeding access

Progressive advancement as healing occurs

Complications:

Persistent sepsis: Indicates inadequate source control; consider repeat CT imaging and possible re-exploration

Anastomotic leak: If esophageal repair performed; presents as persistent fever, drainage of food material from drain, or sepsis recurrence

Mediastinitis recurrence: May require re-operation

ARDS: From prolonged sepsis and mechanical ventilation

Multi-organ failure: If perforation not recognized early

### Descending Necrotizing Mediastinitis (DNM)

### Definition and Pathophysiology

DNM is necrotizing infection originating in the neck (from odontogenic infection, pharyngeal infection, or cervical esophageal perforation) that descends along fascial planes into the thoracic mediastinum and potentially into the abdomen.

Mechanism of spread:

Infection follows anatomic fascial planes of low resistance

Primarily descends along the visceral space (space between esophagus and prevertebral fascia)

Can extend along trachea and esophagus into chest

May extend into posterior mediastinum and along the aorta

Can descend into abdomen through diaphragmatic hiatus

Causative organisms:

Polymicrobial: anaerobes (Prevotella, Peptostreptococcus, Bacteroides), gram-negatives (E. coli, Klebsiella), gram-positives (Streptococcus, Staphylococcus)

Often from oral flora

Commonly includes Group A Streptococcus (especially virulent)

### Sources of DNM

Odontogenic infections (70% of cases):

Dental abscess (typically from lower molars)

Periodontal disease

Tooth extraction with subsequent infection

Pharyngeal and throat infections:

Acute pharyngitis complicated by abscess formation

Retropharyngeal abscess

Epiglottitis

Cervical esophageal perforation:

Spontaneous or iatrogenic (endoscopic)

Extends downward into thoracic mediastinum

Other sources:

Cervical spine osteomyelitis

Penetrating neck trauma with infection

### Clinical Presentation

Neck phase (initial localization):

Fever, malaise

Neck pain and swelling

Cervical lymphadenopathy

May appear as simple pharyngitis or abscess initially

Mediastinal phase (descent):

Timeline: Hours to days after initial neck infection

Symptoms:

Severe chest pain (substernal or interscapular)

Dysphagia

Dyspnea

Fever continuing or spiking higher

Signs of septic shock (hypotension, confusion, tachycardia)

Physical findings:

Subcutaneous emphysema in neck and/or chest (from gas-producing organisms)

Crepitus

Cervical swelling persistent or worsening

Respiratory distress

Abdominal phase (if descends to abdomen):

Abdominal pain

Signs of peritonitis

Organ failure

### Diagnosis

High suspicion required: Any patient with cervical infection (dental abscess, pharyngitis, or other neck source) who develops chest pain, dyspnea, or septic shock.

Imaging:

CT neck, chest, and abdomen with IV contrast (comprehensive evaluation of extent)

Findings:

Cervical soft tissue edema and abscess (neck source)

Pneumomediastinum (gas-producing organisms)

Mediastinal fluid collections and edema

Extent of descent (into thorax, potentially into abdomen)

MRI: Can provide excellent soft tissue detail; useful for delineating extent

Blood cultures: Obtain before intervention; typically positive in 40–60% of cases

### Management

Immediate antibiotics:

Initiate within 1 hour of diagnosis

Coverage: Broad-spectrum (includes anaerobes, oral flora)

Regimen: Ampicillin-sulbactam or piperacillin-tazobactam + vancomycin (covers Staph aureus, MRSA)

Source control:

Cervical phase:

Cervical incision and drainage of cervical abscess

If odontogenic source: Dental extraction of offending tooth

Aggressive debridement of necrotic tissue

Mediastinal phase:

Thoracotomy and mediastinal drainage if mediastinal involvement confirmed

Extensive mediastinal debridement

Drain placement for ongoing drainage

Abdominal phase (if involved):

Laparotomy and drainage of abdominal collections

Debridement as needed

Staged approach:

Initial cervical intervention for source control

Re-assessment at 24 hours

Thoracotomy if mediastinal collections persist or if clinical deterioration

Nutrition and supportive care: As outlined for esophageal perforation above

### Prognosis

Mortality: 15–50% depending on extent and delay in treatment

Early intervention: 15–20% mortality

Delayed diagnosis/intervention: 40–50% mortality

Morbidity:

Survivors often have prolonged critical illness

Multiple operative interventions typically required

High risk of PICS (post-intensive care syndrome)

### 6.2 Esophageal Perforation and Rapidly Progressive Infection

### Contrast with Simple Esophageal Perforation

While we have discussed esophageal perforation-induced mediastinitis above, it is worth emphasizing the spectrum of presentation in esophageal perforation.

### Limited Perforation: Contained versus Free

Contained perforation (15–20% of cases):

Small perforation that is immediately sealed off by adjacent tissues

Inflammation is localized

Contamination is limited

May respond to conservative management (catheter drainage) if recognized early

Lower mortality if managed promptly

Free perforation (80–85% of cases):

Full-thickness esophageal tear not sealed by tissues

Contents spill freely into mediastinum/pleural space

Rapid dissemination of contamination

Rapid systemic deterioration

Requires operative intervention

Higher mortality

### Rapidly Progressive Infection in Esophageal Perforation

Necrotizing esophagitis:

In immunocompromised patients (HIV with CD4 <50, transplant recipients), esophageal infection can progress rapidly even without perforation

CMV, herpes simplex virus (HSV), or Candida esophagitis can progress to transmural infection with perforation

Presentation: Severe dysphagia, chest pain, signs of perforation

Management: Antiviral therapy for HSV/CMV; antifungal for Candida; operative intervention if perforation occurs

Fungal esophagitis:

Candida esophagitis: Usually responds to antifungal therapy; rarely causes perforation

Aspergillus esophagitis: Rare; can be very aggressive in severely immunocompromised

Zygomycosis (Mucormycosis): Rare but extremely aggressive; high mortality

Rapidly invasive

Associated with profound immunosuppression or diabetes

Requires operative debridement + systemic antifungal

### 6.3 Empyema and Pleural Space Infections

### Definition and Classification of Empyema

Empyema (empyema thoracis) is pus within the pleural space. It represents a spectrum of pleural infections ranging from simple parapneumonic effusion to frank purulent fluid.

### Classification by Stage (Light's Classification)

Uncomplicated parapneumonic effusion:

Definition: Fluid accumulation adjacent to pneumonia but without infection of the pleural space itself

Characteristics:

Sterile fluid (negative cultures)

Low LDH, normal glucose

pH >7.00

Management: Treatment of underlying pneumonia; typically resolves without drainage

Outcome: Usually resolves without sequelae

Complicated parapneumonic effusion:

Definition: Sterile or minimally infected fluid in pleural space in association with pneumonia

Characteristics:

Sterile or low-grade bacteria (cultures often negative)

Elevated LDH

Glucose 40–60 mg/dL

pH 7.00–7.20

Management: May require drainage if large (>1 cm on imaging) or associated with respiratory compromise

Outcome: With appropriate antibiotic therapy, usually resolves

Empyema (frank):

Definition: Frank pus in pleural space with positive cultures and advanced degree of infection

Characteristics:

Positive pleural fluid cultures

Very low glucose (<40 mg/dL)

Acidic pH (<7.00)

Elevated LDH

Management: Requires drainage (chest tube or VATS) + antibiotics

Outcome: Requires drainage; without drainage, mortality approaches 30–40%

### Fibrinous/Organizing Phase

Evolution of empyema:

Acute phase: Thin, free-flowing pus in pleural space

Fibrinous phase: Fibrin deposition on pleural surfaces; loculation begins

Organizing phase: Thick, viscous pus; adhesions forming; loculations established

Clinical significance: The stage of empyema determines management approach

### 6.4 Sepsis After Cardiac and Thoracic Surgery

### Incidence and Risk Factors

Surgical site infections occur in 1–3% of cardiac and thoracic surgical procedures, though rates vary based on:

Patient factors:

Age >70 years

Diabetes mellitus

Obesity

Immunosuppression

COPD

Poor nutritional status

Procedural factors:

Prolonged operative time (>2 hours)

Emergent procedure

Redo surgery (adhesions)

Post-operative factors:

Mechanical ventilation (VAP risk)

Indwelling catheters

### Types of Post-Cardiac/Thoracic Surgical Infections

### Sternotomy Wound Infection

Deep sternal wound infection (DSWI):

Incidence: 0.5–2% of cardiac surgery patients

Definition: Infection involving sternum and/or mediastinum

Presentation:

Fever and sternal wound drainage (purulent, serous, or serosanguineous)

Sternal instability

Mediastinitis signs (chest pain, sepsis)

Organisms: Staph aureus (including MRSA), Streptococcus, gram-negatives

Management:

Imaging: CT chest with IV contrast to assess extent

Operative management: Sternotomy revision, drainage, debridement

Flap coverage: Often requires muscle flap (pectoralis major, rectus abdominis) to cover sternal defect

Antibiotics: Broad-spectrum initially; de-escalate based on cultures

Mortality: 10–20% even with optimal management

### Empyema After Thoracic Surgery

Incidence: 1–2% of lobectomy or pneumonectomy

Mechanism:

Staple line leak

Air leak with secondary infection

Pleural space infection

Presentation:

Fever developing days to weeks post-operatively

Respiratory symptoms (dyspnea, cough)

Sepsis signs

Diagnosis:

Chest X-ray or CT: Pleural fluid/collection

Thoracentesis or chest tube drainage: Fluid analysis (glucose, culture)

Bronchoscopy: If airway leak suspected

Management:

Antibiotics: Broad-spectrum

Drainage: Chest tube for simple empyema; VATS for complex/loculate

Continue

30 дек. 2025 г.

empyema

Definitive source control: VATS pleurodesis or pleurectomy if persistent; re-operation if airway leak

### Prosthetic Graft Infection

Incidence: 1–5% depending on graft type and location

Presentation:

Fever with or without wound drainage

Graft-related symptoms (pseudoaneurysm rupture risk)

Sepsis

Organisms: Often more virulent (Staph aureus, Pseudomonas, gram-negatives)

Management:

Graft removal and replacement: Typically necessary; infected grafts have high failure rate even with antibiotics

In-situ replacement: Autogenous tissue preferred (saphenous vein) if available

Revascularization: If graft supplies vital organ (brain, limbs)

Antibiotics: Long-term (4–6 weeks) after graft removal/replacement

### Prevention of Post-Operative Infection

Pre-operative:

Optimization: Control diabetes, treat active infections

Skin antisepsis: Chlorhexidine or povidone-iodine

Oral antisepsis: Chlorhexidine mouth rinse for cardiac surgery

Intra-operative:

Antibiotic prophylaxis: Cephalosporin (cefazolin) within 60 minutes of incision

Redosing: If operative time >2 hours, redose prophylactic antibiotic

Sterile technique: Standard precautions

Avoid hypothermia: Maintain core temperature >36°C

Optimize oxygenation: Adequate FiO₂ to maintain SaO₂ >94%

Post-operative:

Early removal of catheters: Remove central lines, foley catheters as soon as clinically permissible

VAP prevention: Head-of-bed elevation, subglottic secretion drainage, oral care

Wound care: Maintain sterile technique; change dressings per protocol

Glucose control: Target BG 140–180 mg/dL (not <100, which increases infection risk)

### 6.5 Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation (ECMO) as Bridge Therapy

### Role of ECMO in Septic Shock with Respiratory/Cardiac Failure

ECMO (extracorporeal membrane oxygenation) is extracorporeal life support that bypasses the lungs and/or heart to provide gas exchange and/or hemodynamic support.

### Indications for ECMO in Septic Shock

ECMO is NOT a treatment for sepsis itself, but rather a bridge therapy that:

Provides time for source control to take effect

Reduces the need for high-pressure ventilation that may worsen ARDS

Provides hemodynamic support, reducing vasopressor requirement

Permits "lung rest" and recovery of lung tissue

Specific indications:

Refractory ARDS with sepsis:

Criteria for ECMO consideration:

PaO₂/FiO₂ <50–100 despite optimal ventilation

Unable to oxygenate despite high FiO₂ and PEEP

Multiple sequential organ failures

Source control already achieved or being pursued

Patient is "salvageable" (not moribund; likely to survive if organs can be supported)

Refractory cardiogenic shock from septic cardiomyopathy:

Profound myocardial depression

Ejection fraction <20%

Hemodynamic instability despite inotropes and vasopressors

Need for mechanical support

Failures of conventional support:

Despite maximum vasopressor doses (norepinephrine >15 μg/kg/min, additional agents)

Lactate not clearing despite resuscitation

Progressive multi-organ failure despite all supportive measures

### Contraindications to ECMO

Absolute contraindications:

Futility: Patient unlikely to survive even with maximal support (e.g., irreversible multi-organ failure, uncontrolled source)

Coagulopathy preventing cannulation: Severe thrombocytopenia or coagulopathy making cannulation dangerous

Aortic insufficiency (for VA-ECMO): VA-ECMO increases aortic root pressure; AI worsens outcome

Relative contraindications:

Age >75 years: Increased operative risk

Prolonged shock: >48–72 hours of refractory shock before ECMO suggests irreversible organ damage

Uncontrolled infection: ECMO is NOT treatment for infection; infection source must be controlled first

Severe comorbidity: Unlikely to return to functional status even if acute illness resolved

### ECMO Circuit Types

Veno-arterial (VA) ECMO:

Cannulation: Veins (central or femoral) and arteries (femoral, axillary, or aortic)

Function: Provides both oxygenation (through membrane oxygenator) and hemodynamic support (through pump creating forward flow)

Indications: Refractory cardiogenic shock; combined respiratory and cardiac failure

Disadvantages: Requires arterial cannulation; risk of limb ischemia if femoral artery used

Veno-venous (VV) ECMO:

Cannulation: Veins only (typically femoral vein for drainage, internal jugular or femoral for return)

Function: Provides oxygenation only; heart must maintain adequate cardiac output

Indications: Pure respiratory failure (ARDS) without cardiogenic shock

Advantages: Avoids arterial cannulation; lower risk of limb ischemia

### Management During ECMO

Anticoagulation:

ECMO circuit is highly thrombogenic

Continuous heparin infusion required to prevent thrombosis of circuit

INR target: 1.5–2.5; aPTT >45 seconds

Monitoring: Serial aPTT, platelet counts, D-dimer

Monitoring:

Serial lactate to assess tissue perfusion

Continuous blood gas monitoring

Regular imaging (chest X-ray, echocardiography)

Assessment of source control status

Source control: During ECMO, active management of infection source must continue

Operative intervention for abdominal/chest source

Antimicrobial therapy optimization

Regular reassessment of adequacy of source control

Weaning:

Once source is controlled and organ function improves

Gradual reduction of ECMO flow

Trial off ECMO (placing on minimal flow) to assess if patient can sustain oxygenation/perfusion

If successful, decannulation

### Outcomes of ECMO in Septic Shock

Survival: 40–60% in appropriate patient populations

Better outcomes in younger patients with reversible organ dysfunction

Worse outcomes in elderly or those with prolonged shock before ECMO initiation

Complications:

Cannula-related: Arterial injury, limb ischemia, venous stenosis

Circuit: Thrombosis, hemolysis

Bleeding: From anticoagulation

Infection: Cannula site infection, sepsis progression

Organ dysfunction: Despite support, multi-organ failure may progress

Current role: ECMO is increasingly employed in septic shock when conventional support fails and reversible source control has been achieved. Decision for ECMO should be made early (day 1–2 of refractory shock) rather than waiting until multi-organ failure is irreversible.

### Summary: Thoracic Sepsis Management

Thoracic and esophageal sepsis requires:

High clinical suspicion: Early recognition based on clinical presentation

Rapid diagnosis: Imaging (CT, esophagography) to confirm

Emergent intervention: Within 30 minutes to 1 hour

Broad-spectrum antibiotics: Immediately upon diagnosis

Source control:

Esophageal perforation: Operative repair or catheter drainage (selected cases)

Mediastinitis: Operative debridement and drainage

Empyema: Chest tube drainage ± VATS

Organ support: Mechanical ventilation, ECMO if needed

De-escalation: Based on culture results

Outcomes: Mortality remains substantial (15–40%) even with optimal management, reflecting the severity of these infections and the delayed presentation common in practice. Early recognition and intervention are the most critical factors improving outcomes.
