### CHAPTER 7: VASCULAR SURGERY AND SEPSIS ASSOCIATED WITH PROSTHETIC GRAFTS

### 7.1 Prosthetic Graft Infection: Biofilm Formation and Host-Immune Failure

### Epidemiology and Risk Factors

Incidence of prosthetic graft infection:

Early infection (<1 month post-implantation): 0.5–2%

Late infection (>1 month): 0.1–0.5% per year

Total lifetime infection risk: 5–10% for patients with grafts

Incidence varies by graft location:

Aortic grafts: 1–2%

Lower extremity grafts: 2–5%

Upper extremity grafts: 1–2%

Dialysis access grafts: 10–30% (highest incidence)

### Risk Factors for Graft Infection

Patient factors:

Immunosuppression: HIV/AIDS, transplant recipients, chemotherapy

Diabetes mellitus: Associated with increased infection risk

Obesity: Poor wound healing

Age >70 years: Reduced immune function

Malnutrition: Impaired healing

Renal failure: Uremia impairs immune function

Previous graft infections: Significantly increased risk of recurrence

Operative/Procedural factors:

Contamination during implantation: Inadequate sterile technique

Prosthetic material: Some materials (polyester, PTFE) have higher infection rates than others

Extended operative time: >2 hours increases risk

Emergency procedures: Lower standard of care in emergent situations

Complexity of procedure: More complex procedures carry higher risk

Graft factors:

Synthetic material: PTFE and polyester more prone to infection than autologous tissue

Prosthetic valves (in cardiac grafts): Higher infection risk

Size and location: Certain locations (aortic root) carry higher risk

Graft age: Older grafts may have material fatigue, increasing infection risk

Pseudoaneurysm formation: Indicates graft integrity compromise; risk factor for infection

Post-operative factors:

Line infections: Infected central lines, peripheral IVs can seed graft via bloodstream

Nosocomial infections: Healthcare-associated infections (VAP, UTI, SSI) can seed graft

Dental procedures: Transient bacteremia from dental work can seed graft

Inflammatory procedures: Endoscopy, urologic procedures can cause transient bacteremia

### Biofilm Formation on Prosthetic Grafts

### The Biofilm Phenotype

Biofilms are organized communities of microorganisms encased in an extracellular matrix (polysaccharide, protein, DNA). On prosthetic grafts, biofilms have specific characteristics that render them highly resistant to antimicrobial therapy.

### Biofilm Formation on Prosthetic Material: The Pathogenesis

Stage 1: Initial adherence (minutes to hours):

Bacteria encounter prosthetic material surface

Initial reversible adhesion via van der Waals forces, hydrophobic interactions

Specific adhesins on bacterial surface bind to material components

Stage 2: Irreversible adhesion and microcolony formation (hours to days):

Bacteria secrete adhesins and exopolysaccharides

Formation of glycocalyx (polysaccharide matrix)

Upregulation of biofilm-associated genes (quorum sensing)

Microcolonies form—organized structures of hundreds to thousands of cells

Stage 3: Biofilm maturation (days to weeks):

Biofilm becomes increasingly complex

Development of microenvironments:

Aerobic zones (peripheral)

Anaerobic zones (interior)

Nutrient-rich and nutrient-poor zones

Increased thickness (10–1000 micrometers)

Increased antibiotic resistance

Stage 4: Dispersal (weeks to months):

Some cells escape biofilm ("planktonic" bacteria)

Seed new sites

Perpetuate systemic infection

### Biofilm Resistance Mechanisms

Physical barrier:

The glycocalyx provides a physical barrier to antibiotic penetration

Antibiotic concentrations at biofilm interior may be 10–1000-fold lower than in blood

Many antibiotics cannot penetrate biofilm effectively

Reduced metabolic activity:

Biofilm bacteria are often non-growing or slow-growing

Cell cycle-dependent antibiotics (β-lactams) are ineffective against non-replicating cells

Metabolic scavenging reduces oxygen availability; some bacteria enter stationary phase (low metabolic activity)

Genetic resistance:

Biofilm bacteria upregulate antibiotic resistance genes

Horizontal gene transfer within biofilm spreads resistance mechanisms

Efflux pumps (expelling antibiotics from cells) are upregulated

Phenotypic resistance:

Even without genetic mutations, biofilm bacteria exhibit altered phenotype

Reduced cell wall permeability

Altered enzyme expression

Tolerance mechanisms independent of classical resistance

Immune evasion:

Biofilm minimizes exposure to immune cells

Polysaccharide matrix may disguise bacterial antigens

Biofilm bacteria produce immunosuppressive molecules

Host immune response is locally impaired within biofilm

### Organisms in Graft Biofilms

Most common organisms:

Staphylococcus epidermidis (coagulase-negative staph): 30–40% of graft infections

Staphylococcus aureus (including MRSA): 20–30%

Enterococcus species: 10–15%

Streptococcus species: 5–10%

Gram-negative organisms (E. coli, Pseudomonas, Serratia): 10–20%

Candida species: 5–10% (particularly in immunocompromised patients)

Polymicrobial infections: 20–30% of graft infections involve multiple organisms, all within single biofilm

Organism selection:

Early infections (<1 month): Often from skin flora or operating room contamination (Staph epidermidis, Staph aureus)

Late infections (>1 month): Often from hematogenous seeding from distant source (various organisms)

Dialysis graft infections: Often from skin flora (Staph aureus) or from bloodstream during dialysis

### Host-Immune Failure Against Biofilms

### Why Immunity Fails Against Biofilms

Reduced antigen presentation:

Biofilm minimizes bacterial surface antigen exposure

Glycocalyx may shield antigens from immune recognition

T-cell and B-cell responses to biofilm bacteria are inadequate

Local immune suppression:

Biofilm bacteria produce immunosuppressive molecules (IL-10, TGF-β homologs)

Pro-inflammatory cytokines produced in response to biofilm (TNF-α, IL-1β) promote local inflammation but may paradoxically suppress adaptive immunity

Tissue surrounding graft becomes scarred and hypovascular, limiting immune cell infiltration

Complement resistance:

Biofilm bacteria upregulate complement resistance mechanisms

Production of molecules that inhibit complement cascade

Glycocalyx may bind and neutralize complement components

Neutrophil dysfunction:

Neutrophils attempt to phagocytose biofilm bacteria but are often unsuccessful

Large biofilm structures cannot be engulfed

Neutrophils accumulate at biofilm-tissue interface but cannot effectively kill bacteria

Neutrophil death and degranulation may paradoxically damage surrounding tissue

Immune tolerance:

Chronic biofilm presence induces regulatory T cells and anti-inflammatory macrophage phenotype

Chronic inflammation shifts to immune tolerance

### The Foreign Body Response

Foreign body reaction:

Implanted prosthetic material triggers chronic inflammatory response

Macrophages and fibroblasts attempt to encapsulate graft

Fibrosis and scar tissue formation

This chronic inflammation may impair immunity against biofilm bacteria

Fibrosis consequences:

Reduced vascularization of surrounding tissue

Limited immune cell infiltration

Bacteria within biofilm are insulated from immune attack

Even if immune system recognizes bacteria, immune cells cannot reach them effectively

### 7.2 Management of Infected Aortic Aneurysms

### Infected Aortic Aneurysms: Definition and Epidemiology

Infected aortic aneurysm (also called mycotic aneurysm, though the name is misleading—most are bacterial, not fungal) is an aortic aneurysm secondary to infection of the aortic wall.

### Distinction Between Primary and Secondary Infections

Secondary infection of pre-existing aneurysm (most common):

Patient has known aneurysm

Hematogenous seeding occurs (from bacteremia, endocarditis, distant infection)

Bacteria lodge in aneurysm sac or aortic wall

Rapid progression of aneurysm size and inflammation

Primary infected aneurysm (rarer):

Aortic wall is primarily infected by bacteria

Bacterial infection of normal aortic wall leads to formation of aneurysm

Often from aortitis (inflammation of aorta)

### Incidence and Risk Factors

Incidence: 0.7–1.5% of abdominal aortic aneurysms (AAA) are infected

Risk factors:

Bacteremia source: Endocarditis (most common), dental infection, UTI, other source

Atherosclerotic disease: Damaged aortic wall is more susceptible

Immunosuppression: HIV, transplant, chemotherapy

Chronic renal failure: Uremia impairs immunity

Diabetes: Impaired immune function

Advanced age: Reduced immune function

### Organisms in Infected AAA

Most common:

Salmonella species: 30–50% (unusual organism for most infections; predilection for atherosclerotic aorta)

Staphylococcus aureus: 20–30%

Streptococcus species: 10–15%

Gram-negative organisms (E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter): 10–20%

Treponema pallidum (syphilis): Now rare due to treatment, but historically important cause

Mycobacterium tuberculosis: Rare in developed countries; common in endemic areas

Fungal organisms: Rare; Candida, Aspergillus in severely immunocompromised

### Clinical Presentation

Early signs (days to weeks):

Fever

Abdominal or back pain (different from typical AAA pain—more acute and severe)

Systemic inflammatory signs (elevated WBC, elevated CRP)

Positive blood cultures

Late signs (if untreated):

Rapidly enlarging aneurysm

Rupture (catastrophic)

Septic shock

Multi-organ failure

Clinical context:

Septic patient with new-onset aortic pathology

Patient with known AAA and new fever/pain

Endocarditis patient with aortic findings

### Diagnosis of Infected AAA

### Imaging Studies

CT angiography (CT with IV contrast):

Findings suggestive of infection:

Rapid change in aneurysm size (compared to prior imaging)

Eccentric thickening of aortic wall (rather than concentric atherosclerotic change)

Focal areas of enhancement (suggesting inflammation)

Periaortic stranding (inflammation extending beyond aorta)

Gas within aneurysm sac (from gas-forming organisms like E. coli)

Leak or rupture

Vertebral involvement (spondylodiscitis)

FDG-PET/CT:

Increased glucose uptake in infected aorta

Can help confirm infection diagnosis

Useful for assessing response to treatment

### Microbiological Diagnosis

Blood cultures:

Obtain before any intervention

Positive in 50–80% of infected AAA cases

Guide antimicrobial therapy

Aortic wall or aneurysm sac cultures:

Obtained during surgical intervention

Send for aerobic, anaerobic, mycobacterial, and fungal cultures

PCR/Molecular testing:

Rapid identification of organisms

Can be performed on aortic tissue from operative specimens

### Laboratory Studies

Inflammatory markers:

Elevated WBC: 70–80% of infected AAA

Elevated CRP: >95% of infected AAA

Elevated ESR: >90% of infected AAA

Serial measurement: CRP/ESR trend guides treatment response

### Surgical Management of Infected AAA

### Timing and Urgency

Emergency indications:

Rupture or contained rupture: Acute presentation with severe pain, hemodynamic instability

Impending rupture: Large/rapidly enlarging aneurysm, imaging showing leak

Septic shock: Refractory to resuscitation, indicating need for source control

Procedure: Emergent operative intervention

Urgent indications (within 24–48 hours):

### Confirmed infected AAA without rupture

Hemodynamically stable but imaging confirms infection

Fever with positive blood cultures and aneurysm findings

### Operative Approach Options

Option 1: Extra-anatomic reconstruction (historically preferred):

Technique:

Remove infected aorta

Delay/defer aortic reconstruction

Place extra-anatomic graft (axillo-femoral or femorofemoral bypass) for distal perfusion

Extra-anatomic grafts placed through clean, uninfected tissue planes (subcutaneous in axilla, across abdomen)

Advantages:

Avoids infected field entirely

Lower risk of graft infection than in-situ replacement

Can be staged (initial removal, later reconstruction)

Disadvantages:

Extra-anatomic grafts have lower patency rates

Risk of graft limb ischemia

Multiple incisions

Option 2: In-situ reconstruction (increasingly used):

Technique:

Resect infected aorta and aneurysm

Immediate reconstruction with autogenous or allograft material

Aggressive debridement of infected tissue

Routine use of systemic antibiotics

Material choices:

Autogenous vein: Saphenous vein (if available, patient has bilateral lower extremities)

Lowest infection rate (~5%)

Limited length

Aortic homograft: Cryopreserved aorta from cadaver

Infection rate ~10–15%

Better hemodynamics than synthetic

Limited availability

Synthetic graft: PTFE or polyester with antibiotic-bonded coating

Infection rate ~15–30%

Readily available

Higher infection rate than autogenous

Advantages:

Single operative intervention

Better long-term patency than extra-anatomic

Patient not left without aortic reconstruction

Modern antibiotic therapy improves outcomes

Disadvantages:

Higher infection risk than extra-anatomic approach

Requires aggressive intraoperative debridement and careful surgical technique

Current trend: Many centers now favor in-situ reconstruction with autogenous or allograft material, combined with extended antimicrobial therapy, rather than extra-anatomic approach.

### Operative Technique Details

Exposure:

Transperitoneal approach most common (permits adequate exposure and debridement)

Midline incision extending from xiphoid to pubis

Full inspection of peritoneal cavity for additional contamination

Resection:

Suprarenal clamps placed above aneurysm

Infra-renal clamps below

Resect infected aneurysm and surrounding infected tissue

May require resection of infected thrombus within aneurysm sac

Send tissue for culture (aorta, thrombus, surrounding tissue)

Debridement:

Aggressive removal of infected, necrotic, and devitalized tissue

Irrigate debridement bed copiously with saline

Some surgeons advocate for antiseptic irrigation (iodine-based) or antibiotic irrigation

Revascularization:

Place proximal anastomosis to aorta or iliac vessels above infected field

Place distal anastomosis to distal aorta or iliac vessels

Avoid placing graft through infected field if possible

Wrapped aortic graft in clean tissue (peritoneum, omentum) to isolate from any residual infection

### Post-Operative Management

Antibiotics:

Initiation: Within 1 hour of diagnosis

Initial regimen: Broad-spectrum pending culture results

Vancomycin (if Staph aureus suspected)

Third-generation cephalosporin or fluoroquinolone (gram-negative)

If Salmonella suspected: Fluoroquinolone or third-generation cephalosporin (Salmonella often resistant to standard coverage)

De-escalation: Based on culture results and susceptibilities

Duration: Extended therapy essential

Minimum 6 weeks of IV antibiotics

Total therapy (IV + oral): Often 6–12 months

Continued surveillance with imaging and inflammatory markers

Monitoring:

Serial inflammatory markers (CRP, ESR): Should trend downward

Regular imaging (CT or ultrasound): Assess for graft patency, infection recurrence

Physical examination: Assess graft site for signs of infection (drainage, erythema)

Complications:

Graft infection: Despite aggressive approach, recurrent infection risk 5–20%

Aortic rupture: If diagnosed late, rupture can occur

Graft thrombosis: Autogenous vein grafts prone to thrombosis

Limb ischemia: If iliac involvement or poor distal runoff

Renal failure: From aortic clamp or contrast from imaging

Paraplegia: From spinal cord ischemia (if thoracic aorta involved)

### Outcomes in Infected AAA

Mortality:

Ruptured infected AAA: 30–50% mortality even with aggressive treatment

Non-ruptured, diagnosed pre-rupture: 10–20% mortality

With extended antibiotic therapy: Improved outcomes compared to historical series

Long-term graft patency:

Autogenous vein: 70–80% patency at 5 years

Allograft: 60–70% patency at 5 years

Synthetic: 50–60% patency at 5 years

### 7.3 Timing of Graft Removal versus In-Situ Replacement

### The Central Dilemma: Remove or Replace

The decision between removal with delayed reconstruction versus immediate in-situ replacement represents one of the key strategic choices in infected graft management.

### Arguments for Removal Without Immediate Replacement

Theoretical advantages:

Source control: Removes infected material completely

Eliminates biofilm: No foreign material left behind to harbor bacteria

Lower reinfection risk: Without prosthetic material in field, risk of recurrent biofilm formation is eliminated

Tissue healing: Allows tissues to heal and inflammation to resolve before new graft placed

Infection clearance: Time permits infection to be definitively cleared before new graft introduced

Historical approach: Extra-anatomic bypass without aortic reconstruction

First operation: Remove infected aorta

Placement of extra-anatomic graft (axillo-femoral, femorofemoral) via separate incisions through clean tissue

Second operation (months later): Reconstruction with clean tissue planes established

Current status: Less commonly used, but still considered in certain scenarios

### Arguments for In-Situ Replacement

Practical advantages:

Single operative intervention: Avoids multiple operations

Hemodynamic benefits: In-situ graft has better hemodynamics than extra-anatomic

Lower limb ischemia risk: In-situ maintains native aortic circulation

Patient acceptance: Single procedure is less burdensome

Modern results: Aggressive debridement + extended antibiotics achieves good outcomes

Evidence base:

Recent series show in-situ replacement with aggressive debridement and extended antibiotics achieves:

Infection control: 70–90% patients remain infection-free long-term

Graft patency: 70–80% at 5 years (with autogenous/allograft material)

Mortality: 10–20% (acceptable for infected aorta)

Current trend: In-situ replacement becoming standard approach

### Decision Algorithm

Choose extra-anatomic approach (removal without immediate in-situ replacement) if:

Fulminant infection with septic shock: Patient too unstable for complex operation

Massive contamination: Gross contamination of field makes in-situ risky

Prior graft infection: Patient with history of graft infection has increased recurrence risk

Fungal infection: Fungal infections have higher recurrence rates; consider source removal without immediate replacement

Patient unable to tolerate extended antibiotics: If compliance with long-term antibiotics is questioned

Choose in-situ replacement if:

Patient hemodynamically stable (or stabilized with resuscitation)

Well-defined infection without massive contamination

First graft infection (lower risk of recurrence)

Patient reliable for extended antibiotic course and follow-up

Autogenous or allograft material available (preferred over synthetic)

Hybrid approach: Some cases warrant:

Initial removal/debridement of infected aorta

Placement of temporary vascular shunt (bypass tube) to maintain distal perfusion

Extra-anatomic graft placed

Reassessment at 2–4 weeks

If infection resolved: Replace with definitive in-situ graft

If infection persists: Continue extra-anatomic approach

### 7.4 Antimicrobial Lock Therapy and Catheter-Related Sepsis

### Catheter-Associated Bloodstream Infection (CABSI)

While the focus of this chapter is prosthetic vascular grafts, catheter-related sepsis deserves attention as it is increasingly encountered in vascular surgery patients (particularly those with dialysis access grafts or complex vascular grafts requiring long-term central access).

### Pathogenesis of CABSI

Catheter biofilm formation:

Similar to prosthetic graft biofilm

Bacteria adhere to catheter material

Glycocalyx formation

Resistance to both antibiotics and immune system

Source of bacteria:

Skin flora: Migration from insertion site along catheter

Hematogenous seeding: Bacteria in blood lodge on catheter

Infusate contamination: Rare; usually from water used in preparation

Hub contamination: Manipulation of catheter hub introduces bacteria

### Clinical Presentation

Local signs (at insertion site):

Erythema

Warmth

Drainage (purulent)

Tenderness

Systemic signs:

Fever

Sepsis

Positive blood cultures (often during line use)

Resolution of signs after line removal

Diagnosis:

Clinical suspicion with fever and positive blood cultures

Quantitative blood cultures: Simultaneous draws from line and peripheral IV

CABSI likely if line culture has ≥5-fold higher CFU than peripheral

Ultrasound: May show thrombus/biofilm on catheter

### Antimicrobial Lock Therapy

Concept: Instead of removing catheter in CABSI, install high-concentration antibiotic solution within the catheter lumen and leave for 12–24 hours ("lock" the lumen). Antibiotic concentration is orders of magnitude higher than systemically achievable, directly targeting biofilm within catheter.

### Mechanism and Rationale

High local concentration:

Antibiotic concentration in lock solution: 1–20 mg/mL (systemically, max ~50 μg/mL achievable)

Orders of magnitude higher than biofilm-inhibitory concentrations

Biofilm bacteria exposed to lethal concentration

Direct biofilm targeting:

Lock solution in direct contact with catheter biofilm

Bypass systemic clearance and biofilm resistance mechanisms

Effective against organisms resistant to systemic therapy

Catheter preservation:

Avoids loss of vascular access (particularly important in hemodialysis patients, who may have limited access options)

Eliminates need for replacement catheter placement

### Antimicrobial Agents Used

Antibiotics commonly used in locks:

Vancomycin: 2–5 mg/mL (for gram-positive organisms)

Gentamicin: 1–5 mg/mL (gram-negative, synergistic with vancomycin)

Ciprofloxacin: 1–2 mg/mL (gram-negative, fluoroquinolone)

Cefazolin: 10–20 mg/mL (gram-positive, gram-negative coverage)

Tigecycline: 1–2 mg/mL (broad-spectrum)

Combination locks: Often combined:

Vancomycin + gentamicin (broad-spectrum, gram-positive + gram-negative)

Vancomycin + ciprofloxacin (gram-positive + gram-negative)

Adjunctive agents:

Heparin: Often added to lock solution to prevent catheter thrombosis

Ethanol: 70% ethanol added to lock solution; has antimicrobial properties, improves biofilm penetration

EDTA: Chelating agent that disrupts biofilm matrix

### Technique of Antimicrobial Lock

Procedure:

Obtain peripheral blood cultures if not already done (to confirm CABSI)

Prepare lock solution: Vancomycin 2–5 mg/mL + gentamicin 1–2 mg/mL + heparin 100 units/mL in normal saline

Draw blood from catheter (to assess residual antibiotic)

Instill lock solution into catheter lumen: Volume = catheter internal volume (typically 0.5–3 mL depending on catheter type)

Clamp catheter: Prevent back-flow

Dwell time: 12–24 hours (catheter not used during this time)

Flush and reassess: Withdraw lock solution (send for culture if desired), reassess catheter function

Repeat if necessary: Typically repeat locks once daily for 3–5 days

System antibiotic therapy: While lock therapy is ongoing, also provide systemic antibiotics (IV or oral, depending on organism and severity)

### Outcomes of Antimicrobial Lock Therapy

Success rate: 50–80% in various studies

Varies by:

Organism (better outcomes with coagulase-negative Staph, worse with fungi)

Duration of lock therapy

Concurrent systemic antibiotics

Catheter type

Failure: If lock therapy unsuccessful after 3–5 days:

Remove catheter

Consider systemic treatment alone vs replacement catheter

Recurrence: 10–20% of patients with successful initial treatment experience recurrent CABSI

### Vascular Graft-Specific Catheter Issues

Hemodialysis access grafts:

Grafts serve as vascular access for hemodialysis

Repeated punctures for dialysis needle placement

Each puncture is a potential source of infection

Catheter infections associated with graft infections

Prevention:

Meticulous sterile technique during dialysis

Regular assessment for signs of infection

Prompt treatment of fever in dialysis patients

Rotation of puncture sites

Avoid prolonged indwelling lines (use fistulas when possible)

### 7.5 Complications and Special Scenarios

### Mycotic Aneurysm Beyond the Aorta

### Extra-Aortic Mycotic Aneurysms

Visceral arteries:

Splenic artery: Most common extra-aortic site

Hepatic artery: Less common

Superior mesenteric artery (SMA): Rare

Renal artery: Rare

Presentation:

Sepsis with abdominal pain

Imaging (CT, MRA) showing aneurysm

Rupture presenting as hemorrhagic shock

Management:

Diagnosis by imaging: CT angiography or MRA

Operative management: Open or endovascular aneurysm repair

Open approach: Arteriotomy, aneurysm repair or resection

Endovascular: Stent graft placement across aneurysm

Antibiotics: Same as aortic infection (extended therapy)

### Peripheral Artery Aneurysms

Femoral artery:

Most common infected peripheral aneurysm site

Often from groin contamination, injection drug use (in IV drug users), or seeding from distant infection

Can present as groin mass, pain, or rupture

Popliteal artery:

Less common

Presents with leg swelling, pain, or limb ischemia

Management:

Diagnosis: Doppler ultrasound or CT

Operative approach:

Exclusion of aneurysm (ligation)

Revascularization if limb-threatening

Autogenous graft preferred (vein)

Antibiotics: Extended therapy as for aortic infection

### Endocarditis with Septic Emboli to Grafts

Setting: Patient with endocarditis (valve infection) develops septic emboli that lodge in arterial grafts or native vessels.

Pathophysiology:

Bacteria from endocarditis emboli to peripheral arteries

Seed prosthetic grafts

Cause mycotic aneurysms or graft infection

Management:

Treat underlying endocarditis: Prolonged IV antibiotics (4–6 weeks)

Address mycotic aneurysm: Operative repair if at risk of rupture

Consider valve surgery: If endocarditis causing hemodynamic compromise or recurrent emboli

Extended antibiotics: Even if graft salvaged, extended coverage essential

### Septic Thrombophlebitis with Graft Involvement

Definition: Thrombophlebitis (inflammation of vein with thrombosis) that becomes infected

Setting:

IV drug use with infected injection sites

Indwelling catheters with septic thrombophlebitis

Migration to prosthetic grafts in vicinity

Management:

Antibiotics: Broad-spectrum

Graft assessment: Determine if graft infected or merely adjacent

Surgical intervention: If graft involved, removal may be necessary

Anticoagulation: Controversial; typically avoided in infected setting due to bleeding risk

### 7.6 Outcomes and Prevention Strategies

### Prevention of Prosthetic Graft Infection

Pre-operative optimization:

Identify and treat active infection: Pre-operative cultures, dental work, urinary treatment

Optimize nutrition: Ensure adequate protein, micronutrients

Glucose control: Target perioperative BG 140–180 mg/dL

Smoking cessation: At least 4 weeks prior

Skin preparation: Chlorhexidine-based antisepsis

Hair removal: Clipping (not shaving) if necessary

Operative measures:

Prophylactic antibiotics:

Timing: Within 60 minutes of incision

Agent: Cefazolin 2 g IV (or vancomycin if β-lactam allergy)

Redosing: If operative time >2 hours, redose antibiotic

Sterile technique: Standard precautions, sterile field maintenance

Hypothermia prevention: Maintain core temperature >36°C

Adequate oxygenation: Target SpO₂ >94%, FiO₂ sufficient

Graft selection: Prefer autogenous when possible; if synthetic, consider antibiotic-bonded grafts

Graft preparation: Some surgeons soak grafts in antibiotic solution prior to implantation

Post-operative surveillance:

Wound care: Sterile dressing changes, monitor for signs of infection

Line management: Remove unnecessary lines promptly

Infection prevention: Standard precautions, VTE prophylaxis, VAP prevention

Monitoring for infection: Regular assessment for fever, drainage, erythema

Imaging surveillance: Baseline ultrasound/CT post-operatively; serial imaging if clinical concern

### Long-Term Outcomes

Graft survival:

**Non

Continue

30 дек. 2025 г.

-infected grafts**: 70–80% patency at 5 years (varies by location and material)

Previously infected grafts: 60–70% patency at 5 years (slightly lower due to graft material/reconstruction type chosen)

Infection-free survival:

With appropriate prevention: 95–99% of patients never develop graft infection

For patients with infection history: 85–95% infection-free at 5 years (if treated successfully)

Quality of life:

Non-infected grafts: Return to normal function; minimal long-term disability

Infected grafts: Longer recovery; multiple operative interventions; higher morbidity

### Summary: Vascular Surgery and Sepsis

Prosthetic vascular grafts represent a constant challenge in sepsis prevention and management:

Prevention is paramount: Meticulous operative technique, prophylactic antibiotics, and post-operative surveillance prevent majority of infections

Biofilm is the enemy: Biofilm-mediated resistance requires aggressive surgical debridement and extended antibiotic therapy—conventional approaches often fail

Early recognition is critical: Early diagnosis of graft infection permits salvage; late diagnosis often requires graft removal

In-situ replacement is feasible: Modern approach combining aggressive debridement, in-situ reconstruction with autogenous/allograft material, and extended antibiotics achieves acceptable outcomes

Infected AAA requires emergency intervention: Ruptured infected aorta carries extremely high mortality; pre-rupture recognition and intervention are essential

Extended follow-up is necessary: Graft infections may recur; long-term surveillance with imaging and clinical assessment is essential

### CHAPTER 8: SOFT TISSUE INFECTIONS—FROM CELLULITIS TO NECROTIZING FASCIITIS

### 8.1 Classification and Microbiological Profiles

### Clinical Classification of Soft Tissue Infections

Soft tissue infections encompass a spectrum of conditions ranging from superficial skin infections to deep necrotizing infections. This spectrum reflects both the depth of tissue involvement and the virulence/aggressiveness of the causative organisms.

### Classification by Anatomic Depth

Impetigo/Superficial pyoderma:

Depth: Epidermis only

Appearance: Honey-crusted lesions, bullae

Organisms: Staph aureus (most common), Group A Streptococcus

Management: Topical antibiotics usually sufficient; oral antibiotics if extensive

Sepsis risk: Minimal

Erysipelas:

Depth: Dermis and superficial lymphatics

Appearance: Well-demarcated erythematous plaque with raised borders

Organisms: Group A Streptococcus (nearly exclusively)

Characteristics: Rapidly spreading, characteristic serpiginous border

Sepsis risk: Low with prompt antibiotics

Cellulitis:

Depth: Dermis and subcutaneous tissue

Appearance: Non-purulent (though purulence may occur), poorly demarcated, edematous, erythematous

Organisms: Group A Streptococcus, Staph aureus (including MRSA increasingly)

Characteristics: Non-suppurative infection; no abscess formation

Sepsis risk: Moderate; can progress to systemic infection

Abscess:

Depth: Subcutaneous or deeper

Appearance: Fluctuant collection, localized swelling, often with central drainage area ("pointing")

Organisms: Variable; Staph aureus most common, but depends on source

Management: Incision and drainage (I&D) + antibiotics

Sepsis risk: Moderate; source control via drainage essential

Necrotizing fasciitis (NF):

Depth: Fascia and deep soft tissues; can extend to muscle (myonecrosis)

Appearance: Skin may appear initially innocuous (deceptively mild), but rapidly progressive

Organisms: Polymicrobial (Type I), Streptococcus pyogenes + Staph aureus (Type II), or Vibrio/Aeromonas (Type III)

Characteristics: Rapidly progressive; systemic toxicity severe; mortality high without emergent intervention

Sepsis risk: EXTREME—necrotizing fasciitis is sepsis until proven otherwise

Myonecrosis (gas gangrene):

Depth: Muscle involvement; may originate from deep tissues

Appearance: Crepitus (gas in tissues), muscle discoloration visible through skin

Organisms: Clostridium perfringens (most common), other gas-forming organisms

Characteristics: Fulminant infection; toxic shock; extremely high mortality

Sepsis risk: EXTREME—requires emergent amputation or extensive debridement

### Microbiological Classification of Soft Tissue Infections

### Type I Necrotizing Fasciitis: Polymicrobial Infection

Organisms:

Anaerobes (60–70%): Bacteroides fragilis, Prevotella, Peptostreptococcus, Clostridium (non-perfringens species)

Facultative anaerobes: E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterococcus

Aerobes: Variable; Staph aureus less common than in Type II

Average number of organisms: 4–5 per infection (true polymicrobial)

Source:

Often from gastrointestinal tract

Associated with:

Abdominal trauma or surgery with breach of GI tract

Perirectal infection (abscess, fistula)

Perineal/genital trauma or infection

Decubitus ulcers with fecal contamination

Risk factors:

Diabetes mellitus (particularly common)

Immunosuppression

Advanced age

Obesity

Previous surgery in area

Geographic patterns: Often involves lower abdomen, perineum, or lower extremities

Pathophysiology:

Synergistic polymicrobial infection

Aerobes consume oxygen, creating anaerobic microenvironment

Anaerobes thrive in reduced-oxygen conditions

Combined effect produces aggressive, rapidly spreading infection

Anaerobes produce hyaluronidase and other spreading factors

### Type II Necrotizing Fasciitis: Streptococcal Myositis

Organisms:

Group A Streptococcus (Streptococcus pyogenes): Present in 95% of cases

Staph aureus: Present in 50–60% of cases (often MRSA)

Typically 2 organisms (hence "Type II")

Source:

Skin trauma: Minor wound, insect bite, IV injection site

Surgical procedure (post-operative infection)

Spontaneous: Can develop without identifiable source

Risk factors:

Immunosuppression: HIV, chemotherapy, steroids

Diabetes: But less commonly associated than Type I

Prior trauma: Recent surgery or injury to area

Intravenous drug use: IV sites; associated with MRSA

Geographic patterns: Any location; no particular predilection

Pathophysiology:

Group A Streptococcus produces M protein and other virulence factors

Streptopkinase: Breaks down fibrin, permits rapid spread

Hyaluronidase: Degrades connective tissue

Streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins (SPEs): Toxic shock-like syndrome

Rapid spread along fascial planes

Muscle involvement common (myositis)

Streptococcal toxic shock syndrome (STSS):

Systemic toxicity from streptococcal exotoxins

Profound shock often develops within hours

Multi-organ failure

Mortality 30–50% even with treatment

### Type III Necrotizing Fasciitis: Marine/Environmental Organisms

Organisms:

Vibrio vulnificus (60% of Type III)

### Aeromonas hydrophila

### Vibrio parahaemolyticus

Vibrio cholerae (non-01, non-O139)

### Plesiomonas shigelloides

Source:

Exposure to saltwater (Vibrio species)

Exposure to freshwater (Aeromonas)

Seafood handling or consumption (raw oysters, shellfish)

Risk factors:

Immunosuppression: HIV, cirrhosis, diabetes

Hepatic disease: Cirrhosis particularly increases Vibrio risk

Renal failure: Uremia impairs immunity

Open wound exposure: Abrasion, puncture wound exposed to water

Geographic patterns: Coastal regions; fresh/brackish water areas

Pathophysiology:

Vibrio vulnificus produces cytolysin (exotoxin)

Direct tissue invasion and necrosis

Rapid sepsis development

Mortality 30–50% in immunocompromised

### Type IV Necrotizing Fasciitis: Fungal Infections (Rare)

Organisms:

Zygomycetes (Mucormycosis): Rhizopus, Mucor, Rhizomucor

Candida (less aggressive)

Risk factors:

Severe immunosuppression: HIV with CD4 <50, hematologic malignancy, chemotherapy

Diabetes with ketoacidosis: Particularly for Zygomycetes

Neutropenia: <500 ANC

Characteristics:

Rapidly progressive

Often with necrosis and tissue black discoloration (tissue necrosis)

Extremely high mortality (50–80%)

Management: Requires aggressive surgical debridement + systemic antifungal (liposomal amphotericin B)

### Clinical Presentation by Type

### Type I: Polymicrobial, Often Diabetic or Post-Operative

Presentation:

Onset: Usually days after trauma or surgery (when infection source present from GI tract)

Pain: Severe, out of proportion to skin findings

Skin appearance: May appear deceptively mild initially

Erythema may be absent or minimal

Edema present but not necessarily severe

Skin may progress to dusky appearance, crepitus, or bullae

Systemic signs: Fever, tachycardia, altered mental status

Location: Often lower abdomen, perineum, lower extremities

Distinguishing features:

History of abdominal surgery or GI source

Associated with diabetes in many cases

Crepitus present (from gas-forming organisms)

### Type II: Streptococcal, Often Post-Operative or Post-Traumatic

Presentation:

Onset: Rapid; often within 24–48 hours of trauma or surgery

Pain: Severe, rapidly progressive, out of proportion to appearance

Skin appearance:

Initially erythematous, edematous

Rapidly progressive to dusky, purple discoloration

Bullae may form (fluid-filled or hemorrhagic)

Rapid spread is hallmark

Systemic signs: Severe—fever, tachycardia, hypotension, shock developing rapidly

Toxic shock features: Hypotension, multi-organ failure, altered mental status

Location: Any location; often extremities

Distinguishing features:

Rapid clinical deterioration

Severe systemic toxicity

Streptococcal toxic shock syndrome may be present

### Type III: Marine/Environmental Exposure

Presentation:

Onset: Hours to days after exposure

History: Recent exposure to saltwater or seafood

Skin appearance: Rapidly progressive erythema, edema, bullae

Systemic signs: Severe sepsis developing rapidly

Location: Usually site of water exposure

Distinguishing features:

Exposure history

Rapid progression despite initial small wound

### Myonecrosis (Gas Gangrene)

Presentation:

Onset: Rapid; often within 24–48 hours

Pain: Severe, severe, and progressively worse

Skin appearance:

Initially edema, possible erythema

Rapidly progresses to dusky, mottled appearance

Crepitus (pathognomonic): Palpable air in tissues from gas-forming organisms

Muscle visible through skin (in extreme cases)

Systemic signs: Extreme toxicity—shock, renal failure, hemolysis, acidosis

Exudate: Thin, serosanguineous exudate ("dish-water" appearance)

Organisms:

Clostridium perfringens (95% of cases)

Other Clostridium species

Less commonly: other gas-forming organisms (E. coli, Streptococcus, Staph aureus)

Associated conditions:

Trauma with soil contamination

Surgery (post-operative myonecrosis)

Spontaneous (in patients with Clostridium bacteremia, often from GI source)

### 8.2 Imaging Biomarkers and Fascial Involvement

### Clinical Assessment: Limitations of Physical Examination

Physical examination alone is insufficient to diagnose necrotizing fasciitis. Classic teaching emphasizes:

Skin pain out of proportion to appearance

Rapidly progressive edema

Systemic toxicity

However:

Pain out of proportion is non-specific; present in many cellulitis cases

Systemic toxicity may be absent early, when surgical intervention would be most beneficial

Delayed diagnosis is common; average delay is 24 hours from presentation

Clinical pearl: If suspicion is high based on any concerning features, proceed with imaging or operative exploration rather than waiting for fulminant presentation.

### Imaging Modalities

### Ultrasound (Point-of-Care)

Advantages:

Bedside availability

No contrast needed

Real-time assessment

No radiation

Findings suggestive of necrotizing fasciitis:

Hypoechoic or anechoic fluid between fascial planes

Thickened fascia: >2–3 mm suggests inflammation/infection

Echogenic material within fluid: Pus or gas

Absence of normal fascial plane: Loss of normal echogenic fascia line

Limitations:

Operator-dependent

May be difficult to assess deep structures

Cannot definitively exclude necrotizing fasciitis

Current role: Useful as screening tool; absence of findings does NOT exclude NF

### CT with IV Contrast

Advantages:

Excellent soft tissue resolution

Clearly delineates fascia, muscle, and fluid collections

Can assess extent of involvement

Evaluates for complications (abscess, necrotizing myositis)

No operator dependence

Findings suggestive of necrotizing fasciitis:

Thick, enhancing fascia: Fascial edema and inflammation

Fluid in fascial planes: Hypodense (fluid) without normal muscle planes

Stranding of subcutaneous fat: Inflammatory change

Gas in tissues: Pneumatosis (air in fascial planes or muscle)—pathognomonic for certain organisms

Muscle enhancement abnormality: Necrotizing myositis shows edema and hypodensity

Sensitivity: 80–90% for necrotizing fasciitis when performed with IV contrast

Limitations:

Requires transport (not suitable for unstable patients)

Radiation and contrast exposure

If diagnosis is strongly suspected clinically, should not delay surgery

### MRI

Advantages:

Excellent tissue characterization

Superior soft tissue resolution

T2 imaging clearly shows fluid/edema

Can assess muscle involvement precisely

Findings:

T2 hyperintensity: Fluid in fascial planes

T1 hypointensity: Edema

Muscle signal abnormality: T2 hyperintensity in muscle (myositis)

Limitations:

Time-consuming (not suitable for emergent cases)

Contraindications (pacemakers, ferrous metal implants)

Contrast (gadolinium) needed for full assessment

Unlikely to change management if necrotizing fasciitis suspected

Current role: Not recommended for acute suspected NF; information from MRI would delay necessary surgery

### Plain Radiographs

Findings:

Gas in tissues (pneumatosis): Linear or bubbling lucencies in soft tissues

Pathognomonic for gas-forming organisms (Clostridium, E. coli, others)

Limitations:

Insensitive (gas may not be visible early)

Non-specific (gas from recent instrumentation or surgery can mimic infection)

Current role: If gas present, highly concerning; but absence does NOT exclude NF

### Laboratory Biomarkers

### Routine Laboratory Studies

Inflammatory markers:

WBC count: Often elevated (>12,000), but can be normal or low in severe sepsis

CRP: Usually elevated; trending may help monitor treatment response

Procalcitonin: Elevated; prognostic value for severity

Tissue damage markers:

Creatine kinase (CK): Elevated in myonecrosis (necrotizing myositis)

CK >1,000 IU/L suggests significant muscle involvement

CK >5,000 suggests extensive myonecrosis

Myoglobin: Elevated in myonecrosis; urine myoglobinuria indicates myonecrosis

Lactate: Elevated; reflects tissue hypoperfusion and acidosis

Associated with worse prognosis

May be markedly elevated (>4 mmol/L) in fulminant cases

Hemolysis markers (in myonecrosis):

LDH: Markedly elevated

Haptoglobin: Low (consumed in binding free hemoglobin)

Hemoglobin: May decrease from hemolysis

Bilirubin: Elevated from hemolysis

Coagulation abnormalities:

INR/PT: Prolonged (from tissue factor release, DIC)

Platelets: Low (consumption in DIC)

D-dimer/FDP: Elevated (fibrinolysis)

Fibrinogen: Low (consumption)

Organ dysfunction markers:

Creatinine: Elevated (AKI from hypotension, myoglobinuria)

Bilirubin: Elevated (hepatic dysfunction, hemolysis)

Transaminases: May be elevated

### Scoring Systems for Necrotizing Soft Tissue Infections

LRINEC Score (Laboratory Risk Indicator for Necrotizing Fasciitis):

| Parameter | Value | Points |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CRP (mg/L) | <150 | 0 |
|  | ≥150 | 4 |
| WBC (×10³) | <15 | 0 |
|  | 15–25 | 1 |
|  | >25 | 2 |
| Hemoglobin (g/dL) | >135 | 0 |
|  | 120–135 | 1 |
|  | <120 | 2 |
| Sodium (mmol/L) | ≥135 | 0 |
|  | <135 | 2 |
| Creatinine (mg/dL) | ≤1.6 | 0 |
|  | >1.6 | 2 |
| Glucose (mg/dL) | ≤180 | 0 |
|  | >180 | 1 |

Score interpretation:

<6: Low risk (5% NF incidence)

6–7: Intermediate risk (20% NF incidence)

>8: High risk (>60% NF incidence)

Utility: LRINEC helps identify high-risk patients; score >8 warrants imaging or operative exploration

Limitations:

Not 100% sensitive/specific

Cannot definitively exclude NF based on low score

Should not delay intervention if clinical suspicion high

### Microbiological Studies

Cultures:

Blood cultures: Obtain before antibiotics; positive in 50–60% of necrotizing fasciitis

Wound cultures: From drainage or operative specimen

Gram stain: Rapid identification of organism type

Anaerobic cultures: Essential (required for Type I)

PCR/Molecular testing:

Rapid organism identification from operative specimens

Can guide antimicrobial therapy before culture results

### 8.3 Aggressive Debridement: Tissue Diagnosis and Serial Excisions

### The Principle of Early Aggressive Debridement

Fundamental principle: In necrotizing soft tissue infections, aggressive surgical debridement is the ONLY definitive treatment.

Antimicrobial therapy, supportive care, and source control (in other infections) are all important, but in necrotizing fasciitis, the infection progresses faster than antibiotics or immune system can contain it. Therefore, removal of all necrotic and infected tissue is essential for survival.

### Operative Approach and Technique

### Operative Exposure

Incisions:

Sufficient length to fully expose affected area

Principle: "When in doubt, extend the incision"

Multiple incisions may be needed to assess extent

Incisions should allow direct visualization of all tissue planes

Exposure technique:

Skin: Incised sharply

Subcutaneous tissue: Explored with gloved hand or instrument to assess tissue viability

Fascia: Inspected; separated from underlying muscle to assess for fluid/pus

Muscle: Assessed for color, texture, bleeding response

### Tissue Viability Assessment

Normal tissue characteristics:

Color: Pink to red; normal muscle color

Bleeding: Brisk bleeding when cut or cauterized

Consistency: Normal texture; contracts with cautery

Adherence: Fascia adherent to muscle

Necrotic tissue characteristics:

Color: Gray, black, brown, or purple

Bleeding: No bleeding when cut (indicates devascularization)

Consistency: Soft, friable

Odor: Putrid odor (from anaerobic organisms)

### Debridement Technique

Aggressive approach:

Remove all tissue that appears non-viable

When in doubt, remove it

Conservative initial debridement is WRONG in necrotizing fasciitis

Inadequate debridement necessitates re-operation; over-debridement at least removes infection source

Specific tissues:

Skin:

Remove necrotic, blackened, or clearly non-viable skin

Remove skin showing signs of severe inflammation (dusky, mottled)

Preserve borderline tissue if bleeding occurs (indicates viability)

Subcutaneous tissue:

Aggressively debrided

Often largely removed; this tissue is poorly vascularized and frequently involved

Fascia:

Central to necrotizing fasciitis

Removed entirely in affected areas

Removal of fascia may require amputation if extensive lower extremity involvement

Muscle:

Preserved if viable (red, bleeds, contracts)

Removed if not viable

Myonecrosis (gas gangrene) may require amputation

Specimen handling:

Tissue removed should be sent for Gram stain, culture, and ideally pathology

Frozen section can confirm necrotizing fasciitis (shows extensive necrosis, inflammatory infiltrate)

### Serial Debridements: The Key to Survival

Single debridement is almost never sufficient in necrotizing fasciitis. Rather, planned serial debridements at 24–48 hour intervals are essential.

### Rationale for Serial Debridement

Demarcation: The boundary between viable and necrotic tissue is not apparent at initial surgery. Progressive ischemia continues after initial debridement; tissue that appeared viable may necrose within 24 hours.

Infection spread: Despite aggressive debridement, bacteria continue to spread along fascial planes. The infection in areas that appear only mildly inflamed at first surgery may be extensive.

Progressive necrosis: As edema increases and microvascular thrombosis propagates, previously viable tissue becomes necrotic.

Assessment opportunity: Each re-operation provides opportunity to reassess tissue viability with fresh perspective.

### Planned Re-Operation Protocol

Timing of re-exploration:

Initial operation: Aggressive debridement

First re-exploration: 24 hours later

Assessment: Evaluate demarcation, extent of remaining necrosis

Second re-exploration: If extensive necrosis remains, repeat at 24 hours

Continuation: Until clear demarcation exists and only viable tissue remains

Frequency variation:

If infection appears well-controlled and demarcation clear after initial debridement: May extend to 48 hours before re-exploration

If infection appears to be spreading despite debridement: Consider re-exploration within 12–18 hours

Operative time and patient stability:

Each operation imposes physiologic stress

Repeated operations in unstable patient is difficult

However, continued infection mandates continued intervention

Balance between operative necessity and patient stability

### Decision to Stop Re-Operations

Criteria for final debridement (no further re-operations needed):

All necrotic tissue removed

Only viable, bleeding tissue remains

No further evidence of infection spread

Tissue appears clean, well-demarcated

Patient hemodynamically stable

Closure decisions:

If all infected tissue removed and patient stable: May allow primary closure or delayed closure

If skin deficit remains: May require skin grafting (after infection cleared)

If extensive tissue loss: May require flap coverage

### Specific Debridement Scenarios

### Type I: Polymicrobial (Usually Lower Extremity or Perineal)

Extent often extensive:

Often involves entire thickness of soft tissues from skin to fascia and sometimes into muscle

May involve entire lower extremity or large portion of perineum

Debridement approach:

Extensive; often removes large areas of skin and subcutaneous tissue

May require amputation if lower extremity extensively involved

Perineal infections may require colostomy to divert stool away from infected field

Multiple debridements: Almost always necessary; average 3–4 debridements

### Type II: Streptococcal (Usually Extremity)

Extent variable:

Sometimes more localized than Type I

But can be very aggressive

Debridement approach:

Matching debridement to extent

Serial debridements often necessary

Myositis: If muscle involvement (myositis), may require amputation depending on extent

### Type III: Marine Organisms

Similar to Type II: Often extensively involved

Debridement: Serial debridements frequently necessary

### Myonecrosis (Gas Gangrene)

Extent: Usually very extensive; muscle is primary site of involvement

Amputation: Almost always necessary:

High amputation is often required

Debridement must remove ALL affected muscle

Attempting to preserve limb by debridement alone often results in infection recurrence and eventual amputation anyway

Early aggressive amputation is often the most merciful approach, sparing patient prolonged illness

Outcome: With immediate amputation, mortality 10–15%; without amputation, mortality >80%

### 8.4 Hybrid Approaches: Negative Pressure Wound Therapy and Reconstruction

### Negative Pressure Wound Therapy (NPWT)

### Mechanism and Physiology

NPWT (also called vacuum-assisted closure or VAC therapy) applies continuous or intermittent negative pressure to a wound, which:

Removes wound fluid: Exudate and inflammatory mediators are continuously removed

Promotes granulation: Negative pressure stimulates fibroblasts and angiogenesis

Reduces edema: Negative pressure reduces interstitial fluid accumulation

Increases blood flow: Improves tissue perfusion

Approximates wound edges: Gradually closes wound by mechanical traction

### Application in Necrotizing Soft Tissue Infection

Role in management:

Not primary treatment: NPWT does NOT treat the infection; it is an adjunctive measure

After infection controlled: Used after aggressive debridement has removed necrotic tissue and infection appears controlled

Wound management: Facilitates healing in large defects created by debridement

Technique:

Timing: Applied after initial debridement, once infection controlled

Dressing: Foam or gauze dressing placed in wound

Suction application: Negative pressure (75–125 mmHg) applied

Frequency of changes: Typically 2–3 times per week

Duration: Until granulation tissue develops and wound is ready for closure/grafting

Benefits:

Promotes healing of large defects

Reduces need for frequent dressing changes

Improves patient comfort compared to packing

Allows planning for eventual closure/reconstruction

### Complications of NPWT

Fistula formation: If dressing is placed directly over bowel loop (in abdominal/perineal defects)

Bleeding: If dressing adheres to granulation tissue; controlled by gentle removal and hemostasis

Pain: Dressing changes can be painful

Biofilm formation: Bacteria can form biofilms on foam dressing itself

### 8.5 The Role of Hyperbaric Oxygen and Adjunctive Therapies

### Hyperbaric Oxygen Therapy (HBOT)

HBOT involves breathing 100% oxygen at pressures greater than atmospheric (typically 2.0–2.8 ATA, atmospheres absolute).

### Mechanism of Benefit in Soft Tissue Infections

Oxygen delivery:

Increased atmospheric pressure forces oxygen into blood plasma (dissolved oxygen increases ~15-fold)

Allows oxygen delivery even in hypoperfused tissues

Improves tissue oxygenation in infected wounds

Antimicrobial effects:

Direct toxicity: High oxygen concentrations are toxic to anaerobes

Enhanced phagocytosis: Macrophages and neutrophils have enhanced antimicrobial activity in hyperoxic conditions

Collagen synthesis: Improves wound healing

### Evidence for HBOT in Soft Tissue Infections

Anaerobic infections:

Strongest evidence for anaerobic soft tissue infections (Type I necrotizing fasciitis)

Clostridial myonecrosis (gas gangrene): HBOT combined with surgery and antibiotics shows improved outcomes

Meta-analyses suggest 10–20% mortality reduction with HBOT

Aerobic infections:

Limited evidence for Type II (streptococcal) necrotizing fasciitis

Some benefit in improving wound healing after debridement

### Practical Considerations

Timing:

Best if initiated early in treatment course

Sessions: Daily (5 days per week) or twice daily in acute infection

Duration: 20 treatments typical for acute infections; may be extended

Limitations:

Requires specialized facility (not available in all institutions)

Requires patient cooperation (claustrophobia potential)

Oxygen toxicity risk with prolonged/repeated exposure

Not a substitute for surgery: HBOT is ADJUNCTIVE; aggressive debridement remains essential

Current status: Recommended as adjunctive therapy in Type I and Type IV necrotizing fasciitis; increasingly used in Type II

### Antimicrobial Agents: Systemic and Topical

### Systemic Antibiotics

Initiation: Immediately upon suspicion/diagnosis

Empiric regimen (before culture results):

Covers gram-positive (including anaerobes), gram-negative, and anaerobes

Common regimen:

Ampicillin-sulbactam (or piperacillin-tazobactam) PLUS

Clindamycin (for anaerobic and anti-toxin effects) PLUS

Vancomycin (if MRSA risk)

Specific agents:

Clindamycin: Particularly important for Type II (streptococcal)

Not only for bacterial coverage, but anti-toxin effects

Reduces streptococcal exotoxin production

Improves outcomes in streptococcal myositis/toxic shock

Fluoroquinolones: Good for gram-negative, particularly marine organisms (Vibrio)

For marine organisms (Type III):

Doxycycline + fluoroquinolone often used

Cephalosporin + clindamycin alternative

Vancomycin: If MRSA suspected or gram-positive with allergy to β-lactams

De-escalation: Based on culture results and susceptibilities

Duration: Typically 7–14 days IV, followed by oral antibiotics to complete 2–4 week course

### Topical Antiseptics and Antibiotics

Topical agents:

Silver-impregnated dressings: Antimicrobial properties; reduced biofilm formation

Iodine-based solutions: Applied to debridement sites; antimicrobial

Chlorhexidine: Broad-spectrum antimicrobial

Antibiotic ointments: Limited penetration but may reduce contamination

Role: Adjunctive; applied after debridement but not substitute for systemic antibiotics

### Immunoglobulin Therapy

Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG):

Contains pooled antibodies from plasma donors

Potential benefit: Provides opsonizing antibodies against streptococcal antigens

Evidence:

Limited evidence for benefit

Some observational data suggest improved outcomes when combined with aggressive surgery + antibiotics

Not standard therapy but may be considered in severe streptococcal myositis/toxic shock

Current status: Optional; not routinely recommended but sometimes used

### Immune Modulation in Toxic Shock

Septic/toxic shock from necrotizing fasciitis:

Often driven by bacterial exotoxins (particularly streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins in Type II)

Acts as superantigen: Directly activates T cells and causes massive cytokine release

Potential therapies:

Anti-TNF or anti-IL-1β: Block cytokine cascade

Steroids: Controversial; some benefit in toxic shock but may impair immune response

Continue

30 дек. 2025 г.

Nitric oxide inhibitors: Reduce NO-mediated vasodilation

Current evidence: Limited; not standard practice

### 8.6 Post-Operative Management and Reconstruction

### Wound Management After Debridement

Immediate post-operative:

Dressing: Dry dressing or NPWT (per above)

Monitoring: Assess for signs of ongoing infection

Drain management: Drains placed during debridement monitored for character/amount of drainage

Serial examination: Daily wound inspection assesses for spread or improvement

Signs of adequate source control:

Fever resolves

WBC count trends downward

Lactate normalizes

Hemodynamic stability improves

Wound appears clean, without necrotic material

Signs of persistent infection:

Continued fever

Rapid spread of inflammation beyond original site

Persistent or worsening shock

Positive repeat cultures

### Indication for re-exploration

### Reconstruction and Closure

### Timing of Closure

Primary closure:

Appropriate only if:

All necrotic tissue removed

Clean, well-vascularized tissue remains

No signs of ongoing infection

Sufficient tissue to close without tension

Usually 3–5 days after initial debridement, once demarcation clear

Delayed closure:

If tissue edges are distant or tension would be excessive

Allow NPWT to promote granulation for 1–2 weeks

Then skin graft or flap coverage

Skin grafting:

Split-thickness grafts: Applied once clean granulation tissue present

Full-thickness grafts: Less common; used in cosmetically sensitive areas

Grafting typically 2–3 weeks after infection control

Flap coverage:

Muscle flaps: Used for large defects

Examples:

Muscle flap: Transferred from adjacent muscle (e.g., rectus abdominis for abdominal defect)

Osteocutaneous flap: Transferred with bone for reconstructive purposes

Free flaps: Microvascular transfer of tissue from distant site

### Amputation Decision-Making

Indications for amputation:

Extensive myonecrosis: Muscle involvement in >50% of extremity cross-section

Gas gangrene: Affects major muscle groups

Vascular compromise: Arterial injury making limb non-viable

Non-functional limb: After debridement, insufficient function for meaningful use

Uncontrolled infection: Refractory to repeated debridement

Level of amputation:

Through viable tissue: Must be proximal to all infected tissue

High amputation often necessary: Temptation to perform low amputation to preserve length often results in stump infection and need for re-amputation

General principle: Better single high amputation than multiple sequential lower amputations

Timing:

Early amputation for clearly non-viable limbs

Delayed amputation for borderline cases (allow 24–48 hours for demarcation, then amputation if necessary)

### Long-Term Complications and Morbidity

Functional outcomes:

Limb-saving: If reconstruction successful, functional limb preserved

Amputation: Require prosthetic fitting, rehabilitation

Cosmetic: Scars inevitable; may require further reconstruction

Infection recurrence:

Rate: 5–10% even with aggressive treatment

Vigilance necessary: Any signs of infection warrant immediate evaluation

Psychological morbidity:

PTSD: Severe illness and prolonged hospitalization; limb loss

Depression: Adjustment to disability

Support services: Should be offered to all survivors

### Summary: Necrotizing Soft Tissue Infections

Necrotizing soft tissue infections require:

High clinical suspicion: Based on clinical presentation (pain out of proportion, rapid progression, systemic toxicity)

Rapid diagnosis: Imaging (CT or ultrasound) if stable; operative exploration if unstable or high suspicion

Emergent operative intervention: Within 6 hours of diagnosis ideally; earlier if signs of fulminant infection

Aggressive debridement: Extensive removal of all necrotic and infected tissue

Serial debridements: Planned re-exploration at 24–48 hour intervals until infection controlled

Broad-spectrum antibiotics: Covering aerobes, anaerobes, gram-positive, gram-negative

Supportive care: Aggressive hemodynamic support, organ support

Adjunctive therapies: HBOT, NPWT, reconstructive surgery once infection controlled

Outcomes:

Mortality: 15–30% with optimal treatment; up to 60% if delayed diagnosis

Morbidity: Significant; amputation in 20–40% with lower extremity involvement

Quality of life: Survivors often have long-term functional limitations; psychological support essential

Prevention:

High index of suspicion; do not dismiss rapidly progressive soft tissue infection as "just cellulitis"

Operative exploration when doubt exists; cost of negative exploration (<$5,000) far less than cost of delayed diagnosis (amputation, prolonged hospitalization, death)
