### CHAPTER 9: BIOMARKERS AND IMMUNOLOGICAL PROFILING IN SEPSIS

### 9.1 Traditional Markers: Lactate, Procalcitonin, and C-Reactive Protein

### Lactate: The Cornerstone of Tissue Perfusion Assessment

### Pathophysiology of Lactate Accumulation in Sepsis

Normal lactate metabolism:

Lactate is produced by muscles, RBCs, and other tissues as an end product of anaerobic glycolysis

Under normal conditions, serum lactate is <2 mmol/L

Lactate is cleared by the liver (60–70%) and kidneys (20–30%)

Normal lactate clearance rate: ~10% per hour

Lactate elevation in sepsis:

Type A (hypoxic) lactate elevation:

Results from tissue hypoperfusion and oxygen delivery failure

Occurs when DO₂ (oxygen delivery) is inadequate

Formula: DO₂ = (Cardiac Output × Hemoglobin × SaO₂) - CI × Hb × SaO₂

In sepsis, DO₂ is reduced from:

Reduced cardiac output (septic cardiomyopathy)

Reduced hemoglobin (anemia from bleeding or hemodilution)

In rare cases, reduced SaO₂ (severe ARDS)

Tissues forced to rely on anaerobic metabolism

Pyruvate converted to lactate (normal stoichiometry)

Lactate accumulates because:

Continued production from anaerobic metabolism

Reduced hepatic clearance (liver hypoperfusion)

Type B (normoxic) lactate elevation:

Occurs despite adequate oxygen delivery

Mechanism: Mitochondrial dysfunction

Septic shock causes mitochondrial dysfunction (impaired oxidative phosphorylation)

Even with adequate oxygen available, mitochondria cannot utilize it

Pyruvate accumulates, converted to lactate

Results in "pyruvate cycling" without true hypoxia

More common in late sepsis than early

Reflects mitochondrial exhaustion from prolonged sepsis

### Clinical Significance of Lactate

Lactate elevation as prognostic marker:

Baseline lactate elevation: Associated with increased mortality

Lactate 2–4 mmol/L: Increased mortality risk

Lactate >4 mmol/L: Markedly increased mortality (30–50% vs 5–10% if lactate <2)

Lactate clearance as therapeutic target:

Concept: Rather than normalizing lactate acutely, measure the rate of lactate normalization

Lactate clearance definition: (Initial lactate - lactate at 6 hours) / Initial lactate × 100%

Target: ≥10% lactate clearance at 6 hours

Interpretation:

Clearance ≥10%: Associated with better outcomes

Clearance <10%: Associated with worse outcomes despite similar initial lactate levels

Clinical application:

Measure lactate at baseline (diagnosis of sepsis)

Measure again at 3–6 hours after resuscitation

If clearance <10%, indicates:

Ongoing tissue hypoperfusion despite resuscitation attempts

OR mitochondrial dysfunction not responding to conventional resuscitation

Indicates need for escalation of support: Increase vasopressor dose, add inotropic support, consider advanced therapies (ECMO, etc.)

Serial lactate monitoring:

Lactate trending is more important than absolute values

In recovering sepsis: Lactate should progressively decline

Persistently elevated or rising lactate indicates:

Inadequate source control

Ongoing infection

Organ failure progression

### Limitations of Lactate

Non-specific:

Elevated in many non-septic conditions:

Shock from any cause (cardiogenic, hypovolemic, hemorrhagic)

Respiratory failure (from hypoxia)

Liver failure (reduced clearance)

Malignancy (tumor-associated production)

D-lactic acidosis (short-bowel syndrome, bacterial overgrowth)

Cannot distinguish Type A from Type B:

Both present with elevated lactate

Require additional assessment (cardiac output, SvO₂) to distinguish

Delayed response:

Lactate may remain elevated even after source is controlled (hours to days)

Reflects metabolic "debt" from period of hypoperfusion

Takes time for mitochondrial function to recover

### Procalcitonin (PCT): Bacterial Infection Marker

### Physiology of Procalcitonin

Source and synthesis:

Normal state: Procalcitonin is produced constitutively by neuroendocrine cells (C cells of thyroid, others)

Basal level: <0.05 ng/mL (normal)

In infection: Multiple cell types produce PCT:

Monocytes: Primary source in bacterial infection

Hepatocytes: Significant production

Other cells: Neutrophils, endothelial cells, others

Stimuli for PCT production in bacterial infection:

LPS (lipopolysaccharide): Direct stimulus via TLR-4

TNF-α: Indirect stimulus; promotes monocyte PCT production

IL-1β: Amplifies TNF-α-mediated induction

IL-6: Potent inducer of hepatic PCT production

Kinetics:

Rise time: 3–12 hours after bacterial infection

Peak: 24–48 hours

Half-life: 24 hours (longer than CRP)

Decline: Gradual over days as infection resolves

### Clinical Significance of PCT

Bacterial vs viral differentiation:

Bacterial infection: PCT elevation >2 ng/mL indicates bacterial infection (90% specificity)

Viral infection: PCT typically remains <0.5 ng/mL

Distinction useful: Guides antimicrobial initiation (bacterial likely if PCT elevated; viral unlikely if markedly elevated)

Prognostic value:

Baseline PCT >10 ng/mL: Associated with severe sepsis/shock

Baseline PCT >25 ng/mL: Associated with high mortality risk

Serial measurement: Declining PCT trajectory indicates improving prognosis

Persistent elevation: Indicates ongoing infection or inadequate source control

PCT levels and severity:

| PCT Level | Severity | Clinical Implication |
| --- | --- | --- |
| <0.05 ng/mL | Normal | Systemic infection unlikely |
| 0.05–0.5 ng/mL | Low-grade | Consider non-bacterial cause |
| 0.5–2.0 ng/mL | Moderate | Bacterial infection present; mild-moderate sepsis |
| 2–10 ng/mL | High | Bacterial infection; sepsis |
| >10 ng/mL | Very high | Severe sepsis/septic shock; high mortality risk |

### De-escalation of Antibiotics Based on PCT

Procalcitonin-guided antibiotic duration:

Concept: Rather than predetermined antibiotic course duration, use PCT trends to guide when to discontinue antibiotics

Protocol:

Measure baseline PCT at sepsis diagnosis

Initiate broad-spectrum antibiotics

Repeat PCT measurement at 48–72 hours

If PCT decline >50% from peak AND clinical improvement: Consider de-escalation or discontinuation

If PCT plateaued or rising: Continue or escalate antibiotics; reassess source control

Outcome: PCT-guided de-escalation protocols in clinical trials show:

Reduced antibiotic exposure (10–20% reduction in antibiotic days)

No increase in mortality or treatment failures

Reduced antibiotic-associated complications (C. difficile, resistance selection)

### Limitations of PCT

Non-specific for sepsis:

Elevated in non-infectious inflammation:

Major surgery (post-operative peak)

Trauma

Burns

Autoimmune disease (systemic lupus erythematosus, vasculitis)

Myocardial infarction

Malignancy (paraneoplastic)

Must be interpreted in clinical context

False negatives:

Early infection (<3 hours): PCT may still be normal

Localized infection: Non-invasive bacterial infections may not trigger PCT elevation

Immunocompromised patients: May have blunted PCT response

Fungal and viral infections:

Fungal infections: Variable PCT response; generally lower than bacterial

Viral infections: Generally low PCT (but exceptions exist)

Mixed infections: PCT may be intermediate

Cost and availability:

PCT assays expensive (~$50–200 per test)

Not universally available in all hospitals

Requires specialized laboratory

### C-Reactive Protein (CRP): Systemic Inflammation Marker

### Pathophysiology of CRP

Source:

Produced by hepatocytes in response to IL-6

Not produced by immune cells (unlike TNF-α, IL-1β)

Systemic marker of inflammation

Synthesis pathway:

Tissue damage/infection → TNF-α, IL-1β production

These cytokines → IL-6 production

IL-6 → hepatic production of CRP

CRP acts on complement, enhances opsonization, promotes phagocytosis

Kinetics:

Rise time: 6–24 hours

Peak: 48–72 hours

Half-life: 19 hours (longer than TNF-α, shorter than PCT)

Decline: Gradual; reflects ongoing inflammation

### Clinical Significance of CRP

Baseline CRP and severity:

CRP <10 mg/L: Minimal inflammation; infection unlikely

CRP 10–100 mg/L: Moderate inflammation; common in early sepsis

CRP 100–300 mg/L: High inflammation; sepsis likely

CRP >300 mg/L: Severe inflammation; severe sepsis/shock common

Prognostic value:

Baseline CRP: Modest predictive value for mortality

CRP trajectory: More important than absolute value

Declining CRP: Indicates improving infection

Plateaued CRP: Indicates persistent infection or inadequate source control

Rising CRP despite antibiotics: Red flag; reassess source control

Comparison to PCT:

CRP: More sensitive for any inflammation (high sensitivity, lower specificity)

PCT: More specific for bacterial infection (moderate sensitivity, high specificity)

Combined use: CRP + PCT more informative than either alone

### Limitations of CRP

Non-specific:

Elevated in any inflammatory condition:

Non-infectious inflammation (post-operative, trauma, autoimmune)

Chronic diseases (rheumatoid arthritis, inflammatory bowel disease)

Cancer

Even stress and exercise

Slow response:

Rise time 6–24 hours means not useful for ultra-early diagnosis

Lactate increases before CRP in acute sepsis

Persistently elevated in chronic conditions:

Baseline CRP may be elevated in patients with chronic inflammatory diseases

Makes interpretation difficult in these populations

Cost-effectiveness:

Inexpensive (cost $5–20 per test)

But relatively non-specific; must interpret cautiously

### 9.2 Monocytic HLA-DR: A Window into Immunosuppression

### The Immune Checkpoint: HLA-DR as Immune Function Marker

### Physiology of HLA-DR Expression

HLA-DR background:

HLA-DR: Major histocompatibility complex class II molecule

Expressed on antigen-presenting cells: Monocytes, dendritic cells, macrophages

Function: Present processed antigen to T cells (CD4⁺ T cells)

Structure: α-chain + β-chain; forms binding groove for antigen peptides

HLA-DR on monocytes:

Monocytes are primary target for HLA-DR measurement

HLA-DR measured by:

Flow cytometry: Gold standard; measures percentage of monocytes expressing HLA-DR and median fluorescence intensity (MFI)

Clinical usage: Typically report as:

Percentage of monocytes HLA-DR⁺

Absolute count of HLA-DR⁺ monocytes

MFI (intensity of expression per monocyte)

Normal values:

Healthy individuals: 95–100% of monocytes express HLA-DR (essentially all monocytes)

MFI in healthy: 2,000–4,000 (varies by institution/assay)

### HLA-DR Dynamics in Sepsis

Early sepsis (hours 0–24):

HLA-DR expression decreases from baseline

Percentage of HLA-DR⁺ monocytes: 50–95% (reduced from normal 95–100%)

MFI: Reduced to 1,000–2,000

Mechanism:

TNF-α and other inflammatory cytokines downregulate HLA-DR transcription

Internalization of HLA-DR from cell surface

Apoptosis of HLA-DR⁺ monocytes (preferentially removes these cells)

Late sepsis (days 2–7):

HLA-DR remains profoundly suppressed

Percentage of HLA-DR⁺ monocytes: <30–50%

MFI: Often <500

### This suppression is the hallmark of immunosuppression

Correlates with:

Increased susceptibility to secondary infections

Inadequate immune response to primary pathogen

Impaired antigen presentation to T cells

Recovery phase (week 2+):

HLA-DR gradually returns to normal

Recovery of HLA-DR correlates with clinical improvement

Persistent suppression indicates continued immunosuppression

### HLA-DR as Prognostic Marker

Prognostic significance:

| HLA-DR Value | Prognosis | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| >80% of monocytes | Good | Normal immune function |
| 50–80% of monocytes | Intermediate | Early immunosuppression; monitor |
| 30–50% of monocytes | Poor | Significant immunosuppression; increased infection risk |
| <30% of monocytes | Very poor | Profound immunosuppression; >50% mortality |

Clinical trials evidence:

HLADR <30% is associated with:

Increased mortality: 40–50% vs 10–20% in HLA-DR >30%

Secondary infections: 70% vs 20% in HLA-DR >30%

Organ failure: More prolonged, more severe

Predictive value for secondary infection:

HLA-DR <30% at day 2–3 of sepsis predicts 70% risk of secondary infection

This is stronger predictor than severity of primary sepsis

### HLA-DR and Immunotherapy Selection

Potential application in personalized immunotherapy:

HLA-DR >50%: Patient has adequate immune function

Do not give immunosuppressive agents

May benefit from anti-inflammatory therapy if inflammation excessive

Do NOT give checkpoint inhibitors (would further suppress already adequate immunity)

HLA-DR 30–50%: Borderline immune function

Close monitoring

Consider immune support (IL-7, GM-CSF)

Checkpoint inhibitors only if clinical deterioration suggests hyperinflammation

HLA-DR <30%: Profound immunosuppression

Candidate for immunostimulatory therapy:

Checkpoint inhibitors (anti-PD-1, anti-TIM-3)

Cytokine therapy (IL-7, IFN-γ)

GM-CSF

Do NOT give anti-inflammatory agents (would worsen immunosuppression)

### Clinical Implementation Challenges

Measurement accessibility:

Requires flow cytometry capability

Not available in all hospitals

Turnaround time: 24–48 hours (not real-time)

Cost: $200–500 per test

Standardization:

Different institutions use different cutoff values

Assay variability between laboratories

Requires training of technical staff

Integration into clinical workflow:

Not yet standard of care in most hospitals

Some specialized ICUs using HLA-DR to guide immunotherapy

Emerging as important marker in sepsis clinical trials

### 9.3 MicroRNA Signatures: Organ-Specific Dysfunction

### MicroRNAs: Definition and Function

### Biophysiology of MicroRNAs

MicroRNA (miRNA) definition:

Short non-coding RNA molecules (~22 nucleotides)

Regulate post-transcriptional gene expression

Bind to complementary sequences in messenger RNA (mRNA)

Cause mRNA degradation or translational repression

Each miRNA regulates hundreds of target genes

Circulating miRNAs:

Released from cells during stress, apoptosis, or damage

Found in blood in:

Free form (bound to proteins like Ago2)

In extracellular vesicles (exosomes, microvesicles)

Stable in blood; can be measured

### MiRNA Production in Sepsis

Tissue-specific release:

Different tissues release distinct miRNA profiles when damaged

Cardiac myocytes: Release cardiac-specific miRNAs (miR-1, miR-133) when infarcted

Renal tubules: Release renal-specific miRNAs when damaged

Hepatocytes: Release liver-specific miRNAs when injured

Immune cells: Release immune-related miRNAs in response to infection

Mechanisms of release:

Apoptosis: Dying cells release miRNAs

Active secretion: Some cells actively secrete miRNAs in exosomes

Cell damage: Necrotic or dying cells release contents including miRNAs

### MicroRNAs in Sepsis: Emerging Biomarkers

Cardiac-specific miRNAs in septic cardiomyopathy:

miR-1, miR-133, miR-208: Cardiac-restricted miRNAs

Elevation in sepsis: Associated with cardiac dysfunction

Clinical significance: Elevated cardiac miRNAs correlate with:

Reduced ejection fraction

Increased troponin elevation

Worse cardiac outcomes

Renal-specific miRNAs in sepsis-associated AKI:

miR-30: Involved in renal physiology

miR-192, miR-194, miR-215: Renal tubule-specific

Elevation: Associated with development of AKI

Prognostic value: miRNA elevation precedes creatinine increase by hours

Potential for early detection: Could identify AKI before clinical manifestation

Hepatic-specific miRNAs in liver dysfunction:

miR-122: Most abundant miRNA in liver

Elevated in hepatocellular injury: Sepsis-associated acute liver failure

Predicts liver dysfunction: Elevation predicts bilirubin increase and coagulopathy

miRNAs and inflammation:

let-7 family: Involved in inflammatory response regulation

miR-155: Regulates pro-inflammatory cytokine production

miR-146a: Involved in immune tolerance and anti-inflammatory response

Dysregulation: In sepsis, these miRNAs are altered; profile may predict immune status

### Current Limitations and Future Potential

Limitations:

Complex biology: Each miRNA targets hundreds of genes; effects are context-dependent

Lack of standardization: Different studies use different methods for measurement

Reproducibility: miRNA measurements have variable reproducibility between labs

Cost and complexity: Requires specialized equipment and expertise

Lack of causal data: Unclear if miRNA changes cause pathology or are epiphenomenal

Future potential:

Panel approach: Combination of multiple miRNAs more informative than individual

Machine learning: Combined miRNA signatures may predict organ-specific failure

Therapeutic targeting: miRNA modulation (antagomiRs to reduce harmful miRNAs, or miRNA replacement) could treat organ dysfunction

Current clinical status: Primarily research tool; not yet incorporated into standard clinical practice

### 9.4 Circulating Cell-Free DNA: Tissue Damage and Prognosis

### Cell-Free DNA (cfDNA) in Sepsis

### Source and Kinetics of cfDNA

Origin of cfDNA:

Released from dying/dead cells

Apoptotic cells: Release cfDNA in regulated manner

Necrotic cells: Release cfDNA uncontrollably

Immune cells: During activation and death

Neutrophil extracellular traps (NETs): Release DNA as part of NET structure

Measurement:

Total cfDNA: Quantify using PCR or spectrophotometry

Mitochondrial cfDNA (mtDNA): Specific marker from mitochondrial damage

Genomic cfDNA: From nuclear DNA

Kinetics in sepsis:

Elevation within hours of sepsis onset

Correlates with severity: Higher levels in septic shock

Temporal trajectory: Levels decline as patient recovers or worsen with deterioration

### Clinical Significance of cfDNA

Prognostic value:

cfDNA elevation: Associated with worse outcomes

Very high cfDNA (>200 ng/mL): Associated with >50% mortality

cfDNA trajectory: Declining levels indicate improvement

Organ-specific damage:

Mitochondrial DNA: Marker of tissue damage and mitochondrial dysfunction

High mtDNA: Associated with organ failure development

Brain-enriched DNA: If present, indicates CNS damage

Sepsis severity prediction:

APACHE score correlation: cfDNA correlates with APACHE III score

SOFA score correlation: cfDNA correlates with organ dysfunction severity

### Mechanistic Insights from cfDNA

Reflects immune cell death:

Elevated cfDNA correlates with lymphocyte apoptosis

Neutrophil NETosis produces cfDNA

Marker of immune dysfunction

Indicates tissue damage:

cfDNA elevation indicates ongoing tissue death

Distinguished from apoptosis alone by mitochondrial cfDNA (indicates damage/death)

Reflects severity of multi-organ dysfunction

### Current Research and Future Directions

Emerging concept: cfDNA as "damage-associated molecular pattern":

cfDNA itself is immunogenic

cfDNA can activate TLRs

May perpetuate inflammatory cascade

Potential therapeutic target: cfDNA-binding compounds to neutralize

Therapeutic potential:

DNase therapy: Degrade cfDNA to reduce immunostimulation

Under investigation: Whether DNase reduces inflammation and improves outcomes

Current status: Primarily research tool; emerging in clinical trials but not standard care

### 9.5 Point-of-Care Testing and Rapid Molecular Diagnostics

### Rapid Pathogen Identification: Moving Beyond Conventional Cultures

Limitations of conventional cultures:

Time to result: 24–72 hours for organism identification

Time to susceptibilities: 48–96 hours for antimicrobial susceptibilities

During this delay: Patient receives broad-spectrum antibiotics (de-escalation delayed)

Result: Prolonged unnecessary broad-spectrum therapy

### Rapid Molecular Diagnostic Platforms

Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR)-based panels:

Technology: Amplifies specific DNA sequences; detects organism presence

Time to result: 1–4 hours

Organisms detected: Panel-dependent; typically 20–50 common organisms

FilmArray Blood Culture ID Panel:

Methodology: PCR-based identification panel

Organisms identified: 25 common pathogens (gram-positive, gram-negative, fungi)

Resistance markers: Identifies MRSA, VRE, ESBL

Time to result: ~1 hour

Sensitivity/Specificity: >95% for most organisms

Clinical impact: Enables early de-escalation in susceptible organism infections

T2 Candida Panel:

Organism: Rapid identification of Candida species from blood cultures

Time to result: ~3–5 hours

Significance: Candida identification permits early antifungal initiation

Clinical impact: Reduced time on empiric antifungal in non-fungal infections

Sepsifast Panel:

Organisms: 15 most common sepsis pathogens

Time to result: ~4 hours

Geographic variation: More common in Europe; emerging in US

MALDI-TOF Mass Spectrometry:

Methodology: Protein profiling of organisms; matches pattern to known organisms

Time to result: 1–2 hours (after organism growth)

Organisms detected: Essentially all bacteria and fungi

Widespread adoption: Increasingly used in microbiology labs

### Metagenomic Next-Generation Sequencing (mNGS)

Technology:

Methodology: DNA sequencing of all nucleic acid material in sample

Detection: Identifies any organism present (cultivable or not)

Unbiased: No prior knowledge of organism needed

Advantages:

Comprehensive: Single test detects bacteria, fungi, viruses, parasites

Resistance detection: Identifies resistance genes in organisms

Taxonomic identification: Specific species identification for ambiguous organisms

Hospital-adapted microbiomes: Can identify atypical organisms in specific hospital environments

Time to result: 24–48 hours (faster with expedited protocols; 12–18 hours in some centers)

Clinical application:

Particularly useful in cases of:

Negative conventional cultures (empiric coverage needed but organism unclear)

Unusual organisms

Polymicrobial infections (can identify all organisms simultaneously)

Limitations:

Cost: $500–2,000 per test (expensive)

Bioinformatic complexity: Requires specialized interpretation

Still slow: 24–48 hours not fast enough for acute decision-making

Contamination issues: Can identify lab/skin contaminants, not just pathogens

Current status: Emerging in specialized centers; not yet standard care but increasingly used

### Point-of-Care Lactate and Blood Gas Analyzers

Portable devices:

Small, handheld devices measuring:

Lactate

Blood pH, pCO₂, pO₂

Electrolytes (K⁺, Na⁺, Cl⁻)

Glucose

Advantages:

Bedside measurement

Results within 1–2 minutes

Enables real-time decision-making

Limitations:

Less accurate than laboratory analyzers (±0.5 mmol/L error)

Requires calibration and maintenance

Limited to small volume of tests per device

Clinical use: Increasingly used in ICUs, EDs, OR for rapid assessment

### 9.6 Integration of Biomarkers into Decision Algorithms

### Multi-Biomarker Approach: Synergistic Information

Single biomarkers are insufficient. Rather, integrative panels provide more complete assessment:

### The "Sepsis Immune Response Profile"

Proposed integrated assessment:

| Parameter | Normal | Early Sepsis | Late Sepsis | Interpretation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lactate | <2 | 2–4 | May be high or normal | Tissue perfusion; need for escalation |
| PCT | <0.05 | 2–10 | Elevated or declining | Bacterial infection ongoing or resolving |
| CRP | <10 | 50–200 | 100–300+ | Inflammation; may persist despite improving infection |
| HLA-DR | 95–100% | 50–80% | <30% | Immune function; candidate for immunostimulation if <30% |
| Lymphocyte count | 1,500–4,500 | Low-normal to low | Very low (<500) | Lymphopenia; apoptosis ongoing |
| Platelet count | 150–400 | Low-normal to low | Low | DIC risk; coagulopathy |

### Clinical Decision Algorithm Using Biomarkers

Hour 0 (Recognition):

Obtain: Lactate, blood cultures, CBC, CRP, PCT

If lactate >4 mmol/L: Indicates severe tissue hypoperfusion; need aggressive resuscitation

If PCT >2 ng/mL: Bacterial infection likely; initiate broad-spectrum antibiotics

Hour 6 (Re-assessment):

Repeat lactate

If lactate clearance <10%: Inadequate resuscitation response; escalate:

Increase vasopressor

Consider inotrope addition

Reassess source control

If lactate clearance ≥10%: Resuscitation adequate; continue current approach

Day 1 (Source Control Assessment):

Measure HLA-DR (if available)

If HLA-DR >50%: Adequate immune function; do not give immunosuppressive agents

If HLA-DR <30%: Profound immunosuppression; consider:

Checkpoint inhibitor therapy (anti-PD-1)

IL-7 therapy

Reassess source control adequacy

Day 3 (De-escalation):

Repeat PCT

If PCT declined >50% from peak: Consider de-escalation:

Narrow antibiotic spectrum based on cultures

Plan for antimicrobial discontinuation in 1 week

If PCT plateaued or rising: Reassess source control; escalate or broaden therapy

Day 7–10 (Outcome Prediction):

Lactate normal, CRP declining, PCT normal: Good prognosis

Lactate persistently elevated, CRP plateaued: Reassess for:

Inadequate source control

Secondary infection

Organ failure

### Integration with Clinical Assessment

Biomarkers do not replace clinical judgment; they augment it:

Clinical examination remains critical:

Wound assessment: Visual inspection for signs of infection, healing

Hemodynamic signs: Skin perfusion, urine output, mental status

Organ assessment: Breath sounds, abdominal examination, neurologic status

Biomarkers fill gaps in clinical assessment:

Occult sepsis: Patient appears well but biomarkers indicate severe infection

Persistent infection: Clinically improving but biomarkers show ongoing infection

Immune status: Cannot be assessed clinically; requires biomarker measurement

### Summary: Biomarkers as Tools for Sepsis Management

Modern sepsis management integrates multiple biomarkers to:

Confirm diagnosis: Elevated lactate, PCT, CRP indicate systemic infection

Assess severity: Lactate >4, PCT >10, HLA-DR <30% indicate severe disease

Guide resuscitation: Lactate clearance indicates adequacy of fluid/vasopressor therapy

Predict outcomes: Elevated baseline biomarkers predict poor outcomes

Guide antimicrobial duration: PCT clearance enables early de-escalation

Select immunotherapy: HLA-DR guides immunostimulation in profound immunosuppression

Assess organ dysfunction: Organ-specific miRNAs and cfDNA indicate tissue damage

Key principles:

No single biomarker is sufficient for all decisions

Serial measurement (trending) more important than single values

Integration with clinical assessment essential

Rapid diagnostics enable faster organism identification and de-escalation

The next chapter addresses the practical implementation of these biomarkers within the first critical hour of sepsis recognition, where time is most precious and every minute counts.

### CHAPTER 10: MICROBIOLOGICAL DIAGNOSIS AND ANTIMICROBIAL STEWARDSHIP

### 10.1 Blood Cultures: Timing, Collection, and Interpretation

### The Critical Role of Blood Cultures in Sepsis Diagnosis

Blood cultures serve dual purposes:

Microbiological diagnosis: Identifies causative organism

Antimicrobial susceptibility testing: Guides targeted therapy selection

Fundamental principle: Blood cultures must be obtained before antimicrobial administration. Post-antibiotic cultures are far less likely to be positive, delaying organism identification and antimicrobial guidance.

### Timing and Collection Protocol

### Pre-Antibiotic Timing

Gold standard:

Obtain blood cultures BEFORE antibiotics whenever possible

Delays antimicrobial administration by minutes (not hours)

Dramatically increases culture positivity rate

Timing in practice:

Ideal: Obtain cultures within 5–10 minutes of sepsis recognition

Acceptable: Obtain cultures within 15–30 minutes

Unacceptable: Delay >1 hour; by then, antecedent antibiotics likely given

Clinical reality:

In modern practice, obtaining cultures before antibiotics is often challenging

Emergency departments may administer antibiotics before cultures are drawn

Solution: Brief (<15 minute) delay in antibiotic administration to obtain cultures is acceptable if cultures can be drawn expeditiously

However: If obtaining cultures would delay antibiotics >15–20 minutes, administer antibiotics first and obtain cultures immediately

### Collection Technique

Proper technique is critical:

Skin contamination is the most common source of false positives

Contamination rates 10–20% if improper technique used

Contamination rates <1% with meticulous technique

Step-by-step protocol:

Patient preparation:

Identify patient identity (label everything correctly)

Note time of collection

Aseptic site preparation (critical step):

Chlorhexidine (preferred): 0.5–4% in alcohol

Effective against gram-positive and gram-negative organisms

Rapid action

OR povidone-iodine: 10% solution

Effective but slower to act than chlorhexidine

OR alcohol prep: 70% ethanol

Acceptable if chlorhexidine/iodine contraindicated

Technique:

Cleanse skin in concentric circles from center outward

Cover ~4-inch diameter area

Allow to dry completely (at least 30 seconds)

Critical: Do NOT re-palpate vein after skin preparation (recontaminates site)

Venipuncture:

Use sterile needle (typically 18–20 gauge)

Puncture skin cleanly with single pass

Collect 10–20 mL total blood volume

Do NOT touch collection site with gloved hand

Bottle inoculation:

Standard practice: Inoculate both aerobic and anaerobic bottles

Volume per bottle: Typically 5–10 mL per bottle (institution-dependent)

Order of inoculation: Some recommend aerobic first (prevents air entry into anaerobic bottle); others anaerobic first to maximize anaerobe recovery

Technique: Disinfect bottle tops with alcohol before inoculation

Cap: Do NOT recap needle after collection; use safety mechanism to minimize needle sticks

Bottle handling:

Temperature: Keep at room temperature (not refrigerated; cold impairs organism recovery)

Transportation: Transport to lab within 30 minutes

Incubation: Modern automated systems incubate bottles continuously; monitor for growth

### Multiple Culture Sets

Standard recommendation: Obtain 2–3 culture sets (each set = one aerobic + one anaerobic bottle pair)

Rationale:

Increases sensitivity: If organism is intermittently bacteremic, multiple sets increase likelihood of capture

Distinguishes contamination from true bacteremia:

True pathogen: Present in multiple sets

Contaminant: Usually present in only one set

Organism quantitation: Multiple positive sets suggest more robust bacteremia than single positive

Timing of multiple sets:

Simultaneously collected: All sets drawn from different sites at same time (preferred if multiple IV accesses available)

Sequential collection: Sets obtained minutes apart (acceptable; increases chance of capturing organism if bacteremia is intermittent)

NOT sequential from same line: Drawing from same IV catheter contaminates all samples; defeats purpose of multiple sets

### Special Populations

Patients on antibiotics:

If patient has already received antibiotics, obtain cultures anyway

Culture positivity may be reduced, but organism identification still valuable

If cultures negative but strong clinical suspicion, may infer organism based on clinical context

Immunocompromised patients:

May have unusual organisms or organisms not typically considered pathogenic

Comprehensive culture protocol especially important

Consider fungi and atypical organisms

Fungal sepsis:

Standard blood cultures may be negative for fungi (low sensitivity)

Consider:

Fungal blood cultures: Specialized media improving fungal recovery

T2 Candida: Rapid PCR-based identification of Candida from standard culture bottles

Molecular diagnostics: mNGS for comprehensive coverage

### Culture Processing and Interpretation

### Growth Detection

Automated systems:

BACTEC, VersaTREK: Automated incubation and monitoring systems

Continuous monitoring: Detect growth in real-time as organisms metabolize and produce CO₂

Positive threshold: Typically positive after 6–48 hours of incubation

Time to positivity: Faster for rapidly growing organisms (Staph aureus 6–12 hours); slower for fastidious organisms (Streptococcus 12–24 hours)

Gram stain of positive culture:

Preliminary identification: Once culture flagged positive, Gram stain performed

Morphology assessment: Gram-positive cocci, gram-negative rods, etc.

Allows provisional identification within 1–2 hours of culture positivity

Enables de-escalation in some cases (e.g., gram-positive cocci in clusters = Staph aureus likely)

Organism identification methods:

MALDI-TOF: 1–2 hours after growth

16S rRNA: 24 hours

DNA probe: 24 hours

### Susceptibility Testing

Timing:

### Performed after organism identification

Time to result: 24–48 hours typically

Rapid susceptibility: Some labs offer rush susceptibility testing (4–6 hours) for critical organisms

Methods:

Disk diffusion (Kirby-Bauer): Traditional; measures zone of inhibition around antibiotic disk

Microdilution (EUCAST/CLSI): Quantitates minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC)

Automated systems: E-test, Phoenix, others

Broth microdilution: Gold standard; most accurate

Interpretation:

Susceptible (S): Organism inhibited at therapeutic drug concentrations

Intermediate (I): Organism inhibited at high drug concentrations; clinical efficacy variable

Resistant (R): Organism not inhibited at achievable drug concentrations

### Interpretation in Clinical Context

Positive blood culture:

True bacteremia: Organism is causative pathogen

Contamination: Common organisms causing contamination:

Coagulase-negative Staphylococcus (Staph epidermidis)

### Corynebacterium species

### Propionibacterium acnes

### Alpha-hemolytic Streptococcus (oral flora)

Distinguishing contamination from true bacteremia:

| Feature | True Bacteremia | Contaminant |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Number of sets positive | Multiple (2–3 sets) | Single set |
| Time to positivity | Rapid (6–12 hours) | Variable |
| Organism type | Known pathogens | Common skin flora |
| Clinical correlation | Sepsis present; fever, shock | May be absent; patient well |
| Patient demographics | Any | Often immunocompromised |

Clinical principle: If single set positive with common skin flora AND patient clinically well, likely contamination. If multiple sets positive OR patient septic, likely true bacteremia.

Negative blood cultures:

In septic patient: Does NOT exclude sepsis

Possible explanations:

Intermittent bacteremia: Organism only periodically in blood

Localized infection: Bacteria confined to tissue; bloodstream seeding not yet occurred

Antibiotics given before culture: Kills organisms before they can grow in culture

Organism fastidious: Requires special media

Fungal or unusual organism: May not grow in standard media

Technical failure: Improper collection/processing

Management with negative cultures:

Do not stop antibiotics: Continue broad-spectrum coverage if clinical sepsis evident

Obtain source cultures: Culture from presumed infection site (wound, CSF, urine)

Repeat blood cultures: May be positive on re-attempt

Consider molecular diagnostics: mNGS or PCR panels if available

De-escalation: If cultures remain negative and clinical picture suggests non-bacterial cause (viral, fungal), may adjust coverage accordingly

### 10.2 Rapid Molecular Diagnostics: FilmArray, T2 Candida, mNGS

### FilmArray Blood Culture ID Panel

### Technology and Methodology

Polymerase chain reaction (PCR)-based identification:

Amplifies specific DNA sequences characteristic of organisms

Multiplexed PCR: Single reaction identifies 25 organisms simultaneously

Real-time detection: Fluorescence indicates organism presence

Organisms identified (varies by panel version):

Gram-positive bacteria: Staph aureus (including MRSA), Staph epidermidis, Enterococcus faecalis, Enterococcus faecium, Streptococcus pyogenes, Streptococcus pneumoniae, Listeria monocytogenes

Gram-negative bacteria: E. coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Acinetobacter baumannii, Enterobacter cloacae, Serratia marcescens

Fungi: Candida albicans, C. auris, C. glabrata, C. parapsilosis, Aspergillus fumigatus

Antibiotic resistance markers identified:

MRSA: mecA gene (methicillin resistance)

VRE: vanA/vanB genes (vancomycin resistance in Enterococcus)

ESBL: blaSHV, blaCTX-M genes (extended-spectrum beta-lactamase)

### Clinical Workflow and Impact

Workflow:

Blood culture positive on automated system (6–48 hours after inoculation)

Gram stain obtained (1–2 hours)

FilmArray run: 1 hour processing time

Result to clinician: ~2–3 hours after culture positivity

Total time from blood draw: Typically 12–24 hours from initial sepsis presentation

Clinical impact:

Early de-escalation: Once organism identified, can narrow antibiotic coverage before susceptibilities known

Example: If Staph aureus identified (non-MRSA):

Can de-escalate from vancomycin + cephalosporin to oxacillin/nafcillin monotherapy

Prevents unnecessary broad coverage

Antifungal initiation: Candida identification permits antifungal switch from empiric coverage

Duration: ~12–24 hours earlier than conventional identification methods

### Limitations

Sensitivity/Specificity:

Generally >95% for organisms in panel

Organisms not in panel: Not detected (so-called "negative" result)

Polymicrobial infections: Typically identifies only most abundant organism; may miss minority organisms

Cost:

Expensive (~$400–800 per test)

Not universally available

Resistance marker limitations:

Identifies common resistance genes

May miss unusual resistance mechanisms

Requires follow-up conventional susceptibility testing

### T2 Candida Panel

### Technology and Clinical Context

Candida sepsis problem:

Candida is common cause of healthcare-associated sepsis

Antifungal therapy is expensive and has adverse effects

Early initiation improves outcomes

But distinguishing Candida from bacterial contamination is difficult

T2 Candida Panel:

Rapid identification of Candida species from blood

Time to result: 3–5 hours after culture positivity

Organisms identified: Candida albicans, C. glabrata, C. auris, C. parapsilosis, C. tropicalis, C. krusei, C. auris, others

### Mechanism

T2 magnetic resonance (T2MR) technology:

Paramagnetic particles coated with Candida-specific antibodies

When Candida present, particles agglutinate

Agglutination changes magnetic resonance properties

Detected by T2MR device

Result obtained without need for culture growth

### Clinical Impact

Advantages:

Ultra-rapid: 3–5 hours vs 24–48 hours for conventional identification

High sensitivity/specificity: >95%

Early antifungal initiation: Permits switch from empiric antibiotic-antifungal combinations to antifungal monotherapy in Candida sepsis

Clinical outcome:

Reduces unnecessary antibiotics in non-Candida infections

Reduces unnecessary antifungals in bacterial infections

Expedites appropriate therapy

### Limitations

Only Candida:

Does not identify other fungi (Aspergillus, Zygomycetes)

Does not identify bacteria

Requires separate bacterial identification

Cost: Similar to FilmArray (~$400–800)

### Metagenomic Next-Generation Sequencing (mNGS)

### Comprehensive Organism Detection

Unbiased approach:

Sequences all nucleic acid material in sample (bacterial, fungal, viral, parasitic)

Does not require prior knowledge of organism

Can identify essentially any organism present

Advantages:

Comprehensive: Single test covers bacteria, fungi, viruses, parasites

Organism detection without culture: Identifies unculturable organisms

Resistance gene identification: Detects resistance-encoding genes

Hospital microbiome profiling: Identifies patterns of organism colonization

### Workflow and Timeline

Sample processing:

Blood sample received

DNA extraction

Library preparation

Sequencing (hundreds of thousands to millions of reads)

Bioinformatic analysis: Mapping reads to reference genomes

Time to result: 24–48 hours (expedited protocols 12–18 hours in specialized centers)

Bioinformatic interpretation:

Complex; requires specialized expertise

Must distinguish:

True pathogens: Organisms present in clinically significant quantities

Contaminants: Lab contaminants or environmental organisms

Colonizers: Organisms present but not causing infection

### Clinical Applications

Challenging cases:

Negative conventional cultures with sepsis:

Patient septic; conventional cultures negative

mNGS can identify causative organism

Guide targeted therapy

Unusual organisms:

Atypical presentation; organism unclear

mNGS provides comprehensive identification

Polymicrobial infections:

mNGS identifies all organisms simultaneously

Conventional culture prioritizes most abundant

Suspected contamination:

mNGS can distinguish environmental contaminant from pathogen based on organism type and quantity

### Limitations and Current Status

Limitations:

Cost: $1,000–3,000 per test (expensive)

Time to result: 24–48 hours (faster than conventional culture but slower than FilmArray/T2)

Bioinformatic complexity: Requires expert interpretation

Quantitation challenges: Cannot reliably quantitate organism load; RNA-seq could address but less standardized

Not standard of care: Most hospitals do not have mNGS capability

Current status:

Research tool with emerging clinical applications

Being used in specialized centers for difficult diagnostic cases

Increasing availability; cost declining

Not yet standard for routine sepsis workup

Future potential:

As cost decreases and standardized interpretation protocols develop, may become first-line for blood culture-based diagnosis

Could replace conventional cultures in some settings

### 10.3 Source Identification and Culture Site Selection

### Obtaining Source Cultures in Sepsis

Principle: Culture from the presumed source of infection in addition to blood cultures.

Rationale:

Source cultures may identify organism before bloodstream seeding

Organisms at source may differ from bloodstream organisms

Source control may require culture guidance

### Source Culture Sites and Techniques

Urinary tract source:

Specimen: Catheterized urine or clean-catch midstream

Avoid: Urinary bag specimens (contaminated)

Culture technique: Standard urine culture (quantitative)

Interpretation: >10⁵ CFU/mL in symptomatic patient = bacteriuria; >10² CFU/mL in asymptomatic patient on catheter = catheter colonization

Lower respiratory tract source:

Specimen options:

Endotracheal tube aspirate (ETA): If intubated; semi-quantitative

Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL): More invasive; obtained via bronchoscopy; quantitative cultures more reliable

Sputum: If patient can expectorate; highly contaminated by oral flora

Culture technique: Semi-quantitative or quantitative depending on specimen

Interpretation: >10⁴ CFU/mL ETA or >10³ CFU/mL BAL suggests pneumonia

Wound/soft tissue source:

Specimen: Tissue biopsy or needle aspiration (avoid superficial swabs)

Technique: Send for aerobic, anaerobic, fungal cultures

Important: Do NOT culture surface; culture deep tissue to avoid surface flora contamination

Gram stain: Should be obtained immediately for rapid organism assessment

Intra-abdominal source:

Specimen: Fluid aspirated during surgical exploration or percutaneous drainage

Technique: Culture aerobic, anaerobic, fungal

Gram stain: Critical for guiding initial debridement decisions

Quantitation: Not typically done for fluids

Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) (in suspected meningitis):

Specimen: CSF obtained via lumbar puncture (or ventricular puncture if intraventricular catheter present)

Cultures: Aerobic, anaerobic (anaerobes rare in meningitis but sometimes present with anaerobic bacteremia)

Cell count and chemistry: Glucose, protein, lactate

Gram stain: Critical for rapid organism identification in meningitis

PCR panels: Rapid identification of common meningitis pathogens

Bile (in biliary source):

Specimen: Bile obtained during percutaneous drainage or operative drainage

Culture: Aerobic, anaerobic

Polymicrobial: Biliary infections typically polymicrobial with GI flora

Bone (in osteomyelitis):

Specimen: Bone biopsy or aspirate from affected area

Technique: Sterile needle aspiration or tissue biopsy

Culture: Aerobic, anaerobic, fungal

Gram stain: Often disappointing; low organism burden in some cases

### Collection Technique Principles

Sterility is critical:

Skin antisepsis before percutaneous aspiration/biopsy

Sterile technique during collection

Use sterile containers and transport media

Avoid surface contamination:

Culture deep tissue, not surface

Swabs highly contaminated; tissue biopsy/needle aspiration preferred

Volume matters:

Adequate volume required for organism recovery

Minimum: 0.5 mL for fluid; 1–2 g for tissue

Media selection:

Use appropriate transport media

Anaerobic organisms require anaerobic transport

Room temperature transport preferred (some organisms temperature-sensitive)

### 10.4 Resistance Mechanisms and Phenotypic Testing

### Understanding Antimicrobial Resistance

### Major Resistance Mechanisms

Beta-lactamase production:

Mechanism: Enzymatic degradation of beta-lactam antibiotics

Organisms: Staphylococcus aureus (produces penicillinase), gram-negative organisms (produce various beta-lactamases)

Types:

TEM: Common in gram-negative; inhibited by beta-lactamase inhibitors

SHV: Common in gram-negative; inhibited by some beta-lactamase inhibitors

CTX-M: Extended-spectrum beta-lactamase (ESBL); emerging globally; causes resistance to 3rd generation cephalosporins

Carbapenemase: Resistance to carbapenems; most broad-spectrum beta-lactams

Methicillin resistance (MRSA):

Mechanism: Altered penicillin-binding proteins (PBPs)

Organism: Staph aureus

Clinical significance: Resistant to ALL beta-lactams (including carbapenems)

Detection: mecA gene presence or oxacillin disk diffusion test

Vancomycin resistance:

Mechanisms: Altered cell wall synthesis, reduced glycopeptide binding

Organisms:

VRE (Vancomycin-resistant Enterococcus): vanA/vanB genes

VRSA (Vancomycin-resistant Staph aureus): Rare; vanA gene acquisition

Clinical significance: Eliminates glycopeptide options; must use alternatives

Fluoroquinolone resistance:

Mechanisms: DNA gyrase mutations, efflux pump upregulation

Organisms: Pseudomonas, E. coli, others

Detection: Fluoroquinolone disk diffusion

CarbapenEM resistance:

Mechanisms: Carbapenemase production (KPC, OXA, IMP), reduced permeability

Organisms: Carbapenem-resistant Enterobacteriaceae (CRE), carbapenem-resistant Pseudomonas (CRPA), carbapenem-resistant Acinetobacter (CRAB)

Clinical significance: Limits beta-lactam options; may require colistin, tigecycline, or combinations

Detection: Carbapenem disk diffusion, carbapenemase gene PCR

Colistin resistance:

Mechanisms: Lipopolysaccharide modification

Organisms: Usually last-resort organism resistance (E. coli, Acinetobacter)

Clinical significance: Severely limits options; often requires combination therapy

Detection: Colistin disk diffusion

### Phenotypic Testing Methods

Disk diffusion (Kirby-Bauer):

Method: Antibiotic-impregnated disk placed on organism-seeded agar plate

Principle: Antibiotic diffuses into agar; creates zone of inhibition proportional to organism susceptibility

Interpretation: Zone diameter determines S/I/R categorization

Advantages: Inexpensive, widely available

Disadvantages: Requires growth; relatively crude (categorical rather than quantitative)

Broth microdilution:

Method: Serial dilutions of antibiotic in broth; organism inoculated into each dilution

Principle: Organism growth or lack thereof indicates minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC)

Interpretation: MIC compared to breakpoints (CLSI, EUCAST) to determine S/I/R

Advantages: Quantitative; gold standard

Disadvantages: Time-consuming; expensive

Automated systems:

Examples: Vitek, Phoenix, MicroScan

Method: Organism inoculated into proprietary cartridges containing antibiotic gradients

Principle: Automated growth detection

Results: Typically 4–24 hours

Advantages: Fast, reproducible, standardized

Disadvantages: Expensive equipment and cartridges

E-test:

Method: Antibiotic gradient strip placed on organism-seeded agar

Principle: MIC read from where strip crosses zone of inhibition

Advantages: Quick quantitative method

Disadvantages: Somewhat tedious; requires experience for accurate reading

### Resistance Detection Specific to Common Organisms

MRSA detection:

Oxacillin disk: If resistant to oxacillin, MRSA confirmed

Meckit: Latex agglutination test for PBP2a (product of mecA gene)

PCR for mecA: Gold standard but not routine

ESBL detection:

Indicator: Resistance to 3rd generation cephalosporin (ceftriaxone, cefotaxime) with susceptibility to extended-spectrum beta-lactamase inhibitor combination (ceftazidime-clavulanic acid)

Confirmatory test: ESBL confirmation test per CLSI/EUCAST

Clinical significance: Indicates broad-spectrum resistance; limits beta-lactam options

Carbapenem resistance:

Disk diffusion: Meropenem or ertapenem disk

Modified Hodge test: Detects carbapenemase production

PCR for carbapenemase genes: Gold standard but not routine

Vancomycin MIC in Staph aureus:

Heteroresistant VISA/VISA: MIC at high end of susceptible range (2–4 μg/mL) or intermediate range (8 μg/mL)

Significance: Clinical failures reported with high MICs; may warrant alternative to vancomycin

Detection: Requires E-test or broth microdilution (not disk diffusion)

### 10.5 De-Escalation Protocols: Duration and Individualization

### The Principle of De-Escalation

De-escalation is the process of narrowing antimicrobial spectrum once culture results and organism susceptibilities are known.

Rationale:

Reduce toxicity: Narrower-spectrum agents generally have fewer adverse effects

Reduce cost: Narrower-spectrum agents typically less expensive

Reduce resistance selection: Broad-spectrum agents select for resistant organisms

Maintain efficacy: Targeted therapy should be as effective as broad-spectrum for susceptible organisms

### De-Escalation Algorithms

Timeline:

Hour 0: Start broad-spectrum empiric therapy

Hour 24–48: Preliminary organism identification (Gram stain, FilmArray if available)

Hour 48–72: Definitive organism identification and initial susceptibilities

Day 3–5: Complete susceptibility panel and organism confirmation

De-escalation decision point: Typically Day 2–3 when preliminary results available

De-escalation workflow:

Identify organism:

Example: Gram-positive cocci in clusters = Staph aureus

Determine if MRSA or MSSA:

If MRSA suspected: Continue vancomycin or add alternative

If MSSA (likely): De-escalate to oxacillin/nafcillin or cephalosporin

Assess gram-negative coverage:

If gram-negative identified:

Determine if ESBL, carbapenem-resistant, etc.

Select targeted agent

Assess anaerobic coverage necessity:

If source anaerobic (biliary, female genital tract source): Maintain anaerobic coverage

If source purely aerobic: Can discontinue anaerobic coverage

Assess fungal coverage:

If source does NOT suggest fungal: Discontinue antifungal

If Candida isolated: Switch to appropriate antifungal (fluconazole if susceptible; echinocandin if fluconazole-resistant)

### De-Escalation Examples

### Example 1: Gram-positive cocci + gram-negative rods from pneumonia source

Initial broad-spectrum: Vancomycin + ceftazidime + clindamycin + antifungal

Day 2 results: Gram stain shows gram-positive cocci (clusters) and gram-negative rods (few)

Preliminary assessment: Likely Staph aureus (primary) + gram-negative co-pathogen

Day 3 FilmArray: Staph aureus (MSSA) + gram-negative (not identified yet)

De-escalation decision:

Continue ceftazidime (covers both Staph aureus and gram-negative)

Discontinue vancomycin (not needed for MSSA)

Discontinue clindamycin (redundant)

Discontinue antifungal (respiratory source, not fungal)

New regimen: Ceftazidime monotherapy

Day 5 full susceptibilities: Staph aureus susceptible to oxacillin; gram-negative susceptible to multiple agents

Final de-escalation: Switch to ceftriaxone (beta-lactam with good coverage)

### Example 2: Intra-abdominal infection

Initial broad-spectrum: Piperacillin-tazobactam + vancomycin

Day 2 results: Gram stain shows gram-negative rods and gram-positive cocci (mixed); anaerobes likely (mixed flora)

Day 3 culture results: E. coli (ESBL-producing), Enterococcus, Bacteroides

De-escalation assessment:

E. coli ESBL: Requires carbapenem or other option

Enterococcus: Susceptible to ampicillin (typically)

Bacteroides: Susceptible to most broad-spectrum agents

Anaerobic coverage: Essential (Bacteroides present)

De-escalation decision:

Switch to meropenem (covers E. coli ESBL, Enterococcus, Bacteroides)

Discontinue vancomycin (not needed; ampicillin adequate for Enterococcus)

Final regimen: Meropenem monotherapy

### Duration of Antimicrobial Therapy

Determinants of duration:

| Factor | Impact | Considerations |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Source | Primary determinant | GI source 7–10 days; meningitis 10–14 days |
| Organism type | Modifies duration | Pseudomonas often requires longer therapy |
| Immunosuppression | Prolongs therapy | HIV with low CD4: longer courses |
| Clinical response | Guides decision | Rapid improvement: standard duration; slow: consider longer |
| Repeat cultures | Assess clearance | Persistent positive cultures warrant longer therapy |

Standard durations by source:

| Source | Typical Duration |
| --- | --- |
| Pneumonia | 7–10 days (shorter if rapid improvement) |
| UTI | 7–14 days |
| Biliary | 7–10 days (after drainage) |
| Peritonitis | 7–10 days (after source control) |
| Meningitis | 10–14 days |
| Endocarditis | 4–6 weeks |
| Osteomyelitis | 4–6 weeks |
| Prosthetic graft infection | 6 weeks minimum |
| Diabetic foot osteomyelitis | 4–6 weeks |

Exceptions requiring longer courses:

Immunocompromised: May require longer therapy

Persistent bacteremia: Longer therapy warranted

Inadequate source control: Cannot shorten course if source not controlled

Slow clinical response: If fever persists despite antibiotics, culture-directed longer course warranted

### Biomarker-Guided De-Escalation

PCT-guided protocol (as discussed in Chapter 9):

Measure baseline PCT

Repeat at Day 2–3

If PCT declined >50% AND clinical improvement: De-escalate or discontinue therapy

If PCT plateaued or rising: Continue or escalate therapy

Lactate-guided protocol:

If lactate normalized and clinical improvement: Consider de-escalation

If lactate persistently elevate

Continue

30 дек. 2025 г.

: Reassess source control before de-escalating

### Summary: Microbiological Diagnosis and Antimicrobial Stewardship

Modern antimicrobial management requires:

Rapid, high-quality cultures: Before antibiotics whenever possible

Rapid organism identification: Utilizing molecular diagnostics (FilmArray, T2, mNGS)

Early de-escalation: Based on organism identification, not waiting for full susceptibilities

Source-specific therapy: Culture from presumed source to guide coverage

Duration individualization: Guided by organism, source, clinical response, biomarkers

Surveillance for resistance: Institutional antibiogram to guide empiric therapy selection

Antimicrobial stewardship principles:

Start broad, narrow fast

Target source-identified organisms

Use shortest effective duration

Monitor for treatment failure or adverse effects

Educate team on resistance patterns

The integration of rapid diagnostics with biomarker-guided decision-making enables shorter courses of narrower-spectrum therapy, reducing resistance selection and adverse effects while maintaining efficacy.

This completes the diagnostics section. The next natural progression would be to Chapter 11 (The First Hour—Golden Hour Protocol), which synthesizes all diagnostic and initial therapeutic information into a practical, time-based protocol for the most critical period of sepsis management.
