### CHAPTER 11: THE FIRST HOUR—GOLDEN HOUR PROTOCOL AND ORGANIZATIONAL SYSTEMS

### 11.1 Recognition and Triage: qSOFA, SOFA, and Sequential Assessment

### The Challenge of Early Sepsis Recognition

Fundamental problem: Sepsis does not announce itself with a single pathognomonic sign. Rather, it presents with nonspecific symptoms (fever, malaise, altered mental status) that could represent dozens of conditions.

Clinical reality:

Average time to sepsis recognition: 2–4 hours from hospital arrival

Average time to antibiotics: 3–6 hours from hospital arrival

Golden hour concept: First 60 minutes are critical; every hour of delay increases mortality

Delayed recognition consequences: Progression through inflammatory cascade without intervention; irreversible organ damage accumulates

### qSOFA Screening: The Bedside Tool

qSOFA (quick SOFA) score is designed for rapid screening of patients at risk for sepsis in non-ICU settings (emergency department, ward).

### qSOFA Components

Three components, each worth 1 point:

Altered mental status:

Glasgow Coma Scale <15

OR acute change in consciousness

OR disorientation/confusion

Respiratory rate ≥22 breaths/minute:

Elevated from baseline

Measured over 1 full minute

Systolic blood pressure ≤100 mmHg:

Hypotension

OR acute drop from baseline

### qSOFA Interpretation

Score ≥2: High risk for adverse outcomes

Sensitivity: 65–80% for identifying patients at risk of mortality

Specificity: 40–60% for predicting mortality

Clinical application:

qSOFA ≥2 → Trigger sepsis workup and probable antibiotic initiation

qSOFA <2 → Do NOT exclude sepsis (qSOFA has low sensitivity; negative score does not rule out sepsis)

### Critical Caveat: qSOFA Is NOT Diagnostic

Fundamental point: qSOFA ≥2 identifies risk, not diagnosis.

False positives:

Patient with acute pulmonary embolism: Hypoxia → tachypnea; hypotension; altered mental status → qSOFA ≥2

Patient with acute myocardial infarction: Hypotension; chest pain; anxiety → qSOFA may be ≥2

Patient with intracranial hemorrhage: Altered mental status → qSOFA ≥2

False negatives:

Patient with early sepsis (hour 0–6): May have only fever; qSOFA may be <2

Patient with sepsis from surgical source: May not yet show systemic signs

Patient with localized infection: qSOFA <2 despite bacteremia developing

### SOFA Scoring: The Diagnostic Tool

Once qSOFA triggers concern, proceed to SOFA assessment for diagnostic confirmation.

### SOFA Components and Scoring

Detailed in Chapter 1; brief recap:

| Organ System | Score 0 | Score 1 | Score 2 | Score 3 | Score 4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Respiratory (PaO₂/FiO₂) | ≥400 | 300–399 | 200–299 | 100–199 | <100 |
| Coagulation (Platelets) | ≥150 | 100–149 | 50–99 | 20–49 | <20 |
| Liver (Bilirubin) | <1.2 | 1.2–1.9 | 2.0–5.9 | 6.0–11.9 | >12 |
| Cardiovascular (MAP or vasopressor) | MAP ≥70 | MAP <70, no vasopressor | MAP <70, dopamine ≤5 or dobutamine | MAP <70, dopamine >5 or norepinephrine ≤0.1 | MAP <70, dopamine >15 or norepinephrine >0.1 |
| CNS (GCS) | 15 | 13–14 | 10–12 | 6–9 | <6 |
| Renal (Creatinine or UOP) | <1.2 | 1.2–1.9 | 2.0–3.4 | 3.5–4.9 or <500 mL/day | >5.0 or <200 mL/day |

### SOFA Calculation Workflow

### Step 1: Obtain baseline SOFA (if possible)

From medical record or patient history

If patient has known chronic organ dysfunction (cirrhosis, COPD, CKD), baseline SOFA may be elevated

If baseline unknown, assume baseline SOFA = 0

### Step 2: Calculate acute SOFA

Use current clinical and laboratory data

If some values not yet available (e.g., blood gas not obtained), use worst-case assumption (e.g., assume PaO₂/FiO₂ low)

### Step 3: Calculate SOFA delta

Acute SOFA - Baseline SOFA = SOFA delta

Sepsis diagnostic criterion: SOFA delta ≥2

### Step 4: Assess vasopressor requirement

If systolic BP <90 mmHg or MAP <65 mmHg:

Is this despite adequate fluid resuscitation (≥30 mL/kg crystalloid)?

If yes, are vasopressors being administered?

If yes, document dose

Septic shock criterion: Vasopressor requirement to maintain MAP ≥65 mmHg + lactate ≥2 mmol/L

### SOFA Trajectory and Prognosis

Serial SOFA assessments (every 24 hours) have prognostic value:

| SOFA Trend | Implication | Outcome |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Improving (decreasing) | Good response to therapy; source controlled | Better prognosis; ICU mortality <5% if rapid improvement |
| Static (plateauing) | Neither improving nor worsening; mixed picture | Intermediate prognosis; reassess source control |
| Worsening (increasing) | Therapy inadequate; possible inadequate source control or new complications | Poor prognosis; ICU mortality >40% |

### 11.2 The 0–10 Minute Window: Identification and Initial Workup

### Recognition Phase: Identifying the Septic Patient

Triggers for suspicion of sepsis:

Patient presentation:

Fever (>38°C or <36°C)

Tachycardia (>90 bpm)

Tachypnea (>20 breaths/min)

Altered mental status

Hypotension

Recent surgery or invasive procedure

Known infection source (wound, UTI, etc.)

Clinical context:

Post-operative patient with fever

Immunocompromised patient with fever

Patient with indwelling catheters + fever

Diabetic with necrotizing soft tissue signs

Biomarker hints:

Elevated WBC (but can be normal or low in severe sepsis)

Elevated lactate (if measured)

Thrombocytopenia

Coagulopathy

### 0–1 Minute: Alert the Team

As soon as sepsis suspected:

Notify physician/provider: "We have a patient with suspected sepsis"

Initiate sepsis alert (if institutional system in place):

Trigger EHR sepsis alert

Notify ICU/critical care team

Notify infectious disease (if available)

Assemble team for protocol-driven care

Call for equipment:

Code cart (for emergency equipment access)

Blood culture bottles

IV supplies (large-bore peripheral IV, central line kit)

Blood draw supplies

ECG machine

Continuous monitoring equipment

Establish IV access:

Large-bore peripheral IV (18–20 gauge if possible)

Central venous catheter (if not already present and anticipated need for vasopressors)

Note time of IV placement

### 1–5 Minutes: Initial Assessment and Vital Signs

Rapid assessment:

Vital signs (critical):

Temperature (core if possible)

Heart rate

Blood pressure (both arms if possible)

Respiratory rate (count over full minute)

SpO₂ on room air

Quick physical examination:

HEENT: Signs of infection (pharyngitis, sinus tenderness)?

Chest: Breath sounds (pneumonia?); JVD (cardiogenic shock vs sepsis)?

Abdomen: Tenderness, peritoneal signs, distension?

Extremities: Rash, signs of necrotizing infection, cellulitis?

Skin: Cyanosis, mottling?

Neurologic: Mental status, GCS if altered

Calculate qSOFA:

Altered mental status? (Y/N)

RR ≥22? (Y/N)

SBP ≤100? (Y/N)

### qSOFA ≥2 → Proceed with sepsis protocol

Estimate organ dysfunction:

Urine output: Oliguria?

Respiratory: Hypoxia? Tachypnea?

Skin perfusion: Mottled, cool? (indication of hypoperfusion)

### 5–10 Minutes: Blood Draws and Initial Labs

### Critical: Obtain cultures BEFORE antibiotics

Blood cultures (as detailed in Chapter 10):

Two sets (aerobic + anaerobic bottles)

Separate sites if possible

Proper skin antisepsis (chlorhexidine preferred)

10–20 mL total per set

Label with date, time, site

Laboratory studies:

Complete blood count (CBC): WBC, hemoglobin, platelets

Comprehensive metabolic panel (CMP): Creatinine, glucose, bilirubin, transaminases, electrolytes

Lactate: Critical; indicates tissue hypoperfusion

Coagulation studies: PT/INR, aPTT, fibrinogen, D-dimer

Blood gas (arterial or venous): pH, pCO₂, pO₂, HCO₃

Troponin: If myocardial involvement suspected

Prothrombin time (PT): If coagulopathy suspected

Microbiological studies:

Blood cultures (as above)

Culture from suspected source:

If respiratory source: Sputum or BAL culture

If urinary source: Urinalysis + culture

If wound: Wound culture

If abdominal: Peritoneal fluid if available

Rapid testing:

Lactate point-of-care: If available, results within 1–2 minutes

Blood gas analysis: Results within 5–10 minutes

Blood glucose: Point-of-care glucose within 1 minute

Timing note:

Collect ALL blood samples from single IV stick if possible

Multiple sticks delay care and increase infection risk

Use luer-lock connectors to enable multiple tube draws from single access

### 11.3 The 10–30 Minute Window: Primary Interventions

### Antibiotic Initiation: The 1-Hour Target

### Goal: Initiate antibiotics within 60 minutes of sepsis recognition

Time accounting:

Minutes 0–10: Recognition and initial workup (above)

Minutes 10–30: Antibiotic administration (this section)

Minutes 30–60: Reassessment, resuscitation

### Antibiotic Selection: Empiric Coverage

Principle: Broad-spectrum empiric therapy, de-escalate based on cultures.

Empiric regimen must cover:

Gram-positive aerobes (Staph aureus including MRSA, Streptococcus)

Gram-negative aerobes (E. coli, Klebsiella, Pseudomonas)

Anaerobes (if GI or gynecologic source suspected)

Atypical organisms (if respiratory source suspected)

Common empiric regimens:

Option 1: Beta-lactam + beta-lactamase inhibitor monotherapy (preferred if not severely immunocompromised):

Piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6h (good gram-negative coverage; covers anaerobes)

OR Ampicillin-sulbactam 3 g IV q6h (narrower spectrum; acceptable for community-acquired sepsis)

Advantage: Monotherapy; simpler regimen

Disadvantage: May not cover MRSA (if suspected, add vancomycin)

Option 2: Cephalosporin + additional agents:

Ceftazidime 2 g IV q8h (3rd generation; covers Pseudomonas better than ceftriaxone)

PLUS Clindamycin 600 mg IV q6–8h (anaerobic + anti-toxin effects)

PLUS Vancomycin 15–20 mg/kg IV q8–12h (if MRSA risk)

Option 3: Fluoroquinolone-based (alternative if beta-lactam allergy):

Levofloxacin 750 mg IV q24h (respiratory + gram-negative)

PLUS Clindamycin (anaerobes)

PLUS Vancomycin (if MRSA risk)

Antifungal coverage (add if):

Severe immunosuppression (HIV with CD4 <50, recent chemotherapy)

High Candida colonization burden

Suspected fungal source

Agent: Fluconazole 400–600 mg IV q12h OR echinocandin (caspofungin 70 mg loading then 50 mg daily)

### Dosing Considerations

Maximize drug levels:

Use higher end of dosing range in sepsis

Septic patients have altered pharmacokinetics:

Increased volume of distribution (third-spacing from capillary leak)

Altered clearance (organ dysfunction)

Achieving therapeutic drug concentrations requires aggressive dosing

Dose examples:

Vancomycin: 15–20 mg/kg per dose (target AUC 400–600 μg·h/mL)

Piperacillin-tazobactam: 4.5 g q6h (not q8h; more frequent dosing preferred)

Ceftazidime: 2 g q8h (not lower doses)

Renal dosing: Do NOT reduce doses based on baseline creatinine in acute sepsis (assume rapidly changing renal function)

### Fluid Resuscitation: The 30 mL/kg Target

Conservative fluid strategy is now standard (differs from historical aggressive resuscitation).

### Crystalloid Selection and Volume

Crystalloid choice:

Balanced crystalloid preferred: Lactated Ringer's, Plasmalyte

Normal saline acceptable but associated with higher hyperchloremic acidosis risk

No benefit of colloids (albumin, gelatin) over crystalloid

Initial fluid bolus:

30 mL/kg crystalloid over first 1–3 hours

Example: 70 kg patient = 2,100 mL = ~2 L rapid IV infusion

Delivered through:

Large-bore peripheral IV (if available)

Central line (if placed)

Multiple peripheral IVs simultaneously (if necessary)

Route of administration:

IV push in syringe (rapid delivery) is acceptable

Pressure bag can accelerate infusion rate

Goal is rapid infusion, not slow drip

### Monitoring Fluid Response

Assessment at 10–15 minute intervals during initial resuscitation:

Hemodynamic response:

Is blood pressure improving? (target MAP ≥65 mmHg)

Is heart rate decreasing? (or stabilizing)

Is urine output improving? (target >0.5 mL/kg/hr)

Tissue perfusion markers:

Skin perfusion: Is mottling improving? Are extremities warming up?

Mental status: Is confusion improving?

Lactate clearance: If re-checked, has lactate decreased? (unlikely to change significantly in first 15 minutes but trend matters)

Signs of adequate fluid response:

Systolic BP >90 mmHg

MAP >65 mmHg

Urine output >0.5 mL/kg/hr

Improved mental status

Lactate trend downward

Signs of inadequate response (trigger for vasopressor consideration):

Persistent hypotension despite 30 mL/kg fluid

Persistent oliguria

Worsening mental status

Rising or plateaued lactate

→ Proceed to vasopressor initiation (see section 11.4)

### Fluid Monitoring in Context of Organ Dysfunction

### Caution: Not all septic shock is hypovolemic

Scenarios:

Hypovolemic septic shock: Responds well to fluids; lactate clears quickly

Distributive septic shock with myocardial depression: May not respond adequately to fluids alone; requires vasopressor

Sepsis in heart failure patient: Aggressive fluid may worsen pulmonary edema; more cautious approach

Clinical judgment: If patient develops signs of volume overload (rales on lung exam, JVD) before blood pressure normalizes, may need to:

Slow fluid administration

Initiate vasopressor earlier

Consider diuretic (controversial; generally avoided acutely)

### Central Line Placement: Timing and Technique

Indications for central line:

Vasopressor requirement anticipated (will need reliable infusion site)

Inadequate peripheral IV access

Need for monitoring (CVP, ScvO₂)

Anticipated prolonged critical illness

Timing:

During first hour if shock present or anticipated

Can be placed after initial fluid bolus (if patient stabilizes)

Should NOT delay antibiotics or initial fluid resuscitation

Site selection:

Internal jugular vein (preferred): Lower infection risk; easy access

Subclavian vein: Good for long-term; lower infection risk; higher pneumothorax risk

Femoral vein: Easiest to place; higher infection risk; acceptable for temporary use

Avoid upper extremity if possible (higher thrombosis risk)

Technique:

Sterile approach: Full sterile precautions (hat, mask, gown, gloves, large sterile drape)

Ultrasound-guided: Superior to landmark technique; reduces complications

Confirmation: Aspirate blood, observe good flow, obtain chest X-ray to confirm placement

Catheter management:

Aseptic technique during placement

Sterile dressing

Regular assessment for signs of infection

Monitoring lines:

Central venous pressure (CVP): Useful for assessing volume status

Normal: 2–8 mmHg

Low (<2): Suggests hypovolemia

High (>12): Suggests overload or right ventricular failure

ScvO₂ (superior vena cava oxygen saturation):

Normal: >70%

Low (<65%): Suggests inadequate cardiac output or increased oxygen extraction

High (>75%): Suggests low oxygen extraction (mitochondrial dysfunction, peripheral shunting)

### 11.4 The 30–60 Minute Window: Reassessment and Therapeutic Escalation

### SOFA Re-calculation and Source Assessment

At 30-minute mark, reassess patient:

Repeat vital signs:

BP (response to fluids?)

HR

RR

SpO₂

Urine output (if catheter placed)

Repeat SOFA calculation:

Compare to baseline SOFA

Calculate SOFA delta

### SOFA delta ≥2 + hyperlactatemia → Sepsis confirmed

Source localization:

History: Where is infection likely? (respiratory, urinary, abdominal, skin, CNS?)

Imaging: Should proceed to imaging to confirm source

Respiratory source: Chest X-ray

Abdominal source: Abdominal ultrasound or CT (if stable)

Soft tissue source: Physical examination (imaging if needed to delineate extent)

CNS source: Head CT (if meningitis suspected) before LP; consider LP for CSF culture

Physical examination: Look for focused signs

### Vasopressor Initiation: When Blood Pressure Targets Not Met

Criteria for vasopressor initiation:

Systolic BP <90 mmHg despite 30 mL/kg fluid

OR MAP <65 mmHg despite fluids

AND lactate ≥2 mmol/L

### First-line agent: Norepinephrine

Norepinephrine initiation:

Dose: 0.01–0.05 μg/kg/min initially

Titration: Increase by 0.05–0.1 μg/kg/min every 5–10 minutes until target BP achieved

Target: MAP ≥65 mmHg

Typical effective dose: 0.1–0.5 μg/kg/min (70 kg patient = 7–35 μg/min)

Maximum before adding agent: 0.5–1.0 μg/kg/min (but lower thresholds for some guidelines)

Administration:

Central line preferred: Norepinephrine can cause tissue necrosis if extravasated peripherally

Emergency: If central line not available, peripheral administration acceptable temporarily (secure line, monitor for infiltration)

Infusion rate: Use high-flow infusion pump; avoid boluses

Reassessment at 60 minutes:

Is BP target met? (MAP ≥65 mmHg)

Is lactate trend improving? (Ideally declining from baseline)

Is urine output adequate? (≥0.5 mL/kg/hr)

### Is mental status improving?

If BP target not met with norepinephrine alone:

Continue increasing norepinephrine dose

Consider adding second agent:

Epinephrine: If norepinephrine >0.5 μg/kg/min inadequate

Vasopressin: Fixed 0.04 units/min (no titration)

Reassess source control—inadequate source control may necessitate operative intervention

### Source Control Urgency Assessment

At 60-minute mark, determine source control urgency:

Emergent source control needed (within next 1–2 hours):

Necrotizing soft tissue infection: Signs of necrotizing fasciitis or myonecrosis (pain out of proportion, rapidly progressive, crepitus)

Intra-abdominal source: Signs of peritonitis or localized abscess requiring drainage

Esophageal perforation: Imaging showing pneumomediastinum or perforation

Mediastinitis: Neck infection descending to mediastinum

Ruptured viscus: Imaging showing free air

Urgent source control (within 6–12 hours):

Appendicitis: Imaging confirming perforation

Cholecystitis: Imaging confirming gallbladder inflammation; may require percutaneous drainage or surgery

Pyelonephritis with obstruction: Obstruction must be relieved (stent, nephrostomy, or surgery)

Non-urgent source control (can defer initial imaging/intervention):

Pneumonia: Antibiotics primary; source control less urgent

Uncomplicated UTI: Antibiotics adequate; urinary catheter if retention

Non-necrotizing soft tissue infection: Antibiotics + conservative management initially

Surgical consultation: If any question about need for operative intervention, consult surgery immediately (do NOT wait for "perfect" imaging or further deterioration)

### 11.5 Organizational Implementation: Sepsis Response Teams and Electronic Decision Support

### Sepsis Response Team Structure

### Effective sepsis management requires a TEAM, not an individual provider.

### Team Composition

Core team:

Identified "sepsis champion" (nurse, physician, or both):

Responsible for sepsis protocol initiation

Ensures checklist completion

Coordinates team communication

Often the ICU nurse or ED nurse recognizing sepsis

Attending physician/provider:

Directs overall management

Makes decisions on antibiotics, fluids, imaging, source control

Responsible for escalation decisions

Nursing staff:

Places IV lines

Draws blood

Administers fluids and medications

Monitors vital signs and response

Documents times accurately

Laboratory staff:

Processes blood cultures quickly

Provides rapid preliminary results

Alerts team to organism identification

Specialist consultants (called as needed):

Infectious disease: Antimicrobial selection guidance

Surgery: Assessment for source control need

Radiology: Imaging interpretation and guidance

Cardiology: If myocardial involvement suspected

Critical care: If ICU transfer needed

### Communication Structure

Effective communication prevents delays:

Sepsis alert activation:

Clear process for any staff member to activate alert

Notification goes to:

Attending physician

Charge nurse

Pharmacy

Laboratory

Alert includes: Patient location, suspected source, initial presentation

Time-stamped documentation:

Document exact time of:

Sepsis recognition

Antibiotic administration

Fluid bolus initiation

Blood culture draw

Vasopressor initiation

Enables quality assessment and identifies delays

Huddle model:

Brief team meeting at 30–60 minutes

Review what's been done

Identify what's next

Clarify roles

Address barriers to protocol adherence

### Electronic Decision Support Systems (CDSS)

Computerized systems automate many tasks:

### Sepsis Identification

Automated alerts trigger when:

Temperature >38°C or <36°C

AND (WBC >12,000 or <4,000 or bandemia >10%)

AND (tachycardia OR tachypnea OR hypotension)

Alert sent to provider with patient name/location

### Order Set Integration

Sepsis-specific order sets available in EHR:

Single click initiates:

Blood cultures order

Lactate order

CBC, CMP order

Blood gas order

Antibiotic orders (institution-specific regimen)

Fluid order (30 mL/kg over 3 hours)

Vasopressor order template (with dosing)

Advantages:

Reduces cognitive burden

Ensures completeness of initial workup

Reduces time to order entry

### Real-Time Tracking

Dashboard displays:

Time of sepsis recognition

Time of culture draw

Time of antibiotic administration

Time of fluid initiation

Time of vasopressor initiation

Source identified or pending

Alerts for delays:

Red flag if antibiotics not given within 60 minutes

Red flag if cultures not drawn within 15 minutes

Enables real-time intervention to address delays

### Retrospective Audit

Data collected for quality improvement:

Compliance with 3-hour bundle (cultures, antibiotics, fluids)

Compliance with 6-hour bundle (re-assessment, lactate clearance)

Time intervals: recognition to antibiotic, recognition to vasopressor, etc.

Outcomes: ICU mortality, hospital mortality, length of stay

Monthly reporting:

Share data with clinical teams

Identify barriers to compliance

Implement targeted interventions

### Training and Education

All staff require training:

Physician/provider education:

Sepsis-3 definitions

Protocol overview

Antimicrobial selection principles

Source control decision-making

Nursing education:

Sepsis recognition

Blood culture collection technique

Fluid administration

Medication administration

Vital sign monitoring and trending

Laboratory staff education:

Importance of rapid processing

Preliminary identification and reporting

Quality assurance for culture processing

Ongoing reinforcement:

Monthly in-services

Case reviews during M&M conferences

Poster reminders in clinical areas

Simulation training for high-acuity procedures

### 11.6 Integrating Source Control Decisions into the First Hour

### The Surgical Imperative: Early Recognition of Source Control Need

### For certain sources, operative/invasive intervention is ESSENTIAL within the first hour.

### Indicators of Emergent Source Control Requirement

Necrotizing fasciitis:

Red flag: Pain out of proportion to appearance + rapidly progressive

Imaging: If available, shows gas in tissues or extensive fascial involvement

Action: Surgical consult immediately; do NOT delay for "perfect" imaging

Operative intervention: Within 6 hours (ideally sooner)

Diffuse peritonitis:

Red flag: Acute abdomen with peritoneal signs (rebound, guarding)

Imaging: CT showing diffuse contamination or free air

Action: Surgical consult; likely needs operative exploration

Operative intervention: Within 6–12 hours

Esophageal perforation:

Red flag: Acute chest/back pain + subcutaneous emphysema + sepsis

Imaging: CT showing pneumomediastinum or esophageal perforation

Action: Surgical consultation; operative or catheter drainage management

Operative intervention: Within 6 hours for operative approach

### Parallel Pathways: Resuscitation + Source Control

Key principle: Do NOT wait for "perfect" hemodynamic stability before source control.

Approach:

Initiate resuscitation immediately (fluids, antibiotics, vasopressors)

### Simultaneously assess for source control need

If emergent source control indicated, proceed to OR during resuscitation

Operating room can support hemodynamics: Anesthesia can manage vasopressors, fluids, monitoring during surgery

Outcomes: Patients with emergent source control who proceed to OR early (despite ongoing shock) have better outcomes than those who are "optimized" in ICU before surgery (by which point irreversible organ damage has occurred)

### Diagnostic Approach to Suspected Intra-Abdominal Source

Time constraint: Must decide within first hour whether abdomen is source and if urgent intervention needed.

Rapid assessment:

History:

Recent abdominal surgery?

Abdominal pain? (localized or diffuse?)

Nausea/vomiting?

Recent perforation risk (appendicitis, perforated ulcer)?

Physical examination (critical):

Palpate abdomen systematically

Assess for peritoneal signs:

Rebound tenderness: Pain on release of palpating hand (indicates peritoneal irritation)

Guarding: Reflex tensing of abdominal muscles (indicates peritoneal irritation)

Rigidity: Hard, board-like abdomen (indicates severe peritonitis)

Assess for localized findings:

RLQ tenderness (appendicitis)

RUQ tenderness (cholecystitis)

Epigastric tenderness (peptic ulcer, pancreatitis)

Imaging decision:

If peritoneal signs + hemodynamically unstable: Proceed to OR without imaging

If peritoneal signs + hemodynamically stable: Can obtain CT to localize source

If no peritoneal signs but high suspicion: Obtain CT to assess

Imaging protocol (if

Continue

00:11

hemodynamically stable):

CT abdomen/pelvis with IV contrast (gold standard)

Bedside ultrasound (if CT requires transport and patient unstable)

Look for: Free fluid, free air, localized collections, visceral perforation, ascites

Result interpretation:

Free air = perforation; proceed to OR

Loculated fluid/abscess = Source identified; drain (percutaneous if stable, operative if unstable)

Diffuse contamination = Peritonitis; proceed to OR

Non-diagnostic = Proceed to operative exploration if clinical suspicion high

### Summary: The Golden Hour Paradigm

### The first hour of sepsis management determines outcomes.

Structured approach:

| Time Window | Key Actions | Targets |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0–10 min | Recognition, qSOFA, alert team | Identify sepsis candidate |
| 10–30 min | Blood cultures, labs, antibiotics, fluids | Cultures before antibiotics; fluids initiated |
| 30–60 min | SOFA re-assessment, vasopressors, source ID | MAP ≥65; source identified; escalation plans |

Critical metrics:

Antibiotic within 60 minutes: >90% compliance goal

Lactate clearance >10% at 6 hours: Indicates adequate resuscitation

Source control within 12 hours (for surgical sources): Essential for survival

Organizational success factors:

Sepsis alert system: Automated recognition

Team-based response: Clear roles and responsibilities

Standardized protocols: Order sets, checklists

Real-time tracking: Dashboard visibility of compliance

Quality improvement: Monthly audits and targeted interventions

Outcome impact:

Institutions with rigorous golden hour protocols achieve:

20–30% mortality reduction compared to standard care

Shorter ICU and hospital stays

Improved functional outcomes in survivors

Reduced post-ICU syndrome incidence

The next chapters address the details of sustained management beyond the first hour—hemodynamic optimization, antimicrobial de-escalation, and management of organ-specific complications.

### CHAPTER 12: FLUID MANAGEMENT AND HEMODYNAMIC OPTIMIZATION

### 12.1 From Aggressive to Conservative Fluid Strategy: Evidence Evolution

### Historical Context: The Aggressive Resuscitation Era

For decades, sepsis management was dominated by the concept of "maximize oxygen delivery"—the rationale being that sepsis causes tissue hypoperfusion, and aggressive fluid administration would restore perfusion and prevent organ failure.

### The Rivers Trial (2001): The Turning Point

Study design:

Prospective, randomized trial of early goal-directed therapy (EGDT) versus standard care

263 septic patients in emergency department

Intervention: Aggressive fluid resuscitation targeting specific hemodynamic endpoints

Hemodynamic targets:

CVP (central venous pressure) ≥8 mmHg (≥12 if on mechanical ventilation)

MAP ≥65 mmHg

Urine output ≥0.5 mL/kg/hr

ScvO₂ (central venous oxygen saturation) ≥70%

Intervention if targets not met:

Additional fluid boluses (500 mL crystalloid every 30 minutes)

Vasopressors/inotropes if fluids insufficient

Blood transfusion if Hgb <10 g/dL and ScvO₂ <70%

Results:

Hospital mortality: 30.5% (EGDT) vs 46.5% (standard) = 16% absolute mortality reduction

This was a landmark result; EGDT became standard of care

Surviving Sepsis Campaign (SSC) 2004 incorporated EGDT into guidelines

Impact:

Widespread adoption of aggressive fluid strategies

Protocols in virtually all ICUs, EDs, and hospitals

Became standard teaching for sepsis management

### The Challenge: Fluid Overload and Its Consequences

Unintended consequence: Rivers trial succeeded, but widespread adoption revealed a problem—many patients received excessive fluid.

Clinical observation (2008–2015):

Septic patients receiving EGDT-based protocols often developed:

Massive fluid overload (positive fluid balance >10 L by day 3–5)

Pulmonary edema (ARDS incidence higher than expected)

Abdominal compartment syndrome (from bowel edema)

Prolonged mechanical ventilation (from pulmonary edema)

Acute kidney injury (from rhabdomyolysis due to myoglobinuria from hypokalemia/electrolyte derangements)

Key insight: While initial fluid is beneficial, excessive fluid is harmful.

### The ARISE, ProCESS, and PROMISE Trials: The Conservative Shift

### Three major trials (2014–2015) re-evaluated aggressive fluid strategy:

### ARISE Trial (Australia)

Design: Randomized trial of EGDT vs standard care

Sample: 1,600 septic patients

EGDT protocol: Similar to Rivers (CVP ≥8, MAP ≥65, ScvO₂ ≥70%)

Results:

No mortality difference: 18.6% (EGDT) vs 18.8% (standard)

Equivalent outcomes with standard care

EGDT group received more fluids, more vasopressors, more transfusions

No benefit for aggressive approach

### ProCESS Trial (United States)

Design: Three-arm trial comparing EGDT vs protocol-based standard care vs usual care

Sample: 1,351 septic patients in EDs

Results:

No mortality difference between arms (all ~20%)

Outcomes equivalent regardless of protocol intensity

EGDT group had higher vasopressor use, more ICU transfers

Standard protocol-based care was sufficient

### PROMISE Trial (France)

Design: Randomized trial comparing lactate-guided resuscitation vs EGDT

Sample: 420 septic patients

EGDT protocol: CVP ≥8, MAP ≥65, ScvO₂ ≥70%

Lactate protocol: Similar initial fluid but de-escalation based on lactate clearance

Results:

No mortality difference: 24% (EGDT) vs 26% (lactate)

Lactate group received less fluid overall

Lactate group had fewer complications (less fluid overload)

### Contemporary Understanding: The "Goldilocks" Approach

Current evidence supports:

Initial fluid bolus IS necessary (30 mL/kg crystalloid)

### But aggressive ongoing fluid administration is NOT beneficial

Lactate clearance is more important than absolute CVP or ScvO₂

Conservative fluid strategy with lactate-guided resuscitation provides optimal outcomes

Paradigm shift:

NOT: "Give more fluid to maximize oxygen delivery"

YES: "Give enough fluid for tissue perfusion; stop when lactate clears"

### 12.2 The ARISE, ProCESS, and PROMISE Trials: Reinterpreting Resuscitation

### Key Principles Emerging from Trials

### Principle 1: Initial Fluid Bolus Is Essential

### All three trials used initial 30 mL/kg crystalloid

Rationale:

Septic shock is characterized by vasodilation and reduced vascular tone

Initial fluid restores intravascular volume

Enables perfusion pressure restoration

Critical for preventing progression to irreversible organ failure

Timing: Within first 1–3 hours of recognition

Outcome: Initial fluid bolus prevents progression to severe shock

### Principle 2: Ongoing Fluid Administration Should Be Conservative

After initial 30 mL/kg, further fluid administration should be judicious.

Rationale for conservative approach:

Beyond initial bolus, additional fluid has diminishing returns

Capillary leak (from endothelial injury) continues

Additional fluid leaks into interstitium, causing:

Pulmonary edema

Abdominal edema (risk of compartment syndrome)

Peripheral edema

Worsening coagulopathy (from hemodilution)

Target: Avoid positive fluid balance >10 L by day 3–5

### Principle 3: Lactate Clearance > CVP/ScvO₂ Targets

Lactate clearance proved superior to traditional endpoints:

| Endpoint | Rivers Trial | Contemporary View |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CVP ≥8 mmHg | Primary target | Not reliable predictor of fluid responsiveness |
| MAP ≥65 mmHg | Essential | Essential (but achievable with vasopressors) |
| ScvO₂ ≥70% | Primary target | May be normal despite inadequate perfusion (mitochondrial dysfunction) |
| Lactate clearance | Secondary | PRIMARY target for assessment of tissue perfusion |

Why lactate clearance is superior:

Reflects actual tissue perfusion adequacy

Cannot be "gamed" (e.g., giving more fluid to achieve CVP target without improving tissue perfusion)

Indicates restoration of aerobic metabolism

Prognostic for outcomes

### Principle 4: Responsiveness to Fluids Varies

### Not all hypotensive patients are "fluid-responsive"

Fluid-responsive patients: Benefit from fluid boluses (cardiac output increases, lactate clears)

These patients typically show:

Rising lactate from inadequate initial fluid

Response to initial bolus

Lactate clearance after fluid

Fluid-non-responsive patients: Do not benefit from additional fluid (further fluid causes overload without improvement)

These patients typically show:

Myocardial depression (reduced ejection fraction)

Persistent low cardiac output despite adequate filling pressures

Require vasopressor/inotropic support

Clinical challenge: Distinguishing responsive from non-responsive patients in real-time

### 12.3 Goal-Directed Therapy: Lactate Clearance and Tissue Perfusion

### Lactate as the Therapeutic Target

Lactate measurement protocol:

Timing:

Baseline: At sepsis recognition (hour 0)

Follow-up: At 3 hours and 6 hours

Ongoing: Every 6–12 hours until normalized

Interpretation:

| Lactate Level | Implication | Action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| <2 mmol/L | Normal | Tissue perfusion adequate; continue current therapy |
| 2–4 mmol/L | Mild elevation | Ongoing resuscitation needed; monitor lactate clearance |
| 4–8 mmol/L | Moderate elevation | Significant tissue hypoperfusion; escalate support |
| >8 mmol/L | Severe elevation | Severe shock; maximum support needed (consider ECMO) |

Lactate clearance calculation:

(Lactate₀ - Lactate₆ₕᵣ) / Lactate₀ × 100% = % clearance

Target: ≥10% clearance at 6 hours

Interpretation:

≥10% clearance: Resuscitation adequate; tissue perfusion improving

<10% clearance: Inadequate response; escalate interventions

### Targets for Resuscitation Success

Primary endpoints (should be achieved by 6 hours):

Mean arterial pressure ≥65 mmHg:

Achieved with:

Initial 30 mL/kg fluid

Vasopressor (norepinephrine) if needed

Importance: Maintains organ perfusion pressure

Lactate clearance ≥10%:

Indicates tissue perfusion restoration

Requires:

Adequate cardiac output

Adequate oxygen delivery

Restoration of aerobic metabolism

Urine output ≥0.5 mL/kg/hr:

Indicates renal perfusion adequate

Monitors fluid adequacy

Helps prevent AKI

Normalization of mental status:

Indicator of cerebral perfusion restoration

Reflects overall shock reversal

Secondary endpoints (helpful but not primary):

Skin perfusion: Mottling resolution; normalization of skin color

Extremity temperature: Warming of cold extremities

Urine color: Clearing of dark/concentrated urine

Heart rate: Decline toward normal (tachycardia improving)

### The Resuscitation Bundle

Integrated approach combining fluids, vasopressors, and monitoring:

Hour 0–1:

30 mL/kg crystalloid bolus

Blood cultures and labs (including lactate)

Antibiotics initiated

Central line placement if hypotensive

Hour 1–3:

Reassess: Is lactate falling? Is urine output adequate?

If not: Consider vasopressor initiation

Continue cautious fluid (small boluses, not aggressive ongoing infusion)

Hour 3–6:

Repeat lactate measurement

Assess lactate clearance

If ≥10% clearance: Continue current therapy; reassess in 6 hours

If <10% clearance:

Increase vasopressor dose (target MAP >70, not just 65)

Consider inotropic support (dobutamine or milrinone)

Reassess source control adequacy

Repeat lactate at 3 hours

Hour 6+:

Continue monitoring lactate trend

Lactate should normalize by 24–48 hours in responsive patients

Persistent elevation indicates:

Inadequate source control

Myocardial dysfunction

Mitochondrial failure (Type B lactate elevation)

Potential need for advanced support (ECMO)

### 12.4 Fluid Responsiveness Assessment and Dynamic Indicators

### The Challenge: Predicting Who Will Respond to Fluid

Clinical reality: Some patients improve dramatically with 1–2 L of fluid; others show no response even after 10 L.

Goal: Identify fluid-responsive patients early; avoid over-resuscitation in non-responsive patients.

### Static Indicators (Limited Predictive Value)

CVP (Central venous pressure):

Traditional teaching: CVP <8 mmHg indicates need for fluid; CVP >8 mmHg indicates adequate filling

Contemporary evidence: CVP has poor predictive value for fluid responsiveness

Limitation: CVP reflects venous compliance, not actual cardiac output or perfusion

Why it fails: Septic patients have altered compliance; same CVP can represent very different filling states

Pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (PCWP):

Obtained via: Swan-Ganz catheter (pulmonary artery catheter)

Interpretation: PCWP >18 mmHg traditionally indicated adequate preload

Contemporary issue: PCWP is rarely measured now (catheters used less frequently)

Limitation: Like CVP, poor predictor of fluid responsiveness in sepsis

### Dynamic Indicators (Superior Predictive Value)

Dynamic indicators assess change in cardiac output with intervention, rather than absolute values.

### Pulse Pressure Variation (PPV)

Measurement:

Systolic pressure variation with mechanical ventilation (in intubated patients)

Automated calculation on many monitors

PPV = (SBP_max - SBP_min) / SBP_mean × 100%

Interpretation:

PPV <10%: Fluid-non-responsive (don't give more fluid)

PPV 10–13%: Borderline; assess clinical response to fluid trial

PPV >13%: Fluid-responsive (additional fluid likely to improve cardiac output)

Mechanism:

Positive pressure ventilation causes oscillations in cardiac output

Greater oscillations (higher PPV) indicate that heart is "preload-dependent"

Patients with high PPV will respond to fluid; those with low PPV will not

Limitations:

Only valid in mechanically ventilated patients

Requires sinus rhythm (irregular rhythms invalidate measurement)

Not reliable in patients on high PEEP or with low compliance

### Stroke Volume Variation (SVV)

Measurement:

Similar to PPV but measures variation in stroke volume rather than blood pressure

Available on advanced hemodynamic monitors (PiCCO, LiDCO, FloTrac)

SVV = (SV_max - SV_min) / SV_mean × 100%

Interpretation:

SVV <10%: Fluid-non-responsive

SVV 10–15%: Borderline

SVV >15%: Fluid-responsive

Advantages over PPV:

More directly measures cardiac output changes

Some utility in spontaneously breathing patients (though less reliable)

Limitations:

Requires arterial line and special monitor

Not universally available

Cost of equipment

### Passive Leg Raise (PLR)

Procedure:

Patient supine, head elevated 45°

Rapidly move to supine, legs elevated 45°

Observe change in cardiac output (by echocardiography, arterial pulse pressure, or monitor measurement)

Return to original position

Interpretation:

Cardiac output increase >10%: Fluid-responsive (PLR effect is transient; true fluid bolus likely effective)

Cardiac output increase <10%: Fluid-non-responsive

Advantages:

No intervention needed (just repositioning)

Quickly reversible

Useful at bedside

No special equipment required

Disadvantages:

Requires accurate cardiac output measurement (not always available)

Expertise needed for interpretation

Cannot perform if patient unable to tolerate positioning change

### Clinical Application: Fluid Challenge Protocol

Practical approach integrating dynamic assessment:

### Step 1: Initial assessment

Measure baseline cardiac output (if available)

Assess dynamic indicator (PPV, SVV, or PLR)

Obtain baseline lactate

Step 2: Fluid challenge (if any indicator suggests responsiveness)

250–500 mL crystalloid bolus over 5 minutes

Measure cardiac output change

If CO increases >10%: Additional fluid boluses warranted

If CO unchanged: Reassess; likely fluid-non-responsive

### Step 3: Reassess lactate

After 2–3 boluses, repeat lactate

If clearing: Fluid strategy working; continue

If static: Patient likely non-responsive; shift to vasopressor/inotrope

### Step 4: Avoid over-resuscitation

Set maximum cumulative fluid target (e.g., 40–45 mL/kg total by 6 hours)

Monitor for signs of overload (lung crackles, JVD, edema)

Discontinue further boluses once lactate clears or fluid unresponsiveness confirmed

### 12.5 Pulmonary Edema, ARDS, and the Fluid Paradox

### The Fluid Paradox in Sepsis

Fundamental tension:

Initially, fluid is necessary for resuscitation

Subsequently, excessive fluid causes pulmonary edema and ARDS

Clinical challenge: Delivering enough fluid for resuscitation while avoiding excess

### Capillary Leak and Third-Spacing

Pathophysiology:

Normal state:

Endothelial tight junctions maintain fluid in intravascular space

Albumin and other proteins retained intravascularly

Oncotic pressure gradient favors fluid reabsorption

In sepsis:

Endothelial injury increases vascular permeability

Cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1β) directly disrupt tight junctions

Albumin leaks into interstitial space

Oncotic gradient reversed (higher protein concentration outside capillary)

Result: Massive fluid leak into interstitium and third spaces

Consequence: To maintain intravascular volume (necessary for perfusion), clinicians must administer fluid in excess of intravascular needs

Example: 10 L of crystalloid infused; only 2 L remains intravascular; 8 L leaks into interstitium

Interstitial accumulation causes:

Pulmonary edema

Peripheral edema

Bowel edema (impairs absorption, increases infection risk)

Abdominal edema (compartment syndrome risk)

### ARDS Development and Sepsis

ARDS (Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome) is the clinical manifestation of pulmonary edema in sepsis.

### ARDS Definition and Incidence

Berlin Criteria (2012):

Timing: Onset within 1 week of sepsis recognition

Imaging: Bilateral opacities on CXR or CT (not fully explained by effusions, collapse, or nodules)

Oxygenation impairment: PaO₂/FiO₂ ≤300 mmHg (on PEEP ≥5 cmH₂O)

Excludes: Cardiac pulmonary edema (must be sepsis-related)

Severity staging:

Mild: PaO₂/FiO₂ 201–300

Moderate: PaO₂/FiO₂ 101–200

Severe: PaO₂/FiO₂ ≤100

Incidence in sepsis:

Develops in 30–50% of septic patients

More common in:

Aggressive fluid resuscitation

Delayed source control

Severe shock requiring high-dose vasopressors

### Pathophysiology of Sepsis-ARDS

Mechanisms:

Increased capillary permeability: Endothelial injury directly increases lung capillary permeability

Elevated capillary hydrostatic pressure: From aggressive fluid administration

Reduced plasma oncotic pressure: From hemodilution and albumin leak

Impaired lymphatic drainage: Pulmonary lymphatics become edematous, cannot clear fluid

Inflammatory cell infiltration: Neutrophils infiltrate lung tissue; release proteases and ROS

Alveolar epithelial injury: Apoptosis of alveolar epithelial cells; loss of epithelial barrier

Result: Protein-rich fluid accumulates in alveolar space; impairs gas exchange

### Clinical Management: Balancing Resuscitation and ARDS Prevention

### Conservative Fluid Strategy in ARDS

### Landmark study: FACTT Trial (2006)

Randomized trial comparing liberal vs conservative fluid strategies in ARDS

Liberal strategy: Target CVP 12–15 mmHg; additional fluid if needed

Conservative strategy: Target CVP 4–8 mmHg; diuretics if needed

Results:

Fewer ventilator days: 14.6 (liberal) vs 12.1 (conservative) days

Shorter ICU stay: 19.2 (liberal) vs 15.7 (conservative) days

No mortality difference: ~25% both groups

Conclusion: Conservative strategy reduces complications without harming survival

Application to sepsis-ARDS:

If ARDS develops during sepsis resuscitation:

Halt further fluid boluses

Initiate diuretics (furosemide) to achieve negative fluid balance (-500 mL/day or more)

Maintain adequate perfusion pressure with vasopressors (not fluid)

Lung-protective ventilation (see Chapter 21)

### When to Suspect Over-Resuscitation

Clinical signs:

Lung crackles (rales) on auscultation

Elevated JVD

Peripheral edema

Abdominal distension

Positive fluid balance >5–10 L

Radiographic signs:

Bilateral infiltrates

"White out" appearance on CXR

Ground-glass opacities on CT

Laboratory signs:

Coagulopathy worsening (INR increasing despite FFP)

Hypoalbuminemia (from hemodilution and albumin leak)

Electrolyte abnormalities

Management if over-resuscitation suspected:

### Halt further fluid boluses

Initiate diuretics (furosemide 20–40 mg IV q4–6h initially, titrate to goal negative balance)

Increase vasopressor (to maintain MAP without fluid)

Monitor fluid balance closely (target -500 to -1,000 mL/day)

Reassess lactate (ensure tissue perfusion adequate despite reduced fluids)

### 12.6 Crystalloids, Colloids, and Blood Products: Contemporary Recommendations

### Crystalloid Solutions: The Standard

### Crystalloids are the FIRST-LINE fluid for sepsis resuscitation.

### Types and Composition

Balanced crystalloids (preferred):

| Fluid | Na⁺ | K⁺ | Cl⁻ | HCO₃⁻/Lactate | pH | Osmolarity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lactated Ringer's (LR) | 130 | 4 | 109 | 28 (lactate) | 6.5 | 273 |
| Plasmalyte A | 140 | 5 | 98 | 27 (acetate) | 7.4 | 294 |
| Plasma-Lyte 148 | 140 | 5 | 98 | 27 (acetate) | 7.4 | 294 |

Unbalanced crystalloid:

| Fluid | Na⁺ | K⁺ | Cl⁻ | pH | Osmolarity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Normal Saline (0.9% NS) | 154 | 0 | 154 | 5.5 | 308 |

Advantages of balanced crystalloids:

Chloride content closer to plasma (prevents hyperchloremic acidosis)

Buffer systems (lactate, acetate, citrate) help prevent acidosis

More physiologic electrolyte composition

Disadvantage of normal saline:

High chloride (154 mEq/L vs 98 in plasma)

Associated with hyperchloremic acidosis with large volumes

May impair renal function if large volumes used

Current evidence: Balanced crystalloids are preferred, though normal saline remains acceptable for initial resuscitation

### Dosing and Infusion Rate

Initial bolus:

30 mL/kg as calculated above

Example: 70 kg patient = 2,100 mL

Infusion rate: As fast as possible (typically 250–500 mL push through large-bore IV, repeated every 10–15 minutes until initial 30 mL/kg delivered)

Maintenance:

After initial bolus, reduce rate

Typical maintenance: 50–100 mL/hr (adjusted based on clinical response)

Reassess frequently; adjust based on lactate clearance and fluid responsiveness

In mechanically ventilated patients:

Can use central line and rapid infusion pump

Can deliver 1–2 L over 30 minutes if needed

### Colloids: Limited Role

Colloids are large molecules (proteins, polysaccharides) that remain intravascular longer than crystalloids.

### Types of Colloids

Human albumin:

Obtained from pooled donor plasma

5% solution (physiologic) or 25% (concentrated)

Expensive (~$30–100 per 50 mL vial)

Synthetic colloids:

Hydroxyethyl starch (HES): Manufactured starch polymer

Gelatin: Modified bovine collagen

Dextran: Polysaccharide

### Evidence Against Colloid Use

Major trials show NO benefit of colloids over crystalloids in sepsis:

SAFE Trial (2004):

Randomized trial comparing albumin vs normal saline in ICU patients

6,997 septic patients

Results: No mortality difference; albumin no better than saline

Cost: Albumin significantly more expensive

Conclusion: Saline is preferred

VISEP Trial (2008):

Randomized trial comparing HES vs Ringer's lactate in sepsis

537 septic patients

Results:

HES group had higher AKI incidence: 35% vs 29%

Increased need for RRT: 24% vs 16%

Trend toward higher mortality: 35% vs 31% (not statistically significant)

Conclusion: HES associated with worse outcomes; avoided

Current recommendation:

Do NOT use synthetic colloids (HES, dextran, gelatin) in sepsis

Albumin may be used if massive fluid requirements (>30 L) anticipated, but not routine

### Crystalloid is standard

### Blood Products: Judicious Use

RBC transfusions:

Indications in sepsis:

Hemoglobin <7 g/dL (restrictive transfusion strategy)

Hemoglobin <9 g/dL if ongoing significant bleeding or hemodynamic instability expected

Risks of transfusion:

Immunologic reaction

Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI)

Infection transmission (rare with modern screening)

Alloimmunization (repeated transfusions)

Mechanism:

RBC transfusion increases oxygen-carrying capacity

Improves hemoglobin and oxygen delivery (DO₂)

In non-bleeding septic patients with stable hemoglobin, transfusion does NOT improve outcomes

Restrictive strategy (Hb >7 before transfusing) is standard

FFP (Fresh Frozen Plasma):

Indications:

Coagulopathy (INR >1.5) with active bleeding

Urgent source control (surgery) with coagulopathy

NOT for isolated coagulopathy without bleeding (FFP transfusion itself contributes to fluid overload)

Dosing: 10–15 mL/kg per transfusion; repeat only if bleeding continues

Platelets:

Indications:

Platelet count <10,000/μL (spontaneous bleeding risk)

Platelet count <20,000/μL if fever or infection present

Platelet count <50,000/μL with active bleeding or planned invasive procedure

Risks: Same as RBC transfusion

Fibrinogen:

Indications:

Fibrinogen <100 mg/dL with bleeding

Fibrinogen <50 mg/dL even without bleeding (low threshold)

Dosing: Cryoprecipitate (10 units = 1.5–2.5 g fibrinogen) or fibrinogen concentrate (higher concentration, smaller volume)

### Viscoelastic Testing (ROTEM, TEG)

Advantage over conventional coagulation studies:

ROTEM (Rotational Thromboelastometry) and TEG (Thromboelastography) assess coagulation dynamics

Provide information about:

Clot initiation (reaction time)

Clot propagation (K-time, angle)

Clot strength (maximum amplitude)

Clot breakdown (lysis)

Advantages:

More complete picture of coagulation than INR/aPTT alone

Enables goal-directed transfusion (give specific product based on specific defect)

May reduce overall transfusion volume

Disadvantages:

Requires specialized equipment

Operator-dependent

Not universally available

Cost

Current status: Increasingly used in massive transfusion protocols; not routine for standard sepsis management

### Summary: Hemodynamic Optimization in Sepsis

Modern hemodynamic management emphasizes:

Early fluid resuscitation: 30 mL/kg crystalloid is essential

Conservative subsequent fluid administration: Avoid over-resuscitation

Lactate clearance as primary target: Not CVP or ScvO₂

Vasopressor support: For patients not responding to fluid alone

Dynamic assessment of fluid responsiveness: PPV, SVV, or PLR to guide decisions

Prevention of ARDS: Balance resuscitation with pulmonary protection

Judicious transfusion: Restrictive strategy for RBC; avoid unnecessary FFP

Outcomes with optimized hemodynamic management:

Shorter duration of shock

Reduced ARDS incidence

Faster organ function recovery

Lower ICU mortality (2–5% reduction from optimization)

The next chapter addresses the details of vasopressor selection, dosing, and titration—the agents used when fluid alone is insufficient.

### CHAPTER 13: VASOPRESSOR SUPPORT AND INOTROPIC THERAPY

### 13.1 Pathophysiology of Septic Shock: Vasodilation and Myocardial Dysfunction

### The Hemodynamic Paradox of Septic Shock

Septic shock presents a fundamental paradox: hypotension coexists with elevated or normal cardiac output.

This distinguishes septic shock from other forms of shock:

| Shock Type | Cardiac Output | SVR | MAP | Mechanism |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cardiogenic | ↓↓ | ↑↑ | ↓ | Myocardial failure |
| Hypovolemic | ↓ | ↑ | ↓ | Volume loss |
| Septic (early) | ↑ or normal | ↓↓ | ↓ | Vasodilation |
| Septic (late) | ↓ | ↓ | ↓ | Myocardial depression + vasodilation |

### Vasodilation in Sepsis: The Mechanism

### Nitric Oxide: The Primary Culprit

Normal physiology:

Endothelial cells produce nitric oxide (NO) constitutively

NO diffuses into adjacent vascular smooth muscle

NO activates guanylate cyclase → increases cGMP

cGMP causes smooth muscle relaxation → vasodilation

Normal level: ~1–2 nmol/L NO production

In sepsis:

Macrophages and endothelial cells express inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS)

iNOS produces NO at rates 1,000-fold higher than constitutive sources

NO production becomes pathologic: ~500–1,000 nmol/L

Result: Severe, sustained vasodilation

### Molecular Mechanism of NO-Mediated Vasodilation

Cascade:

PAMPs/DAMPs → TLR-4/NLR activation

### NF-κB and MAPK pathway activation

### iNOS gene transcription and translation

### Sustained NO production

Continuous guanylate cyclase activation → cGMP accumulation

### Smooth muscle relaxation → arterial vasodilation

Key consequence: Vasodilation is essentially irreversible with physiologic doses of catecholamines

Cannot overcome the enormous NO production through adrenergic stimulation alone

Requires vasopressin or other non-adrenergic agents for additional support

### Additional Vasodilatory Mediators

Beyond NO, other molecules contribute to vasodilation:

Prostacyclin (PGI₂):

Produced by endothelial cells in response to TNF-α, IL-1β

Activates adenylate cyclase → cAMP → smooth muscle relaxation

Synergizes with NO for greater vasodilation

Bradykinin:

Released from contact system activation

Direct smooth muscle vasodilation

Increases vascular permeability

Adenosine:

Accumulates from tissue hypoxia and ATP breakdown

Activates adenosine receptors → vasodilation

Hypoxia-triggered mechanism

Endogenous cannabinoids (anandamide, 2-AG):

Produced during stress

Activate CB1 receptors on smooth muscle

Contribute to vasodilation

### Myocardial Dysfunction in Sepsis

While vasodilation dominates early sepsis, myocardial depression develops over hours to days.

### Mechanisms of Septic Cardiomyopathy

### 1. Mitochondrial Dysfunction

Mechanism:

Septic mediators (TNF-α, IL-1β, ROS) directly damage mitochondrial structure

Oxidative phosphorylation becomes inefficient

ATP production declines → energy crisis in myocytes

Cannot generate sufficient ATP for contraction

Molecular basis:

ROS damage mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA)

Damage to electron transport chain complexes

Uncoupling of oxidative phosphorylation

Increased mitochondrial membrane permeability

Consequence: Myocardial contractility declines despite adequate preload

### 2. Reduced β-Adrenergic Responsiveness

Mechanism:

Downregulation of β₁-adrenergic receptors on myocytes

Reduced G-protein coupling

Impaired signaling cascade downstream of receptor

Molecular basis:

TNF-α and IL-1β promote β₁ receptor desensitization and internalization

Reduced adenylate cyclase activity

Reduced cAMP production in response to catecholamines

Result: Myocardium becomes less responsive to catecholamine infusion

Clinical implication: High-dose catecholamines may be necessary but will have blunted effect

### 3. Myocyte Apoptosis

Mechanism:

TNF-α and Fas-FasL interactions trigger apoptosis

Myocyte death reduces contractile mass

Inflammation amplified by dying cells

Markers:

Troponin elevation: Indicates myocyte death/injury

Troponin I >0.04 ng/mL associated with worse prognosis

### 4. Calcium Handling Abnormalities

Mechanism:

### Sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium release dysfunction

Ryanodine receptor impaired

Reduced calcium availability for contraction

Altered phospholamban function (impairs calcium reuptake)

Consequence: Excitation-contraction coupling is disrupted despite electrical depolarization

### 5. Increased Afterload from Fluid Overload

Mechanism:

Aggressive fluid administration causes:

Left ventricular dilation (ventricular remodeling)

Increased wall stress (LaPlace's law)

Increased afterload

Myocardium, already weakened, cannot overcome increased afterload

### The Time Course of Myocardial Dysfunction

Early sepsis (0–6 hours):

Vasodilation dominant

Cardiac output normal or elevated (compensatory tachycardia)

Ejection fraction maintained

MAP low from SVR reduction

Middle phase (6–24 hours):

Myocardial depression beginning

Ejection fraction declining (50–60%)

Cardiac output beginning to fall despite catecholamines

Increased heart rate (compensatory)

Increased end-diastolic volume (ventricular dilation)

Late phase (>24 hours):

Profound myocardial depression

Ejection fraction markedly reduced (20–30%)

Cardiac output low despite high vasopressor doses

Severe tachycardia (150+ bpm) attempting compensation

Hepatic congestion from right ventricular failure

Peripheral edema from fluid overload

Recovery phase (if survives acute phase):

Gradual improvement in ejection fraction

Over 7–10 days, most regain normal function

Complete recovery typical in survivors

### Clinical Manifestation: The Spectrum of Septic Shock

Warm shock (early):

Warm extremities (from vasodilation)

Normal or elevated cardiac output

Low SVR

Low MAP

Treatment responsive: Fluid + mild vasopressor usually sufficient

Cold shock (late):

Cold extremities (from low cardiac output, peripheral vasoconstriction)

Low cardiac output

Normal or elevated SVR (compensatory)

Very low MAP

Treatment resistant: Requires high-dose vasopressors and inotropes

Poor prognosis if develops

### 13.2 Norepinephrine as First-Line Agent: Mechanism and Dosing

### Why Norepinephrine?

Norepinephrine (noradrenaline) is the recommended first-line vasopressor in septic shock.

Mechanism of action:

α₁-adrenergic effect (predominant): Vasoconstriction of arterioles

β₁-adrenergic effect: Myocardial inotropic and chronotropic effects

α₂-adrenergic effect: Minor

Why preferred to alternatives:

Better outcomes than dopamine: Multiple trials show superior outcomes with norepinephrine vs dopamine in septic shock

Better renal perfusion: Preserves renal blood flow better than pure vasoconstrictors

Less tachycardia: β₁ effect more modest than with epinephrine

Less arrhythmia risk: Lower incidence of tachyarrhythmias compared to epinephrine

### Mechanism of Effect in Septic Shock

Restoration of vascular tone:

α₁ stimulation directly opposes NO-mediated vasodilation

Myocardial α₁-mediated vasoconstriction increases MAP

Splanchnic vasoconstriction may impair mesenteric perfusion (concern with excessive doses)

Myocardial support:

β₁ effects increase contractility (positive inotropic effect)

β₁ effects increase heart rate (chronotropic effect)

Improves cardiac output despite myocardial depression

Renal perfusion preservation:

At therapeutic doses, maintains renal artery vasodilation

Does NOT cause renal vasoconstriction like phenylephrine

Preserves urine output

### Dosing and Titration

Preparation:

Concentration: Typically 4 mg in 250 mL D5W or NS = 16 μg/mL

Alternative: 8 mg in 250 mL = 32 μg/mL (for rapid escalation)

Route: IV only; central line preferred (peripheral acceptable short-term with close monitoring)

Initial dosing:

Starting dose: 0.01–0.05 μg/kg/min IV infusion

Example: 70 kg patient = 0.7–3.5 μg/min

Route: IV infusion pump (never push/bolus)

Titration:

Goal: MAP ≥65 mmHg

Reassessment interval: Every 5–10 minutes

Titration increment: Increase by 0.05–0.1 μg/kg/min each time

Typical effective dose range: 0.1–0.5 μg/kg/min

Maximum before adding agent: 0.5–1.0 μg/kg/min (some use up to 1.5 μg/kg/min before escalation)

Dose-response curve:

Low doses (0.01–0.1 μg/kg/min): Predominantly β effect; increases heart rate, minimal vasoconstriction

Moderate doses (0.1–0.5 μg/kg/min): Balanced α and β effects; good MAP restoration with maintained cardiac output

High doses (>0.5 μg/kg/min): Predominantly α effect; risk of excessive vasoconstriction, limb ischemia

### Monitoring Parameters During Norepinephrine Infusion

Hemodynamic parameters:

MAP: Goal ≥65 mmHg (preferably 70–80 mmHg acutely)

Heart rate: Should decrease from tachycardic baseline as perfusion improves; persistent tachycardia (>120) suggests inadequate perfusion

Urine output: Should improve to ≥0.5 mL/kg/hr

Lactate: Should clear (↓10% at 6 hours)

End-organ perfusion signs:

Skin perfusion: Mottling should resolve; extremities should warm

Mental status: Confusion should clear

Urine character: Dark/concentrated urine should clarify

Complications to monitor:

Peripheral IV extravasation: Can cause tissue necrosis; if peripheral line used, inspect frequently

Arrhythmias: Tachyarrhythmias; less common than with epinephrine but monitor

Myocardial ischemia: Monitor for ST changes on ECG if troponin elevated

Limb ischemia: Risk at very high doses; pale/mottled extremities warrant dose reduction

### Tapering and Discontinuation

Timing:

As shock resolves (lactate clears, hemodynamics stabilize), begin gradual dose reduction

Typically by day 2–3 if source controlled

Weaning protocol:

When MAP >75 mmHg on current dose, reduce dose by 0.1–0.2 μg/kg/min

Reassess in 10–15 minutes

If MAP remains ≥65 mmHg: Continue reducing

If MAP drops: Return to previous dose, reassess in 1 hour before next reduction attempt

Off vasopressor: When dose reaches minimal (0.01 μg/kg/min) and MAP sustained ≥65 mmHg

Avoid abrupt discontinuation: Sudden withdrawal can cause hemodynamic collapse; taper gradually

### 13.3 Epinephrine: Indications, Risks, and Arrhythmia Profiles

### Mechanism and Pharmacology

Epinephrine (adrenaline):

### Dual α and β agonist

At low-moderate doses (<0.05 μg/kg/min): β effects predominate (inotropy, chronotropy)

At higher doses (>0.05 μg/kg/min): α effects predominate (vasoconstriction)

Effects:

β₁: Increased contractility and heart rate

β₂: Skeletal muscle vasodilation, bronchodilation

α: Vasoconstriction

### When to Use Epinephrine

Indications:

Inadequate response to norepinephrine:

MAP target not reached despite norepinephrine ≥0.5 μg/kg/min

Add epinephrine rather than escalate norepinephrine further

Myocardial depression severe (ejection fraction very low):

Epinephrine's inotropic effect may be beneficial

Higher β₁ effect provides more inotropy than norepinephrine

Cardiac arrest in sepsis (as part of advanced cardiac life support)

NOT first-line: Never start with epinephrine; always start with norepinephrine

### Dosing

Starting dose: 0.05–0.1 μg/kg/min (often started at higher dose than norepinephrine since it's being added as second agent)

Titration: Similar to norepinephrine (increase 0.05–0.1 μg/kg/min q5–10 min)

Typical dose range: 0.1–0.5 μg/kg/min

### Risks and Complications of Epinephrine

### Increased Tachycardia

Mechanism: β₁ effects cause increased heart rate

Clinical consequence:

Tachycardia often >120–140 bpm on epinephrine

Increased myocardial oxygen demand

May precipitate myocardial ischemia if coronary disease present or septic cardiomyopathy severe

### Supraventricular and Ventricular Arrhythmias

Incidence: 10–20% of patients on epinephrine develop significant arrhythmias

Mechanisms:

Increased automaticity: Catecholamines increase ectopic focus activity

Shortened refractory period: β effects reduce refractory period

Enhanced afterdepolarizations: Can trigger ectopic beats

Hypokalemia: Catecholamine shifts K⁺ intracellularly; if serum K⁺ low, increases arrhythmia risk

Types:

SVT (supraventricular tachycardia): Often atrial fibrillation

### PVCs (premature ventricular contractions)

Ventricular tachycardia: Rare but possible

Ventricular fibrillation: Rare except in extreme overdose

Management:

Monitor ECG continuously

Maintain serum K⁺ >3.5 mEq/L (replicate if low)

Consider dose reduction if arrhythmias develop

Have antiarrhythmic agents available

Consider switching agents if persistent arrhythmias

### Increased Lactate (Lactic Acidosis)

Mechanism:

### β₂-mediated skeletal muscle hypermetabolism

Increased glucose uptake and glycolysis in skeletal muscle

Pyruvate → lactate (even with adequate oxygenation)

Results in elevated serum lactate despite adequate perfusion (Type B lactate elevation)

Clinical consequence:

Rising lactate despite hemodynamic improvement

Can be mistaken for inadequate resuscitation

May complicate assessment of tissue perfusion

Management:

Monitor lactate trend; expect transient elevation

If lactate remains elevated despite improving hemodynamics and improving organ function, likely epinephrine effect

Reduce epinephrine dose if possible

### Splanchnic Vasoconstriction

Mechanism:

High-dose epinephrine causes splanchnic vasoconstriction

Reduces mesenteric blood flow

Risk of bowel ischemia

Risk factors:

High doses (>0.5 μg/kg/min)

Prolonged use

Pre-existing splanchnic ischemia risk

Signs:

Elevated lactate (from bowel ischemia)

Abdominal pain

Diarrhea (if mild) or bloody stools (if severe)

Prevention: Use minimum effective dose; discontinue as soon as possible

### 13.4 Vasopressin and Terlipressin: Adjunctive Roles

### Vasopressin Physiology

Endogenous vasopressin:

Hormone produced by posterior pituitary

Released in response to osmotic changes or hemodynamic stress

V₁ receptor (vascular): Vasoconstriction

V₂ receptor (renal): Water reabsorption

In sepsis:

Paradoxical: Vasopressin levels are LOW despite severe hypotension

Pathophysiology: Loss of normal osmotic regulation; relative vasopressin deficiency

Clinical consequence: Cannot rely on endogenous vasopressin; must supplement

### Vasopressin as Adjunctive Therapy

VASST Trial (2008): Randomized trial comparing vasopressin vs placebo added to catecholamines

Study design:

778 septic shock patients

All received standard catecholamine therapy (dobutamine + epinephrine/dopamine)

Randomized to vasopressin 0.04 units/min or placebo

Results:

No mortality difference: 35.4% (vasopressin) vs 37.5% (placebo)

Subgroup analysis: Benefit in less severe shock (norepinephrine requirement <15 μg/min) but not in severe shock

Interpretation:

Vasopressin not a breakthrough therapy for all septic shock

May be helpful in specific subgroup (moderate shock, lower vasopressor requirement)

### Dosing and Use

Standard dose: 0.04 units/min (fixed dose; do NOT titrate)

This is the maximal physiologic dose (replicates endogenous high-stress levels)

Infuse IV continuously

Continue while on catecholamines

Advantage: Non-adrenergic mechanism; may restore vascular tone even when catecholamines inadequate

Disadvantage:

Fixed dose (cannot titrate up if inadequate response)

May cause mesenteric vasoconstriction at higher doses

Risk of myocardial ischemia in patients with coronary disease

### Terlipressin: Selective V₁ Agonist

Terlipressin is a V₁-selective agonist (compared to vasopressin which acts on both V₁ and V₂).

Theoretical advantages:

Selective vascular effect (less V₂ effect on water reabsorption)

Longer half-life (permits intermittent dosing vs continuous infusion)

Potentially less risk of hyponatremia

Dosing: 0.5–2 mg IV q4–6h (intermittent boluses)

Evidence: Limited clinical trials; some benefit shown in European studies but not widely adopted in US

Current status: Available in Europe and some countries; not FDA-approved in US

### 13.5 Dobutamine and Low-Dose Inotropes: Myocardial Support

### Dobutamine: Selective β₁ Agonist

Mechanism:

β₁-selective agonist (some β₂ effect)

Increases contractility (positive inotropic effect)

Increases heart rate

β₂ effect causes peripheral vasodilation

Effects:

Increased contractility: Improves stroke volume and cardiac output

Peripheral vasodilation: β₂-mediated; can worsen hypotension

Increased heart rate: Chronotropic effect

Increased myocardial oxygen demand: Risk of ischemia

### Indications for Dobutamine

When to consider:

Myocardial depression with low cardiac output despite adequate preload

Ejection fraction <30% despite vasopressor support

Persistent organ dysfunction despite vasopressor-maintained MAP

NOT indicated:

As first-line therapy (vasopressors indicated first)

For hypotension (causes vasodilation; worsens BP)

Routine use (limited evidence of benefit)

### Dosing

Starting dose: 2–5 μg/kg/min IV infusion

Titration: Increase by 1–2 μg/kg/min q5–10 min

Typical dose range: 5–20 μg/kg/min (rarely >20 μg/kg/min)

Important caveat: When added to norepinephrine, often reduces catecholamine requirement

Combination: Norepinephrine + dobutamine often more effective than higher doses of single agent

### Disadvantages

Tachycardia: Often significant; 120–150 bpm common

Myocardial ischemia risk: Increased heart rate + increased contractility = increased myocardial O₂ demand

Arrhythmia risk: Similar to epinephrine; SVT/VT possible

Systemic hypotension: Peripheral vasodilation can worsen BP despite improved cardiac output

Usually managed by increasing vasopressor dose

### Current Status

Limited evidence of mortality benefit in sepsis

Surviving Sepsis Guidelines:

Suggest dobutamine may be considered in patients with myocardial dysfunction and low cardiac output

Not recommended for routine use

Trend: Less commonly used now than 10–15 years ago; checkpoint inhibitors and other immune modulators being investigated as alternatives

### 13.6 Monitoring Adequacy: MAP, ScvO₂, Lactate Integration

### Hemodynamic Endpoints: The Trinity of Monitoring

Effective vasopressor management requires simultaneous assessment of three parameters:

### 1. Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP)

Target: ≥65 mmHg (some prefer 70–80 mmHg initially)

Why 65?:

Pragmatic threshold

Below 65: Risk of organ hypoperfusion

Above 80: Risk of unnecessary vasoconstriction, increased myocardial workload

Assessment:

Arterial line preferred (continuous monitoring)

Non-invasive BP cuff acceptable but less reliable during shock

Repeat measurements frequently (every 5–15 minutes during titration)

Interpretation:

MAP target met + lactate clearing + urine output adequate: Resuscitation working

MAP target met + lactate NOT clearing: Myocardial dysfunction; may need inotrope

MAP not met despite escalating vasopressors: Refractory shock; consider ECMO

### 2. ScvO₂ (Central Venous Oxygen Saturation)

Measurement: Via central venous catheter; blood drawn from distal port

Normal: >70% (typically 70–75%)

Interpretation:

ScvO₂ <65%: Suggests low cardiac output or high oxygen extraction

May indicate inadequate resuscitation

Or myocardial dysfunction

ScvO₂ >75%: Suggests adequate oxygen delivery

OR mitochondrial dysfunction preventing oxygen utilization (sepsis-specific issue)

Use in sepsis:

Rivers trial used ScvO₂ ≥70% as primary endpoint

Contemporary view: ScvO₂ is helpful but not primary target

Can be normal despite inadequate perfusion (mitochondrial dysfunction)

Advantage: Noninvasive continuous ScvO₂ monitors available (via special arterial catheters)

### 3. Lactate Clearance

Most important parameter (as discussed in Chapter 12)

Target: ≥10% clearance at 6 hours

Why lactate > MAP/ScvO₂:

Reflects actual tissue metabolic function

Cannot be "gamed" (MAP can be high from excessive vasoconstriction without true tissue perfusion)

Prognostic for outcomes

Guides therapy: if lactate not clearing, something is wrong (inadequate source control? myocardial dysfunction?)

### Integrated Assessment: Titration Algorithm

### Step 1: Initial assessment (hour 0)

Measure: MAP, lactate, urine output, signs of hypoperfusion

If hypotensive: Initiate norepinephrine

### Step 2: First 30 minutes

Target: MAP ≥65

Titrate norepinephrine to achieve this

Continue fluid resuscitation if responding

### Step 3: 30–60 minutes

Reassess: Is lactate trend downward?

Is urine output adequate?

Is MAP sustainable at current vasopressor dose?

If lactate clearing and urine output adequate:

Continue current therapy

Plan for source control

If lactate NOT clearing:

Options:

Increase vasopressor further (if MAP <70)

Add dobutamine (if myocardial dysfunction suspected)

Add vasopressin (if moderate shock and available)

Reassess source control (is it adequate?)

Consider ECMO if refractory

### Step 4: 6-hour reassessment

Repeat lactate

Calculate clearance

If ≥10%: Resuscitation adequate

If <10%: Escalate or reconsider diagnosis

### Step 5: 24-hour reassessment

Should have normalized if source controlled

If persistently elevated: Problem remains (inadequate source control? secondary infection?)

### Use of Continuous Cardiac Output Monitoring

Advanced hemodynamic monitors can provide real-time cardiac output measurement:

FloTrac/Vigileo:

Analyzes arterial waveform

Derives stroke volume variation, cardiac output, SVR

Non-calibrated (no need for thermodilution)

PiCCO:

Pulse contour analysis + transpulmonary thermodilution

Provides cardiac output, SVR, extravascular lung water

More accurate but requires calibration

Swan-Ganz catheter (pulmonary artery catheter):

Gold standard for cardiac output measurement

Thermodilution-based

Provides mixed venous oxygen saturation

Less commonly used now (less correlation with outcomes in early trials)

Current use: Specialized centers; not routine

Limitation: Additional monitoring does NOT improve outcomes if not acted upon (i.e., if MAP/lactate already inform management, additional cardiac output data doesn't change therapy)

### Summary: Vasopressor Strategy in Septic Shock

Rational approach:

Start with norepinephrine (0.01–0.05 μg/kg/min)

Target: MAP ≥65–70 mmHg

Monitor: Lactate clearance (primary), ScvO₂, urine output

If MAP adequate but lactate not clearing:

Escalate norepinephrine to 0.5–1 μg/kg/min

Add epinephrine if further escalation needed

Consider dobutamine if myocardial dysfunction evident

Reassess frequently: Every 5–15 minutes during titration

Taper: As shock resolves and hemodynamics improve

Discontinue: Once off vasopressor and maintaining MAP ≥65 mmHg

Key principle: Vasopressors are temporizing measures

Ultimate outcome depends on source control and immune function restoration

Adequate vasopressor management buys time for source control to take effect

If vasopressor requirement continues to escalate without improvement, suggests inadequate source control

### [END OF CHAPTER 13]

This concludes the detailed section on hemodynamic management (Chapters 12–13). The next chapter would address antimicrobial selection and de-escalation (Chapter 14), followed by advanced immunotherapy (Chapter 15) and organ-specific complications (Chapters 16–22).
