### CHAPTER 14: ANTIMICROBIAL THERAPY—EMPIRICAL SELECTION AND SOURCE CONTROL

### 14.1 Empirical Antibiotic Combinations: Organ-Specific and Resistance Patterns

### The Principle of Empiric Therapy

Fundamental paradox: Antibiotics must be started immediately based on clinical suspicion, but organism identification takes 24–72 hours.

Solution: Empiric broad-spectrum therapy that covers the most likely organisms for the presumed source.

Key principle: Empiric regimen must be adequate coverage (no organism resistant to chosen agents) while being as narrow as possible (avoiding unnecessary broad spectrum).

### Local Epidemiology and Institutional Antibiogram

Empiric selection should be guided by:

Presumed source of infection (respiratory, urinary, intra-abdominal, etc.)

Local resistance patterns (institutional antibiogram)

Patient risk factors for resistant organisms

### Prior antibiotic exposure

Institutional antibiogram:

Summary of susceptibility patterns for common organisms in specific institution

Usually compiled annually by microbiology department

Example:

"E. coli in our hospital: 85% susceptible to fluoroquinolones, 90% to 3rd-gen cephalosporins, 5% ESBL-producing"

"Pseudomonas aeruginosa: 80% susceptible to fluoroquinolones, 70% to carbapenems, 30% multidrug-resistant"

Using antibiogram:

If fluoroquinolones have >90% coverage of E. coli in your institution, reasonable to use fluoroquinolone for presumed UTI/respiratory source

If significant MRSA prevalence (e.g., >20% of Staph aureus), should include vancomycin for unknown source

### Empiric Regimens by Presumed Source

### Community-Acquired Pneumonia (CAP)

Most likely organisms:

Streptococcus pneumoniae (30–40%)

Haemophilus influenzae (5–15%)

Atypical organisms: Mycoplasma, Chlamydophila, Legionella (10–20%)

Gram-negatives: E. coli, Klebsiella (5–10%)

Staph aureus: Rare in CAP unless preceding influenza

Empiric regimen (for septic CAP):

Option 1: Beta-lactam + macrolide (best for atypical coverage):

Ceftriaxone 2 g IV q12h PLUS azithromycin 500 mg IV q24h

Option 2: Beta-lactam + fluoroquinolone:

Ceftriaxone 2 g IV q12h PLUS levofloxacin 750 mg IV q24h

Option 3: Fluoroquinolone alone (if β-lactam allergy):

Levofloxacin 750 mg IV q24h (covers atypicals, gram-negatives, gram-positives)

Do NOT include:

Vancomycin (MRSA not typical in CAP unless specific risk)

Pseudomonas coverage (not typical in CAP)

De-escalation:

Once organism identified:

If S. pneumoniae: Ceftriaxone alone adequate

If Mycoplasma/Chlamydophila: Azithromycin or fluoroquinolone alone

If H. influenzae: Ceftriaxone alone

### Healthcare-Associated Pneumonia (HCAP) / Hospital-Acquired Pneumonia (HAP)

Higher risk for resistant organisms:

MRSA: Present in 20–30% of HAP

Pseudomonas aeruginosa: Present in 20–40% of HAP

ESBL-producing gram-negatives: Present in 10–20%

Anaerobes: If aspiration suspected

Empiric regimen (for septic HAP/HCAP):

Standard approach:

Piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6h PLUS vancomycin 15–20 mg/kg IV q8–12h

OR Ceftazidime 2 g IV q8h PLUS vancomycin + clindamycin

If Pseudomonas coverage needed:

Ceftazidime 2 g IV q8h (covers Pseudomonas) PLUS vancomycin

OR Piperacillin-tazobactam (covers Pseudomonas) PLUS vancomycin

Do NOT use (in HAP/HCAP):

Non-antipseudomonal agents (ceftriaxone, cefazolin)

Fluoroquinolone monotherapy (suboptimal for severe HAP)

De-escalation:

Once cultures identify organism, narrow based on susceptibilities

### Intra-Abdominal Infection

Polymicrobial, including anaerobes

Most likely organisms:

Gram-negatives: E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter (90%+)

Anaerobes: Bacteroides fragilis, Prevotella, Peptostreptococcus (70%+)

Gram-positives: Enterococcus, Streptococcus (50%+)

Community-acquired: ESBL and resistant gram-negatives less common

Healthcare-associated: MRSA, resistant gram-negatives common

Empiric regimen (for septic peritonitis/intra-abdominal):

Option 1: Monotherapy (broad beta-lactam) (preferred):

Piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6h (covers gram-negative, gram-positive, anaerobes)

OR Ampicillin-sulbactam 3 g IV q6h (narrower but acceptable for community-acquired)

Option 2: Beta-lactam + anaerobic coverage:

Ceftazidime 2 g IV q8h PLUS clindamycin 600 mg IV q6–8h

OR Cefoxitin 2 g IV q6h (older agent; covers anaerobes)

If MRSA risk: Add vancomycin

Do NOT use:

Fluoroquinolone monotherapy (inadequate anaerobic coverage)

De-escalation:

Once cultures return: Narrow based on susceptibilities

If anaerobes NOT isolated: Discontinue anaerobic coverage

If E. coli susceptible to fluoroquinolone: May narrow to fluoroquinolone monotherapy (if no Pseudomonas/MRSA)

### Urinary Tract Infection (UTI) with Sepsis

Most likely organisms:

E. coli (80–90%)

Klebsiella (5–10%)

Proteus, Enterobacter, others (5%)

ESBL-producing gram-negatives: 5–20% depending on institution

Pseudomonas: Unusual unless catheterized or healthcare-associated

Empiric regimen (for urosepsis):

Option 1: Beta-lactam:

Ceftriaxone 2 g IV q12h (excellent UTI penetration)

OR Cefepime 2 g IV q12h (for ESBL coverage)

Option 2: Fluoroquinolone:

Levofloxacin 750 mg IV q24h (good UTI penetration)

OR Ciprofloxacin 400 mg IV q12h

If ESBL suspected (prior infection, healthcare-associated):

Cefepime 2 g IV q12h (more stable to ESBL)

OR Carbapenem (meropenem, ertapenem)

If Pseudomonas possible: Cefepime or carbapenem

Do NOT use:

Cephalexin or other oral agents (for sepsis; need IV)

Ampicillin alone (E. coli resistance common)

De-escalation:

Once organism identified and susceptibilities available, narrow significantly

Example: If E. coli susceptible to fluoroquinolone → fluoroquinolone monotherapy

Shorter course acceptable: 7–10 days adequate for uncomplicated urosepsis

### Soft Tissue/Necrotizing Infection

Source-dependent:

Community-acquired cellulitis:

Organisms: Staph aureus (including MRSA increasingly), Group A Streptococcus

Empiric: Vancomycin 15–20 mg/kg IV q8–12h PLUS clindamycin 600 mg IV q6–8h

Clindamycin for anti-toxin effects and better tissue penetration than vancomycin alone

Necrotizing fasciitis:

Type I (polymicrobial): Gram-negative, gram-positive, anaerobes

Piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6h PLUS clindamycin PLUS vancomycin

Type II (Streptococcal): Group A Strep + Staph aureus

Vancomycin PLUS clindamycin (clindamycin critical for anti-toxin effects)

Add fluoroquinolone if gram-negative coverage needed

Type III (Marine/environmental):

Doxycycline 100 mg IV q12h PLUS fluoroquinolone PLUS cephalosporin (if needed)

Vibrio requires fluoroquinolone or doxycycline

De-escalation: Based on culture results and clinical response

### Meningitis with Sepsis

Most likely organisms (vary by age/risk):

Adults: Neisseria meningitidis, Streptococcus pneumoniae, Listeria monocytogenes

Neonates/infants: Group B Streptococcus, E. coli (K1), Listeria

Empiric regimen (for bacterial meningitis):

Standard:

Ceftriaxone 2 g IV q12h (or cefotaxime) PLUS vancomycin 15–20 mg/kg IV q8–12h

PLUS rifampin 600 mg IV/PO q24h (for CNS penetration and Listeria coverage)

If Listeria suspected (age >50, immunocompromised):

Add ampicillin 2 g IV q4h (for Listeria coverage; cephalosporins not effective for Listeria)

Do NOT use:

Fluoroquinolone monotherapy (inadequate)

Cefazolin (does not penetrate CSF adequately)

Note on vancomycin dosing in meningitis: Higher doses needed due to poor CSF penetration

Target CSF vancomycin levels >15 μg/mL

Intrathecal/intraventricular vancomycin may be needed in severe cases

De-escalation: Once organism identified, narrow significantly

Meningococcal meningitis: Penicillin G alone adequate

Pneumococcal meningitis: Cephalosporin adequate (if susceptible)

### 14.2 Surgical Site Infections: Prophylaxis versus Therapeutic Coverage

### Distinction Between Prophylaxis and Treatment

Prophylactic antibiotics:

Given before surgery to prevent SSI

Single or short course (discontinued within 24 hours post-op)

Goal: Prevent colonization of surgical field during operation

Therapeutic antibiotics:

Given after infection diagnosed

Longer courses required

Must cover wider range of organisms (resistant strains possible)

### Surgical Prophylaxis: Timing and Agents

Standard prophylaxis principles:

Timing:

Optimal: Within 60 minutes of incision (within 120 minutes for vancomycin)

Redosing: If operative time >2 half-lives of drug

Discontinuation: Within 24 hours post-op (48 hours acceptable for cardiac surgery)

Agent selection (by procedure type):

| Procedure | Organism Risk | Agent | Dose |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Clean (no GI/GU/biliary involvement) | Skin flora (Staph, Streptococcus) | Cefazolin | 2 g IV x1 |
| Clean-contaminated (biliary, GI, GYN, head/neck) | Enterobacteriaceae + anaerobes | Cefazolin + metronidazole OR cefoxitin | 2 g + 500 mg |
| Contaminated (major break in sterility) | Broader coverage | Broader agent (ceftazidime, piperacillin-tazobactam) | Institution-dependent |
| Dirty/infected (pre-existing infection) | Full treatment | Therapeutic regimen | See therapeutic section |

### Therapeutic Antibiotics for Surgical Site Infections

SSI diagnosis:

Superficial: Skin and subcutaneous only

Deep: Includes muscle/fascia

Organ/space: Involves organ system

Organisms (vary by timing):

Early SSI (<48 hours post-op): Usually skin flora (Staph aureus, MRSA increasingly)

Late SSI (>48 hours): Can include gram-negatives, anaerobes (if GI surgery)

Healthcare-associated: Higher resistance rates

Therapeutic regimen:

Superficial SSI: Often oral antibiotics sufficient

Fluoroquinolone (levofloxacin) or trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole

If MRSA suspected: Clindamycin or linezolid

Deep SSI/wound infection:

IV antibiotics required

Empiric: Vancomycin (for MRSA coverage) + broader gram-negative coverage

Example: Vancomycin 15–20 mg/kg q8–12h + piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g q6h

Post-operative intra-abdominal SSI:

Similar to intra-abdominal infection (polymicrobial)

Broad coverage needed: Piperacillin-tazobactam + vancomycin

Management:

Incision and drainage (I&D) of purulent collection essential

Source control: Remove infected material, drains

Antibiotics: Start empirically, de-escalate based on cultures

Duration: Typically 7–14 days (longer if extensive involvement)

### 14.3 De-Escalation Decision-Making: Culture Results and Clinical Response

### The Principle of De-Escalation

De-escalation = narrowing antimicrobial spectrum once organism identification and susceptibilities known

Goals:

Reduce toxicity (narrower agents usually have fewer adverse effects)

Reduce costs

Reduce resistance selection

Maintain efficacy

Timing:

First opportunity: Gram stain results (usually 1–2 hours after culture positivity)

Better opportunity: Organism identification (24–48 hours from culture positivity)

Optimal: Full susceptibility results (48–72 hours from culture positivity)

### De-Escalation Based on Gram Stain

Gram stain interpretation:

Performed on positive blood culture bottle shortly after flagged positive

Results in 1–2 hours typically

De-escalation decisions:

Gram-positive cocci:

If cocci in clusters → Likely Staph aureus

Check for MRSA status (if known from prior cultures, or if MRSA-risk patient)

If MSSA likely: Can de-escalate from vancomycin to cefazolin or oxacillin/nafcillin

If MRSA possible: Continue vancomycin; await susceptibilities

If cocci in chains → Likely Streptococcus

Can de-escalate from vancomycin to penicillin G or cephalosporin

Await full ID for speciation

Gram-negative rods:

Short, plump rods → Enterobacteriaceae (E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter)

If organism susceptible to fluoroquinolone (which typically are): Can de-escalate to fluoroquinolone monotherapy

If organism susceptible to cephalosporin: Can de-escalate to cephalosporin monotherapy

Slim, curved rods → Possibly Pseudomonas

Continue antipseudomonal coverage (ceftazidime, piperacillin-tazobactam, fluoroquinolone) until confirmed

Gram-positive rods → Listeria monocytogenes (rare)

De-escalate to ampicillin (cephalosporins not effective)

De-escalation example:

Initial broad coverage: Piperacillin-tazobactam + vancomycin

Gram stain: Gram-positive cocci in clusters

De-escalation decision: Likely Staph aureus; discontinue piperacillin-tazobactam (not needed for gram-positive)

New regimen: Vancomycin monotherapy (await susceptibilities to confirm MRSA vs MSSA)

Further de-escalation (once susceptibilities): If MSSA confirmed, switch to oxacillin

### De-Escalation Based on Organism Identification

Rapid organism identification (FilmArray, MALDI-TOF, T2 Candida):

Once organism identified:

| Organism | Susceptibility | De-Escalated Agent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MSSA | Susceptible to β-lactams | Cephalosporin or oxacillin/nafcillin |
| MRSA | Only vancomycin/linezolid/daptomycin | Vancomycin (or alternatives if allergy) |
| E. coli | Usually susceptible to fluoroquinolone | Fluoroquinolone or cephalosporin |
| Klebsiella (non-ESBL) | Usually susceptible to fluoroquinolone | Fluoroquinolone or cephalosporin |
| Klebsiella (ESBL) | Resistant to cephalosporins | Carbapenem (meropenem) or fluoroquinolone |
| Pseudomonas | Depends on susceptibilities | Ceftazidime or fluoroquinolone (if susceptible) |
| Streptococcus (Gram+) | Susceptible to penicillin/cephalosporin | Penicillin G or cephalosporin |
| Enterococcus faecalis | Susceptible to ampicillin | Ampicillin monotherapy |
| Enterococcus faecium | Resistant to ampicillin | Linezolid or daptomycin |
| Anaerobes | Susceptible to clindamycin/metronidazole | Clindamycin (if susceptible) |

### De-Escalation Based on Clinical Response

Clinical response (independent of culture results) can guide de-escalation:

Signs of adequate clinical response:

Fever resolving (or resolved)

Hemodynamics stable off vasopressors

Lactate normalized

Organ function improving

WBC trending down

Overall clinical picture improving

If clinical response adequate:

Even if full susceptibilities not yet available, can de-escalate based on:

Gram stain organism identification

Presumed source

Rapid organism ID (FilmArray, MALDI-TOF)

De-escalation example:

Clinical scenario: Patient with community-acquired pneumonia, septic, on broad coverage

Day 2:

Clinically improving (fever resolving, BP stable, lactate clearing)

Gram stain: Gram-positive cocci (likely pneumococcus)

Rapid ID: Streptococcus pneumoniae identified

De-escalation: Switch from piperacillin-tazobactam + vancomycin to ceftriaxone monotherapy

Rationale: Pneumococcus susceptible to cephalosporins; clinical improvement suggests adequate coverage

### Timing of De-Escalation

Pathways:

Ultra-rapid (6–12 hours): Based on Gram stain + rapid ID (FilmArray)

Gram stain + FilmArray identifying organism enables quick de-escalation

Example: MSSA identified on FilmArray → de-escalate to oxacillin/nafcillin

Early (24–48 hours): Based on organism ID + preliminary susceptibilities

MALDI-TOF identification + preliminary susceptibilities from automated system

Can narrow spectrum reasonably confidently

Standard (48–72 hours): Based on full susceptibility results

Complete antimicrobial susceptibility panel available

Can make final de-escalation decision with confidence

### 14.4 Optimal Duration: Local Factors and Clinical Trajectories

### Principles of Duration Determination

Duration of antimicrobial therapy should be guided by:

Source of infection: Different sources require different durations

Organism type: Some organisms require longer courses

Clinical response: Rapid improvement suggests standard duration; slow improvement suggests longer course

Repeat cultures: Persistence of organism in cultures warrants longer therapy

Immunosuppression: Severely immunocompromised may require longer courses

### Duration by Source of Infection

General framework:

| Source | Typical Duration | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pneumonia (CAP) | 5–7 days | Shorter if rapid clinical improvement |
| Pneumonia (HAP/HCAP) | 7–10 days | Longer courses for Pseudomonas/resistant organisms |
| UTI | 7–14 days | Depends on whether uncomplicated or complicated |
| Intra-abdominal | 7–10 days | After adequate source control |
| Meningitis | 10–14 days | Longer for difficult-to-treat organisms |
| Endocarditis | 4–6 weeks | Minimum; some require longer |
| Osteomyelitis | 4–6 weeks | Often requires prolonged therapy |
| Soft tissue/cellulitis | 7–14 days | Depends on depth; necrotizing may require longer |

### Specific Source Considerations

### Community-Acquired Pneumonia (CAP)

Duration: 5–7 days typical

Shorter course (5 days) if:

Clinical improvement rapid (defervescence, improved oxygenation)

Organism non-resistant (Strep pneumoniae, H. influenzae)

No complications (ARDS, empyema)

Longer course (7–10 days) if:

Slow clinical response (fever persists, prolonged hypoxemia)

Pseudomonas (requires 10–14 days)

Cavitation or abscess formation

### Healthcare-Associated Pneumonia (HAP)

Duration: 7–10 days typical

Longer course (10–14 days) if:

Pseudomonas (10–14 days recommended)

Resistant gram-negatives

Clinical deterioration or slow response

### Intra-Abdominal Infection

Duration: 7–10 days typical (after adequate source control)

Key principle: Duration depends on source control adequacy

Well-controlled source: 7–10 days adequate

Incomplete source control: Longer courses; duration extends if repeat surgery needed

Example:

Patient with perforated appendicitis

Initial appendectomy: Start day 1 antibiotics

If source controlled at initial surgery: 7–10 days antibiotics

If source not adequately controlled at first surgery: Plan re-operation; extend antibiotics until source definitively controlled

### Urinary Tract Infection (UTI)

Duration varies by type:

Uncomplicated cystitis: 3–5 days (oral fluoroquinolone)

Complicated UTI/urosepsis: 7–14 days

Definition: Structural abnormality (catheter, stent, obstruction) or underlying renal disease

Longer courses needed due to difficulty eradicating infection

Pyelonephritis: 7–14 days

Catheter-associated UTI:

Asymptomatic bacteriuria: Do NOT treat

Symptomatic: 7–14 days; remove catheter if possible (essential for cure)

Prostatitis (if present): 4–6 weeks (fluoroquinolone preferred for prostatic penetration)

### Soft Tissue Infections

Cellulitis: 7–14 days (oral or IV depending on severity)

Necrotizing fasciitis: 10–14 days minimum (often longer)

Multiple debridements may extend antimicrobial course

Continue until infection source definitively controlled

Diabetic foot osteomyelitis: 4–6 weeks (often requires prolonged therapy; recurrence risk if shorter)

### Monitoring for Adequate Duration

Indicators that therapy can be discontinued:

Fever resolved: >24 hours afebrile

Hemodynamics normalized: Off vasopressors, stable BP

Lactate normalized: <2 mmol/L

Organ function improving:

Creatinine normalizing

Bilirubin normalizing

PaO₂ improving

Repeat cultures negative: If initially positive, should be negative with adequate therapy

Clinical examination: Source healing, wound improving, no signs of ongoing infection

Indicators for prolonged therapy:

Persistent fever: >7 days into therapy despite seemingly adequate coverage

Consider: Inadequate source control, secondary infection, resistant organism, PICS (post-ICU complications)

Positive repeat cultures: If organism still cultured despite therapy and apparent source control

Suggests inadequate drug levels, resistant organism, or continued bacterial replication

Clinical deterioration: Return of fever/sepsis signs after initial improvement

Suggests secondary infection or inadequate initial source control

### 14.5 Antifungal Therapy: Indications and Timing

### When to Consider Antifungal Coverage

Indications for empiric antifungal:

Severe immunosuppression:

HIV with CD4 <50

Recent chemotherapy (neutropenic)

Organ transplant recipients

Note: These patients warrant empiric antifungal in sepsis

High Candida colonization burden:

Multiple sites colonized (urine, sputum, wound, stool)

Candida colonization index >0.5 (colonized sites / total sampled sites)

Risk factors for invasive Candida:

Central venous catheter

Total parenteral nutrition (TPN)

Abdominal surgery

Broad-spectrum antibiotics (select for Candida)

Prolonged ICU stay

Clinical signs/cultures suggestive:

Positive blood culture for Candida

Candiduria

Unexplained persistent fever despite antibiotics

Specific clinical scenarios:

Intra-abdominal sepsis: Higher Candida risk; some protocols include empiric antifungal

ICU-acquired sepsis: Higher Candida risk; consider empiric

Do NOT include empiric antifungal if:

Immunocompetent host

No Candida colonization

Community-acquired sepsis (CAP, UTI, etc.)

Clear bacterial organism identified

### Antifungal Agents and Selection

### Fluconazole

Mechanism: Inhibits fungal cytochrome P450; prevents ergosterol synthesis

Spectrum:

Candida species:

C. albicans: Excellent coverage

C. parapsilosis: Excellent coverage

C. tropicalis: Good coverage

C. glabrata: Reduced susceptibility; higher doses needed

C. auris: Resistant (do NOT use)

C. krusei: Resistant (do NOT use)

NOT effective against:

Aspergillus

Cryptococcus

Zygomycetes

Dosing:

IV: 400–600 mg loading dose, then 200–400 mg daily

Advantage: Excellent CNS penetration (useful for cryptococcal meningitis)

Advantage: Well absorbed orally (transition to PO possible)

Drug interactions: Inhibits CYP3A4; many drug interactions

Use in sepsis:

First-line for non-critically ill with mild-moderate Candida risk

Less preferred in severe sepsis (slower action; echinocandins preferred)

### Echinocandins

Agents: Caspofungin, micafungin, anidulafungin

Mechanism: Inhibit fungal cell wall synthesis; fungicidal

Spectrum:

Candida species: Excellent coverage (including resistant C. glabrata)

C. auris: Echinocandin-susceptible strains only (resistance emerging)

NOT effective: Cryptococcus, Aspergillus (limited), Zygomycetes

Dosing (caspofungin example):

Loading: 70 mg IV day 1

Maintenance: 50 mg IV daily

Advantages:

Broad coverage of Candida species

Faster action than fluconazole

Lower drug interactions

Preferred in septic shock with Candida risk

Use in sepsis:

First-line for severe Candida sepsis or high-risk septic patients

Preferred over fluconazole for critically ill

### Liposomal Amphotericin B

Mechanism: Binds fungal cell membrane; causes leakage

Spectrum:

Broad antifungal: Candida, Aspergillus, Zygomycetes

### Most comprehensive coverage

Disadvantages:

Significant toxicity: Nephrotoxicity (dose-limiting), electrolyte abnormalities

Liposomal formulation: Reduces toxicity vs conventional; expensive

Slow infusion required: 2–4 hours infusions

Use in sepsis:

Reserved for:

Confirmed Aspergillus or Zygomycetes

Severe immunosuppression with empiric coverage needed

Candida resistance to echinocandins

NOT first-line for routine Candida coverage

Dosing: 3–5 mg/kg IV daily (liposomal formulation)

### Timing of Antifungal Initiation

Early vs. late initiation debate:

Evidence: Limited prospective data on timing in sepsis

Clinical practice:

Early initiation (within 24 hours) if high-risk patient with sepsis of unknown source

Delayed initiation (after negative bacterial cultures at 48 hours) if non-immunocompromised

De-escalation:

If bacterial organism identified and clinically improving: Discontinue empiric antifungal

If Candida isolated: Continue or switch to appropriate agent based on species and susceptibilities

### 14.6 Combination Therapy: Synergy and Pharmacokinetic Considerations

### When Combination Therapy Is Indicated

Mono-therapy is preferred when possible (simpler, fewer drug interactions, lower cost)

Combination therapy indicated for:

Empiric broad-spectrum (pending organism identification)

Multiple organism coverage needed

Example: Gram-negative + anaerobic coverage

Specific pathogen-combination synergies:

Pseudomonas: Beta-lactam + fluoroquinolone (synergistic)

Enterococcus: Beta-lactam + gentamicin (synergistic for some

Continue

00:15

strains)

Bacterial meningitis: Beta-lactam + rifampin (CNS penetration)

Resistant organisms:

Carbapenem-resistant organisms: Multiple agents for coverage

Colistin + other agents (for pan-resistant gram-negatives)

Fungal infections:

Amphotericin B + flucytosine (for cryptococcal meningitis)

### Synergistic Combinations

Defined: Combination effect greater than additive effect of individual agents

Mechanisms of synergy:

Cell wall + intracellular target:

Beta-lactam (damages cell wall) + protein synthesis inhibitor (penetrates to ribosome)

Example: Penicillin + gentamicin for Enterococcus

Different metabolic pathways:

Sulfonamide (inhibits folic acid synthesis) + trimethoprim (blocks dihydrofolate reductase)

Blocks successive steps in same pathway

Sequential action:

Rifampin enhances penetration of other agents by disrupting cell integrity

### Drug-Drug Interactions

Critical interactions in combination therapy:

Acetaminophen (paracetamol) + antibiotics:

Generally well-tolerated

Monitor: Flucloxacillin (rare hepatotoxicity)

Warfarin + antibiotics:

Many antibiotics enhance warfarin effect (inhibit CYP2C9)

Risk: INR elevation, bleeding

Management: Monitor INR closely, adjust warfarin dose

Aminoglycosides + beta-lactams:

Generally synergistic

Timing: Infuse beta-lactam first, then aminoglycoside (synergy improved)

Monitoring: Aminoglycoside peak/trough levels

NSAIDs + antibiotics:

Aminoglycosides + NSAIDs: Increased nephrotoxicity risk

Avoid combination if possible

Fluoroquinolones + NSAIDs:

Increased risk of CNS effects (seizures, delirium)

Avoid if possible

Metronidazole + alcohol:

Disulfiram-like reaction

Counsel patients to avoid alcohol

### Pharmacokinetic Considerations in Sepsis

Altered pharmacokinetics in septic shock affect drug levels:

Increased volume of distribution:

Third-spacing from capillary leak

Drugs distributed into larger volume

Consequence: Standard doses may achieve suboptimal concentrations

Solution: Higher doses needed

Reduced clearance:

Renal function may be acutely reduced (AKI)

Hepatic function may be impaired

Consequence: Prolonged drug half-life; accumulation risk

Solution: Adjust dosing for renal/hepatic impairment

Altered protein binding:

Albumin reduced (from hemodilution, acute phase response)

Reduced protein binding → increased free drug

Consequence: Better penetration into tissues; higher free concentration

Solution: Monitor drug levels; adjust if needed

Therapeutic drug monitoring:

Recommended for: Aminoglycosides, vancomycin

Goal: Achieve therapeutic concentrations despite altered pharmacokinetics

Vancomycin: Target trough 15–20 μg/mL

Gentamicin: Target peak 5–10 μg/mL, trough <1 μg/mL

### Summary: Antimicrobial Strategy in Sepsis

Effective antimicrobial management requires:

Empiric breadth: Start broad based on presumed source

Rapid de-escalation: Once organism identified, narrow quickly

Source-specific duration: Match duration to source

Clinical monitoring: Adjust based on response

Pharmacokinetic optimization: Dose appropriately for sepsis physiology

Resistance awareness: Know local resistance patterns

Combination rationale: Use combinations when synergistic, not routinely

Outcome impact:

Appropriate empiric therapy followed by rapid de-escalation reduces:

Antibiotic-associated adverse effects

Resistance selection

Treatment duration

Costs

This concludes the management section of the monograph. The next major section would address advanced topics: immunotherapy (Chapter 15), organ-specific complications (Chapters 16–22), and long-term outcomes (Chapters 23–24).

### CHAPTER 15: PHASE-SPECIFIC IMMUNOTHERAPY—BEYOND ONE-SIZE-FITS-ALL

### 15.1 Early Phase (Days 0–3): Hyperinflammatory Modulation

### The Hyperinflammatory Phase: Pathophysiology and Therapeutic Logic

Characteristics of early sepsis (days 0–3):

Overwhelming cytokine production: TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6 elevated 10–1000-fold

Systemic inflammation maximal: Fever, tachycardia, vasodilation

Endothelial injury: Increased vascular permeability, capillary leak

Coagulation activation: Procoagulant state; microthrombi

SIRS criteria: Temperature abnormality, tachycardia, tachypnea, elevated WBC

Mortality mechanism in early phase:

Refractory hypotension from NO-mediated vasodilation

ARDS from pulmonary capillary leak

Multi-organ failure from widespread endothelial injury

DIC from uncontrolled coagulation activation

Therapeutic logic: If inflammation is excessive and causing organ damage, modulating inflammation should theoretically improve outcomes.

### NLRP3 Inflammasome Inhibition in Early Sepsis

### Rationale for NLRP3 Targeting

NLRP3 pathway:

Central hub of inflammasome-mediated inflammation

Produces IL-1β (one of most potent inflammatory mediators)

Activated by PAMPs and DAMPs in sepsis

Hyperactivation produces excessive IL-1β → inflammatory cascade

Hypothesis: Blocking NLRP3 → reduced IL-1β → reduced inflammation → improved outcomes

### NLRP3 Inhibitors in Development

MCC950 (CP-456773):

Mechanism: Direct NLRP3 protein inhibitor; prevents inflammasome assembly

Preclinical evidence: Improves survival in murine sepsis (cecal ligation and puncture model)

Clinical status: Phase 2b trials in septic ARDS (2024–2025)

Dosing in trials: 10–50 mg daily

Potential: May reduce ARDS progression, reduce multi-organ failure

Oridonin:

Mechanism: Natural product; NLRP3 inhibitor

Preclinical evidence: Benefits in LPS-induced endotoxemia

Clinical status: Early-stage development

VX-765 (Belnacasan):

Mechanism: Caspase-1 inhibitor (blocks IL-1β and IL-18 processing)

Clinical status: Phase 2 trials in sepsis; mixed results

Challenge: Blocking caspase-1 universally may impair some beneficial immune responses

### Current Evidence and Challenges

Challenges with early anti-inflammatory therapy:

Timing paradox: While reducing inflammation in hyperinflammatory phase sounds logical, clinical trials of anti-TNF, anti-IL-1β, and anti-IL-6 agents have been largely negative.

Immune suppression risk: Excessive anti-inflammatory therapy may impair pathogen killing

TNF-α needed for granuloma formation

IL-1β needed for T-cell activation

Blocking these may paradoxically worsen infection control

Heterogeneity: Not all septic patients are hyperinflammatory

Some show modest cytokine elevation

Some show mixed inflammatory-immunosuppressive phenotype

Universal inhibition inappropriate

Current approach: NLRP3 inhibition being investigated in specific population (severe ARDS, high inflammatory markers) rather than all septic patients.

### Corticosteroids in Early Septic Shock

### Rationale for Steroid Use

Mechanism:

Suppresses NF-κB pathway → reduces cytokine production

Reduces endothelial activation

Reduces capillary leak

Anti-coagulant effects

Hypothesis: Corticosteroids might reduce excessive inflammation and improve outcomes

### Evidence in Septic Shock

CORTICUS Trial (2008):

Randomized trial of hydrocortisone vs placebo in septic shock

499 patients

Results:

No mortality benefit: 39.2% (hydrocortisone) vs 36.1% (placebo)

Slightly faster reversal of shock (but temporary)

Increased secondary infections in steroid group

Surviving Sepsis Guidelines (2021):

Recommendation: Do NOT use corticosteroids routinely in sepsis

Exception: Consider low-dose hydrocortisone (50 mg q6h) in refractory septic shock (persistent hypotension despite adequate fluid and vasopressors)

Mechanism: Possible relative adrenal insufficiency in some patients

### Current Practice

Selective use:

Adrenal insufficiency suspected: Cosyntropin stimulation test; if inadequate response, consider hydrocortisone

Refractory shock: Some centers use low-dose hydrocortisone empirically

Not routine: Most guidelines recommend against universal corticosteroid use

Monitoring if used:

Increased hyperglycemia risk

Increased infection risk

Psychological effects (delirium possible)

Taper gradually (avoid abrupt discontinuation)

### 15.2 Middle Phase (Days 3–7): Transition and Immunological Checkpoint

### The Transition Period: Mixed Phenotype

Characteristics (days 3–7):

Pro-inflammatory mediators begin to decline from peak

Anti-inflammatory signals emerge: IL-10, TGF-β, Treg expansion

T-cell exhaustion markers rising: PD-1, TIM-3 expression increasing

Clinical picture mixed: Some patients improving; others deteriorating

Organ dysfunction variable: Some improving; others progressing

Critical juncture: Outcomes diverge dramatically based on:

Adequacy of source control

Individual immune phenotype

Organ reserve and recovery capacity

### Assessment of Immunological Status

### HLA-DR Measurement at Day 3–5

Timing: Measure monocytic HLA-DR at day 3–5 of sepsis

Interpretation:

| HLA-DR % | Immune Status | Clinical Significance | Therapy Consideration |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| >80% | Normal | Adequate immunity | Continue standard care |
| 50–80% | Mild-moderate suppression | Early immunosuppression | Monitor closely |
| 30–50% | Significant suppression | Risk of secondary infection | Consider immune support |
| <30% | Profound suppression | Very high secondary infection risk | Candidate for immunostimulation |

Clinical meaning:

HLA-DR >50%: Adequate immune function; do NOT give anti-inflammatory agents

HLA-DR <30%: Profound immunosuppression; candidate for immune checkpoint inhibitors or cytokine therapy

### Alternative Immune Markers

If HLA-DR not available:

T-cell count: Absolute CD4+ T cells <200/μL suggests immunosuppression

Lymphocyte count: <1,000/μL suggests T-cell attrition

Clinical response: Rapid improvement suggests adequate immunity; slow improvement suggests suppression

### Decisions at Middle Phase

### If Immune Function Adequate (HLA-DR >50%)

Approach:

Continue source control: Complete operative/interventional management

Continue antibiotics: Standard duration

NO anti-inflammatory agents (would suppress adequate immunity)

NO immunostimulatory agents (not indicated if immunity adequate)

Supportive care: Organ support, nutrition, prophylaxis

Prognosis: Usually good if source controlled; recovery expected by day 7–10

### If Immune Function Suppressed (HLA-DR <30%)

Approach:

Complete source control: Ensure adequate source control already achieved

Consider immune checkpoint inhibitors: Anti-PD-1 therapy (see section 15.3)

Consider cytokine therapy: IL-7, IFN-γ (see section 15.3)

Continue antibiotics: Maintain coverage (immunity impaired; bacteremia risk)

Enhanced monitoring: Watch closely for secondary infections

Prognosis: High risk if profound immunosuppression; secondary infections common unless immunity restored

### 15.3 Late Phase (Days 7–21+): Immunosuppression and Adoptive Immunity

### The Immunosuppressive Phase: Characteristics and Consequences

Late phase immunosuppression:

T-cell exhaustion profound: 70–90% of T cells express PD-1, TIM-3, CTLA-4

Lymphoid organ atrophy: Spleen and thymus depleted of lymphocytes

Lymphopenia severe: Absolute lymphocyte counts <500/μL

Treg/MDSC expansion: Continue to suppress immunity

Secondary infections common: Candida, Aspergillus, Pseudomonas, Acinetobacter

Mortality mechanism in late phase:

Inadequate immune response to secondary pathogen

Persistent primary infection (inadequate immune clearance)

Multi-organ failure progression despite antibiotics

PICS complications (cognitive, physical, psychiatric)

### Immune Checkpoint Inhibition: Anti-PD-1 Therapy

### Mechanism and Rationale

PD-1 pathway in sepsis:

T cells express PD-1 at high levels

Engagement of PD-L1 (on APCs) inhibits T-cell function

PD-1 blockade removes this inhibition → restores T-cell function

Hypothesis: Blocking PD-1 → restores T-cell killing of pathogens → controls secondary infections

### Preclinical Evidence

Murine sepsis models:

CLP (cecal ligation and puncture) followed by anti-PD-1:

Improved survival (30–40% improvement)

Restored CD8+ T-cell IFN-γ production

Better bacterial clearance

Timing: Anti-PD-1 given days 3–5 showed benefit; earlier gave no benefit (early phase may be harmful)

Mechanism in mice:

Restores exhausted T-cell population

Permits differentiation into Th1 cells (producing IFN-γ)

Enhances bacterial killing

### Early Clinical Evidence

Phase 1b trials (2024–2025):

Small patient numbers (10–20 patients typically)

Safety: Generally well-tolerated; no unexpected adverse events

Efficacy signals: Some improvement in immune markers (T-cell function restoration)

Challenges: Small sample sizes; heterogeneous patient populations

Agents in trials:

Nivolumab (Opdivo): Anti-PD-1 monoclonal antibody

Pembrolizumab (Keytruda): Anti-PD-1 monoclonal antibody

Pidilizumab: Anti-PD-1 (earlier-stage trials)

### Dosing and Administration

Typical regimen (from cancer literature, adapted for sepsis trials):

Nivolumab: 3 mg/kg IV infusion over 30–60 minutes

Frequency: Every 2–4 weeks (in cancer; may differ in sepsis trials)

Route: IV only

Timing in sepsis:

Days 3–7 of sepsis (when immunosuppression emerging but not terminal)

Combined with ongoing source control and standard care

NOT day 0–1 (too early; may interfere with initial immune response)

### Risks and Considerations

Immune-related adverse events (from cancer experience):

Pneumonitis: Immune-mediated lung inflammation

Hepatitis: Liver inflammation

Colitis: GI tract inflammation

Endocrinopathies: Thyroiditis, hypophysitis, adrenal insufficiency

Neurotoxicity: Encephalitis, Guillain-Barré

Incidence: 10–20% in cancer patients; unknown in sepsis

In sepsis context:

Patients already have ARDS, coagulopathy, multi-organ dysfunction

Immune-related adverse events could be catastrophic

Critical question: Can benefit of restored immunity outweigh risk of immune-related toxicity?

Current status: Phase 1b trials ongoing to answer this question

### Anti-TIM-3 Therapy

### Mechanism

TIM-3 in sepsis:

T cells express high TIM-3 (co-expressed with PD-1)

TIM-3 engagement (by galectin-9) inhibits T-cell function

Anti-TIM-3 blocks this inhibition

Potential advantage over anti-PD-1:

May be more specific to exhausted T cells (TIM-3 more restricted expression)

Less off-target effects?

### Clinical Status

Preclinical: Limited sepsis data; more data in cancer/chronic infection

Clinical: Early-stage development; not yet in sepsis trials

### Cytokine Therapy in Late Sepsis

### Interleukin-7 (IL-7)

Mechanism:

Growth factor for T cells and NK cells

Promotes proliferation of remaining T cells

Promotes thymic regeneration

May reverse T-cell exhaustion

Rationale in sepsis:

Lymphopenia is major feature of late sepsis

IL-7 could promote T-cell expansion

Restore functional T-cell numbers

Clinical evidence:

Phase 2 trial (CytoTherapeutics, 2020s):

Enrolled septic patients with lymphopenia

IL-7 administration (or placebo)

Preliminary data: Improved lymphocyte expansion and some immune markers

Challenge: Did immunity restoration translate to better outcomes?

Dosing: 10 μg/kg IV (experimental; not standardized)

Status: Phase 2 ongoing; not yet standard care

### Interferon-Gamma (IFN-γ)

Mechanism:

Activates macrophages

Promotes Th1 differentiation

Enhances antimicrobial responses

Rationale:

Late sepsis characterized by reduced IFN-γ production

IFN-γ replacement could restore antimicrobial immunity

Clinical evidence: Limited in sepsis; more data in immunodeficiency

Status: Not widely used in sepsis; mainly research

### Granulocyte-Macrophage Colony-Stimulating Factor (GM-CSF)

Mechanism:

Stimulates production of myeloid cells

Activates macrophages

Enhances antigen presentation

Rationale:

Late sepsis characterized by MDSC accumulation (not macrophage dysfunction)

GM-CSF might promote myeloid differentiation and activation

Clinical evidence: Mixed results; some trials showed benefit, others did not

Status: Not routine; some centers use selectively

### Immunoglobulin Therapy

Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG):

Contains pooled antibodies from healthy donors

Potential benefit: Provides opsonizing antibodies, modulates inflammation

Clinical evidence in sepsis: Limited; some observational benefit

Status: Not routine; not recommended by guidelines

### Cellular Therapies in Late Sepsis

### Mesenchymal Stem Cell (MSC) Therapy

Mechanism:

MSCs have immunomodulatory properties

Produce anti-inflammatory cytokines (IL-10, TGF-β)

Promote tissue repair

May restore barrier function

Rationale in sepsis:

Widespread tissue damage in late sepsis

MSCs could promote tissue regeneration

Reduce inflammation

Clinical evidence:

Phase 1/2 trials (ongoing in various countries):

Small numbers (10–30 patients)

Preliminary safety: Generally well-tolerated

Preliminary efficacy: Some improvement in organ function markers

Challenges:

MSC sourcing and manufacturing standardization

Mechanism of benefit unclear

Heterogeneous patient populations

Status: Experimental; not standard care; clinical trials ongoing

Administration: IV infusion of cultured MSCs (10⁶–10⁷ cells)

### CAR-T Cell Therapy

Concept: Engineered T cells expressing chimeric antigen receptors; used in cancer

Application to sepsis:

Engineer T cells to target specific pathogens

Example: CAR-T cells targeting Pseudomonas antigens

Highly speculative; very early stage

Challenges:

Sepsis is polymicrobial (would need multiple CAR-T cell types)

Pathogens vary between patients

Extremely expensive

Unknown safety profile

Status: Highly experimental; no clinical trials in sepsis

### 15.4 Complement Inhibition and Adjunctive Strategies

### Complement Pathway Targeting in Sepsis

### Rationale for Complement Inhibition

Complement role in sepsis:

Beneficial: C3b opsonization, MAC-mediated bacterial killing

Harmful: C3a and C5a anaphylatoxin-mediated inflammation, endothelial injury

Hypothesis: Blocking complement (especially C5a) might reduce harmful inflammation while preserving some beneficial effects

### C5a Antagonists

Mechanism: Block C5a receptor (C5aR) on immune cells

Agents:

Eculizumab: Complement C5 inhibitor (converts to iC3b, preventing C5a generation)

FDA-approved for paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria

Cost: Very expensive ($200,000+ per year)

Iptacopan: Factor B inhibitor (blocks alternative pathway)

Pegcetacoplan: C3-specific inhibitor

Preclinical evidence:

Block C5a reduces inflammation in sepsis models

Improves survival in some models

But also shows potential immunosuppression

Clinical status: No completed sepsis trials; theoretical interest

### Tissue Factor (TF) Inhibition

Rationale: TF initiates coagulation in sepsis; blocking TF might prevent DIC

Mechanism: Tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI)

Clinical status: No benefit shown in sepsis trials; abandoned

### Synergistic and Combined Immunotherapy Approaches

### Rationale for Combination Immunotherapy

Single-agent immunotherapy has shown limited benefit in most sepsis trials.

Hypothesis: Multiple simultaneous immune modulation might be more effective

Anti-PD-1 (restore T-cell function) + IL-7 (promote T-cell expansion)

Anti-PD-1 + anti-TIM-3 (block multiple checkpoints)

Anti-CTLA-4 (selective Treg depletion) + anti-PD-1 (restore Tconv)

### Preclinical Combination Studies

CLP + anti-PD-1 + IL-7:

Superior survival vs either agent alone

Restored T-cell numbers and function

Better bacterial clearance

CLP + anti-PD-1 + anti-TIM-3:

Synergistic improvement in T-cell function

More complete checkpoint blockade

Status: Preclinical only; no clinical trials yet

### Clinical Trial Design Challenges

Why combination trials delayed:

Safety uncertainty: Each agent alone has potential risks; combinations even more uncertain

Cost: Multiple expensive biologics; cost-benefit analysis needed

Trial complexity: More variables; larger sample sizes needed

Regulatory pathway: FDA guidance on combination immunotherapy still evolving

### 15.5 Clinical Trial Landscape 2024–2025

### Major Ongoing Trials

Anti-PD-1 in Septic Shock:

Sponsor: National Institutes of Health, pharmaceutical companies

Phase: 1b/2a

Status: Actively enrolling (2024–2025)

Target enrollment: 20–50 patients per trial

Primary endpoints: Safety, immunological markers

Secondary endpoints: Mortality, secondary infection rates

NLRP3 Inhibitors (MCC950) in Septic ARDS:

Sponsor: Pharmaceutical company

Phase: 2b

Status: Patient enrollment ongoing

Target enrollment: 100–200 patients

Primary endpoint: Reduction in ARDS progression

Secondary endpoints: Mortality, organ dysfunction

IL-7 in Sepsis with Lymphopenia:

Sponsor: Academic/pharma collaboration

Phase: 2

Status: Enrolling

Target enrollment: 50–100 patients

Primary endpoint: T-cell expansion

Secondary endpoints: Secondary infection rates

MSC Therapy in Septic ARDS:

Multiple trials worldwide

Phase: 1/2

Status: Various stages

Endpoints: Safety, organ function markers

### Barriers to Rapid Translation

Why aren't these therapies in widespread use:

Clinical trial timelines: Typical Phase 2 trial 2–3 years; Phase 3 trial 3–5 years

Safety concerns: Immunotherapy carries risks; require careful evaluation

Cost: Biologic agents expensive; cost-benefit ratio uncertain

Complexity: Personalized medicine approach (select HLA-DR <30%) requires infrastructure

Regulatory: FDA approval pathway for sepsis indication relatively new

### Expected Timeline for Clinical Integration

Optimistic scenario:

2025–2026: Phase 2 data emerging; some centers begin using in research context

2026–2027: Initial Phase 3 trial results

2028–2030: FDA approval for selected agents in selected populations

2030+: Widespread clinical adoption with refinement of patient selection

### Summary: Phase-Specific Immunotherapy Strategy

Contemporary approach to immunotherapy:

| Phase | Dominant Immune Problem | Therapeutic Strategy | Agents | Goal |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Days 0–3 (Early) | Hyperinflammation | Anti-inflammatory | NLRP3 inhibitors (experimental) | Reduce inflammation |
| Days 3–7 (Middle) | Transition | Phenotype-guided | Assess HLA-DR; decide escalation | Personalize therapy |
| Days 7–21+ (Late) | Immunosuppression | Immunostimulation | Anti-PD-1, IL-7, MSC (experimental) | Restore immunity |

Critical principles:

One-size-fits-all is dead: Future is phase-specific and phenotype-specific

Biomarker-guided: HLA-DR, T-cell markers guide decisions

Experimental: Most advanced immunotherapies still investigational

Source control paramount: Immunotherapy cannot substitute for source control

Combination likely: Single-agent approaches may be insufficient

Future vision (5–10 years):

Septic patients routinely phenotyped at presentation

Early phase: NLRP3 inhibitors given if hyperinflammatory (high CRP/IL-6)

Middle phase: HLA-DR measured; results guide days 3–5 decisions

Late phase: Anti-PD-1 given if HLA-DR <30%; IL-7 if lymphopenia

Outcomes: 5–10% absolute mortality reduction from personalized immunotherapy

### CHAPTER 16: SEPSIS-ASSOCIATED ENCEPHALOPATHY (SAE) AND NEUROLOGICAL COMPLICATIONS

### 16.1 Pathophysiology: Blood–Brain Barrier Disruption and Endothelial Injury

### Sepsis-Associated Encephalopathy: Definition and Epidemiology

Sepsis-associated encephalopathy (SAE) is altered mental status occurring in the context of sepsis without direct CNS infection (i.e., not meningitis, encephalitis, or brain abscess).

### Clinical Presentation

Spectrum:

Mild: Difficulty concentrating, mild confusion, reduced attention

Moderate: Disorientation to time/place, hallucinations, agitation

Severe: Coma, inability to follow commands, unresponsiveness

Timing:

Can occur within hours of sepsis recognition

Often appears before other organ dysfunction

May be the earliest sign of severe sepsis in some patients

### Epidemiology

Incidence: 8–70% of septic patients (wide range due to variable definitions and patient populations)

Risk factors:

Severity of sepsis (septic shock → higher incidence)

Advanced age (>65 years)

Pre-existing dementia or cognitive impairment

Severe infection sources (intra-abdominal, CNS proximity)

Organ dysfunction (renal, hepatic)

Prognostic significance:

Associated with worse outcomes:

Increased ICU mortality (2–3-fold)

Longer mechanical ventilation

Longer ICU stay

Increased post-ICU cognitive impairment

Not just a marker: SAE may be causal (hypoxia, seizures, further ischemia worsening injury)

### Pathophysiology of SAE: Multiple Mechanisms

### 1. Blood–Brain Barrier (BBB) Disruption

Normal BBB function:

Anatomical basis: Endothelial tight junctions (claudins, occludin, ZO-1, ZO-2)

Functional basis: Selective permeability; excludes large molecules and pathogens

Maintenance: Astrocytes and pericytes provide supporting signals

BBB disruption in sepsis:

### Mechanism 1: Cytokine-mediated tight junction disruption

TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6 produced systemically cross into CNS

Act on brain endothelial cells and astrocytes

Activate myosin light chain kinase (MLCK) → phosphorylation of tight junction proteins

Internalization of occludin and claudins

Result: Increased paracellular permeability

### Mechanism 2: Direct inflammatory mediator entry

Bradykinin, complement components (C3a, C5a) enter CNS

Activate brain parenchymal cells

Trigger glial activation

### Mechanism 3: Matrix metalloproteinase (MMP) activation

TNF-α and IL-1β induce MMP-2 and MMP-9 expression in endothelium

MMPs cleave tight junction proteins and extracellular matrix

Severely compromises BBB integrity

Consequence: Brain becomes accessible to peripheral pathogens and inflammatory mediators

### 2. Endothelial Activation and Leukocyte Infiltration

Endothelial activation:

TNF-α and IL-1β upregulate adhesion molecules (ICAM-1, VCAM-1, selectins)

Peripheral neutrophils and monocytes adhere to brain endothelium

Extravasation into brain parenchyma

Leukocyte-mediated injury:

Infiltrated neutrophils produce ROS and proteases

Damage to neuronal and glial structures

Release of additional inflammatory mediators (local amplification)

### 3. Microglial Activation and Neuroinflammation

Microglia: Brain-resident macrophages; normally quiescent

Activation in sepsis:

Systemic LPS/PAMPs enter CNS (through compromised BBB)

TLR-4 activation on microglia

Microglial transformation from resting to activated morphology

Production of TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6, ROS in brain parenchyma

Local amplification: Microglia produce mediators that further activate other microglia and astrocytes

Neuroinflammation cascade:

Activated microglia → cytokine production

Astrocytes activated → additional cytokine production

Oligodendrocytes damaged

Neuronal synaptic dysfunction

### 4. Neuronal Dysfunction and Apoptosis

Mechanisms of neuronal injury:

Excitotoxicity:

Glutamate accumulation in extracellular space

Excessive NMDA receptor activation

Calcium influx → mitochondrial dysfunction → apoptosis

Quinolinic acid (produced by microglia) is potent NMDA agonist

Oxidative stress:

ROS production from activated microglia and infiltrated neutrophils

Overwhelms neuronal antioxidant defenses

Lipid peroxidation in cell membranes

Mitochondrial damage

Mitochondrial dysfunction:

Direct ROS damage to mitochondria

Impaired ATP production

Inadequate energy for neuronal function

Triggers apoptotic pathways

Apoptosis:

Multiple pathways activated: TNF-R1 (extrinsic), cytochrome c release (intrinsic)

Caspase cascade → neuronal death

Widespread neuronal loss in severe SAE

### 5. Neurotransmitter Dysfunction

Glutamate-GABA imbalance:

Glutamate (excitatory) increases from neuronal injury and astrocyte dysfunction

GABA (inhibitory) production may be reduced

Net effect: Hyperexcitability or, paradoxically, inhibition (depending on timing and severity)

Serotonin, dopamine dysregulation:

Altered monoamine metabolism

Contributes to behavioral changes, mood disturbance

### 6. Seizure Risk in SAE

Seizure incidence: 5–10% of SAE patients

Mechanisms:

Cytokine-induced neuronal hyperexcitability

Metabolic derangements (hypoglycemia, hyponatremia, hypoxia)

Microglial activation → increased glutamate

Cerebral edema → increased intracranial pressure

Clinical significance:

Seizures worsen prognosis: Associated with increased mortality and cognitive impairment

Often non-convulsive: Subtle behavioral changes may indicate seizure activity (require EEG to detect)

Status epilepticus possible: Refractory seizures can occur; life-threatening

### Distinction Between SAE and Other Causes of Altered Mental Status in Sepsis

Differential diagnosis (must exclude):

| Condition | SAE | Alternative Diagnosis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Timing | Develops with sepsis | May predate sepsis or be unrelated |
| Direct CNS infection | No meningitis/encephalitis | Positive CSF culture/imaging |
| Structural lesion | No mass/hemorrhage on imaging | CT/MRI shows lesion |
| Drug-induced | Related to sepsis-specific meds | Sedatives/analgesics are cause |
| Metabolic | Multiple abnormalities from sepsis | Single metabolic abnormality (hypoglycemia alone) |
| Hypoxic-ischemic | Often accompanies SAE | Primary etiology clearly hypoxia |

Clinical approach: Exclude other causes through:

CSF examination (if no contraindications)

Neuroimaging (CT ± MRI)

EEG (if seizure suspected)

Metabolic workup

### 16.2 Delirium versus Encephalopathy: Diagnostic Clarity

### Definitions and Distinctions

Delirium:

Definition: Acute fluctuating disturbance of consciousness and attention with disturbed sleep-wake cycle

Duration: Hours to days of fluctuation

Reversibility: Often reversible with treatment of underlying cause

Prevalence in ICU: 50–80% of mechanically ventilated patients

Causes: Medication (sedatives, opioids), hypoxia, sleep deprivation, pain, ICU environment

Encephalopathy:

Definition: Diffuse brain dysfunction from systemic disease

Underlying pathology: Multi-system effects on brain (BBB disruption, neuroinflammation, metabolic derangement)

Associated with: Organ dysfunction (hepatic encephalopathy with liver failure, uremic encephalopathy with renal failure)

SAE: Encephalopathy specifically due to sepsis-related systemic inflammation

### Clinical Distinction

In practice: Septic patient with altered mental status may have both delirium AND encephalopathy

Key distinguishing features:

Pure delirium (likely not SAE):

Fluctuates rapidly (hour to hour)

Responsive to environmental stimuli

Easily arousable

Normal CSF

Normal EEG (except for slowing)

Improves quickly when specific cause treated (e.g., sedation reduced)

Pure encephalopathy (likely SAE):

More sustained dysfunction (present most of time)

Persists despite resolution of confounding factors

Abnormal CSF (pleocytosis, elevated protein, elevated lactate)

Abnormal EEG (triphasic waves, rhythmic activity)

Improves slowly over weeks as sepsis resolves

May leave residual cognitive impairment

Combined picture (most common):

Fluctuating course with underlying persistent dysfunction

Sedation worsens fluctuation

Reducing sedation reveals persistent encephalopathy

Requires careful evaluation to distinguish components

### 16.3 Neuroimaging and Neuromonitoring

### Structural Neuroimaging

### CT Head Indications in SAE

When to obtain CT:

Focal neurological deficit: Weakness, facial droop, speech abnormality (suggests stroke or hemorrhage)

Severe altered mental status disproportionate to sepsis severity (may indicate SAE or alternative diagnosis)

Seizure activity: To exclude hemorrhage or mass

Concern for raised intracranial pressure: Headache, vomiting unrelated to sepsis

Clinical deterioration: Worsening mental status despite appropriate sepsis management

Typical CT findings in SAE:

Often normal: CT may show no abnormality despite clinical SAE

Cerebral edema: Sulcal effacement, ventricular compression (in severe cases)

Leukoencephalopathy: Symmetric white matter hypodensity (rare; suggests severe SAE)

Microhemorrhages: Scattered small hemorrhages (rare; indicates severe injury)

Stroke-like changes: Territorial ischemia (in severe cases)

### MRI in SAE

Advantages over CT:

Superior soft tissue contrast

Can detect microhemorrhages, white matter changes

Can assess for edema, inflammation

Typical findings:

Normal: Many mild-moderate SAE cases have normal MRI

Cerebral edema: Vasogenic edema (white matter) or cytotoxic edema (gray matter)

Microhemorrhages: T2* gradient echo sequences most sensitive

White matter changes: T2/FLAIR hyperintensities

Limitation: Too time-consuming for acute sepsis workup; reserved for persistent unexplained encephalopathy

### DWI (Diffusion-Weighted Imaging)

Indication: If stroke suspected

Findings in SAE:

May show acute ischemic changes (bright signal on DWI, low ADC)

Suggests sepsis-related stroke (likely from coagulopathy, cardiac embolism, or vasculitis)

### Functional Neuroimaging

PET, SPECT: Not routinely used; research tools

### EEG (Electroencephalography) in SAE

### Role of EEG

Primary indication in sepsis: Detect non-convulsive seizures

Why important:

Non-convulsive seizures may occur without obvious motor manifestations

Can only be detected by EEG

Occur in 5–10% of SAE patients

Associated with worse prognosis if missed

Treatable with antiepileptic drugs

### EEG Findings in SAE

Typical patterns:

Generalized slowing:

Background frequency <8 Hz (theta or delta)

Correlates with severity of encephalopathy

Non-specific (seen in many encephalopathies)

Triphasic waves:

Characteristic pattern: 1–2 Hz waves with three phases

Seen in metabolic encephalopathies (hepatic, uremic, septic)

Nonspecific but suggests metabolic cause

Extreme delta brush:

Mixture of slow waves (delta) and fast activity (beta)

Seen in severe encephalopathies including SAE

Associated with worse prognosis

Seizure activity:

Electrographic seizures: Rhythmic activity 4–15 Hz with clear evolution

Status epilepticus: Continuous or near-continuous seizure activity

Requires treatment with antiepileptic drugs

Periodic patterns:

Periodic lateralized epileptiform discharges (PLEDs): Suggest focal lesion

Generalized periodic discharges: Suggest diffuse brain dysfunction

May indicate more severe encephalopathy

### Clinical Interpretation

EEG shows non-convulsive seizure → Start antiepileptic therapy

EEG shows triphasic waves/generalized slowing → Confirms encephalopathy; guides supportive care

EEG normal with altered mental status → Consider other causes; encephalopathy not ruled out (EEG sensitivity <100%)

### When to Obtain EEG

Indications:

### Altered mental status disproportionate to sepsis severity

Suspected seizures: Behavioral changes, unresponsiveness without clear explanation

No improvement in mental status despite controlled sepsis

Escalating sedation requirement: May indicate non-convulsive seizures being masked by sedation

### 16.4 Sedation Strategies in the Context of SAE

### The Paradox: Sedation Worsens Encephalopathy While Being Necessary

Clinical challenge:

SAE causes altered mental status, agitation, combativeness

Clinicians use sedation to allow mechanical ventilation, reduce agitation

But: Sedation worsens underlying encephalopathy

Creates difficult balance: Sedate enough for patient safety vs. minimize drug-induced worsening of cognitive dysfunction

### Sedation Assessment Tools

### Sedation Scales

RASS (Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale):

Ranges from -5 (unarousable) to +4 (combative)

Goal in SAE: Usually RASS 0 to -2 (alert/calm to lightly sedated)

Avoid excessive sedation (RASS <-2) if possible in SAE

SAS (Sedation-Agitation Scale):

1–7 scale; goal usually 3–4 (calm, awakens to commands)

### Delirium Assessment

CAM-ICU (Confusion Assessment Method for ICU):

Detects delirium in mechanically ventilated patients

Four features: (1) acute onset, (2) fluctuation, (3) inattention, (4) disorganized thinking or altered level of consciousness

Positive if 1 + 2 + (3 or 4)

ICDSC (Intensive Care Delirium Screening Checklist):

Eight items; total score 0–8

Score ≥4 indicates delirium

Value: Distinguish delirium from sedation

Patient sedated is arousable but not delirious

Patient delirious shows fluctuation and inattention even when aroused

### Sedation Agents and Impact on SAE

### Benzodiazepines (e.g., Midazolam, Lorazepam)

Effects on SAE:

Worsen: Cognitive dysfunction, delirium risk

Mechanism: GABA-mediated CNS depression; impair neuronal function further

Clinical consequence: Prolonged encephalopathy after sedative discontinuation

Current recommendation: Avoid if possible in SAE; use only if absolutely necessary for agitation/safety

### Propofol

Effects on SAE:

Mixed: Short-term sedation but enables quick assessment of mental status

Mechanism: GABA-mediated; but rapid offset permits frequent reassessment

Advantage: Can titrate down quickly for neurologic checks

Current recommendation: Acceptable if used for short periods with frequent interruptions for assessment

### Dexmedetomidine

Effects on SAE:

Potentially beneficial: Provides sedation without deep CNS depression

Mechanism: Alpha-2 agonist; maintains some arousal

Advantage: Allows patient to follow commands even while sedated

Permits: Neurologic assessment without fully waking patient

Current recommendation: Preferred in SAE if sedation needed; allows assessment while permitting some rest

### Opioids (Morphine, Fentanyl, Hydromorphone)

Role in SAE:

Primarily for analgesia, not sedation

Depress consciousness at high doses but less delirium-inducing than benzodiazepines

Appropriate use: Treat pain (which contributes to agitation)

Current recommendation: Use appropriately for analgesia, but not as primary sedative in SAE

### Sedation Strategy in SAE

Goal: Minimize sedation while maintaining patient safety

Approach:

Daily sedation interruption:

Turn off sedation daily

Assess mental status while patient awakens

Permits neurologic assessment

Restart sedation only if necessary for ventilator tolerance

Benefit: Reduces delirium incidence, shortens ICU stay

Prefer light sedation:

Target RASS 0 to -1 (awake/calm to drowsy but arousable)

Avoid RASS <-2 (deep sedation) unless necessary

Benefit: Patient more alert; encephalopathy more apparent; faster recovery of consciousness

Non-pharmacologic measures:

Early mobilization (sitting in chair, standing if possible)

Reorientation and cognitive stimulation

Sleep hygiene (lights off at night, minimize noise)

Involve family (familiar faces improve orientation)

Benefit: May reduce agitation without sedation

Assess for reversible causes:

Pain: Treat with opioids

Hypoxia: Optimize oxygenation

Infection: Treat sepsis aggressively

Metabolic: Correct electrolyte abnormalities

Full bladder: Catheterize if necessary

Benefit: May resolve agitation without sedation

Antipsychotics: Haloperidol, quetiapine

For delirium management

Use cautiously; can worsen outcomes

Current trend: Move away from antipsychotics toward supportive care

### 16.5 Neurorehabilitation and Cognitive Recovery

### Post-SAE Cognitive Impairment

Incidence: 30–50% of SAE survivors have persistent cognitive impairment at ICU discharge

Types of cognitive impairment:

Memory deficits: Difficulty with new learning, recall of ICU events

Attention deficits: Difficulty concentrating, easily distracted

Executive dysfunction: Difficulty with planning, judgment, organization

Processing speed: Slow thinking, delayed responses

Visuospatial dysfunction: Difficulty with spatial tasks, navigation

Time course of recovery:

At ICU discharge: Significant impairment common (30–50% have measurable deficits)

At hospital discharge: Gradual improvement

At 3 months: Further improvement; but 20–30% still have deficits

At 6–12 months: Most recover substantially; but 10–15% have persistent long-term deficits

### Mechanisms of Cognitive Recovery

Neuroplasticity:

Brain has remarkable ability to reorganize after injury

Surviving neurons form new connections

Dendritic sprouting and synaptogenesis occur

Over weeks to months, network reorganization permits functional recovery

Time-dependent factors:

Recovery is not linear: Initial rapid improvement in first 1–2 months, then plateau

Some recovery occurs even up to 1 year post-illness

Younger age associated with better recovery

### Rehabilitation During ICU and Hospital Stay

Early mobilization:

Benefit: Improves outcomes compared to immobilization

Timing: Begin as soon as hemodynamically stable (often within days of ICU admission)

Activities:

Passive range of motion (if unable to move)

Sitting upright in bed

Transfer to chair with assistance

Walking with assistance if able

Progressive ambulation

Cognitive component: Movement stimulates brain; cognitive recovery enhanced by early mobilization

Cognitive stimulation in ICU:

Reorientation: Frequent reminders of date, location, why in ICU

Engagement: Conversation, reading, music

Family involvement: Familiar faces and voices improve orientation and mood

Early data: Structured cognitive stimulation reduces delirium duration

### Post-Hospital Cognitive Rehabilitation

Formal neuropsychological testing:

Timing: At hospital discharge or 1–2 weeks post-discharge

Purpose: Quantitate deficits, identify areas needing rehabilitation

Domains tested:

Memory (immediate, delayed, recognition)

Attention and executive function

Language (naming, comprehension, fluency)

Visuospatial skills

Cognitive rehabilitation therapy:

Speech-language pathology: For cognitive rehabilitation, not speech only

Techniques:

Cognitive training: Exercises for specific deficits (memory games, attention tasks)

Strategy training: Teach compensatory strategies (external reminders, lists, routines)

Metacognitive training: Teach awareness and self-monitoring of cognitive abilities

Duration: Usually 8–12 weeks, 2–3 sessions/week

Neuropsychiatric support:

Depression and anxiety common after SAE

Treatment: Counseling, antidepressants (SSRIs)

Benefit: Treatment of mood improves cognitive function

### Return to Work and Social Function

Prognostic factors for return to work:

Pre-illness job demands (cognitive work harder to return to)

Severity of SAE (more severe → longer recovery)

Age (younger → better outcomes)

Social support

Motivation and effort in rehabilitation

Timeline:

Mild SAE: Return to work 3–6 months

Moderate SAE: Return to work 6–12 months

Severe SAE: May not return to previous level; may require job modification or retraining

Some patients: Permanent disability from cognitive impairment

### Summary: SAE as a Critical ICU Complication

Key points:

SAE is common: Occurs in 8–70% of septic patients; often overlooked

SAE has mechanisms: Not "just delirium"; results from BBB disruption, neuroinflammation, endothelial injury, neuronal apoptosis

SAE has prognosis: Associated with increased mortality, longer ICU stay, and persistent cognitive impairment

SAE is treatable: Aggressive sepsis management, appropriate sedation strategy, and early rehabilitation improve outcomes

SAE requires follow-up: Cognitive impairment persists beyond ICU discharge; rehabilitation support essential

Mechanisms-based therapy emerging: Future immunotherapy for sepsis may include neuroprotective agents

### [END OF CHAPTER 16]
