### CHAPTER 17: SEPSIS-ASSOCIATED ACUTE KIDNEY INJURY

### 17.1 Hemodynamic and Non-Hemodynamic Mechanisms

### Epidemiology and Clinical Significance of AKI in Sepsis

Incidence of AKI in sepsis:

Overall: 20–50% of septic patients develop AKI

In septic shock: 50–70% develop AKI

Severe AKI requiring RRT: 5–10% overall; up to 30% in septic shock

Prognostic impact:

Mortality: Each stage of AKI increases mortality

No AKI: ~20–25% mortality

Stage 1 AKI: ~30% mortality

Stage 2 AKI: ~40–50% mortality

Stage 3 AKI: ~60–80% mortality

Long-term consequences: AKI survivors have increased risk of chronic kidney disease (20–30% develop CKD)

KDIGO Classification (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes):

| Stage | Creatinine Change | Urine Output | Clinical Significance |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stage 1 | 1.5–1.9× baseline | <0.5 mL/kg/hr for 6–12 hr | Risk of progression |
| Stage 2 | 2–2.9× baseline | <0.5 mL/kg/hr for ≥12 hr | Established dysfunction |
| Stage 3 | ≥3× baseline OR ≥4.0 mg/dL | <0.3 mL/kg/hr for ≥24 hr OR Anuria ≥12 hr | Critical; RRT likely needed |

### Mechanisms of Sepsis-Associated AKI

### 1. Hemodynamic Mechanisms: Renal Hypoperfusion

Normal renal hemodynamics:

Renal blood flow: ~1,200 mL/min (20–25% of cardiac output)

Autoregulation: Maintains glomerular filtration rate (GFR) despite systemic BP changes (65–160 mmHg)

Mechanism: Myogenic autoregulation (afferent arteriole constricts if systolic BP low) and tubuloglomerular feedback

In septic shock:

### Phase 1: Vasodilation with hypotension

Systemic vasodilation (NO-mediated) reduces systemic BP

Even if renal blood flow maintained absolutely, it may be inadequate relative to demand

Glomerular filtration pressure = (Pgc - Pbs - πgc)

Pgc (glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure) reduced from low systemic BP

Pbs (Bowman space hydrostatic pressure) may increase from edema

πgc (glomerular capillary oncotic pressure) may increase from reduced GFR

Net result: Reduced filtration pressure

### Phase 2: Myocardial depression with low cardiac output

Late sepsis develops myocardial depression

Cardiac output falls

Renal blood flow decreases proportionally

Severe renal hypoperfusion develops

### Phase 3: Compensatory intense renal vasoconstriction

Sympathetic nervous system activation

Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS) activation

Vasopressin (AVP) secretion

Attempt to maintain renal perfusion pressure

Consequence: Intense vasoconstriction of afferent arteriole → severely reduced GFR

Net effect of hemodynamic mechanisms:

Renal perfusion pressure falls

GFR acutely falls

Creatinine rises (acute renal dysfunction)

Urine output falls (oliguria)

### 2. Non-Hemodynamic Mechanisms: Direct Tubular Injury

Beyond hemodynamics: Even with adequate resuscitation and renal perfusion, many septic patients develop AKI from direct tubular epithelial injury

### Mechanism 1: Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN)

Pathophysiology:

Tubular epithelial cells undergo apoptosis and necrosis

Loss of tubular epithelial mass

Impaired tubular reabsorption and secretion

Cast formation (cellular debris + Tamm-Horsfall protein) → tubular obstruction

Loss of tight junctions → "leak" of filtrate back into peritubular space (reduces effective GFR)

Causes of ATN in sepsis:

ROS-mediated injury:

Neutrophils infiltrating kidneys produce ROS

Mitochondrial dysfunction in tubular cells

Lipid peroxidation of cell membranes

Result: Cell death via apoptosis/necrosis

Myoglobinuria (in severe sepsis with rhabdomyolysis):

Myoglobin precipitates in tubules

Toxic to epithelial cells

Causes dark urine ("tea-colored")

Hemoglobinuria (in sepsis with severe hemolysis):

Hemoglobin precipitates in tubules

Toxic effects similar to myoglobin

Crystal-induced AKI:

Uric acid crystals (from cell lysis)

Sulfonamide crystals (if sulfonamide antibiotics used in dehydration)

Acyclovir crystals (if acyclovir used in high doses/dehydration)

### Mechanism 2: Inflammatory Cytokine-Mediated Injury

Pathophysiology:

TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6 produced systemically and locally in kidney

Directly activate tubular epithelial cells

Trigger pro-apoptotic signaling cascades

Increase vascular permeability in peritubular capillaries

Promote leukocyte infiltration

Tubular cell apoptosis:

Initiated by cytokine-mediated signaling

Results in organized cell death with minimal inflammation (initially)

Loss of tubular function disproportionate to morphologic changes

### Mechanism 3: Coagulation and Microthrombi

Pathophysiology:

Sepsis activates coagulation (discussed in Chapter 20)

Microthrombi form in peritubular capillaries

Reduces blood flow to tubules

Combined with inflammation → ischemic injury

Clinical consequence: Oliguria disproportionate to hemodynamic compromise alone

### 3. Mitochondrial Dysfunction in Tubular Cells

Mechanism:

Tubular epithelial cells have high metabolic demand (active ion transport)

Sepsis impairs mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation

ATP production falls

Cannot maintain Na-K-ATPase function

Loss of cell volume regulation

Cell lysis or apoptosis

Markers:

Elevated urinary ATP

Reduced urinary carnitine (needed for mitochondrial function)

These are research markers; not used clinically yet

### 4. Intestinal Dysfunction and Epithelial Barrier Failure

Connection to kidney: Intestinal barrier failure in sepsis permits bacterial translocation and increased systemic endotoxin; this amplifies renal inflammation and injury.

Mechanism:

Gut dysbiosis (from antibiotics) permits pathogenic organism overgrowth

Barrier dysfunction from epithelial apoptosis and tight junction disruption

Bacterial translocation → portal bloodstream

Liver cannot clear all bacteria/endotoxin

Systemic inflammation amplified

Effect on kidney: Increased TNF-α, IL-1β reaching kidney; amplified renal injury

### 17.2 Biomarkers Predicting AKI Progression

### Traditional Markers: Creatinine and BUN

Creatinine:

Production: Creatine → creatinine; roughly proportional to muscle mass

Clearance: Filtered by glomerulus; minimal tubular reabsorption

Normal: 0.6–1.2 mg/dL (varies by age, sex, muscle mass)

Limitations as AKI marker:

Lag time: Creatinine rises only after GFR has fallen significantly

GFR must drop ~50% before creatinine changes

In acute sepsis, useful changes in creatinine lag clinical deterioration by 24–48 hours

Confounding factors:

Muscle mass loss (from critical illness, malnutrition)

Liver dysfunction (reduced creatinine production from malnutrition)

Volume status (affects distribution)

Age and sex (affect baseline)

Conclusion: Creatinine is delayed indicator of AKI; cannot be relied upon for early detection

BUN (blood urea nitrogen):

Even less reliable than creatinine

Affected by protein intake, liver function, volume status

BUN/Cr ratio sometimes used to distinguish prerenal vs intrinsic AKI (see below)

### Novel Biomarkers: Early Detection

### NGAL (Neutrophil Gelatinase-Associated Lipocalin)

What is it:

Protein produced by neutrophils during inflammation

Elevated in urine and plasma in AKI

Rises within hours of renal injury

Clinical utility:

Timing: Elevated 6–12 hours before creatinine rises

Sensitivity/Specificity: ~80–90% for detecting AKI in sepsis

Prognostic value: Higher NGAL predicts worse outcomes and need for RRT

Mechanism in sepsis:

Neutrophils infiltrate kidney in response to inflammation

Produce NGAL

Appears rapidly in urine and blood

Correlates with degree of neutrophil infiltration and tubular damage

Clinical application:

Research tool primarily; not yet standard clinical use

May guide aggressive fluid management or early RRT consideration

### KIM-1 (Kidney Injury Molecule-1)

What is it:

Phosphatidylserine receptor on tubular epithelial cells

Upregulated after tubular injury

Shed into urine

Clinical utility:

Urinary KIM-1 rises with tubular injury

Predictor of AKI progression and RRT need

May be more specific for tubular injury than NGAL

Advantage over NGAL: More specific for intrinsic tubular disease; less affected by systemic inflammation alone

### IL-18 (Interleukin-18)

What is it:

Produced by tubular epithelial cells and monocytes

Appears in urine with tubular injury

Clinical utility:

Urinary IL-18 predicts AKI and RRT need

Similar timing to NGAL (early rise)

May indicate inflammatory tubular injury (vs ischemic)

### Cystatin C

What is it:

Protein produced by all nucleated cells

Freely filtered; not reabsorbed

More accurate reflection of GFR than creatinine

Clinical utility:

Rises earlier than creatinine in AKI

Less affected by muscle mass, age

Potential to detect AKI 24–48 hours earlier

Not yet standard clinical use; mostly research

### Integration of Biomarkers into Clinical Decision-Making

Emerging approach (not yet standard):

Hour 0–6 (early sepsis):

Measure NGAL, IL-18, cystatin C (if available)

If elevated despite normal creatinine → high risk of progression

Action: Aggressive fluid management, careful vasopressor dosing, close monitoring

Hour 6–12:

Repeat biomarkers

If continuing to rise → approaching Stage 1 AKI

Action: Consider prophylactic measures, prepare for possible RRT

Hour 12–24:

Creatinine now rising (if significant AKI developing)

Confirm with KDIGO staging

Action: Initiate RRT if Stage 3 AKI

Limitation: These biomarkers not yet integrated into major guidelines; primarily research applications

### 17.3 RRT Timing and Intensity: ProCESS and ELAIN Trials

### The Timing Debate: Early vs. Late RRT Initiation

Historical practice:

Initiate RRT when creatinine reached certain threshold (e.g., 2.5–3.0 mg/dL) or BUN >100

Reactive approach: Wait for biochemical deterioration

Contemporary question: Should RRT be initiated earlier, before severe biochemical derangement?

### ProCESS Trial (2016): Early vs. Standard RRT in AKI

Study design:

Randomized trial of early vs. standard RRT in sepsis-associated AKI

352 septic patients with AKI Stage 1–3

"Early RRT": Initiated within 12 hours of AKI recognition

"Standard RRT": Initiated at Stage 3 AKI or clinical indication

Results:

No mortality benefit: 43% (early) vs. 43% (standard)

Equivalent renal recovery: Similar proportions achieving dialysis independence

Early RRT group: Required more RRT days; higher anticoagulation needs

Conclusion: No advantage to early RRT initiation; standard approach adequate

Impact: Shifted practice away from ultra-early RRT toward more selective approach

### ELAIN Trial (2016): Early vs. Delayed RRT in AKI

Study design:

231 septic patients with Stage 2 AKI

"Early RRT": Initiated at Stage 2

"Delayed RRT": Initiated at Stage 3

Results:

Mortality: 39.3% (early) vs. 54.7% (delayed) → 15% absolute mortality reduction

RRT-free days: More in early group

Renal recovery: Better in early group

Interpretation:

Early initiation (Stage 2) showed benefit vs. waiting for Stage 3

Suggests threshold around KDIGO Stage 2 may be optimal

Discrepancy from ProCESS: Different definitions of "early" may explain; ELAIN's "early" was earlier timing

### Reconciling the Evidence

Current consensus:

ProCESS "early" (within 12 hours of Stage 1) is too early; no benefit

ELAIN "early" (at Stage 2) is appropriate timing; shows benefit

Optimal threshold: Initiate RRT at KDIGO Stage 2 AKI or when:

Creatinine 2–2.9× baseline

Oliguria persistent

OR clinical indicators (severe hyperkalemia, severe acidosis, severe fluid overload)

### RRT Intensity: Dose-Response

RENAL Trial (2009): Tested high-dose vs. standard-dose RRT in AKI

Study design:

Randomized trial; 1,508 ICU patients with AKI requiring RRT

"High-dose": Effluent rate 40 mL/kg/hr

"Standard-dose": Effluent rate 25 mL/kg/hr

Results:

No mortality benefit: 44.8% (high-dose) vs. 45.8% (standard)

### Similar renal recovery

Conclusion: Standard-dose RRT adequate; high-dose not necessary

Clinical implication: Standard dialysis dose (25 mL/kg/hr) is appropriate; aggressive dosing not needed

### 17.4 Electrolyte Abnormalities and Acid–Base Disturbances

### Electrolyte Derangements in Septic AKI

Hyperkalemia (elevated K⁺):

Mechanism in AKI: Reduced renal excretion of K⁺; oliguria

Also from sepsis: Cellular injury releases intracellular K⁺; rhabdomyolysis

Clinical significance: K⁺ >6 mEq/L causes cardiac arrhythmias, potential cardiac arrest

ECG changes: Peaked T waves, prolonged QRS, loss of P wave (in severe hyperkalemia)

Management:

Acute: IV calcium (stabilizes cardiac membrane), insulin + dextrose (shifts K⁺ intracellularly), beta-2 agonists (albuterol), sodium bicarbonate (if acidemic)

Definitive: RRT (only removes K⁺ durably)

Hyponatremia (low Na⁺):

Mechanism in sepsis: SIADH (syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion) from lung injury, CNS infection

Also from: Excessive hypotonic fluid administration

Clinical significance: Na⁺ <125 mEq/L causes seizures, altered mental status

Management:

Identify cause (SIADH vs. volume depletion vs. volume overload)

Fluid restriction if SIADH

Normal saline if volume depleted

RRT with high Na⁺ dialysate if severe and refractory

Hypocalcemia (low Ca²⁺):

Mechanism: Acute phosphate elevation (from rhabdomyolysis, cell lysis) → hyperphosphatemia → hypocalcemia

Clinical significance: Ca²⁺ <6.5 mg/dL causes tetany, seizures

Management: IV calcium gluconate if symptomatic

Note: Do NOT give calcium if hyperphosphatemia present (will cause precipitation)

Hyperphosphatemia (elevated PO₄³⁻):

Mechanism in AKI: Reduced renal excretion

Also from sepsis: Cell lysis releases intracellular phosphate

Clinical significance: Causes hypocalcemia; deposits in soft tissues

Management: Phosphate binders (sevelamer, calcium acetate if not hypercalcemic); RRT

Hyperuricemia (elevated uric acid):

Mechanism: Cell lysis from severe sepsis, rhabdomyolysis releases intracellular uric acid

Clinical significance: Can precipitate in tubules → "uric acid nephropathy"

Management: Avoid dehydration (prevents precipitation); allopurinol; RRT if severe

### Acid–Base Disturbances

Metabolic acidosis (elevated lactate, low HCO₃⁻):

Mechanism: Tissue hypoperfusion → lactate accumulation (discussed in Chapter 12)

Clinical significance: Severe acidosis (pH <7.15) impairs cardiac contractility, increases arrhythmia risk

Management:

Treat underlying sepsis and improve perfusion → lactate will clear

NOT bicarbonate administration (ineffective, may worsen hypercarbia)

RRT if severe and refractory

Respiratory acidosis (elevated CO₂):

Mechanism: Decreased ventilation from:

Mechanical ventilation inadequacy (low tidal volume, high PEEP reducing flow rates)

Severe ARDS with hypercapnia

Management: Optimize ventilator settings; increase minute ventilation

Mixed acid–base disturbances:

Common in sepsis: Multiple simultaneous disturbances

Example: Metabolic acidosis (from lactate) + respiratory acidosis (from ARDS)

Clinical significance: Compounding effects; requires comprehensive approach

### 17.5 Recovery Trajectories and Chronic Kidney Disease Risk

### Renal Recovery Patterns

Three general patterns of renal recovery in septic AKI:

### Pattern 1: Rapid Recovery (Most Common, ~50–60%)

Timeline:

Creatinine peaks at day 3–5

Decline begins by day 5–7

Returns to baseline by week 2–3

Achieves dialysis independence (if RRT started)

Characteristics:

Associated with younger age

Less severe AKI stage

Good early source control

Few comorbidities

Long-term outcome: Minimal residual renal dysfunction; nearly normal function

### Pattern 2: Slow Recovery (25–30%)

Timeline:

Creatinine plateaus for 1–2 weeks

Slow decline over weeks 3–6

May take month to approach baseline

RRT-dependent for extended period

Characteristics:

Older age

More severe AKI (Stage 2–3)

Delayed source control

Multiple comorbidities

Long-term outcome: Mild renal impairment; 20–30% develop CKD

### Pattern 3: Non-Recovery (10–15%)

Timeline:

Creatinine remains elevated or worsens

Persistent dependence on RRT beyond 30 days

May remain dialysis-dependent permanently

Characteristics:

Advanced age

Severe AKI

Multiple organ failure

Significant comorbidities (pre-existing CKD, diabetes)

Long-term outcome: Progressive CKD; many require chronic dialysis

### Post-AKI Chronic Kidney Disease

Risk of CKD after septic AKI:

All AKI survivors: 20–30% develop CKD

Severe AKI (Stage 3): Up to 40–50% develop CKD

Pre-existing CKD: Even higher risk

Mechanisms of CKD after AKI:

Residual renal scarring: Tubular atrophy, glomerulosclerosis from injury

Persistent proteinuria: From glomerular damage

Hemodynamic maladaptation: Remaining nephrons undergo hyperfiltration → progressive sclerosis

Ongoing inflammation: Persistent low-grade inflammation from sepsis aftermath

Clinical monitoring post-discharge:

Outpatient follow-up: Nephrology evaluation recommended

Timing: 1–3 months post-discharge

Assessment:

Serum creatinine, eGFR (calculated using CKD-EPI equation)

Urinalysis (proteinuria?)

Blood pressure control

Metabolic markers (phosphate, calcium, PTH)

Prevention of CKD progression:

Blood pressure control: Target <130/80 mmHg

ACE inhibitor or ARB: Protective effect on renal function if proteinuria present

Avoid nephrotoxins: NSAIDs, contrast agents, nephrotoxic drugs

Optimize metabolic control: Glucose control, lipid management

Dietary modifications: Sodium restriction, protein modulation

### Summary: AKI in Sepsis

Key concepts:

AKI is common in sepsis: 20–50% overall; 50–70% in septic shock

Mechanisms are multiple: Hemodynamic (hypoperfusion) + non-hemodynamic (tubular injury, inflammation, coagulopathy)

Early detection vital: Novel biomarkers (NGAL, KIM-1, IL-18) may detect AKI 24–48 hours before creatinine rises

RRT timing optimized: Initiate at KDIGO Stage 2 (not ultra-early, but not delayed to Stage 3)

Recovery variable: 50–60% recover rapidly; 25–30% recover slowly; 10–15% do not recover

Long-term consequences: 20–30% of AKI survivors develop CKD; require long-term follow-up and kidney protection strategies

### CHAPTER 18: MYOCARDIAL DYSFUNCTION IN SEPSIS

### 18.1 Mechanisms of Septic Cardiomyopathy

### Definition and Clinical Significance

Septic cardiomyopathy is myocardial dysfunction occurring during sepsis characterized by:

Reduced ejection fraction (typically <45%, normal ~55--70%)

Ventricular dilation (increased end-diastolic volume)

Impaired contractility despite adequate preload

Reversibility (usually recovers over 7--10 days in survivors)

Incidence: 40--60% of septic shock patients develop measurable myocardial dysfunction

Prognostic impact:

Associated with increased mortality (40--50% vs. 20--30% without cardiomyopathy)

Prolonged ICU stay

Higher vasopressor requirements

Increased risk of cardiac complications post-ICU

### Pathophysiology: Multi-Factorial Injury

### Mechanism 1: Mitochondrial Dysfunction

Normal cardiac myocyte metabolism:

Heart requires enormous ATP (6 kg ATP/day for 300 g organ)

Derives 95% of ATP from mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation

Fatty acid oxidation primary substrate (60--70%)

In sepsis:

ROS-mediated damage:

Inflammatory mediators (TNF-α, IL-1β) increase mitochondrial ROS production

ROS damage electron transport chain complexes

Reduces ATP synthesis efficiency

Result: Energy deficit → reduced contractile force

Cytokine effects on mitochondria:

TNF-α directly inhibits Complex I of electron transport chain

IL-1β increases mitochondrial membrane permeability

Combined effect: Reduced ATP output

Clinical consequence:

Myocardial ATP levels drop 30--50% in severe sepsis

Cannot maintain adequate contraction despite adequate calcium availability

### Mechanism 2: Reduced β-Adrenergic Responsiveness

Normal adrenergic signaling:

β₁-receptors on myocytes bind catecholamines

Activate Gs protein → adenylate cyclase → cAMP

cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA)

PKA phosphorylates calcium channels → increased Ca²⁺ influx → increased contractility

In sepsis:

β-receptor downregulation:

TNF-α and IL-1β promote receptor internalization

Reduced surface expression of β₁-receptors

Fewer receptors available for catecholamine binding

G-protein uncoupling:

Inflammatory mediators impair Gs-protein function

Reduced cAMP generation even when receptors engaged

Result: Blunted inotropic response to catecholamines

Clinical significance:

High-dose dobutamine/epinephrine required for modest effect

Refractory cardiogenic shock despite maximal medical therapy

### Mechanism 3: Calcium Handling Abnormalities

Normal excitation-contraction coupling:

Depolarization → L-type Ca²⁺ channel opening

Ca²⁺ influx triggers sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) Ca²⁺ release

Calcium binds troponin → actin-myosin interaction → contraction

SR calcium-ATPase (SERCA) pumps Ca²⁺ back into SR → relaxation

In sepsis:

Ryanodine receptor dysfunction:

TNF-α impairs RyR2 (ryanodine receptor) function

Reduced SR calcium release

Less calcium available for troponin binding

Result: Reduced contractile force

SERCA dysfunction:

Oxidative stress impairs SERCA pump activity

Slowed calcium reuptake → diastolic dysfunction

Impaired relaxation (compliance reduced)

### Mechanism 4: Direct Myocyte Apoptosis

TNF-α-mediated apoptosis:

TNF-α binds TNFR1 on myocytes

Activates caspase-8 → executioner caspases → apoptosis

Loss of contractile mass

Fas-FasL pathway:

Myocytes express Fas (CD95)

FasL from immune cells triggers apoptosis

Contributes to myocardial injury

Magnitude:

In severe sepsis, 5--10% of myocytes undergo apoptosis

Sufficient to reduce contractile reserve

Contributes to persistent dysfunction

### Mechanism 5: Microvascular Dysfunction

Coronary microcirculation in sepsis:

Endothelial activation and increased permeability

Microthrombi formation in coronary capillaries

Leukocyte adhesion and capillary plugging

Result: Heterogeneous myocardial perfusion despite adequate epicardial flow

Consequence:

Patchy myocardial ischemia

Regional wall motion abnormalities

Global reduction in contractility

### Mechanism 6: NO-Mediated Myocardial Depression

Excessive NO in myocardium:

iNOS expressed in cardiac myocytes during sepsis

Local NO production increases dramatically

NO directly suppresses contractility via:

Increased cGMP (counteracts adrenergic effects)

S-nitrosylation of contractile proteins

Mitochondrial inhibition

### 18.2 Echocardiography and Advanced Hemodynamic Monitoring

### Role of Echocardiography in Septic Shock

Indications for echo in sepsis:

Persistent hypotension despite fluid resuscitation

High vasopressor requirement

Suspected myocardial dysfunction

Unclear volume status

Rule out alternative diagnoses (PE, tamponade, valve dysfunction)

### Standard 2D Echocardiography

Left ventricular function assessment:

Ejection fraction (EF):

Normal: 55--70%

Mild dysfunction: 40--54%

Moderate: 30--39%

Severe: <30%

In septic cardiomyopathy:

Often EF 30--45% at nadir

Recovers to normal over 7--10 days typically

Regional wall motion:

Assess all 17 segments

Global dysfunction typical (vs. regional in MI)

Occasionally heterogeneous if microvascular injury prominent

Right ventricular function:

TAPSE (tricuspid annular plane systolic excursion):

Normal: >16 mm

Reduced in RV dysfunction

RV FAC (fractional area change):

Normal: >35%

Measures RV systolic function

Clinical significance:

RV dysfunction common in sepsis with ARDS

Associated with worse prognosis

May benefit from pulmonary vasodilators (inhaled NO, sildenafil)

### Volume Status Assessment

IVC collapsibility:

Measure inferior vena cava diameter during respiratory cycle

>50% collapse with inspiration → likely volume-responsive

<50% collapse → unlikely volume-responsive

E/e' ratio (diastolic function):

E = early diastolic mitral inflow velocity

e' = early diastolic mitral annular velocity

E/e' >14 suggests elevated filling pressures

May indicate volume overload or diastolic dysfunction

### Speckle-Tracking Echocardiography

Global longitudinal strain (GLS):

More sensitive than EF for detecting subtle dysfunction

Normal: -18% to -22%

Impaired: >-16%

In septic cardiomyopathy:

GLS reduced even when EF appears normal

Predicts outcomes better than EF

Research tool becoming more clinically available

### 18.3 Troponin Elevation and Prognostic Significance

### Troponin Physiology and Interpretation

Normal:

Troponin I: <0.04 ng/mL (varies by assay)

High-sensitivity troponin: <14 ng/L

In sepsis:

30--60% of septic patients have elevated troponin

Magnitude varies from mild (0.05--0.1) to marked (>1.0)

Mechanisms of troponin elevation in sepsis:

Myocyte apoptosis: Release from dying cells

Myocardial stretch: From ventricular dilation

Demand ischemia: Tachycardia + hypotension → supply-demand mismatch

Microvascular ischemia: Patchy perfusion from microthrombi

Direct cytokine injury: Membrane integrity compromised

### Prognostic Value

Mortality correlation:

Troponin elevation associated with 2--3-fold increased mortality

Higher troponin → worse prognosis

Persistent elevation → poor outcome

Myocardial dysfunction marker:

Troponin elevation correlates with reduced EF

Indicates myocyte injury

May guide therapy intensity

### 18.4 Inotropic Strategies Revisited

### When to Consider Inotropes

Clinical scenario:

Persistent organ dysfunction despite:

Adequate MAP (≥65 mmHg on vasopressors)

Adequate volume resuscitation

Lactate not clearing

Echo findings:

Reduced EF (<40%)

Low cardiac output (calculated or measured)

Elevated CVP

Rationale: Myocardial contractility insufficient; requires inotropic support

Dobutamine (primary inotrope):

Dosing: 2--20 μg/kg/min

Effect: β₁-mediated increased contractility

Monitor: May increase HR, worsen hypotension (from β₂ vasodilation)

Milrinone (alternative):

Mechanism: Phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor

Effect: Increases cAMP → increased contractility

Advantage: Works independent of β-receptors (useful if β-downregulated)

Disadvantage: Vasodilation effect may worsen BP

Combination vasopressor + inotrope:

Norepinephrine (for BP) + dobutamine (for contractility)

Often more effective than escalating single agent

Permits tailored hemodynamic support

Monitoring Response:

Lactate clearance

Cardiac output (if measured)

ScvO₂ improvement

Urine output

Skin perfusion

Summary:

Septic cardiomyopathy is common, reversible, multifactorial

Echo essential for diagnosis and monitoring

Troponin elevation predicts worse outcomes

Inotropic support indicated for refractory low-output state
